## THE

# DEAN OF LISMORE'S BOOK <note>This refers to the 16th-century manuscript compiled by James MacGregor, the Dean of Lismore in Argyll, and his brother Duncan. It is one of the most significant collections of Gaelic poetry in existence.</note>

<insert>la</insert> <note>original: "la". This is likely a folio or library shelf-mark.</note>

<term>The Dean of Lismore's Book</term>: A famous manuscript compiled between 1512 and 1526. It is unique because the Gaelic poems were written using the phonetic spelling of Middle Scots rather than traditional Gaelic orthography.

<term>James MacGregor</term>: The Dean (a high-ranking church official) of Lismore Cathedral and a notary public. He and his family were members of the scholarly class in the Scottish Highlands.

<term>Gaelic poetry</term>: The collection contains a wide variety of verse, including "Ossianic" ballads about the legendary hero Fionn mac Cumhaill, as well as works by Scottish and Irish "bardic" poets.

<term>Scottish literature</term>: This book is a cornerstone of Scottish heritage, providing a rare window into the literary culture of the Gaelic-speaking West Highlands before the modern era.

<summary>This page is the title leaf for the "Book of the Dean of Lismore," a crucial 16th-century compilation of Scottish and Irish Gaelic verse. Its preservation is vital for the study of medieval and early modern Celtic literature.</summary>

<keywords>Dean of Lismore, James MacGregor, Gaelic poetry, Scottish literature, 16th century, Ossianic ballads, Highland history</keywords>

## THE

# DEAN OF LISMORE'S BOOK <note>This refers to the 16th-century manuscript compiled by James MacGregor, the Dean of Lismore in Argyll, and his brother Duncan. It is one of the most significant collections of Gaelic poetry in existence.</note>

<insert>la</insert> <note>original: "la". This is likely a folio or library shelf-mark used by catalogers.</note>

<term>The Dean of Lismore's Book</term>: A famous manuscript compiled between 1512 and 1526. It is unique because the Gaelic poems were written using the phonetic spelling of Middle Scots (the language of the Lowlands) rather than traditional Gaelic orthography.

<term>James MacGregor</term>: The Dean (a high-ranking church official) of Lismore Cathedral and a notary public. He and his family were members of the scholarly class in the Scottish Highlands.

<term>Gaelic poetry</term>: The collection contains a wide variety of verse, including "Ossianic" ballads about the legendary hero Fionn mac Cumhaill, as well as works by Scottish and Irish "bardic" poets.

<term>Scottish literature</term>: This book is a cornerstone of Scottish heritage, providing a rare window into the literary culture of the Gaelic-speaking West Highlands before the modern era.

<summary>This page contains the publication details and formal title for the 19th-century printed edition of the "Book of the Dean of Lismore," a vital 16th-century Gaelic manuscript. It lists the prominent publishers across Edinburgh, London, Cambridge, Dublin, and Glasgow who brought this Highland treasure to a wider scholarly audience.</summary>

<keywords>Dean of Lismore, James MacGregor, Thomas Constable, Edmonston and Douglas, Gaelic poetry, Scottish literature, 19th-century publishing, Ossianic ballads</keywords>

->THE<-

# DEAN OF LISMORE'S BOOK
<note>James MacGregor (died c. 1551) served as the Dean of Lismore Cathedral in Argyll. Between 1512 and 1526, he and his brother Duncan compiled a unique manuscript—now known by this title—which is the oldest surviving collection of "Heroic" Gaelic poetry from the Scottish Highlands.</note>

->A SELECTION OF<-

## ANCIENT GAELIC POETRY
<term>Gaelic: The native Celtic language of the Scottish Highlands and Ireland. The poetry in this collection represents a shared cultural heritage between the two regions, including many poems attributed to the legendary warrior-poet Ossian.</term>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page serves as the opening of the introduction. It follows a blank leaf in the physical manuscript. The text establishes the geographical setting of the work in the Scottish Highlands.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

IN the heart of the Perthshire Highlands, and not far from the northern shore of Loch Tay, there lies a secluded vale of about six miles long. The river Lyon, which issues from the long and narrow valley of Glenlyon through the pass of Chesthill, hardly less beautiful than the celebrated pass of Killiecrankie <note>A famous mountain pass in Perthshire, known for its scenic beauty and the 1689 Jacobite battle.</note>, meanders through it. On the east bank of a small stream which falls into the Lyon about the centre of the vale, is the <term>Clachan</term> <note>Gaelic: a small settlement or hamlet, typically associated with a church.</note> or <term>Kirkton</term> <note>Scots: a village or hamlet containing a parish church.</note> of Fortingall, anciently called Fothergill, from which it takes its name; and on the west or opposite bank is the mansion of Glenlyon House, anciently called Tullichmullin.

A stranger stationed at the clachan or little village of Fortingall, would almost fancy that there was neither egress from nor ingress to this little district, so secluded and shut in among the surrounding mountains does it appear to be. It is a spot where one could well suppose that the traditions of former times, and the remains of a forgotten oral literature, might still linger in the memories of its inhabitants; while the local names of the

<summary>This introductory page describes the geography of Fortingall in the Perthshire Highlands, emphasizing its isolation and potential as a repository for ancient oral traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Perthshire Highlands, Loch Tay, River Lyon, Fortingall, Glenlyon, Scottish geography, oral tradition</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction from page i, moving from the physical landscape of Fortingall to its mythological associations and the history of the Macgregor family who lived there.</meta>

mountains and streams about it are redolent of the mythic times of the <term>Feine</term> <note>The Fenians, a legendary band of warriors in Gaelic mythology led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>. On the west is the glen of Glenlyon, the ancient *Crooked Glen of the Stones* <note>original: *Cromgleann nan Clach*</note>, associated with many a tradition of the <term>Feine</term>, and where the remains of those rude forts, termed *Castles of the Fenians* <note>original: *Caistealan na Feine*</note>, crown many a rocky summit; and the vale is bounded on the south and east by the ridge of *Finn’s Ridge* <note>original: *Druimfhionn*</note>.

In the latter part of the fifteenth and beginning of the sixteenth centuries, there dwelt here a family of the name of Macgregor. They were descended from a vicar of Fortingall, who, at the time when, during the century preceding the Reformation, the Catholic Church was breaking up, and their <term>benefices</term> <note>A church office that granted the holder property and income.</note> passing into the hands of laymen, secured for himself and his descendants the vicarage of Fortingall and a lease of the church lands.

Of the history of this family we know somewhat from an obituary commenced by one of his descendants, and continued to the year 1579 by the Curate of Fothergill, which is still preserved.

His son, whether legitimate or illegitimate we know not, was *John the Grizzled* <note>original: *Ian Rewych*</note>, termed *Son of the Vicar* <note>original: *Makgewykar*</note>.^1

His grandson was *Dougall the Bald* or *Tonsured* <note>original: *Dougall Maol*; "Tonsured" refers to the shaved crown of a head, common for monks or clergy.</note>, called <term>patronymically</term> <note>A name passed down from a father or male ancestor.</note> Dougall Johnson, or the son of John.

This Dougall Johnson appears in 1511 as a notary—

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

public,¹ and lived at Tullichmullin, where his wife Katherine, daughter of Donald M'Clawe, *otherwise* Grant, died in 1512.² He is mentioned twice in the obituary or Chronicle of Fortingall; first in 1526, as repairing the base of the cross in Inchadin (the old church of Kenmore, situated on the north bank of the river Tay, nearly opposite Taymouth Castle); and again in 1529, as placing a stone cross in Larkmonemerkyth, which is the name of a mountain pass leading south from Inchadin.³

Of Dougall the Bald, the son of John the Grizzled, we have no further records; but we know of two sons from his family, James and Duncan.

James was a member of the clergy. He appears as a <term>notary-public</term> <note>A legal official, a role typically held by educated clerics at the time, responsible for drafting and witnessing legal documents.</note>, an office then held by churchmen, alongside his father in the year 1511. He achieved honor and influence early in his career, though the exact path is unknown; by 1514, we find him serving as the Dean of Lismore,⁴ an island in Argyllshire located between the districts of Lorn and Morvern, which served as the <term>Episcopal seat</term> <note>The official headquarters or "see" of a Bishop.</note> for the Bishops of Argyll. He was, in addition, the Vicar of Fortingall and the *Firmarius* <note>original Latin: "Firmarius," meaning a tacksman or a tenant who pays a fixed rent for the rights to church lands.</note> or tenant of the church lands. He died holding these church offices in the year 1551, and

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

was buried in the choir of the old church of Inchadin.¹

In 1552, a year after his death, Gregor Macgregor—son of the deceased Sir James Macgregor, Dean of Lismore—acting as the leader of a small but independent <term>sept</term> <note>A branch or sub-division of a larger clan.</note> of the Macgregors and with a careful eye toward its safety, bound himself to Colin Campbell of Glenurchy and his heirs. In doing so, he "took him for his chief, in place of the Laird of Macgregor, and gave him his <term>calp</term>." ² <note>A "calp" was a traditional Gaelic tribute or death-duty paid by a follower to a chief to acknowledge their authority and secure protection.</note>

In 1557, Gregor and Dougall Macgregor, the <term>natural sons</term> <note>Children born outside of a legal marriage; "legitimation" was a legal process to give them the rights of legitimate heirs.</note> of Sir James Macgregor, received letters of legitimation;³ and, in 1574, Dougall Macgregor appears as the Chancellor of Lismore.⁴

It is unnecessary for our purpose to follow the history of this family any further; suffice it to say, that the two brothers, James and Duncan,⁵ members of a clan which—though under the ban of the Government and exposed to the grasping aggression of their powerful neighbors, the Campbells of Glenurchy—considered themselves as peculiarly Highland, and had high pretensions, as descendants of the old Celtic monarchs of Scotland—connected with the Church, and as such, possessing some

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

cultivation of mind and such literary taste as Churchmen at that time had, yet born and reared in the farm-house of Tullichmullin, in the secluded vale of Fortingall, and imbued with that love of old Highland story and cherished fondness for Highland song, which manifests itself in so many a quiet country Highlander, and which the scenery and associations around them were so well calculated to foster—the one, from his high position in the Church of Argyll, having peculiar facilities for collecting the poetry current in the West Highlands—the other, though his brother, yet, as was not uncommon in those days, his servitor <note>A personal attendant or assistant, often a younger relative acting as an apprentice or secretary.</note> or <term>amanuensis</term> <note>A person employed to write or type what another dictates or to copy manuscripts.</note>, and himself a poet—and both natives of the Perthshire Highlands—collected and transcribed into a <term>commonplace book</term> <note>A personal journal used to compile knowledge, poems, and notes for future reference; in this case, it preserved a vast oral tradition.</note> Gaelic poetry obtained from all quarters.

This collection has fortunately been preserved. It is, unquestionably, a native compilation made in the central Highlands, upwards of three hundred years ago. It contains the remains of an otherwise lost literature. In it we find all that we can now recover of native compositions current in the Highlands prior to the sixteenth century, as well as the means of ascertaining the extent to which the Highlanders were familiar with the works of Irish poets.

It is a <term>quarto</term> <note>A book size where each sheet of paper is folded twice, creating four leaves or eight pages; usually a medium-sized volume.</note> volume of some 311 pages, and is written in the current <term>Roman hand</term> <note>The style of handwriting common in Western Europe, distinct from the specialized 'Gaelic script' typically used by professional Irish and Scottish scribes of the era.</note> of the period. Though much injured by time, the leaves in part worn away, and the ink faint, it is still possible to read the greater part of its contents.

The manuscript, known as a quarto volume, is a vital record of Highland and Irish literature that would otherwise have been lost to history.</summary>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

With the exception of a short Latin obituary <note>A record of a person's death and a brief account of their life, common in church records.</note>, and one or two other short pieces, it consists entirely of a collection of Gaelic poetry made by the two brothers.

At the bottom of the 27th page appears the following note in Latin:—*The book of Lord James Macgregor, Dean of Lismore.* <note>original Latin: "Liber Dni Jacobi Macgregor Decani Lismoren."</note>

At page 78, there is a chronological list of Scottish kings written in the Scottish language <note>Referring here to Scots, the Germanic language of the Lowlands, which was often used for official records in this period.</note>, which ends thus:— “*James the Fifth reigns now in great happiness.*” <note>original Scots: "James the Fyfte reignis now in great felicitie."</note> He reigned from 1513 to 1542; and, on page 144, there is a genealogy of the Macgregors, written by the brother Duncan, deducing their descent from the old Scottish kings, and he adds a <term>docquet</term> <note>A brief summary or official note written on a document to identify its contents.</note> in Gaelic, which may be thus translated:—Duncan the Servitor <note>A personal assistant or secretary, often of noble birth but serving a higher-ranking relative.</note>, the son of Dougall, who was son of John the Grizzled, wrote this from the Book of the History of the Kings, and it was done in the year 1512.¹

There can be no question, therefore, that this collection was formed during the lifetime of the Dean of Lismore, and a great part of it as early as the year 1512. How it was preserved through that and the succeeding century is unknown. In the last century it passed into the possession of the Highland Society of London, by whom it was transferred to the custody of the Highland Society of Scotland, when a committee of that Society was engaged in an inquiry into the authenticity of the *Poems of Ossian*, published by Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796) claimed to have translated ancient epic poems by a bard named Ossian; the discovery of this manuscript was vital in investigating his claims.</note>. It has now been deposited, along

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

with other Gaelic <term>manuscripts</term> <note>Abbreviated as MSS. in the original text.</note> in the possession of that Society, in the Library of the Faculty of Advocates <note>The professional body for Scottish lawyers; their library in Edinburgh eventually became the core of the National Library of Scotland.</note>, and forms part of that collection of Gaelic <term>manuscripts</term> which have been brought together within the last few years, and contain nearly all the Gaelic <term>manuscripts</term> which are known still to exist.¹

The Dean’s <term>manuscript</term> differs from all the other <term>manuscripts</term> in that collection in two essential particulars. It is not, like the other <term>manuscripts</term>, written in what is called the Irish character <note>The traditional, decorative script used for Gaelic across Ireland and Scotland until the early modern period.</note>, but in the current Roman character <note>The standard alphabet used for English and most Western European languages.</note> of the early part of the sixteenth century; and the language is not written in the orthography <note>The conventional system of spelling a language.</note> used in writing Irish, and now universally employed in writing Scotch Gaelic, but in a peculiar kind of phonetic orthography, which aims at presenting the words in English orthography as they are pronounced.

The peculiar orthography employed is, however, evidently not the mere attempt of a person ignorant of the—

---

¹ This collection has been formed within the last few years mainly through the instrumentality of the writer <note>Likely William Forbes Skene, the historian and antiquary who edited this volume.</note>. The writer formed the plan of collecting the remains of the <term>manuscript</term> Gaelic literature of Scotland, which was rapidly disappearing, into one place, where they could be preserved, by inducing the possessors of Gaelic <term>manuscripts</term> to deposit them in some public library for preservation; and as the Faculty of Advocates were already in the possession of some <term>manuscripts</term>, their library was evidently the most appropriate depository for this purpose. It is hoped that, if any Gaelic <term>manuscripts</term> still remain in the hands of individual possessors, they will add to the value of this collection by making them known, and depositing them in the Advocates' Library for preservation.

The <term>manuscripts</term> are preserved in a locked cabinet, and a general catalogue of the whole has been prepared by the writer.

<meta>This page continues the introduction’s discussion of the unique writing system used in the Dean of Lismore's manuscript. It transitions from the previous page's point that the author was not merely ignorant of standard spelling, but was likely following a distinct regional tradition.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

proper <term>orthography</term> <note>The conventional system of spelling a language.</note> to write the words in English letters in an arbitrary manner, so as to present, as nearly as possible, the sound of the words as they struck his ear when repeated to him, but bears evident marks of having been a regular and known system of <term>orthography</term>, which, although we have few specimens of it left, may not the less have once prevailed in that part of the Highlands <note>The mountainous region of northern Scotland where Gaelic culture and language remained strongest.</note> more removed from the influence of Irish teaching.

It is a peculiarity of all the Celtic dialects, that the consonants suffer a change in the beginning of words, from the influence of the preceding words, or in forming the <term>oblique cases</term> <note>Grammatical forms of a noun used when it is the object of a sentence or follows a preposition, rather than being the subject.</note>, and likewise change their sound in the middle of words by being <term>aspirated</term> <note>In Celtic languages, this refers to a softening of a consonant sound (like 'p' becoming 'ph'), often indicated by adding an 'h'.</note>.

In the Irish <term>orthography</term>, the original consonant is invariably preserved; and the change is indicated by prefixing another consonant when the sound is affected by <term>eclipsis</term> <note>A linguistic feature where the initial sound of a word is suppressed and replaced by a different consonant sound.</note>, or the influence of the preceding word, or by adding the letter *h*, when it is changed by aspiration.

In the Welsh dialects, however, and in the Manx <note>The Gaelic language native to the Isle of Man.</note>, which is a dialect of the Gaelic division of the Celtic languages, a different system of <term>orthography</term> has always prevailed. Instead of retaining the original letter, and indicating the change in the sound by prefixing or adding another, a different letter expressing simply the new sound, is substituted for the original letter; and hence the <term>orthography</term> bears more of a <term>phonetic</term> <note>A system where words are spelled exactly as they sound.</note> and less of an <term>etymologic</term> <note>A system where spelling reflects the history and origin of the word rather than just its current sound.</note> character.

Both systems have their advantages and disadvantages. In the one, the original form of the word is preserved,

<summary>This page argues that the phonetic spelling in the Dean of Lismore's manuscript was a formal regional system rather than a mistake, comparing it to the spelling conventions of Irish, Welsh, and Manx.</summary>
<keywords>orthography, Scottish Highlands, Gaelic linguistics, Irish spelling, Manx language, phonetic vs etymological spelling, consonant mutation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Introduction's comparison between two methods of writing Gaelic. The previous page ended while describing the Welsh system, and this page begins mid-sentence, concluding the description of the more traditional "etymologic" system (associated with Irish Gaelic) before contrasting it with the phonetic system used in the Dean of Lismore's manuscript.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

and the primary sounds of the letters are retained. The alteration in their sound in <term>inflection</term> <note>The modification of a word to express grammatical categories such as tense, case, or number.</note> is marked by pre-fixing another letter, or adding the letter *h*. It is by the application of grammatical rules that the pronunciation of the word is ascertained, and that pronunciation may deviate from time to time to a greater degree from the original standard, while the <term>orthography</term> <note>The conventional system of spelling a language.</note>, always re-maining the same, fails to chronicle it.

In the other <note>Referring to the phonetic system used by the author of this manuscript.</note>, a new letter is substituted for the original letter, when the sound is changed by <term>inflection</term>, or by the position of the word in a sentence, and the <term>orthography</term> employed expresses the pronunciation of the word in its inflected, without reference to its original form.

The one presents the language in its <term>etymologic form</term> <note>A way of spelling that focuses on the historical origin and "root" of a word rather than its current sound.</note>, without reference to its pronunciation, and is of value in preserving the original form of the written speech.

The other stereotypes its sounds as spoken at the time; it is committed to writing without reference to the ori-ginal form or primary shape of the words; and is of value in exhibiting the living <term>dialects</term> <note>Regional varieties of a language.</note> as spoken by the people.

An apt illustration of this is afforded by the English language and its dialects.

The English <term>orthography</term> exhibits the language as it once was, but from which it has greatly deviated in pro-nunciation; and it is hardly possible to frame rules by applying which, to the <term>orthography</term>, the present pro-nunciation can be deduced. It is obvious that if the words, which are differently pronounced in the Scotch dialect, were spelt according to the English <term>orthography</term>,

The author argues that while standard spelling preserves history, the manuscript's phonetic approach preserves the living language of its time.</summary>

no clue would be afforded to its peculiarities. On the other hand, when the Scotch <note>Often referred to today as the Scots language or dialect.</note> dialect is spelt phonetically, as is done, for instance, by Sir Walter Scott in his Scotch romances, the peculiarities in the pronunciation of a living dialect are vividly presented, and these elements of the original language, which may have been preserved in this dialect, are made available for <term>philological</term> <note>The study of the history and development of languages.</note> purposes.

The collection of Gaelic poetry made by the Dean of Lismore <note>James MacGregor (c. 1480–1551), a church official in the Scottish Highlands who compiled a vital collection of Gaelic verse.</note> and his brother is thus written in an <term>orthography</term> <note>A system of spelling.</note> of this latter class <note>Meaning it was written as it sounded to the editors, rather than using traditional Irish/Gaelic spelling rules.</note>. It attracted some notice when the Highland Society was engaged in its inquiry into the authenticity of **Ossian’s Poems** <note>A famous 18th-century literary controversy regarding whether James Macpherson's "Ossian" poems were genuine translations of ancient Gaelic epics.</note>, from its including among its contents some poems attributed to Ossian. Three of these are printed in the report, though incorrectly and imperfectly, but little was known of the other contents of the <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript.</note>

A transcript was made of the <term>MS.</term> for the Highland Society by the late Mr. Ewen M'Lachlan, an accomplished Gaelic scholar, who was employed to examine their <term>MS.</term> It, however, passed into the possession of the Rev. J. Macintyre of Kilmanievaig, who allowed it to be examined for a short time by the editors, but no full or correct account was given of the <term>MS.</term> till the Rev. T. M'Lauchlan, one of the editors of this work, read an account of it to the Society of Scottish Antiquaries in the year 1856, which is printed in their proceedings.¹ This account attracted considerable notice to the <term>MS.</term>, and led to its value being more appreciated.

<meta>This page concludes the opening argument for the publication’s necessity, moving from the history of the Highland Society’s transcripts (mentioned on the previous page) to the specific value of the Dean of Lismore’s manuscript as a unique record of both the Gaelic language and its oral traditions.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

The present publication has, in consequence, been undertaken.

The Dean’s <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript; referring to the Book of the Dean of Lismore, compiled in the early 16th century.</note> has a double value, philological <note>Related to the historical and comparative study of languages.</note> and literary, and is calculated to throw light both on the language and the literature of the Highlands of Scotland. It has a philological value, because its peculiar orthography <note>A system of spelling; here, the Dean used a phonetic system based on Middle Scots rather than standard Gaelic rules.</note> presents the language at the time in its aspect and character as a spoken language, and enables us to ascertain whether many of the peculiarities which now distinguish it were in existence three hundred years ago; and it has a literary value, because it contains poems attributed to **Ossian** <note>The legendary blind bard of the 3rd century to whom much ancient Gaelic poetry is attributed.</note>, and to other poets prior to the sixteenth century, which are not to be found elsewhere; and thus presents to us specimens of the traditionary poetry current in the Highlands prior to that period, which are above suspicion, having been collected upwards of three hundred years ago, and before any controversy on the subject had arisen <note>Refers to the 18th-century debate over the authenticity of James Macpherson’s translations of Ossian.</note>.

It has been found impossible to present so large a collection entire, but the selection has been made with reference to these two objects. Each poem selected for publication has been presented entire. There is a literal translation of the poem made by the Rev. T. M‘Lauchlan, and appended is the original Gaelic text of the poem in the Dean’s orthography, exactly as it appears in the <term>MS.</term>; and, on the opposite page, the same Gaelic has been transferred by Mr. M‘Lauchlan into the modern orthography of the Scotch Gaelic, which is nearly the same as that of the Irish, so as to afford the means of comparing—

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

...the one <term>orthography</term> <note>The conventional system of spelling; here referring to the contrast between the Dean's phonetic spelling and standard Gaelic spelling.</note> with the other, and the modern spoken dialect in the Highlands with the language of the poems collected by the Dean <note>James MacGregor, the Dean of Lismore, who compiled the manuscript between 1512 and 1526.</note> upwards of three hundred years ago, as well as to furnish a test of the accuracy of the translation, by showing the rendering given to the Dean's language.^1

The present spoken language of the Highlands of Scotland is, as is well known, a dialect^2 of that great branch of the Celtic languages termed the **Gwyddelian** <note>A term for the Goidelic or Gaelic branch of Celtic languages.</note> or **Gaelic**, and to which belong also the Irish and **Manx**, or spoken language of the Isle of Man. These three dialects of the Gaelic branch of the Celtic languages—the Irish, the Scotch Gaelic, and the Manx—approach each other so nearly as to form in fact but one language; and the peculiarities which distinguish them from one another are not of a nature sufficiently broad or vital to constitute either of them a distinct language.

The language spoken by the Highlanders of Scotland is termed by them simply **Gaelic**; but the name of **Erse** <note>A historical Scots/English term for Gaelic, derived from "Irish."</note>...

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

<columns>2</columns>

...has occasionally been bestowed upon it during the last few centuries by the Lowlanders.^1 As early as the year 1690, a short vocabulary of Scottish Gaelic words was added to an edition of Bedel’s Irish Bible <note>William Bedell (1571–1642) was an Anglican bishop whose translation of the Old Testament into Irish was a landmark linguistic work.</note>, to adapt it for the use of the Scottish Highlanders; and a somewhat fuller vocabulary, by the same author, was published in Nicolson’s *Scottish Historical Library* in 1702.

In 1741, a more complete vocabulary was published by the Society for Propagating Christian Knowledge <note>A missionary organization founded in 1709, often abbreviated as the SPCK, which played a major role in Highland education.</note>, for the use of their schools in the Highlands. It was compiled by Mr. Alexander MacDonald, a schoolmaster at Ardnamurchan. Another vocabulary was published in 1795 by Robert MacFarlane; and, in 1815, a further step forward was made by the larger vocabulary of Mr. P. MacFarlane.

In 1825, a large quarto dictionary of Scottish Gaelic was published by R. A. Armstrong; and three years later, the splendid dictionary compiled by the foremost Gaelic scholars, under the auspices of the Highland Society of Scotland, appeared. In this dictionary, Gaelic words from all sources are included; but those which belong to the everyday dialect of the Scottish Highlands are carefully distinguished.

The small dictionary compiled by MacAlpine, a schoolmaster in Islay, provides a genuine representation of the Gaelic spoken on that island.

It is curious that the same reproach was also

<column-break/>

applied by the English to the Scots in the twelfth century. Lambarde <note>William Lambarde (1536–1601), an English antiquary and legal historian.</note> records that, at the Battle of the Standard <note>A significant battle in 1138 where English forces defeated a Scottish army led by King David I.</note>, when the Scots shouted *Albany, Albany!* <note>original: "Albany, Albany!" — a cry referencing the Gaelic name for Scotland, Alba.</note> the English soldiers retorted with *Yry, Yry!* <note>original: "Yry, Yry!" — an archaic, derogatory phonetic spelling of "Irish."</note> “a term of great reproach at that time.”

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

<columns>2</columns>

The only grammar of Scottish Gaelic that is necessary to mention is the capable and philosophical grammar by Alexander Stewart, a Minister of the Gospel at Dingwall <note>A town in the northern Highlands of Scotland.</note>, the first edition of which was published in 1801 and the second in 1812. As a first attempt to organize the spoken language of the Scottish Highlanders into a grammatical system, it is a work of rare excellence and accuracy, and all other grammars have been more or less based upon it.

This grammar, then, and the vocabularies and dictionaries mentioned above, contain the Gaelic language as it was spoken in the Highlands of Scotland at the time of their compilation. They provide the materials for judging the nature of those specific traits which distinguish it from Irish and Manx Gaelic <note>The Gaelic language spoken on the Isle of Man.</note>.¹

The differences between the spoken language of the Scottish Highlands and the Irish language exist partly in the pronunciation, where the stress is placed differently, and in that peculiar change to the initial consonant produced by the influence of the preceding word—termed by Irish grammarians <term>eclipsis</term> <note>A linguistic feature where the first letter of a word is silenced or "eclipsed" by another letter; while central to Irish, it is largely absent in Scottish Gaelic.</note>. This feature is unknown in Scottish Gaelic except in words beginning with 's'. Furthermore, the vowel sounds are different,

---

¹ I exclude from this list the grammar and dictionary by the Rev. William Shaw, published in 1778 and 1780, because, insofar as they claim to be a grammar and dictionary of the Scottish Gaelic dialect, they are deceptive and untrustworthy.

Shaw was a native of Arran <note>An island in the Firth of Clyde; its dialect was often considered a bridge between Scottish and Irish Gaelic.</note>, where a corrupted and Irish-influenced Gaelic is spoken. It is well known that he failed in his attempt to compile his dictionary from the spoken language in the Highlands, where he made a tour for this pur-

<column-break/>

pose, and instead resorted to Ireland. There, he manufactured his works from Irish sources and authorities, adapting Irish grammar to his own very imperfect knowledge of the language.

The subscribers to his book complained of the deception and refused to accept the work until they were compelled to do so by a lawsuit. The evidence gathered in this legal process is very instructive regarding the status of Shaw’s grammar and dictionary—specifically their Irish elements—compared to the actual Scottish Gaelic dialect of that period.

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

and there are even traces of a change in initial consonants <term>consonantal permutation</term> <note>A linguistic process where the start of a word changes based on the word preceding it; this relates to the "eclipsis" mentioned on the previous page.</note>; partly in the grammar, where Scottish Gaelic prefers the analytic form of the verb <note>A style of grammar that uses separate helper words—like "I am" or "I will"—instead of changing the ending of the verb itself.</note>, and has no simple present tense. In Scottish Gaelic, the old present tense is now used to express the future, and the present is formed instead by using an auxiliary <note>helper</note> verb. Furthermore, the plural of one class of nouns is formed in a peculiar manner that resembles Anglo-Saxon <note>Old English</note>, and a different word for "not" is used. 

Differences are also found in the idioms of the language, where there is a strong preference for expressing ideas using nouns <note>substantives</note> while carefully avoiding the use of verbs. Finally, the vocabulary varies to a considerable extent; words that are now obsolete in Irish are still living words in Scotland, and other words are used with entirely different meanings.¹

Scottish Gaelic is spoken in its greatest purity in the central districts of the Highlands, including Mull, Morvern, Ardnamurchan, Ardgowar, Appin, Lochaber, and the district termed the *Garbh chriochan*, or rough bounds <note>original: "Garbh chriochan"</note>, consisting of Arisaig, Moydart, Moror, and Knoydart. The language spoken here is characterized by a closer adherence to grammatical rules, a fuller and more careful pronunciation of the vowel sounds, and a selection of the best words to express an idea in their primary sense.

In the county of Argyll and the islands which face the coast of Ireland, the language approaches much more nearly to the Ulster dialect of Irish. In these areas, there is probably no perceptible difference between the form of the—

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the regional variations of the Gaelic language across Scotland and Ireland, specifically contrasting the dialects found in the northern and southern Highlands against the "purest" central dialect.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

language in Islay and Rathlin <note>original: "Isla and Rachrin"</note>, or in Kintyre <note>original: "Cantyre"</note> and the opposite coast of Antrim.

In the Gaelic of Sutherland and Caithness, there are again notable differences of a distinct and opposite nature; a native of Sutherland and someone from the southern regions of Argyll may have some difficulty understanding each other. In Perthshire, on the other hand, the influence of the English language is evident: the pronunciation is less precise, the choice of words is less traditional, and words are often used only in their secondary or indirect meanings.

The central districts provide the best example of the variety of Gaelic that serves as the spoken language for the Highlands of Scotland.

There are two ways to look at this language. One view is that these linguistic quirks emerged quite recently, and that not so long ago, the language was exactly the same as Irish in both form and sound. In this view, Scottish Gaelic would be seen as a corrupted *patois* <term>patois</term> <note>A regional dialect often considered "unrefined" or informal compared to a standard or literary language.</note> of a people whose lack of formal education led them to adopt informal patterns that are not backed by grammatical rules. 

On the other hand, these unique traits might be seen as dialectal differences that are deeply woven into the very structure of the language. These characteristics may have defined the language since ancient times, when geographic separation and political isolation led to the development of a distinct dialect within the common language in the Highlands of Scotland.

<summary>The author examines whether Scottish Gaelic is a "corrupted" version of Irish or a distinct, ancient dialect formed by geographic isolation, noting how regional influences like English have impacted certain districts.</summary>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the status of Scottish Gaelic. It critiques the dismissal of the language by Irish grammarians and argues for the scientific importance of studying living dialects to understand the evolution of language from its prehistoric roots.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

The first perspective is the one held by Irish grammarians; if they are right, then the differences in Scottish Gaelic cannot be considered to have any linguistic <term>philological</term> <note>Philology is the historical and comparative study of how languages change over time.</note> significance. However, these scholars have not approached the question with much objectivity or a deep understanding of the matter. Their conclusions would need to be based on a much more precise knowledge of the actual spoken dialect and a more balanced study of linguistic principles before they could be considered final. If, on the other hand, the second view is correct, then Scottish Gaelic is clearly worth studying as a distinct branch of the Gaelic language once shared by both Scotland and Ireland. In that case, anything that helps us understand its origin and unique features becomes very valuable to our understanding of language.

When studying language, spoken dialects are extremely important. By looking at living dialects—which are not just "broken" versions of a language but have their own unique patterns—we can truly understand the nature and direction of the original "mother tongue."

It is the fate of every language that as soon as it enters recorded history, it begins to change, break down, and branch off into various dialects. The actual creation of a mother tongue happens in prehistoric times; it is a hidden process that takes place during the very early development of a society. By the time the speakers of a language first emerge into

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of language, specifically how languages begin to change and branch into dialects the moment they enter the historical record.</meta>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

view, and take their place in the history of nations, opposing influences have already begun to act upon them. Their language has already started its decline, and we can only observe its process of breakdown and change—reaching back to its original state only by studying its various dialects.

There are two conflicting forces that affect every language—the <term>etymological</term> <note>Etymology refers to the logic of how words are originally formed and their historical roots.</note> and the <term>phonetic</term> <note>Phonetics refers to the actual sounds produced by speakers.</note>. The etymological principle is all-powerful during the initial formation and structure of a language; it creates combinations of sounds required by the laws of its own composition, without considering whether they are harmonious or easy for the human voice to produce. However, this principle carries the seeds of its own destruction. No sooner has it finished building the language than a process of modification and decay begins. This is caused by the unique speech habits of the people, their desire for pleasant-sounding words and ease of pronunciation, and the physical environment or other factors that affect the organs of speech.

These phonetic causes immediately clash with the strict etymological structures of the language, reshaping its sounds, breaking down its original patterns, and changing how the vocal organs produce speech. These effects are made permanent when groups of people are separated or isolated. Meanwhile, new words and combinations are added to their vocabulary as new needs arise in their separate state, through their social progress, or by the unique qualities of their

<vocab>etymologic, phonetic, decomposition, dialects, harmony, speech, social condition</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the tension between the "etymological" rules that form a language and the "phonetic" changes made by speakers for ease and harmony. It argues that these forces, combined with social isolation, inevitably lead to the creation of distinct dialects.</summary>

<keywords>linguistics, etymology, phonetics, language evolution, dialects, speech patterns, social development</keywords>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

new condition. Thus innumerable dialects spring up. Whenever a difference of situation takes place among the people composing the aggregate by whom the original language was spoken, a diversity of dialect is at once created. In these dialects are preserved the bones of the <term>mother tongue</term> <note>The "mother tongue" refers to the original, common parent language from which various dialects or related languages descend.</note>; and it is only by a comparison of these that her full character can be ascertained.

This tendency of the mother tongue, to break up into as many dialects as there are shades of difference in the position and tendencies of its speakers, is only arrested by the formation of a <term>cultivated dialect</term> <note>A "cultivated dialect" is what we might today call a "standard language"—a version of speech used for formal communication, law, and literature that remains stable even as local dialects continue to change.</note>, created when the wants of an educated or cultivated class in the community demand a common medium of interchanging their ideas. This cultivated language is usually first formed by poetry, completed by writing, and adopted by education. Its first stage is that of the language in which the songs and poems, the first literature of a <term>rude</term> <note>In this context, "rude" means unrefined, primitive, or living in a simple, early stage of social development.</note> people, are recited by its <term>bards</term> <note>Bards were ancient poet-singers who served as the primary keepers of history, genealogy, and law before writing became widespread.</note>, its earliest literary class; and, by the introduction of the art of writing, it passes over into the written speech. It then becomes a common dialect, spoken and written by the cultivated class of the community, and to a knowledge of which a portion of the people are raised by education.

This cultivated or written language may have been originally one of the numerous dialects spoken by the people composing the community, and which circumstances have elevated into that position; or it may have been introduced from another country speaking a sister dialect, which has preceded it in cultivation; or it may,

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

In the one case, the language first cultivated by poetry passes over into the written language. In the other, it remains an indigenous, cultivated, spoken language, which is antagonistic to, and contends with, the imported written speech till the influence of the latter prevails, and it is either extinguished by it, or remains as popular poetry in the <term>vernacular</term> <note>The "vernacular" refers to the native, everyday language spoken by ordinary people in a specific region, rather than a formal literary or "high" language.</note> tongue, while everything prose is absorbed.

But however it originates, the spoken dialects still remain as the vernacular speech of portions of the community. They are not the children or creatures of the written speech, still less corruptions of it, but are equally ancient, and retain much of the elements of the original language which the written speech has rejected.

The formation of a cultivated or written language is always an <term>eclectic process</term> <note>An "eclectic process" refers to a method of selecting and combining the "best" or most useful parts from different sources to create a new, standardized whole.</note>. It selects, it modifies, and it rejects, while the living dialects retain many of the forms and much of the structure modified and rejected by it. Hence, for the study of the character and formation of the mother tongue, the living spoken dialects are of the first importance; and a restricted attention to the written language, and the contemptuous rejection of everything in the spoken dialects which vary from it, as <term>barbarisms</term> <note>In the 19th century, scholars often used the term "barbarism" to describe words or speech patterns that they considered unrefined or "incorrect" compared to the standard written language.</note> and corruptions, is simply to part with much valuable material for the study, and to narrow the range of inquiry.<sup>1</sup>

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

Perhaps the English language affords an illustration of these remarks. As a written and cultivated language, it took its rise in England, but was introduced from England into Scotland.

In England, the provincial dialects have remained as the spoken language of the uncultivated class in the respective provinces side by side with it; but their antiquity and their value for <term>philological</term> <note>Philological refers to the scientific study of the history and development of languages.</note> purposes is fully acknowledged. No one dreams of viewing them as merely corruptions of the written language, arising from rudeness and ignorance.

In Scotland, the English language has been introduced as the written or cultivated language, but a different form

in its dialects; and in spite of the tyranny exercised by the classical or literary idioms, the day is still very far off which is to see the dialects entirely eradicated. . . .

> "It is a mistake to imagine that dialects are everywhere corruptions of the literary language. . . . Dialects have always been the feeders rather than the channels of a literary language; anyhow, they are parallel streams which existed long before one of them was raised to that temporary eminence which is the result of literary cultivation."

The whole of the lecture in which this passage occurs is well worthy of perusal, in regard to the proper view and position of the spoken dialects in the study of language.

<term>Schleicher</term> <note>August Schleicher (1821–1868) was a pioneering German linguist who treated languages as living organisms that evolve and branch out like a tree.</note> takes the same view in his masterly work, *The German Language* <note>original: "Die Deutsche Sprache"</note>. He says, in relation to the German language, what is equally true of the Gaelic:

> "The dialects are the natural forms—developed according to the laws of linguistic-historical changes—

<column-break/>

in contrast to the more or less artificial language of literature, which is regulated and trimmed by the schoolmaster's hand. <note>original: "Die mundarten sind die natürlichen nach den Gesetzen der Sprachgeschichtlichen Veränderungen gewordenen Formen im Gegensatze zu der mehr oder minder gemachten und schulmeisterisch geregelten und zugestutzten Sprache der Schrift."</note> From this alone follows their great value for the scientific investigation of our language; here is a rich abundance of words and forms which, though good and genuine in themselves, were rejected by the written language; here we find much that we can use to explain older linguistic monuments, and even to understand the current written language, quite apart from the historical and phonetic interest offered by the exceedingly rich variety of our dialects.

> "Whoever is knowledgeable in a dialect has a great head start in the study of Old German over those who are only at home in the written language.

> "*Nothing is more foolish, nothing reveals more a lack of true education, than the contempt for our dialects.*" <note>original: "Nichts ist thörichter, nichts verräth mehr den Mangel wahrer Bildung als das Verachten unserer Mundarten."</note> — P. 110.

It is certain that, while we have reason to believe that the Cruithne formed the original population of the whole of Ulster, we find them in the historic period confined to certain districts in Ulster only, although their kings retained the title of kings of Ulster.

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

occupied by the <term>Cruithne</term> <note>The Gaelic name for the people known in Latin as the Picts.</note>, who were Pagans, while the <term>Dalriadic Scots</term> <note>Gaelic settlers from the kingdom of Dalriada in Ireland who established themselves in western Scotland.</note> were Christians.

In the year 563, an event took place which was destined to exercise a powerful influence both on the condition and the language of the population. This was the mission of <term>Saint Columba</term> <note>Also known as Colum Cille, an Irish abbot and missionary credited with spreading Christianity in what is now Scotland.</note>, a <term>Scot</term> <note>At this time, "Scot" specifically referred to a Gaelic person from Ireland.</note> from Ireland, to convert the Cruithne to the Christian faith, and the consequent foundation of the **Monastery of Iona**, which became the seat of learning, and the source of all ecclesiastical authority, both for the Cruithne and the Dalriadic Scots, from whence innumerable Scottish clergy issued, who spread over the country and founded churches among the Cruithne under its influence and authority.

The platform occupied by the two populations, embracing both Ireland and the Highlands of Scotland, in the sixth century, thus showed in the south and west of Ireland pure Scots ; in the north and east settlements of Scots among the Cruithne, gradually confining the latter to isolated districts ; in **Argyll**, a Scottish settlement among the Cruithne of <term>Alban</term> <note>The Gaelic name for Scotland, specifically the territory north of the Forth and Clyde.</note> ; and in the rest of the Highlands pure Cruithne ; but over both Scots and Cruithne in Alban a Scottish clergy, who brought a cultivated and literary language with them.

In Ireland the **Gaelic** spoken in the different provinces varies, and probably has always varied from each other. They differ in words, pronunciation, and idiom ; and in grammatical construction and idiom there is a marked difference between the Gaelic of the northern and of the southern half of Ireland. The written language resem-

<summary>This page describes the 6th-century mission of Saint Columba and the establishment of Iona, which served to unify the Scots and Cruithne (Picts) under a shared Christian and literary culture. It also notes the emerging geographic and linguistic divisions within the Gaelic-speaking world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of the Gaelic language and the political independence of the Scottish kingdom from its Irish origins during the late 6th and early 7th centuries.</meta>

->xxvi<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

resembles most the language of the south and west of Ireland. It seems to have been formed from it, and to have become the common language of the literary and cultivated class, while the other dialects remained as the spoken language of their respective populations.

This written language was brought over to Scotland in the sixth century by <term>Columba</term> <note>Saint Columba (Colum Cille), the Irish missionary who founded the monastery on Iona.</note> and his clergy, who introduced it, with Christianity, among the <term>Cruithne</term> <note>The Gaelic name for the people known in Latin as the Picts.</note>; where, however, the native dialect must have received some cultivation, as we find that he was opposed by <term>Magi</term> <note>Latin for "wise men" or "magicians," here referring to the Druids of the Pictish royal court.</note>, which implies a literary class among the Pagan Cruithne. At this time there was so little political separation between the two countries, that the Scots of British <term>Dalriada</term> <note>The Gaelic kingdom that spanned northeastern Ireland and western Scotland.</note> remained subject to the Irish Dalriada, from which they emerged, till the year 573, when **Aedan, son of Gabran**, became king of Scotch Dalriada, and, at the great **Council of Drumceat**, it was declared independent of Irish Dalriada, and he was crowned as its first independent monarch. The Cruithne of Ireland, likewise, formed part of that great Cruithnian kingdom, which had its head-quarters in Scotland, till the reign of **Fiacha mac Baedan**, King of Ulster, who ruled over the Irish Cruithne from 589 to 626, and probably in the year 608, when they threw off the yoke of the Cruithne of Scotland.^1

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

The <term>Cruithne</term> <note>The native Gaelic name for the people known to the Romans as the Picts.</note> of Scotland remained under the influence of the Scottish clergy till the beginning of the eighth century, when their king, **Nectan**, adopted the usages of the Roman party <note>This refers to the dispute over the dating of Easter and the style of the clerical tonsure, where Nectan chose the Roman customs over the traditions of the Iona (Scottish) church.</note>, and in 717 expelled the Scottish clergy out of his dominions across the boundary of <term>Drumalban</term> <note>Literally "The Spine of Britain," the mountain range (the Grampians) that served as the natural border between the Picts in the east and the Scots in the west.</note>,¹ which separated them from the Scots of <term>Dalriada</term> <note>The Gaelic kingdom of the western Highlands and islands.</note>, and a new clergy was introduced into that part of the country, occupied by the Cruithne, from the English kingdom of **Northumberland**. In 731, we learn from <term>Bede</term> <note>The Venerable Bede, an English monk and historian whose "Ecclesiastical History of the English People" is a primary source for this era.</note>, who then closes his history, that the Scots of Dalriada were still confined within the same limits ; and that no change had up to that date taken place in the relative positions of the two populations, the Cruithne and the Scots. After this date we know little of the history of the population of the Highlands till the middle of the ninth century, when we find that a great change had, in the interval, taken place in their political condition. The two populations had now become united in one kingdom, and a family of undoubted Scottish race ruled over the united people.

Of the events which brought about this great change, authentic history tells us nothing—of the fact there can be no doubt ; and the question arises of how the <term>Gaelic</term> <note>The Goidelic Celtic language brought from Ireland, which eventually replaced the Pictish tongue.</note> language originated in the parts of the Highlands outside of Dalriada.

->xxviii<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

Prior to this date, it was exclusively occupied by people of the race of the <term>Cruithne</term> <note>The native Gaelic name for the people known to the Roman world as the Picts.</note>, who were first Pagan under the influence of native <term>Magi</term> <note>original: "Magi"; here referring to the Druids or native spiritual leaders.</note>, then Christian, and for 150 years under clergy of the <term>Scottish race</term> <note>In this historical context, "Scottish" refers to the "Scoti"—the Gaelic-speaking people originally from Ireland who settled in Dalriada (modern-day Argyll).</note>, who were, however, driven out in the year 717. Either then these Cruithne spoke a Gaelic dialect, or, if they spoke a different language, we must suppose that the language of Gaelic <term>Dalriada</term> <note>The Gaelic kingdom of the western Highlands and islands.</note> had, subsequently to the ninth century, spread, along with the rule of a Scottish king, over the whole of the Highlands not embraced in that limited territory. We have no materials for determining this question. The latter supposition—that Gaelic spread late—has always been assumed by the Irish historians, but without proof; and they have never attempted to account for the entire disappearance of the previous language, and the expulsion of the previous population of so extensive a district, so mountainous and inaccessible in its character, and so tenacious of the language of its early inhabitants in its <term>topography</term> <note>The study of place-names; the author argues that if the names of mountains and rivers remained Gaelic, the people must have spoken it for a very long time.</note>, which such a theory involves.

If the first supposition be the correct one, and the Cruithne spoke a Gaelic dialect, we can easily understand how, though originally different from the Gaelic dialect of Dalriada, it may, by the influence of the written language, and its vernacular use by their clergy for so long a period, have become modified and assimilated to it; and if, as is probable, their dialect had been so far cultivated, as the existence of popular poetry—the first literature of a rude people—was calculated to effect, the influence of the clergy would probably be antagonistic to such literature, and be employed to suppress it; and

->xxix<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

the language in which it was conveyed might remain for some time in opposition to the written language, as a vernacular and popular form of the language, the type and symbol of the anti-Christian party, till it was finally amalgamated with, or assimilated to it as the party itself was ultimately overcome.

But another event had taken place during this obscure period, extending to little more than a century, and in which the union of the two populations under a Scottish royal race had been effected, which must have interposed an obstacle to the spread of an influence from Ireland into the <term>nondalriadic</term> <note>Referring to the parts of Scotland outside the kingdom of Dalriada, which was the original Gaelic-speaking territory.</note> portion of the Highlands, and greatly counteracted that arising from a dominant royal family of Scottish descent. This was the destruction of <term>Iona</term> <note>The sacred island monastery founded by St. Columba, serving as the heart of the Gaelic church in Scotland.</note> by the Scandinavian pirates, and their subsequent occupation of the Western Isles and western sea-board of Scotland. Towards the end of the eighth century, these hordes of Vikings or sea-robbers, issuing from Norway and Denmark, had appeared in the western sea, ravaging and plundering the coasts and islands, and their course was everywhere marked by the burning and sacking of the monasteries and religious establishments. In 794, the ravaging of the islands and the destruction of Iona by these <term>Gentiles</term> <note>original: "Gentiles"; a historical term used by Christian chroniclers to describe the pagan Vikings.</note>, as they were termed, is recorded in the Irish <term>annals</term> <note>Year-by-year historical records maintained by monks.</note>. In 802, the Monastery of Iona was burnt by them, and in 806 the community or <term>family of Iona</term> <note>original: "familia"; the collective term for the monks and residents of a monastery.</note>, as it was termed, slain, to the number of sixty-eight. In consequence of the insecurity of Iona, the abbot even—

->xxx<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

Derry then became the head of the Columban monastic houses in Ireland, while a portion of the relics were moved to Dunkeld <note>A site in Perthshire that served as a new religious capital for the Scots, intended to be a safer inland alternative to Iona.</note>, which then represented the Columban clergy in Scotland. The influence of Iona, as the core and center of Gaelic learning, and of its Scottish clergy upon the population of the Highlands, thus ceased for ever.

The islands were, by degrees, occupied by these pirates <note>The Scandinavian Vikings mentioned on the previous page.</note>, till eventually the Norwegian Kingdom of Man and the Isles was formed; and after they had converted from Paganism to Christianity, and their power became established, the bishopric of the Isles became included in the Norwegian diocese of Man. During this period, which lasted until the middle of the twelfth century, while the Western Isles and the western shores of the mainland were occupied by the Norwegians, and a royal family of Scottish race sat on the throne of the united population, all that remained intact of the Gaelic population of the Highlands was mainly represented by the great province of Moray. This province contained the mainland parts of the modern counties of Inverness and Ross, and its chiefs or <term>maormors</term> <note>original: "maormors"; high-ranking Gaelic regional rulers or "great stewards," comparable in status to earls.</note> are found, during the whole of this period, maintaining a struggle for local independence against the ruling powers, whether Scottish or Saxon, till they were finally suppressed in the year 1130 by the great battle in which Angus, the Celtic Earl of Moray, was defeated and slain by King David I.

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the political and religious shifts in the Highlands. ->xxxi<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

The Norwegian Kingdom of Man and the Isles <note>A realm established by Scandinavian settlers that encompassed the Hebrides, the islands in the Firth of Clyde, and the Isle of Man.</note> was now approaching its fall, and a new power arose on the ruins of that of the Maormors of Moray <note>The powerful hereditary rulers or "high stewards" of the northern Scottish province of Moray, who often resisted the authority of the Scottish kings.</note>, which soon became paramount in the Western Highlands, and exercised a very different influence upon its language and population. This was the dynasty of the Celtic kings or Lords of the Isles <note>The MacDonald dynasty, who ruled the Western Isles and parts of the mainland as a semi-independent principality.</note>, which took its rise under Somarled <note>Also known as Somerled (died 1164), a Norse-Gaelic leader who successfully challenged the Vikings to reclaim the Isles for the Gaels.</note>, the founder of the race in the twelfth century, and maintained a powerful sway in the West Highlands till the Lord of the Isles was forfeited <note>The legal process in 1493 by which the Scottish Crown seized the lands and titles of the MacDonalds due to their perceived rebellion.</note> at the close of the fifteenth century; and, after several ineffectual attempts to maintain their ground against the Government, finally fell in the middle of the succeeding century.

Whether the race of the Lords of the Isles was of Irish descent or not, is a question which depends upon the precise degree of credibility to be given to a Celtic pedigree which reaches back to the beginning of the fourth century; but certain it is, that the spirit and tendency of the whole race was essentially Irish. The history of Somarled, the founder of the family, who may, from female connexion, have possessed a Norwegian name <note>The name Somerled is derived from the Old Norse "Sumarliði," meaning "summer traveler" or "viking."</note>, is quite incompatible with the idea of his representing a Norwegian house, or deriving his position by inheritance from them. The names of his father Gilla-bride <note>A Gaelic name meaning "Servant of Saint Bridget."</note>, and his grandfather Gillaagamnan <note>A Gaelic name meaning "Servant of Saint Adomnán," the biographer of St. Columba.</note>, are purely Celtic. The interest of his family was antagonistic to that of the Norwegians; their efforts were to supplant and drive them out, and to elevate a Gaelic kingdom <note>A sovereign state or territory defined by Gaelic language, culture, and law, as opposed to the Norse-influenced administration.</note>

In the foundation of this kingdom, Irish aid and Irish interest entered largely, and the connexion of the family with Ireland became always more and more closely connected,—an influence and connexion extending to the powerful Celtic families who rose under their auspices and owned their sway.

Somarled <note>Somerled (died 1164), the celebrated Norse-Gaelic leader who established the Kingdom of the Isles.</note>, the founder of the race, first appears in history as <term>petty king</term> <note>original Latin: "regulus"</note> of *Oirirgaidheal*, or, as it is given in the Irish form, *Airergaidheal*, and, in the Latin, *Argathelia* <note>Modern Argyll. The name literally means the "Coastland of the Gaels."</note>, a name which had sprung up subsequently to the ninth century, signifying the coast-lands of the Gael, and embracing the entire west coast from Cowall to Loch Broom.

To this district he must have had hereditary claims; and an ancient <term>traditional historian</term> <note>original: "sennachy"; a professional keeper of genealogy and oral history in Gaelic society.</note> of the race thus details the steps by which he recovered possession of it:—

Gillabride <note>The father of Somerled.</note>, the son of Gillaagamnan, the son and grandson of the <term>Chief</term> <note>original: "Toiseach"; a title denoting a leader, captain, or noble of a district.</note> of Argyll, and descendant of Colla <note>Referring to Colla Uais, a legendary 4th-century Irish king from whom many Western Highland clans claimed descent.</note>, being amongst his kindred in Ireland, the clan Colla, that is, the Macguires and Macmahons, held a great meeting and assembly in Fermanagh, the country of Macguire, regarding the affairs of Gillabride, how they might restore him to his patrimony, from which he had been driven by the power of the <term>Norsemen</term> <note>original: "Lochlans"; literally "People of the Lakes," a common Gaelic term for Scandinavians.</note> and <term>Norwegians</term> <note>original: "Finngalls"; literally "Fair Foreigners," a term often used to distinguish Norwegians from the "Dubhgalls" or "Dark Foreigners" (Danes).</note>. When Gillabride saw such a large body of men assembled together, he besought them to embark in his cause, and to assist the people of <term>Scotland</term> <note>original: "Alban"</note>, who were favourable to him in an attempt to win back the possession of the country. The people declared themselves willing to go, and four or five hundred put themselves under his command. With this company Gillabride proceeded to <term>Scotland</term> <note>original: "Alban"</note>, and landing there, commenced a series of skirmishes and sudden assaults, with the assistance of friends, for his name was then very powerful. The <term>Norsemen</term> <note>original: "Lochlans"</note> possessed the islands from Man to

Somarled, the son of Gillabride, was now becoming manly and illustrious, and a band attached themselves to him, who had possession of the hills and woods of Ardgowar and Morvern. Here he came upon a large army of Norsemen <note>original: "Lochlans"; literally "people of the land of lakes/fjords," referring to Vikings.</note> and Norwegians <note>original: "Fingalls"; literally "Fair Foreigners," often used to distinguish Norwegians from Danes.</note>, and assembled round him all the soldiers he could muster and the people engaged in herding the flocks, and ranged them in order of battle. He practised a great deception on his enemies, for he made the same company pass before them three times, so that it appeared to them as if there were three companies, and then attacked them. The enemy were broken by Somarled and fled, till they reached the north bank of the river Shiel, and part escaped with their king to the Isles. Somarled did not desist from his efforts till he had cleared the whole west side of Scotland <note>original: "Alban."</note> from the Norsemen, except the islands of the fair-Norsemen <note>original: "Finnlochlans."</note>, called the Isles of the Strangers <note>original: "Innsegall"; the Gaelic name for the Hebrides during the period of Norse control.</note>.

Having married the daughter of Olave the Red, the Norwegian king of Man and the Isles, he succeeded in 1154 in obtaining one-half of the Western Isles for his eldest son Dougall, the ancestor of the MacDougalls, Lords of Lorn, in right of his mother. The portion of the Isles thus added to the Gaelic kingdom of Argyll <note>original: "Oirirgaidheal."</note> were those south of the point of Ardnamurchan, including Mull, Islay, and Jura. Ten years afterwards, in 1164, he showed still further his close relation with Ireland, by placing the Monastery of Iona under the leadership <note>original: "Abbacy."</note> of Derry, and in the same year he was slain in Renfrew, in an attempt which he made to subvert the Scottish throne itself, with Irish assistance. His successors remained in possession of this territory, consisting of Argyll, or—

On the conquest of the Norwegian kingdom of Man and the Isles by the King of Scotland in 1266, Skye and Lewis were annexed to the earldom of Ross, the rest of the Isles went to Somarled's family, their possessions were further increased by grants from Robert Bruce, and in the end of the fourteenth century they inherited the possessions of the earldom of Ross, by which they regained possession of the <term>Northern Coast</term> <note>original Gaelic: "Oirir a tuath"; referring to the coastal lands of the northern Highlands.</note>. A petty kingdom was thus formed, consisting of <term>Argyll</term> <note>original Latin: "Argathelia"</note>, or the western districts, from Cowall to Loch Broom and the Western Isles, nominally subordinate to the Scottish throne, but in reality all powerful among the population of the Highlands; the clans of the centre and north Highlands, which had once looked up to the almost equally powerful <term>High Stewards</term> <note>original Gaelic: "Maormors"; an ancient title for a high-ranking official or regional ruler, similar to an Earl.</note> of Moray, alone representing a different interest.

The marriage of the Lord of the Isles, the head of this race, with a daughter of the great Irish house of O'Cathan <note>Modernized as O'Kane or O'Cahan; a powerful ruling family in Ulster.</note>, princes of an extensive territory in the north of Ireland, towards the close of the thirteenth century, still further cemented the connexion with Ireland. <meta>This page continues the historical narrative of the descendants of Somarled, specifically focusing on the Lordship of the Isles and its deep-seated political and cultural ties with Ireland before its eventual collapse under the Scottish Crown.</meta>

->XXXV<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

Ulster to the Scottish Highlands, and founded as many houses there, and, in the subsequent century, a descendant <note>original: "scion"</note> of the House of the Isles acquired land in the north of Ireland, and founded the Antrim family <note>The MacDonnells of Antrim, who became influential figures in both Irish and Scottish politics.</note>. In all the Irish wars this race generally took a part, and, in their own wars at home, were rarely without assistance from Ireland.

The struggle between this great Celtic family and the Crown reached a point which could only end in the ruin of the former or the humiliation of the latter, and at length resulted in the forfeiture of the Lord of the Isles in 1478, and his subsequent submission, when he resigned his hereditary Celtic title, and received in exchange the feudal dignity of Lord of the Isles; a humiliation which gave deep offence to his subjects, and was not accepted by them, and produced such internal dissensions, that the Crown took advantage of them to enforce the final forfeiture of the Lordship of the Isles in the year 1493. A series of insurrections followed in favour of the descendants of the forfeited Lord, which finally terminated in the utter extinction of the kingdom of the Isles in the year 1545.

During this period, which extends to nearly four centuries, there was not only a close political connection between the Western Highlands and Islands and Ireland, but the literary influence was equally close and strong; the Irish <term>sennachies</term> <note>Gaelic: *seanchaidh*; traditional historians, genealogists, and keepers of oral lore for a clan or royal house.</note> and <term>bards</term> <note>Gaelic: *bard*; professional poets and singers who recorded the deeds of heroes and the history of the family.</note> were heads of a school which included the West Highlands, and the Highland <term>sennachies</term> were either of Irish descent, or, if of native

The Annals of the Four Masters <note>A comprehensive chronicle of medieval Irish history compiled in the 17th century.</note> record the following Irish historians <term>sennachies</term> <note>Gaelic: *seanchaidh*; traditional historians and genealogists.</note> as being recognized masters in the Highlands:—

In 1185 died Maolosa O'Daly, chief sage <term>ollav</term> <note>The highest rank of scholar or poet in the Gaelic system.</note>, or chief poet of Ireland <note>original: "Erin"</note> and Scotland <note>original: "Alban"</note>; a man renowned for his poetry, hospitality, and nobility.

In 1328 died the blind O'Carril, chief minstrel of Ireland and Scotland in his time.

In 1448 died Tadg the Younger <note>original: "Tadg og"</note>, son of Tadg, son of Giolla-coluim O'Higgin, chief teacher of the poets of Ireland and Scotland.

In 1554 died Tadg, son of Aodh O'Coffey, chief teacher of poetry in Ireland and Scotland.

The oldest of the Gaelic manuscripts <term>MSS.</term>, preserved in the Library of the Faculty of Advocates <note>Now part of the National Library of Scotland.</note>, belong to this period. They are all written in the Irish script; the language is the formal written language of Ireland; and they contain numerous examples of the poetry of these Irish masters, especially of Tadg the Younger and the O'Dalys.

The Betons, or, as their name was in Gaelic, Macbheatha <note>Literally "son of life."</note>, who were hereditary physicians in Islay and Mull, and who were also historians <term>sennachies</term> of the Macleans, were of Irish descent, being O'Neills. They are said by tradition to be one of the families who accompanied the daughter of O'Cathan <note>A powerful chieftain from Ulster.</note> to Scotland; and many of these manuscripts belonged to them. The M'Vurichs, who were hereditary historians <term>sennachies</term>

A powerful influence must thus have been exercised upon the language and literature of the Highlands, which must have become by degrees more and more assimilated to that of Ireland; and it may well be doubted whether, towards the close of this period, there existed the means of acquiring the art of writing the language except in Ireland, or the conception of a written and cultivated literature, which was not identified with its language and learning.

We have no reason, however, to conclude, on that account, that there was not a vernacular <term>Gaelic</term>, which preserved many of the independent features of a native language, and existed among the people as a spoken dialect; or that a popular and unwritten literature may not at the same time have existed in that native and idiomatic <term>Gaelic</term> in the poetry handed down by tradition, or composed by native <term>bards</term> <note>Professional poets and singers, often distinct from the more academic sennachies.</note>, innocent of all extraneous education in the written language of Ireland.
