The fall of the great <term>House of the Isles</term> <note>The powerful MacDonald lordship that ruled the Hebrides and the western coast of Scotland as a semi-independent Gaelic state until its forfeiture in 1493.</note> was coincident with another event, destined to effect a great change in the position of the Highland population and of their literature. This was the <term>Reformation</term> <note>The 16th-century religious movement that led to the establishment of Protestantism.</note> of the sixteenth century and its attendant events, the establishment of a Reformed Church, the introduction of printing, and the translation of the Scriptures and religious works for the instruction of the people.

From this source sprung up a religious literature, which, commencing in the written or Irish Gaelic <note>At this time, "Irish Gaelic" (Classical Gaelic) was the standard literary language for both Ireland and Scotland.</note>, gradually approached nearer and nearer to the spoken dialect of the country, and, accompanied by the preaching of the clergy in the vernacular dialect, tended to preserve and stereotype the language spoken in the Highlands in its native form and idiom.

The first printed book was a translation of the *Form of Prayer* issued by John Knox, which was made by John Carsewell, the Protestant Bishop of the Isles, and printed at “at Edinburgh, which is also called Dunmonaidh,” <note>original: “*Dunedin darab comhainm Dunmonaidh*”</note> that is, at Dunedin or Edinburgh, otherwise called Dunmonaidh, 24th April 1567. The second printed book was a translation of Calvin’s Catechism, which was published, along with an English edition, in 1631. This translation seems likewise to have been made in Argyllshire, and is in the Irish orthography and idiom.

In 1659, the Presbyterian Synod of Argyll took up the work of issuing translations into Gaelic of the metrical Psalms and of the Scriptures, and commenced with a portion of the Psalter, which was completed in 1694. It will be seen that the earlier printed books emanated entirely from Argyllshire <note>A county in western Scotland where the dialect was historically very similar to Irish due to close maritime links</note>, where the spoken dialect approaches more nearly to the Irish; and the work of translating and publishing the Psalter <note>The Book of Psalms</note> and Scriptures into Gaelic being a new and difficult task, the translators resorted to Ireland and to the written and cultivated dialect of the Irish as the medium through which to convey it; but as subsequent editions were issued, they were brought more and more near to the spoken language of the Scotch Highlands in its purest form and idiom, and the Irish orthography by degrees adapted to it, till at length the Scotch Gaelic became clothed in that orthography in which we now find it, and elevated to the position of a written and cultivated language.

Throughout the whole of this period, however, there existed, side by side with this printed religious literature, another literature in the popular poetry of the uncultivated native bards, removed from the influence of Irish training, whose compositions were expressed in the pure idiom of the spoken dialect; and in the poems of Ian Lom <note>John MacDonald (c. 1624–c. 1710), known as "Bare John" due to his sharp wit; a famous poet from Lochaber and a Royalist during the Wars of the Three Kingdoms</note>, the Lochaber poet of the Wars of Montrose; Duncan Ban Macintyre <note>Donnchadh Bàn Mac an t-Saoir (1724–1812), "Fair-haired Duncan," one of the most celebrated Gaelic poets</note>, whose exquisite poem of Bendoran <note>Moladh Beinn Dòbhrain, or "Praise of Ben Doran," a masterpiece of nature poetry</note> is a beautiful specimen of pure Gaelic, and whose poems were printed in 1778; Ailen Buidhe Macdougall <note>"Yellow-haired Alan"</note>, W. ->xlii<-

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

evidence of the existence and character of a vernacular dialect <note>The common, everyday spoken language of the people, distinct from formal or religious registers</note>, in which the people interchanged their homely ideas, and their favourite bards <note>Traditional poets and storytellers who held high social status in Gaelic society</note> composed their poems which found an immediate access to the hearts and imagination of the people; while the language in which their scriptures and formularies <note>Standardized religious texts or liturgy</note> were conveyed was looked upon as a sort of sacred dialect, through which they received their religious teaching.

There was thus, throughout, a double influence exercised upon the language and literature of the Highlands. One from Ireland, which was associated with the written and cultivated dialect of Gaelic which had there been formed, and brought over with Christianity to Scotland. With it came the Irish orthography <term>orthography: the conventional system of spelling for a language</term>. It was mainly connected with learning and religious teaching, and its influence was most powerful in the western districts and islands, and the territories subject to the power of the Lord of the Isles <note>The title of the powerful Gaelic rulers who controlled the Hebrides and the western coast of Scotland between the 12th and 15th centuries</note>. The other, indigenous <note>Originating or occurring naturally in a particular place; native</note> and antagonistic to it, falling back upon a literary influence from the south and east, when not predominant, and associated more with the popular poetry of the Highlands. Its orthography seems to have resembled that of the other Celtic languages, the Welsh and the Manx; and its influence prevailed in the central and north Highlands, where the best and purest type of the Scotch Gaelic is still to be found.¹

->xliii<-

-># INTRODUCTION.<-

The literary history of the Highlands falls into periods as these influences respectively prevailed.

The first period is prior to the seventh century, when there was no political separation between Ireland and Gaelic Scotland. The great divisions of the people were regulated by race rather than by geographical distribution. The Cruithne <note>The ancient people often identified as the Picts, who inhabited both Ireland and Scotland</note> everywhere were united by common origin and ties of race; and the Scots <note>In this period, "Scots" refers to the Gaelic-speaking people who migrated from Ireland to Scotland</note>, wherever settled, owned the Milesian Ardrigh <note>The legendary 'High Kings' of Ireland, who claimed descent from the mythical ancestor Milesius</note> in Ireland. The countries were simply viewed as the east and the west, and were known as Erin and Alban <note>The Gaelic names for Ireland and Scotland, respectively</note>, and the communication between them was free and unrestrained. The second period commences with the separation of the Scotch Dalriada <note>A Gaelic kingdom that spanned across the North Channel, including parts of western Scotland and northeastern Ireland</note> from the Irish in 573, and of the Irish Cruithne from the present race, some thirty years later, when a political as well as a geographical separation between the Celtic tribes of the two countries took place; but, for upwards of a century afterwards, the church and clergy of the Highlands were Irish, and the written Irish dialect imported by them must still have remained in use, and exercised its accustomed influence on the spoken language.

After the expulsion of the Scotch clergy in 717 <note>The historical event where the monks of Iona were expelled by the Pictish King Nechtan for refusing to adopt Roman religious customs</note>, a period of great obscurity in the history of Scotland occurs, extending to nearly a century and a half, during which the ecclesiastical influence exercised was from the south, taking its origin from the Anglic kingdom of Northumbria <note>A powerful Anglo-Saxon kingdom in what is now Northern England and Southern Scotland</note>; some revolution also took place, which placed a

But during the same period another event took place, of great significance in the literary history of the country; the Monastery of Icholumkill <note>Also known as Iona; the name Icholumkill comes from the Gaelic *Ì Chaluim Chille*, meaning "The Island of Columba’s Church"</note> or Iona, the time-honored seat of Gaelic learning, went down amidst the troubled waters of Scandinavian piracy <note>Referring to the devastating Viking raids of the 8th and 9th centuries</note>, and its position, as head of the learning and religion of the country, was gone for ever.

During the fourth period, which lasted for three hundred years, the Norwegian kingdom of Man and the Isles, which likewise embraced the western seaboard of the Highlands, interposed itself between the Highlands and Ireland; and the influence from the latter country must for the time have been paralyzed, while the indigenous and native influence maintained itself in the extensive Highland province of Moray.

At the close of this period we have a hint of the existence of an Albanic <note>A term used here to refer to an early Scottish form of Gaelic, distinct from the Irish dialect</note> dialect of Gaelic in the Life of St. Kentigern, first Bishop of Glasgow, by Jocelyn, the biographer also of St. Patrick, who wrote in the year 1180. He says that the name of Kentigern was justly given to one who might be called their chief lord <note>original Latin: *dominus capitaneus*</note>; “for *ken* is head in Latin <note>original: *ken caput Latine*</note>, and *tyern* in the Albanic tongue is interpreted as lord in Latin <note>original: *tyern Albanice, dominus Latine interpretatur*</note>.” This is nearly a phonetic orthography <note>Writing words exactly as they sound rather than using traditional spelling</note>, and not unlike that of the Dean of Lismore’s <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for Manuscript</note> In Irish orthography the words would be *cend*, signifying head <note>Latin: *caput*</note>, or a head; *tigerna*, lord <note>Latin: *dominus*</note>, or lord; but in pronunciation the *d* in *cend* is quiescent <note>Silent</note>, and the aspirated

Jocelyn <note>A 12th-century monk who wrote the lives of Scottish and Irish saints</note> seems to recognise the existence of a native dialect designated as "Scottish" <note>original Latin: *Albanice*</note>; and one of the peculiarities of Scotch Gaelic is also present in the omission of the final *a* from the word for lord <note>original: *tigerna*</note>.^1

The fate of the great Celtic earldom of Moray, and the decay of the Norwegian power in the Isles, was followed by the powerful sway of the Celtic Lords of the Isles <note>A powerful dynasty of Hebridean chiefs who ruled the west of Scotland as a semi-independent maritime kingdom</note>, who, during the fifth period, extending from three to four centuries, were dominant in the western districts; and, as far as their sway extended, the spirit, influence, and literature were all Irish, and it was only when the fall of the almost independent kingdom of the Isles, and the Reformation again separated the country from Ireland, that a reaction towards the vernacular and spoken Scotch Gaelic took place, which has resulted in a clear development of its grammatical rules and construction, and the establishment of a fixed orthography.

It was at the close of the fifth period, during which the Lords of the Isles were all-powerful in the west, and just before the middle of the sixteenth century ushered in the Reformation, that the collection, of which selections are now published, was made by the Vicar of Fortingal, who was also Dean of Lismore <note>James MacGregor, the 16th-century compiler of the "Book of the Dean of Lismore," a vital source of early Gaelic poetry</note>. There are poems by the Irish bards, whose schools extended also to the Highlands: by the O’Dalys <note>a prolific family of professional hereditary poets active in both Ireland and Scotland</note>, who lived during the fifteenth century; by Teague og O’Higgin <note>Tadhg Óg Ó hUiginn (d. 1448), one of the most famous medieval Irish poets</note>, who died in 1448; by Dermod O’Hiffernan; and by Turn O’Meilchonair <note>Torna Ó Maolchonaire</note>, <term>Ollav</term> <note>the highest rank of professional poet and scholar in the Gaelic system</note> of the Sil Murray <note>the Síl Muiredaig, the royal dynasty of Connacht</note>, who died in 1468. There are poems by Allan M‘Ruadrie and Gillecallum Mac an Olla, who seem to have been native bards; by John of Knoydart, who celebrates the murder of the young Lord of the Isles by his Irish harper in 1490; by Finlay M‘Nab, called the Good Poet; and by the transcriber of the greater part of the manuscript, Duncan, the Dean’s brother, who wrote in praise of the M‘Gregors.

The great value of this collection, as regards the language, arises from the peculiar <term>orthography</term> <note>system of spelling</note> used, which presents it as it must have been pronounced, and affords a means of testing one of the chief differences which characterize the different provincial <term>dialects</term>, the vowel and consonantal sounds, and the presence or absence of <term>eclipsis</term> <note>a grammatical feature where a consonant sound is suppressed or "eclipsed" by another</note> and <term>aspiration</term> <note>now commonly called lenition; the softening of a consonant sound, such as 'p' becoming 'ph'</note>.

It has been found impossible to print the whole of the contents of the <term>MS.</term> <note>manuscript</note>, but the selection which has been made, chiefly with reference to the literature of the Highlands, will also afford a fair specimen of the shades of difference which characterized the language in which the poems are written. Some are in pure Irish, and must

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

have been transferred from the Irish orthography into that used in the <term>MS.</term> <note>abbreviation for Manuscript</note>. Others are in pure Scotch Gaelic, as the poems of Duncan, son of Dougall Maol <note>Dugald the Bald</note>, Finlay M’Nab, the bard roy <note>the Red Bard; "roy" is an anglicization of the Gaelic "ruadh"</note>, and John of Knoydart. Others are in a mixed dialect, in some of which the Irish idiom, in others the Scotch, predominates.

In general, it will be found that the language approaches more or less nearly to the Irish, as the writers appear to have had more or less cultivation in the written language, or were more or less removed from Irish influence; and the <term>MS.</term> may be viewed as the only known record of those vernacular <note>spoken or local</note> dialects of Gaelic in the sixteenth century which differed in any degree from the written and cultivated language.

But while the Dean of Lismore’s <term>MS.</term> has in this respect a philological <note>the study of how languages develop</note> value, it has likewise no mean literary value, from the circumstance that it contains no fewer than twenty-eight Ossianic poems <note>poems concerning the legendary hero Oisín and the warrior band known as the Fianna</note>, extending to upwards of 2500 lines, nine directly attributed to Ossian, two to Farris or Ferghus Filidh <note>Fergus the Poet</note>, and one to Caolte M’Ronan <note>Caoilte Mac Rónáin</note>, the three bards of the Feine <note>the Fianna, a legendary group of warriors in Irish and Scottish mythology</note>; two to Allan M’Ruadri, and one to Gillecallum Mac an Olla <note>Gille-caluim Mac an Ollaimh</note>, bards hitherto unknown; and eleven poems, Ossianic in their style and subject, to which no author’s name is attached.

The circumstances under which the controversy regarding the authenticity of the poems of Ossian, published by James Macpherson, arose, and the extent to which it for the time agitated the minds of the men of letters <note>original: "literati," referring to the scholarly and educated elite</note> of England and Scotland, are well known.

->### INTRODUCTION.<-

In the summer of 1759, Mr. John Home, the author of *Douglas* <note>a famous verse tragedy first performed in 1756 that was highly popular in Scotland</note>, met Mr. James Macpherson, then a tutor in the family of Graham of Balgowan, at Moffat <note>a spa town in southern Scotland popular with the Edinburgh literati</note>. Mr. Home had previously been told by Professor Adam Fergusson <note>a renowned philosopher and historian of the Scottish Enlightenment</note>, a native of Atholl and acquainted with Gaelic, that there existed in the country some remains of ancient Gaelic <note>the native Goidelic language of the Scottish Highlands</note> poetry. Mr. Home mentioned the circumstance to James Macpherson, also a Highlander <note>a person from the mountainous regions of northern and western Scotland</note>, and a native of Badenoch <note>a historic district in the central Highlands</note>, and was told by him that he had some pieces of ancient Gaelic poetry in his possession. After some difficulty, Mr. Home obtained translations of them from Macpherson, and took them to Edinburgh, and showed the translations to Drs. Blair <note>Hugh Blair, a noted preacher and literary critic</note>, Fergusson, and Robertson <note>William Robertson, a famous historian and Principal of the University of Edinburgh</note>, by whom they were much admired. Macpherson was importuned <note>persistently urged</note> to translate all he had, and the translations furnished by him were published in a little volume in June 1760, under the title of "Fragments of Ancient Poetry collected in the Highlands of Scotland."

There seems little reason to doubt that these translations were made from genuine fragments in Macpherson's possession. If they existed at all, they were in his possession before any talk had arisen of translating Gaelic poetry. There was no pretext of going to the Highlands to collect them. There was no idea, at the time the translations were produced, that such poetry could have any value in the eyes of the literary world, and there seems no motive for any deception. In the fragments, or rather short poems, contained in this little work, the proper names are smoothed down from their

Macpherson had already hit upon that successful prose style—the concept of which was quite brilliant and played a large part in the immediate popularity these works enjoyed. In other respects, however, they appear to be genuine translations of short Gaelic <note>the native Goidelic language of the Scottish Highlands</note> poems that actually existed. The praise they received from men of great literary standing, such as Home, Blair, Fergusson, and Robertson <note>referring to the leaders of the Scottish Enlightenment mentioned on the previous page</note>, must have first surprised and then greatly flattered someone of Macpherson’s character. 

He was urged to travel to the Highlands to collect whatever remained of this type of poetry, and a subscription <note>a collection of money from private donors</note> was raised to pay for his expenses. This prospect must have seemed dazzling to the poor Highland tutor; it offered a chance to trade a life of poverty, obscurity, and tedious duty for one of independence and literary fame. He agreed to the proposal, though he acted as if he were doing so reluctantly. 

At that time, the spirit of strict and critical **antiquarianism** <term>the study of history through ancient manuscripts and artifacts, prioritizing literal accuracy over artistic polish</term>—which values ancient relics precisely because they are authentic fragments and demands they be kept exactly as they are found—was completely unknown. That perspective is a product of later times. In those days, people looked primarily for literary beauty. The authenticity of these works was usually taken on trust, and it was believed that the requirements of such criticism were satisfied as long as the remains of the...

### INTRODUCTION.

...authenticity of the past were woven into an elegant and flowing narrative. With Homer and other classical epics before him, such a proposal as the publication of the ancient poetry of the Highlands—assuming, as we now know to be the fact, that Ossianic <note>relating to Ossian, a legendary blind poet and hero in Gaelic mythology</note> poetry of some kind did exist, and looking to the high expectations formed—must have at once suggested to him the idea that he should not do justice to the task he had undertaken if he could not likewise produce a Gaelic epic. This idea seems early to have suggested itself to Macpherson’s mind; it is obscurely hinted at in the preface to the *Fragments* <note>Macpherson's first collection, *Fragments of Ancient Poetry collected in the Highlands of Scotland*, published in 1760</note>; and Macpherson seems to have started on his tour with the pre-conceived determination to view any short poems and fragments he might find as parts of longer poems, and, if possible, by welding them together, to produce a national epic which should do honour to his country, and confirm his own reputation as its recoverer and translator. He was accompanied, in the earlier part of his journey, by a countryman of his own, Mr. Lachlan Macpherson of Strathmashie, who was a better Gaelic scholar than he was himself, and an excellent Gaelic poet. It is certain that, in this tour, a number of <term>MSS.</term> <note>an abbreviation for "manuscripts," referring here to handwritten documents containing old Gaelic lore</note> were collected by them, and poetry taken down from recitation; and that he was joined in it by another Gaelic scholar, Captain Alexander Morrison, who likewise assisted him. On his return, he proceeded to Badenoch, his native place and that of Lachlan Macpherson, and here he remained till January 1761, engaged, with the assistance of Lachlan Macpherson and Alexander Mor-

His task, whatever it was, had then been accomplished; and after remaining some time in Edinburgh, engaged in preparing the English version for the press, he went to London, and early in 1762 issued a quarto <note>A book size created by folding a standard sheet of paper twice, forming four leaves or eight pages</note> volume, containing the epic poem of *Fingal*, in six books, and sixteen other poems. In the following year another quarto appeared, containing another epic poem in eight books, called *Temora* <note>Likely derived from *Teamhair*, the Gaelic name for Tara, the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note>, and five other poems. This volume also contained what was called “a specimen of the original of *Temora*,” being a Gaelic version of the seventh book, and the only Gaelic bearing to be the original of any of the poems which appeared.

The English version, contained in these two quartos, possessed the same character as the English of the *Fragments* <note>Referring to Macpherson's first 1760 publication, *Fragments of Ancient Poetry*</note>; the same accommodation of the Gaelic proper names to the supposed requirements of English ears, and the prose style, originated by Macpherson, sustained with equal spirit; the poems, however, were longer, and more elaborate.

### INTRODUCTION.

the epic of *Fingal* <note>The first of Macpherson's major "Ossianic" epics, published in 1762</note> with startled but silent acquiescence, and exploded under the eight books of *Temora* <note>The second epic, published in 1763, which faced much harsher scrutiny</note>. It seemed incredible that poems such as these could have been handed down by oral recitation from the supposed age of Ossian <note>A legendary 3rd-century Gaelic bard</note>; the refined manners described, and the allusions to the Roman Emperors, awakened suspicion, and a storm of adverse criticism and questioning incredulity arose, in which Dr. Johnson <note>Samuel Johnson (1709–1784), the famous English lexicographer and critic who famously doubted the poems' authenticity</note>—at that time in the zenith of his reputation—took the lead.

Macpherson, who found the fair breeze of flattery and laudation, before which he had been sailing so smoothly on his heavy quartos <note>A book size created by folding a sheet of paper twice, typically used for prestigious works</note>, without a suspicion that he had not attained the full success he aimed at, so suddenly changed into an adverse storm of criticism and depreciation, knew not how to meet the crisis. He had not courage to avow the truth, and state candidly to the world how much of his work was based on original authority, and to what extent he had carried the process of adapting, interpolating <note>The act of inserting new or different material into a text</note>, and weaving into epic poems. He took refuge first in sulky silence, and eventually seemed to find a sort of compensation for his denunciation, as a detected forger, in the necessary alternative, the credit of having been a successful composer, and by half hints encouraged that view.¹

# INTRODUCTION.

This led to the Highland Society of Scotland <note>Founded in 1784 to preserve the language, poetry, and music of the Highlands</note> undertaking an inquiry into the authenticity of the poems of Ossian <note>The legendary narrator and purported author of a cycle of epic poems discovered/created by James Macpherson</note> published by Macpherson, which involved the subsidiary inquiry of whether such poems existed in the Highlands in the original Gaelic. The result of this inquiry is contained in the elaborate report prepared by Henry Mackenzie, the author of the *Man of Feeling* <note>A famous sentimental novel of the late 18th century; its author was a prominent member of the Edinburgh literati</note>, and published in 1806.

The inquiry was conducted with much candour. The committee were aided by receiving from Mr. John Mackenzie, Secretary of the Highland Society of London, and executor of Mr. James Macpherson, all the Gaelic manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note> in his possession, including those which Macpherson had left behind him; and they resorted to every means within their reach to obtain information.

The whole of the materials for forming a judgment which they had collected were placed impartially before the public; and the subject, so far as such materials then existed or were at their command, is really exhausted by this report.

The committee were cautious in giving an opinion, but the result they arrived at seems to have been—

1st, That the characters introduced into Macpherson’s poems were not invented, but were really the subjects of tradition in the Highlands; and that poems certainly existed which might be called Ossianic <note>Relating to the "Fenian" or "Ossianic" cycle of Gaelic mythology, centered on the hero Fionn mac Cumhaill and his son Oisín</note>, as relating to the persons and events of that mythic age.

<summary>This page details the Highland Society of Scotland's 1806 formal investigation into the authenticity of James Macpherson's Ossian poems, concluding that while based on genuine Highland tradition, the poems were not simple discoveries.</summary>
<keywords>Highland Society of Scotland, James Macpherson, Ossian, Henry Mackenzie, Malcolm Laing, Gaelic manuscripts, Ossianic poetry, authenticity controversy</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

2d, That such poems, though usually either entire poems of no very great length, or fragments, had been handed down from an unknown period by oral recitation, and that there existed many persons in the Highlands <note>The mountainous region of western and northern Scotland where Gaelic culture and language were preserved</note> who could repeat them.

3d, That such poems had likewise been committed to writing, and were to be found to some extent in manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note>.

4th, That Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), the Scottish writer who claimed to have discovered and translated ancient epic poetry by the legendary bard Ossian</note> had used many such poems in his work; but by joining separated pieces together, and by adding a connecting narrative of his own, had woven them into longer poems, and into the so-called epics.

No materials existed, however, to show the extent to which this process had been carried, and the amount of genuine matter the poems, as published by Macpherson, contained. <note>1</note>

<note>1 A comparison of the poems in the *Fragments* <note>Referring to Macpherson’s first publication, *Fragments of Ancient Poetry* (1760)</note>, with those in the first quarto volume containing the epic of *Fingal*, shows indications of the mode in which Macpherson dealt with his materials.
There are sixteen poems in the *Fragments*, all short; and some bearing the usual mark of a complete poem, by the first line being repeated at the end.
Thus, the second fragment begins with the sentence, "I sit by the mossy fountain; on the top of the hill of winds." And the same expression is introduced at the end — "By the mossy fountain I will sit; on the top of the hill of winds;" marking a complete poem.
The first and fourth fragments we find in the quarto volume containing *Fingal*, forming part of a longer poem termed *Carrickthura*, and here they are joined together by intermediate passages of some length, evidently interpolated <note>inserted or added by the editor, often altering the original sense</note> by Macpherson.</note>

<column-break/>

The sixth and twelfth fragments contain dialogues between the poet and the son of Alpine. This was no doubt, in the original, the usual dialogue between Ossian <note>The legendary warrior-poet of the Fenian cycle</note> and Patrick <note>Saint Patrick, the patron saint of Ireland; in traditional Gaelic "Ossianic" ballads, the elderly Ossian often debates with the saint</note>, always called in Scotch Gaelic poems **Macalpine**. The sixth fragment appears also in the quarto, in the so-called Epic of *Fingal*; but the dialogue is omitted, and the translation greatly altered.

To the fourteenth fragment there is appended the following note: — "This is the opening of the epic poem mentioned in the preface. The two following fragments are parts of some episodes of the same work," and they accordingly appear in the quarto, in the epic poem of *Fingal*.

The fourteenth fragment, however, relates to Cuchullin <note>Cú Chulainn, the hero of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology</note> alone; and in those tales and poems which we know to be genuine, Cuchullin and Fingal <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill, the hero of the Fenian Cycle</note> are never brought together <note>Macpherson was often criticized for merging characters from different mythological cycles and time periods into a single narrative</note>.

# INTRODUCTION.

<columns>2</columns>

Such was the result to which the committee appeared to come, and which may fairly be deduced from this inquiry; all intelligent inquirers seemed now to adopt this result, and the unbiased public generally acquiesced in it—the only difference of opinion being as to the greater or less extent to which Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), the Scottish writer who claimed to have discovered ancient epic poetry by the legendary bard Ossian</note> carried his process of adaptation and amalgamation.

The publication in 1806 of what was called the original Gaelic of Ossian <note>A legendary warrior-poet in Gaelic mythology, the supposed narrator of the poems</note>, did not affect this conclusion, or tend to alter the general acquiescence of the public in it. Instead of consisting of genuine extracts from old <term>manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note></term>, or copies of pieces taken down from oral recitation, it proved to be a complete version in Gaelic poetry of the English version transcribed under James Macpherson’s eye, and left by him in a state for publication. It was a smooth and polished version in Gaelic verse of the entire poems, in the same shape as they were presented in English, and written in the modern Gaelic of that time.

It is very difficult, however, to believe that this Gaelic version had been composed subsequently to the publication of the English Ossian, and translated from it. To any one capable, from a knowledge of Gaelic, of judging, such a theory seems almost impossible; and it is difficult to acquiesce in it. A review of all the circumstances which have been allowed to transpire regarding the proceedings of James Macpherson, seems rather to suggest that he intended, from the very beginning of

# INTRODUCTION.

...lead to the conclusion that the Gaelic version, in the shape in which it was afterwards published, had been prepared in Badenoch <note>A region in the Scottish Highlands known for its strong Gaelic traditions and as the home of James Macpherson</note>, during the months Macpherson passed there after his return from his Highland tour. This was done with the assistance of Lachlan Macpherson of Strathmashie and Captain Morrison. It suggests that the English translation was then made from this Gaelic text by Macpherson in the same manner in which he had translated the earlier fragments. This conclusion is all the more probable because, while James Macpherson’s own acquaintance with the language seems not to have been sufficiently complete to qualify him for such a task, there appears to be no doubt of the Laird of Strathmashie’s <note>Lachlan Macpherson (1723–1767), a kinsman of James and a skilled Gaelic poet</note> perfect ability to accomplish it.¹

But while from this date the controversy in England may be said to have terminated, with the exception of—

¹ <note>Some years ago I happened to pass a couple of months in the immediate neighbourhood of Strathmashie, and I recollect having been informed at that time—though by whom I cannot now tell—that after Lachlan Macpherson’s death, a paper was found in his repositories containing the Gaelic of the seventh book of Temora <note>One of the epic poems attributed to Ossian</note> in his own handwriting, with numerous corrections and alterations, bearing this title: "First rude draft of the seventh book of Temora."</note>

Mr. Gallie <note>Andrew Gallie, a clergyman who assisted Macpherson during his search for manuscripts</note> sent to the Highland Society a part of the Gaelic of *Fingal*, which afterwards appeared in the Gaelic version subsequently published. He said he had taken it from a <term>manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note></term> he had recovered, written by a friend "who was at that time with Mr. Macpherson and me, a gentleman well known for an uncommon acquaintance with the Gaelic, and a happy facility for writing it in Roman characters."

<column-break/>

On being pressed to say who this friend was, he says, "his name was Lachlan Macpherson of Strathmashie. He died in 1767."

This Gaelic version seems, therefore, to have been put together before 1767; and if it was completed before 1762, it would account for the original of the seventh book of *Temora* having been published in that year. It also explains an advertisement which appeared soon after the publication of the second quarto volume, stating that the "originals" were lying at the publisher’s office and would be published if a sufficient number of subscribers came forward; but as few subscribers appeared, and even fewer came to look at the documents, they were withdrawn.

The so-called originals were, no doubt, this Gaelic version, which there is every reason to believe had preceded the English version in its preparation.

It cites evidence of a "rude draft" found in Strathmashie's papers to support the theory that the English text was translated from a newly-composed Gaelic one, rather than ancient manuscripts.</summary>

The Irish, who had been long murmuring under the neglect of their claims to literary notice, and the absorbing attention obtained by the Highlands, suddenly burst forth with a succession of violent and spasmodic attacks, of which the partial detection of the Ossian of Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796) claimed to have translated ancient epic poems by the legendary bard Ossian, sparking a massive international literary sensation and a heated debate over their authenticity.</note> afforded a favourable opportunity.

In 1784 Dr. Young, afterwards Bishop of Clonfert <note>Matthew Young (1750–1800), an Irish researcher and clergyman interested in the scientific and literary history of Ireland.</note>, a good Irish scholar, had made a tour in the Highlands, with the view of collecting Gaelic poems, and ascertaining from what materials Macpherson had constructed his Ossian. He published an account of his journey in the first volume of the Transactions of the Royal Irish Academy, in which he maintained that any poems that existed were Irish, and that Macpherson had founded his Ossian on some of these, “retrenching, adding, and altering as he judged proper.”

In 1789 a collection of Irish Ossianic poems was published by Miss Brooke <note>Charlotte Brooke (c. 1740–1793), whose "Reliques of Irish Poetry" was a landmark work that sought to show the refinement of ancient Irish culture.</note>, termed *Reliques of Irish Poetry*. They consisted of short poems, either attributed to Ossian or on Ossianic subjects, and were accompanied by the original Irish version from which they were translated. Where that was obtained is not stated.

In 1807 the Dublin Gaelic Society was formed, for the purpose of publishing the contents of Irish <term>MSS.<note>An abbreviation for "manuscripts"—handwritten historical documents.</note></term> ;

In the fifth volume there is an essay on the poems of Ossian <note>The narrator and purported author of the poems, based on the Irish warrior-poet Oisín.</note> by Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), the Scottish writer who claimed to have discovered and translated ancient epic poems by Ossian.</note>, which may be considered as a summary of the case of Ireland against Scotland as to these poems.

This essay is, like the others, violent in language and uncandid <note>Lacking in fairness or sincerity; biased.</note> in spirit. It deals with the controversy as it existed in the last century, and its strength consists in simply ignoring altogether the inquiry made by the **Highland Society of Scotland**, the whole of the great mass of facts collected by them being passed over in silence, and in imputing to the Scots of the present day the views and feelings of those of a century back, before the rise of a true spirit of historic inquiry and genuine criticism had led them to a just appreciation of their national history, and of the claims of Macpherson's Ossian to be viewed as an authentic work.

The publications of the **Dublin Gaelic Society** and of the **Ossianic Society** have, however, not merely assailed the Ossian of Macpherson as a fabrication, and denied to Scotland the possession of any Ossianic poems whatever; they have at length given to the world those genuine Ossianic poems alleged to exist solely in Ireland.

# INTRODUCTION.

There are only eleven <term>Ossianic<gloss>Relating to the legendary warrior-poet Oisín and the "Fenian Cycle" of Irish and Scottish mythology.</gloss></term> poems dating from before the fifteenth century—seven attributed to **Fionn** <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill, the legendary leader of the Fianna.</note> himself, two to his son **Oisín**, one to **Fergus Filidh** <note>Fergus the Poet, a brother of Oisín known for his eloquence.</note>, and one to **Caoilte** <note>Caoilte mac Rónáin, a member of the Fianna renowned for his speed and his role as a storyteller who survived into the era of Saint Patrick.</note>. Most of these are extremely short and are found primarily in the *Book of Leinster*, which is believed to have been compiled in the twelfth century, and in the *Book of Lecan* from the fifteenth century.

The theory that **Macpherson** <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), the Scottish poet who claimed to have discovered and translated ancient epics by Oisín (Ossian).</note> stole his poems directly from Ireland is clearly indefensible and inconsistent with everything we know of his activities. He never traveled to Ireland and apparently had no communication with Irish scholars or access to their manuscripts. The material he obtained was gathered in the Highlands of Scotland. This is supported by the collection of poems made by the **Dean of Lismore** <note>James MacGregor (c. 1480–1551), whose manuscript is one of the earliest and most important collections of Gaelic poetry.</note> and his brother. Their work confirms the results later reached by the inquiry of the **Highland Society of Scotland**, as it contains poems attributed directly to **Ossian** and others of the same tradition collected in the Scottish Highlands over three hundred years ago. The figures named and the subject matter are identical in character to those in Macpherson’s work. Such poems must have been preserved through oral recitation, as many of the poems recorded from oral tradition during the Highland Society’s inquiry match those found in this ancient manuscript.

Assuming, then, that Ossianic poems existed in the Highlands of Scotland and were preserved both by oral tradition and in written manuscript collections, the question arises: What is their real position in the literature of the Highlands? This question leads to a preliminary inquiry that will materially help us find the solution.

<summary>This page argues that Ossianic poetry has a legitimate, centuries-old tradition in the Scottish Highlands, independent of Irish sources, as evidenced by the Dean of Lismore's manuscript and oral traditions.</summary>

<keywords>Ossianic poetry, Fionn mac Cumhaill, Oisín, James Macpherson, Dean of Lismore, Highland Society of Scotland, Scottish Highlands, oral tradition, Book of Leinster, Book of Lecan</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION.

Who were the <term>Feinne<gloss>The Fianna; legendary semi-independent warrior bands of early Ireland and Scotland, most famously led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</gloss></term> of tradition, and to what country and period are they to be assigned?

To this question the Irish historians give a ready response.

They were a body of Irish militia, forming a kind of standing army, employed for the purpose of defending the coasts of Ireland from the invasion of foreign foes. They were billeted upon the inhabitants during winter, and obliged to maintain themselves by hunting and fishing during summer. Each of the four provinces had its band of these warriors, termed <term>Curaidhe<gloss>Champions or heroic warriors.</gloss></term> or champions. Those of Ulster were termed the <term>Curaidhe na Craoibh Ruaidhe<gloss>The Champions of the Red Branch, the elite warrior order of the ancient kingdom of Ulster.</gloss></term>, or champions of the red branch, and were stationed at <term>Eamhain<gloss>Emain Macha, also known as Navan Fort, the legendary capital of Ulster near modern-day Armagh.</gloss></term> or **Eamania**, near Ulster. To this body belonged the celebrated **Cuchullin** <note>Cú Chulainn, the central hero of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology, known for his superhuman prowess in battle.</note> and the sons of **Uisneach** <note>The brothers of Naoise, central figures in the tragic tale of Deirdre of the Sorrows.</note>. The militia of Connaught were the <term>Curaidhe</term> or champions of **Jorras Domnan** <note>Now Erris in County Mayo.</note>, otherwise called the <term>Clanna Morna<gloss>The Clan Morna, a rival faction within the Fianna often led by Goll mac Morna.</gloss></term>, to which belonged **Goll Mac Morn**, stationed at **Dun Domnan**, in Mayo. The militia of Munster were the <term>Curaidhe Clann Deaghadh<gloss>The Champions of the Clan Deaghadh.</gloss></term>, to which belonged **Curigh Mac Daire** <note>Cú Roí mac Dáire, a powerful king and sorcerer of Munster.</note>, stationed at **Cathair Conrigh**, in Kerry. The militia of Leinster were the <term>Curaighe Clanna Baoisgne<gloss>The Champions of the Clan Baoisgne, the family line of Fionn mac Cumhaill.</gloss></term>, to which belonged the renowned **Finn Mac Cumhal**, his sons, **Ossin** and **Fergus Filidh**, his grandson **Oscar**, and his relation, **Caoilte Mac Ronan**. **Cuchullin** lived in the first century, in the reign of **Conaire Mac Eidersgeoil**, King of Ireland, and **Conchobar Mac Nessa**, a king of Ulster; and at the same period lived **Curigh Mac Daire**, who was slain by him. **Finn**

Such is the account of the <term>Feinne</term> given by the Irish. If this is history, <note>original: "cadit questio"; Latin for "the question falls" or "the case is settled."</note>. The ancient Irish militia, like their more modern representatives, could not, it is presumed, be called upon to leave their country, except in case of invasion; and poems narrating their adventures and exploits must have been as Irish as the heroes which were the subject of them.

But we cannot accept it as history in any sense of the term. It is as illusory and uncertain as are the dates of **St. Patrick**, and the narrative of which the one forms a part, is as little to be regarded as a veracious chronicle, as the life of the other can be accepted as a genuine biography. The chronology of the one is as questionable as the era of the other.

Prior to the year 483, the Irish have, strictly speaking, no chronological history. The battle of <term>Ocha<gloss>A pivotal battle in early Irish history that shifted political power to the Uí Néill dynasty.</gloss></term>, fought in that year, which established the dynasty of the <term>Hy'Neills<gloss>The Uí Néill, the dominant dynasty of northern and central Ireland for several centuries.</gloss></term> on the Irish throne, and the order of things which

# INTRODUCTION.

existed subsequent to that date, is the great chronological era which separates the true from the <term>empirical<gloss>In this context, the author uses "empirical" to mean speculative or based on tradition and folklore rather than verifiable documentary evidence.</gloss></term>, the genuine annals of the country from an artificially constructed history.

Prior to that date, we find the reigns of a long succession of monarchs recorded, with a strange mixture of minute detail, chronological exactness, and the wildest fable, a wonderful structure of history palpably artificial, and ranging over a period of upwards of 3000 years. Passing over the arrival of <term>Casar<gloss>Cessair, who in the "Lebor Gabála Érenn" (Book of Invasions) is described as a granddaughter of Noah and the leader of the first settlers to reach Ireland before the Flood.</gloss></term>, Noah’s niece, who landed in Ireland forty days before the deluge, on the fifteenth day of the moon, the so-called Irish history records the arrival of four colonies before that of the **Milesians**. 

First, that of <term>Partolan<gloss>Partholón, the leader of the second group of people to settle in Ireland according to legend.</gloss></term> and his followers, who landed at <term>Inversceine<gloss>Inver Scene, traditionally identified as Kenmare Bay in County Kerry.</gloss></term>, in the west of **Munster**, on the 14th day of May, in the year of the world 2320 or 2680 years <term>B.C.</term>, and who all perished by a pestilence in one week to the number of 9000 on the **Hill of Howth**, thirty years after their arrival. 

Secondly, the <term>Nemedians<gloss>The followers of Nemed, the third group to settle Ireland in the mythological cycle.</gloss></term>, under their leader **Nemedius**, thirty years after, who, after remaining 217 years in the island, left it, in consequence of the tyranny and oppression of the pirates, termed the <term>Fomorians<gloss>A supernatural race often depicted as hostile, monstrous giants or sea-raiders who fought the early settlers of Ireland.</gloss></term>, in three bands,—one going to **Thrace**, from whom descended the **Firbolg**; the second to the North of Europe or <term>Lochlan<gloss>Lochlann, a term later used for Scandinavia, but here referring to the mystical northern homelands of the Tuatha Dé Danann.</gloss></term>, from whom descended the **Tuatha De Danann**; and the third to **Alban** or **Scotland**, from whom descended the **Britons**. 

The third colony were the **Firbolg**, who returned to Ireland 217 years after the arrival of the Nemedians, and

# INTRODUCTION.

Is it possible, however, to accept this extraordinary bead-roll <note>A "bead-roll" refers to a long list or catalogue, originally a list of people to be prayed for.</note> of shadowy monarchs during Pagan times, with their exact chronology, and the strange and almost ludicrous peculiarities by which each are distinguished, as serious history, or even to attempt to discriminate between what may be true and what is false? Are there any materials, or any data upon which we can even fix upon a date, within a reasonable compass of time, and say all before that is fable, all after may be history, till we arrive on firm ground, after the introduction of Christianity? Professor O'Curry <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862) was a foundational scholar of early Irish literature and history.</note> is right when he says, in his admirable lectures on the <term>ms.<gloss>manuscript</gloss></term> materials of ancient Irish history, that he cannot discover any ground on which the annalist Tighernac <note>Tigernach Ua Bráin (died 1088) was a famous Irish chronicler known for his critical approach to historical sources.</note> was able to say, "all records of the Scots up to Cimbaoth <note>A legendary king of Ulster, traditionally said to have founded the royal site of Emain Macha.</note> (a king of Ulster, who flourished in the seventh century, <term>B.C.<gloss>Before Christ</gloss></term>) were uncertain." <note>original: "omnia monumenta Scotorum usque Cimbaoth... incerta erant."</note>

From Slainge <gloss>Sláine mac Dela, the legendary first High King of Ireland.</gloss>, the first king of the <term>Firbolgs<gloss>A mythological race of early settlers in Ireland.</gloss></term>, who began to reign 1934 years <term>B.C.</term>, and ruled only one year, or even from Eremon, the first monarch of all Ireland of the <term>Milesian<gloss>The descendants of Míl Espáine, considered the ancestors of the Gaelic Irish.</gloss></term> race, who began to reign 1700 years <term>B.C.</term>, down to Dathy <note>Also known as Nath Í, traditionally the last pagan High King of Ireland.</note>, who was killed by a flash of lightning at the foot of the Alps in the year 428, and Laogare <note>Lóegaire mac Néill, the king traditionally associated with the arrival of St. Patrick.</note>, his successor, who was slain by the elements <note>According to legend, Lóegaire was killed by the sun and the wind for breaking an oath sworn by them.</note> for refusing obedience to St Patrick’s mission which is said to have taken place during his reign, every reign is stamped with the same character; and what to accept and what to reject is a problem, for the solution of which the history itself affords no materials.

# INTRODUCTION.

Is the later portion any less an object of such criticism than the earlier? There seems no reason why we should accept the history of Niall of the Nine Hostages <note>Niall Noígíallach (traditionally reigned 379–405 AD), the ancestor of the Uí Néill dynasties; he is a semi-historical figure whose exploits often border on the legendary.</note>, who reigned from 379 to 405, and had subjected all Britain and part of France to his sway, and reject that of Ugony the Great <note>Úgaine Mór, a legendary High King of Ireland said to have ruled 1,000 years earlier and to have conquered parts of Europe.</note>, who reigned 1,000 years earlier, and whose conquests were equally extensive and equally unknown to European history. Nor is it clear why Ugony’s twenty-five sons are less worthy of credit than the thirty sons of Cathair Mór <note>Cathair Mór was a legendary King of Leinster and High King of Ireland in the 2nd century AD.</note>, who reigned 750 years later. 

Why should the division of Ireland into the two great portions of north and south—between Conn of the Hundred Battles <note>Conn Cétchathach, a legendary High King whose territory, the northern half of Ireland, became known as "Leth Cuinn" or Conn’s Half.</note> and Modha Nuadhat <note>Mug Nuadat, King of Munster, whose territory, the southern half, became known as "Leth Moga" or Moga’s Half.</note> in the second century—be accepted in preference to the original division into the same districts between Eremon and Eber, the sons of Milesius <note>The Milesians were the legendary final race to settle Ireland; Eremon and Eber are said to have divided the island between them after their conquest.</note>? Are the conquests in Scotland by Crimthann the Great <note>Crimthann Máer mac Fidaig, a legendary king said to have expanded Irish influence into Britain.</note> and Dathy <note>Nath Í, the last pagan High King, said to have been killed by lightning in the Alps while on campaign.</note> in the fourth and fifth centuries to be accepted, while the equally detailed battles of Aongus Olmucadha and Rechtgidh Righ-derg <note>Both are legendary High Kings from the centuries before the Christian era.</note> some centuries earlier are to be rejected simply because they occupy a different place in this succession of unreal monarchs? <meta>This page continues the author’s argument that early Irish history, rather than being a factual record, is a carefully constructed narrative. ->lxxi<-

### INTRODUCTION.

<columns>

In fact, the whole of this history presents a structure so artificial, so compact, and so alike in all its features, that it is impossible for any one, like Samson, to withdraw any two pillars without bringing the whole edifice about his ears, and crushing the entire list <note>original: "bead-roll"</note> of unbaptized monarchs beneath its ruins.

The truth is, that notwithstanding the claims of the Irish to an early cultivation and to a knowledge of letters in Pagan times, the art of writing was unknown in Ireland until after the introduction of Christianity, and written history there was none. The only materials that existed for it were poems, legends, historic tales, and pedigrees, handed down by tradition; and from these, at a subsequent period, when, as in all countries, the leisure hours of monks and ecclesiastics were employed in constructing a history of pre-Christian times, in imitation of more classical histories, a highly artificial system was by degrees constructed, embodying the substance of traditions and myths, real facts and imaginative poems, with bardic and monkish creations, and the whole based upon the classical model, by which the different ethnic <note>original: "ethnological"</note> elements which entered into the population of the country were cloaked under an artificial and symbolical genealogy.

But it is not chronological history. The dates are quite artificial, and the whole creation melts and resolves itself into its original elements upon investigation. The <term>pre-Milesian</term> <note>The Milesians were the legendary last wave of Gaelic settlers to arrive in Ireland; "pre-Milesian" refers to the mythical groups said to have lived there before them.</note> colonies are found existing with the first introduction of Christianity into Ireland. 

The same tract states, that the mythic

<column-break/>

King Cormac Mac Art “was slain by Siabhras <note>Supernatural beings, spirits, or phantoms.</note>, *that is* <note>original: "id est"</note>, the <term>Tuatha De Danann</term> <note>The "People of the Goddess Danu," a race of supernatural beings or deities in Irish mythology who were said to have inhabited the island before the Milesians.</note>, for they were called Siabhras.”

</columns>

The author asserts that writing did not exist in Ireland before Christianity, making the precise dating of pagan monarchs historically unreliable.</summary>

The provincial kings, when closely examined, lose their <term>Milesian</term> <note>The Milesians were the legendary Gaelic ancestors of the Irish, said to have arrived from Spain.</note> name, and are found ruling over the <term>Firbolgs</term>, <term>Firdomnan</term> <note>Ancient tribes that supposedly inhabited Ireland before the arrival of the Gaels.</note>, and Picts <note>original: "Cruithne"</note>; and notwithstanding that the Milesians had supposedly been in possession of the country for 1600 years, and a flourishing monarchy is thought to have existed for so long a period, we find, as late as the second century after Christ, the "vassal tribes" <note>original: "Attachtuatha"; these were the non-Gaelic populations who staged a famous revolt against the Milesian nobility.</note>—as the descendants of the Firbolg, Firdomnan, and the <term>People of the Goddess Danu</term> <note>original: "Tuatha De Danann"; a supernatural race in Irish mythology.</note> were termed during the Milesian monarchy—in full possession of the country for nearly a century, and in close alliance with the Picts <note>original: "Cruithne"</note> of Ulster. The race of Ir, son of Milesius, who possessed the whole of Ulster until the settlements of the descendants of Eremon almost within the period of recorded history, are found calling themselves on all occasions Picts <note>original: "Cruithne"</note>. The descendants of Ith called themselves the Children of Breogan <note>original: "Clanna Breogan"</note>, and occupy the territory where Ptolemy <note>A 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian geographer.</note>, in the second century, places an offshoot of the British <term>Brigantes</term> <note>A powerful Celtic tribe from northern Britain.</note>. lxxiii

The legend of St. Patrick, too, in its present shape, is not older than the ninth century; and, under the influence of an investigation into older authorities, he dissolves into three personages: <term>Old Patrick</term> <note>original: "Sen-Patricius"</note>, whose day in the calendar is the 24th August; <term>Palladius</term> <term>who is Patrick</term> <note>original: "qui est Patricius"</note>, to whom the mission in 432 properly belongs, and who is said to have retired to <term>Scotland</term> <note>original: "Alban"</note>, where he died among the <term>Picts</term> <note>original: "Cruithne"</note>; and <term>Patrick</term> <note>original: "Patricius"</note>, whose day is the 17th of March, and to whom alone a certain date can be assigned—for he died, in the chronological period, in the year 493—and from the acts of these three saints the subsequent legend of the great apostle of Ireland was compiled, and an arbitrary chronology applied to it.

The <term>Fenians</term> <note>original: "Feine"; the legendary warrior bands of early Ireland, often associated with Fionn Mac Cumhaill.</note> also, when looked at a little more closely, emerge from under the guise of a <term>Milesian</term> <note>The Milesians were the legendary Gaelic ancestors of the Irish, said to have arrived from Spain.</note> militia, and assume the features of a distinct race. <term>Cuchulainn</term> <note>original: "Cuchullin"</note>, <term>Conall the Victorious</term> <note>original: "Conall cearnach"</note>, and the children of <term>Uisneach</term> belong to the race of <term>Ir</term> <note>One of the legendary sons of Milesius; his descendants were often associated with the Ulaid (Ulster) and the Picts.</note>, and are <term>Picts</term> <note>original: "Cruithne"</note>. <term>Goll son of Morna</term> <note>original: "Goll Mac Morn"</note> and his <term>Clan Morna</term> <note>original: "Clanna Moirne"</note> are <term>Firbolg</term> <note>An ancient, pre-Gaelic population of Ireland.</note>; The fact is, when the fictitious catalogue of <term>Milesian</term> kings was extended over so many centuries, and the <term>Milesian</term> monarchy pushed back to so remote a period, it became necessary to account for the appearance of non-<term>Milesian</term> races in the old traditional stories. They were either clothed with a <term>Milesian</term> name and pedigree, or some device was hit upon to account for their separate existence; and thus the <term>Fenians</term> <note>original: "Feinne"; the legendary warrior bands led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>, a pre-<term>Milesian</term> warrior race they could not account for, appear under the somewhat clumsy guise of a standing body of <term>Milesian</term> militia, having peculiar privileges and strange customs.

The Irish <term>Ossianic</term> <note>Pertaining to the legends of Oisín and Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note> poems, as well as those in the <term>Dean’s Manuscript</term> <note>The Book of the Dean of Lismore, a 16th-century collection of Gaelic poetry compiled in Scotland.</note>, indicate that the <term>Fenians</term> were not a body of troops confined to Ireland, but belonged, whoever they were, to a much wider extent of territory.

Thus, the poem on the battle of <term>Gowra</term> <note>original: "Gabhra"; the legendary final battle that led to the destruction of the Fenian bands.</note>, published in the first volume of the Transactions of the Ossianic Society—a battle in which <term>Oscar</term> the son of <term>Ossian</term> was

<summary>The author argues that the Fenians (Feinne) were originally an indigenous, pre-Gaelic warrior race whose history was later distorted by medieval scribes to fit into the "Milesian" (Gaelic) royal lineage. He highlights that ancient poems suggest the Fenians were a widespread group not limited to Ireland alone.</summary>

lxxvi

There is every reason to believe that the Low German race were preceded, in the more ancient <term>Scandinavia</term>, by a Celtic people.

The <term>Fianna</term> <note>original: "Feinne"</note> then belonged to the pre-Milesian races <note>Those peoples who inhabited Ireland before the arrival of the Milesians, the final legendary Gaelic settlers.</note>, and were connected, not only with <term>Ireland</term> <note>original: "Erin"</note>, but likewise with <term>Scotland</term> <note>original: "Alban"</note>, <term>Britain</term> <note>original: "Breatan"</note>, and <term>Scandinavia</term> <note>original: "Lochlan"</note>. The traditionary migration of the <term>Tuatha Dé Danann</term> brings them from <term>Scandinavia</term>, where they possessed four cities, to <term>Scotland</term>, where they inhabited a district termed <term>Dobhar</term> and <term>Ir Dobhar</term> <note>Likely referring to the regions of 'Water' and 'Outer Water' in northern Scotland.</note>; and from thence they went to <term>Ireland</term>, where they drove out the <term>Firbolg</term> <note>An earlier mythological race of settlers in Ireland.</note>, to be subdued in their turn by the <term>Milesian Scots</term>.

The <term>Picts</term> <note>original: "Cruithne"</note> are likewise brought from <term>Scandinavia</term> to <term>Ireland</term> and from <term>Ireland</term> to <term>Scotland</term>, where they founded a kingdom, which included, till the seventh century, the <term>Picts</term> of <term>Ulster</term>, and which was subverted in the ninth century by the <term>Milesian Scots</term>.

These two tribes were thus the prior race in each country. Both must have been prior to the Low German population of <term>Scandinavia</term>. The <term>Picts</term> were the race prior to the <term>Scots</term> in <term>Scotland</term>, and the <term>Tuatha Dé Danann</term> the prior colony to the <term>Milesian Scots</term> in <term>Ireland</term>. # INTRODUCTION.

lxxviii

->*<term>Huasein</term> <note>A legendary poet or bard, possibly a variation of Oisín.</note> was the name of his poet,*<-
->*Who sought out the path of pleasantry.*<-

In answering, then, the preliminary questions of who were the <term>Fianna</term> <note>original: "Feinne"; the legendary bands of semi-independent warriors, most famously led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>? and to what period do they belong? we may fairly infer that they were of the population who immediately preceded the <term>Scots</term> <note>In this historical context, "Scots" refers to the Gaelic-speaking people (the Milesians) who migrated from Ireland to settle in northern Britain.</note> in <term>Ireland</term> <note>original: "Erin"</note> and in <term>Scotland</term> <note>original: "Alban"</note>, and that they belong to that period in the history of both countries, before a political separation had taken place between them, when they were viewed as parts of one territory, though physically separated, and when a free and unrestrained intercourse took place between them; when race and not territory was the great bond of association, and the movements of their respective populations from one country to the other were not restrained by any feeling of national separation.

It was natural that the deeds and events connected with this warrior race, associated as they must have been with the physical features of the country in which they dwelt, should have formed the subject of the early poems and legendary tales of their successors, and that a body of popular poetry should have sprung up in each country, which occupied itself with adventures, expeditions, and feats of bravery of this previous race, which were common to both countries, and which, attributed to their mythic poets, and full of the names of heroes, and of the scenes of their exploits, would be appropriated by the <term>bards</term> <note>The professional poets and keepers of history and genealogy in Celtic society.</note> of each country to their own districts. The names of the places connected in tradition with these events would, as they were localized in the respective countries,

# INTRODUCTION.

lxxix

be identified with its scenery and physical features, and thus a species of <term>Fenian topography</term> <note>The practice of naming geographical landmarks—such as hills, caves, or stones—after the legendary Fianna warriors.</note> would spring up in each country. Because these traditions shared a common origin, they would bear the same character and possess a mutual resemblance. Each country would thus claim the <term>Fianna</term> <note>original: "Feinne"; the legendary bands of semi-independent warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note> as their own exclusive property. They could each point to a body of popular <term>Ossianic poetry</term> <note>Ballads and poems attributed to the bard Oisín, Fionn's son, which describe the exploits of the Fianna.</note> to support their claim, and to the Fenian names of their own localities as proof that the events described in the poems had occurred right there.

The references to <term>Fingal</term> <note>The name used by James Macpherson in the 18th century for Fionn mac Cumhaill; the author argues here that the name was not Macpherson's invention.</note> in the works of older Scottish historians—who wrote long before <term>Macpherson’s Ossian</term> <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796) published "translations" of ancient Gaelic poetry that sparked a massive international controversy over whether they were authentic or forged.</note> appeared or the controversy arose—show that stories of the Fianna were current in Scotland. They show that these figures were regarded as belonging to Scotland as much as to Ireland. Furthermore, the presence of Fenian names for locations in land charters and other old documents proves that a Fenian topography likewise existed in Scotland before that period.¹

---

¹ It is unnecessary here to repeat these references. They will be found in the *Report of the Highland Society*, page 21.

The quotation from <term>Barbour</term> <note>John Barbour (c. 1320–1395), a Scottish poet known for his epic "The Brus."</note> shows that the name of Fingal was known long before the time of Macpherson. Since most Gaelic proper names had a corresponding name in English that resembled it in sound and was used to represent it—such as Hector for <term>Eachin</term> <note>Gaelic: "Eachann."</note> or Hugh for <term>Aodh</term>—it is not unlikely that "Fingal" may have been recognized as the English representative of the name <term>Finn</term> <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>.

In fact, Finn and Fingal are both real names and are closely related to each other.

*Gal* is a syllable of unknown origin and meaning, which enters largely into

<column-break/>

the composition of Gaelic proper names. Thus we have <term>Aedgal</term> <note>Derived from "Aodh" (fire) and "gal" (valor/stranger).</note>, Aelgal, Angal, <term>Ardgal</term> <note>Meaning "High valor."</note>, Artgal, Bodgal, <term>Comgal</term> <note>Commonly Comhghall.</note>, <term>Congal</term> <note>Meaning "Fierce as a hound."</note>, Donngal, <term>Dubhgal</term> <note>Meaning "Dark stranger," the root of the name Dougal.</note>, Dungal, <term>Feargal</term> <note>Meaning "Man of valor," the root of the name Farrell.</note>, <term>Fingal</term> <note>Gaelic: "Fionnghal," meaning "Fair stranger" or "Fair valor."</note>, Gormgal, Leargal, Maengal, Riagal, Saergal, and Smiorgal. Some also take the form of *galach*, such as Congalach, Dungalach, Fiangalach, and Irgalach.

Those names in which the first syllable expresses a color appear both alone and with the added suffix *gal*, such as *Dubh* (Dark) and *Dubhgal*, or *Finn* (Fair) and *Finngal*; they are essentially the same name. The <term>Annals of the Four Masters</term> <note>A 17th-century chronicle of Irish history gathered from older medieval sources.</note> mention several persons by the name of Finn, and, in the year 741, they record a man named <term>Finghal of Lismore</term>.

The "Roughbounds" <note>original: "Garbh-chriochan"; a traditional name for the rugged coastal districts of the West Highlands.</note> were the districts from Morvaren to Glenelg, which, with the Isles, are thus called the land of the Feinne.

The districts in which the Fenian names enter most largely into the topography of the Highlands are Atholl, Lochaber, Lorn, and Morvaren, Glenelg, and the districts about Loch Ness; and the antiquity of this topography in the Highlands of Scotland is proved by an old <term>gloss</term> <note>A brief marginal or interlinear explanation of a word in a text.</note> to a charter by Alexander the Second to the monks of Kinloss of the lands of Burgyn, within the ancient Celtic province of Moray, which is preserved in the Chartulary of the Bishopric. # INTRODUCTION.

lxxxii

->[Garbh-a]bhainn and the waterfall of Assaroe <note>original: "Eassroy"; refers to Eas Ruaidh. While famous in Ireland, the author is noting a Scottish equivalent.</note>, and also on the south bank of Loch Ness. Daire Donn <note>A legendary figure often styled as the "King of the World" who invades Ireland in Fenian myths.</note>, who appears in the *Battle of Ventry* <note>original: "Cath Finntragha"; a central tale of the Fenian Cycle describing a massive battle against foreign invaders at Ventry Harbor.</note> identified by the Irish with Ventry, has also deposited his name on a mountain in Ardgour, close to the west sea, called Meall Dayre Donn <note>The Hill of Daire Donn.</note>.<-

The mountain streams and lakes in these districts of the Highlands are everywhere redolent of names connected with the heroes and actions of the <term>Fenians</term> <note>original: "Feinne"; the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill in Gaelic mythology.</note>, and show that a body of popular legends connected with them, whether in poetry or in prose, preserved by oral recitation or committed to writing, must have existed in the country when this topography sprung up, though it does not follow that the events, though now associated with the scenery of the country, originally happened there any more than does the Fenian topography of Ireland.

These legendary poems and tales seem to have passed through three different stages.

In the first and oldest form they were pure poems, of more or less excellence, narrating the adventures and deeds of these warrior bands, whose memory still lingered in the country ; each poem being complete in itself, and constructed upon a metrical system which brought the aid of alliteration and of rhyme, or correspondence of sounds, to assist the memory in retaining what had been received by oral recitation, and to render it less easy to forget or lose a part. These poems seem generally to have been attributed to one mythic poet <note>A reference to Ossian (Oisín), the legendary warrior-bard and son of Fionn mac Cumhaill, to whom most Fenian lays are traditionally attributed.</note> of the race they celebrate.

Then, as the language in which these poems were composed became altered or modified, or as the reciters were

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the evolution of Gaelic legendary literature. The previous page established that these stories began as pure poems about the warrior bands known as the Fenians, which were then tied to the local geography of both Ireland and the Scottish Highlands.</meta>

# INTRODUCTION.

lxxxiii

...less able to retain the whole, they would narrate, in ordinary prose, the events of the part of the poem they had forgotten, and merely recite the poetry of what they recollected; and thus they would pass into the second stage of prose tales, interspersed with fragments of poetry.

<term>Bards</term> <note>In Gaelic society, bards were professional poet-singers who preserved history, genealogy, and legends through song.</note> who were themselves composers as well as reciters, besides composing poems on the subjects of the day in which they lived, would likewise select the <term>Fenian</term> <note>Relating to the Feinne, the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note> legends as their themes, and become imitators of the older <term>Ossianic</term> <note>Refers to Oisín (or Ossian), the son of Fionn mac Cumhaill, who was traditionally credited with composing the poems that record the deeds of the Fenians.</note> poetry. The prose narrative would form the basis of their poem; and thus would arise the third stage of their poems, in which they were reconstructed from the prose tales, and again appear as long poems, the names and incidents being the same as in the older poems, and the fragments of them preserved in the prose tale, imbedded in the new poem.

The poems of the first stage were probably common to Ireland and to Scotland, and traces of them are to be found wherever the <term>Feinne</term> <note>The "Feinne" or Fenians were a legendary heroic order of warriors in Gaelic mythology.</note> were supposed to have once existed; though, in countries where their successors were of a different race, and spoke a different language, the continuity of the tradition would be at once broken.

Among the ancient poems in the Welsh language which have been preserved, there exists an <term>Ossianic</term> poem called *The Death-song of Curoi, son of Daire* <note>original Welsh: "Marwnad Coire map Daire"</note>, the traditionary head, according to Irish history, of the <term>Fenian</term> militia of <term>Munster</term> <note>The southernmost province of Ireland, where Curoi mac Daire was said to rule from his stone fortress.</note>, but who, as we have seen, appears to belong to the body called the <term>Fenians of Britain</term> <note>original: "Feinne of Breatan"; referring to the branch of these legendary warriors associated with the British (Welsh) territories.</note>; and the poem, no doubt, belongs to the...

<summary>This page describes the three-stage evolution of Fenian legends from original oral poetry to prose tales, and finally into reconstructed long poems. It also highlights the shared mythological heritage between Ireland, Scotland, and Wales, specifically regarding the figure of Curoi mac Daire.</summary>

Curoi <note>Cú Roí mac Dáire, a legendary king of West Munster and a powerful sorcerer in Irish mythology.</note> is called Chief of the Southern Sea, and the contest between him and Cuchullin <note>Cú Chulainn, the central hero of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology, known for his superhuman prowess in battle.</note> is mentioned.¹

Poems of the same character seem also to have been known in the Isle of Man, as O’Connor, in his catalogue of the Stow <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for Manuscript.</note>, mentions a <term>MS.</term> containing “Finn and Ossian,—a Manx coronach <note>A funeral song or lament, typically associated with the Highlands of Scotland or Ireland.</note>, with Manx on one side of the page, and Irish on the other.”

The oldest which has been found in any <term>MSS.</term> preserved in the Highlands is a poem of five quatrains, at the end of a glossary contained in a <term>MS.</term> written prior to the year 1500. It is in old Gaelic, and there is an interlineal gloss <note>Explanatory notes written between the lines of a text to help the reader understand archaic or difficult language.</note>, explaining the meaning of the expressions in more familiar language. At the end there is a line stating that Ossian <note>Oisín, the legendary warrior-poet of the Feinne.</note> was the author of the poem.

It may be not uninteresting to insert here the text of the poem, with its glosses <note>The original text is in Old Gaelic; the translator has rendered both the poem and the medieval explanatory notes into English.</note>:—

-> <gloss>that is: my eyes slept</gloss> <-

-> <gloss>that is: my sword in my hand</gloss> <-
-> my sword was in my hand <-
-> <gloss>that is: my hand under my ear</gloss> <-
-> and my hand under my ear <-

-> <gloss>that is: a vision, that is: it happened</gloss> <-
-> A great vision was upon me <-
-> <gloss>that is: which I let loose, that is: my hound</gloss> <-
-> when I swiftly unleashed my hound <-

My eyes slumbered in sleep,
My spear was with my shield,
My sword was in my hand,
And my hand under my ear.

A strange dream happened to me,
I set swiftly my dogs
On a sow in the plain upon flesh.
She was fat to the tusk in her jaw,
Thirty feet for me with my shoes
In her side to the beard of her snout.
Thirty inches for Finn in her tusk
Fat above on her under her hide.
Large as a cauldron was each eye,
Large as a vessel the hollow beneath.
My sword hewed in her neck,
And my dogs fixed on her ear.
Sow of the sea of eastern Tallann,
Which strikes the rock where the wave touches.
My limbs were to me a protection to me strong,
As thyself not weak like thee.

Ossian <note>The legendary poet-warrior of the Fenian cycle, son of Finn Mac Cumhaill</note> sung this at the shore of the sea of Tallann, for the champion of the sow.¹

The tales of Cuchullin <note>The central hero of the Ulster Cycle, often compared to Achilles</note> and Conlaoch, and the tale of the Sons of Uisneach, are good specimens of the second class. The latter is one of three tales, called the Three Woes, the two others relating to families of the Tuatha De Danann <note>The "People of the Goddess Danu," a supernatural race in Irish mythology said to have inhabited Ireland before the Celts</note>; but though these tales may be Irish, and of this
