The third class of Ossianic poems <note>Poems attributed to the legendary bard Ossian, son of Finn Mac Cumhaill.</note> belongs principally to that period when, during the sway of the Lords of the Isles <note>The MacDonald rulers of the western coast and islands of Scotland from the 12th to 15th centuries.</note>, Irish influence was so much felt on the language and literature of the Highlands, and when the Highland bards and sennachies <term>sennachies: traditional Gaelic historians or genealogists</term> were trained in bardic schools, presided over by Irish bards of eminence. It was at this period mainly that the Irish poems assumed so much the shape of a dialogue between the Ossianic poets and St. <note>The text likely refers to St. Patrick, who in legend debates the merits of pagan heroism versus Christian piety with the aged Ossian.</note>

¹ The oldest copy of this tale, which was the foundation of Macpherson's *Darthula* <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796) published "translations" of ancient Gaelic poetry that sparked a global literary sensation, though their authenticity was hotly debated.</note>, is in the Glenmasan Manuscript, in the collection in the Advocates' Library, which bears the date of 1238, and this translation is made from it.
The scenery is all in Argyllshire. Inis Draighen is Inistrynich in Loch Awe; Dun Suibhne, Castle Sween; Glenlaidhe is now called Glenlochy, where is Benlaoidhe; Glenmasan still bears the name; Gleneitche, in another copy called Loch Eitche, is Glenetive and Loch Etive; Glenurchain is Glenurchay; and Glendaruadh is now called Glendaruail.

## INTRODUCTION.

Patrick, the apostle of Ireland; and the Highland bards imitated this form, often adding or prefixing a few sentences of such dialogue to older poems, or composing poems in imitation of Ossian <note>The legendary Gaelic bard and warrior, son of Finn Mac Cumhail</note> in this form; but the imitation, in this respect, of Irish poems by native bards is apparent from this, that Patrick is in the Irish poems correctly called Mac Calphurn or M'Alphurn, his father, according to his own “Confession,” <note>original: "Confessio"; the autobiographical work written by Saint Patrick in Latin</note> having been Calphurnius, but the Highland bards, to whom Patrick's history was strange, and this epithet <term>epithet: a descriptive name or title</term> unintelligible, have substituted the peculiarly Scotch form of Alpine, and styled him Patrick Mac Alpine.

One of the poems in Macpherson's fragments <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), who published controversial "translations" of Ossianic epic poetry</note> has been one of these—the sixth fragment,—which begins and ends with a dialogue between Ossian and the son of Alpin.

It was at the same period that the collection of Gaelic poems was made by the Dean of Lismore <note>James MacGregor, who compiled a significant manuscript of Gaelic poetry in the early 16th century</note>, and it includes many poems in which this dialogue occurs, but in most the saint is termed Macalpine, showing its non-Irish source.

The Ossianic poems in this collection attributed to Ossian, Fergus the Poet <note>original: "Fergus Filidh"; Fergus was the brother of Ossian and the chief bard of the Fenians</note>, and Caoilte, the three Fenian bards, and those which are either anonymous or composed by imitators, as Gillecalum Mac an Olla and Allan Mac Ruadhri, with the other poems which are not Ossianic, afford a fair specimen of the poetic literature current in the Highlands of Scotland at the close of this period, and before the fall of the Lords of the Isles <note>The semi-independent rulers of the west coast and islands of Scotland</note>, and the Reformation again severed that country from Ireland,

It highlights the Dean of Lismore's collection as a representative sample of Highland literature before the Reformation.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the introductory argument regarding the shared cultural heritage of Fenian lore between Ireland and Scotland, likely written by the scholar William Forbes Skene (W. F. S.).</meta>

<header>INTRODUCTION.</header>
and ushered in a period of reaction and return towards the native dialect and literature.

On the whole, then, we fully admit the claims of Ireland to Fenian <term>Fenian: relating to the Fianna, a legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill</term> legends and tales, and their attendant poems, but not to an exclusive possession of them.

We admit that its Fenian topography <note>The naming of physical landmarks after legendary heroes</note> is authentic, but it is not the only one.

We admit its claim to an early written and cultivated speech, but not to the only dialect of Gaelic in which such poems once existed.

We hold that Scotland possesses likewise Fenian legends and Ossianic <note>Attributed to Ossian, the legendary bard and son of Fionn</note> poetry derived from an independent source, and a Fenian topography equally genuine; and we consider her dialect of the common Gaelic tongue not undeserving of the attention of philologers <note>Historical linguists who study the development of languages</note>.

->W. F. S.<- <note>William Forbes Skene, a prominent 19th-century Scottish historian and antiquary</note>

<summary>The author concludes the introduction by arguing that while Ireland has a legitimate claim to Fenian and Ossianic traditions, Scotland possesses an equally authentic and independent version of these legends and landscapes.</summary>

<sig>A</sig>

->THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE.<-

->The author of this is Ossian,¹ the son of Finn :²<-

->I've seen the household of Finn.<-
->No men were they of coward race.<-
->I saw by my side a vision<-
->Of the hero's household yesterday.<-

<margin>¹ The name of this poet has given rise to some controversy between the Scottish and Irish Gaels. By the latter it is pronounced *Oisin*, the accent falling on the last syllable; by the former it is pronounced *Ossian*, the accent falling on the first. Dean McGregor <note>James McGregor, the 16th-century compiler of this manuscript</note> spells the word sometimes *Ossan*, but usually *Ossin*. It is manifest from the use of the *ss* that he intends the accent to fall upon the first syllable, according to the Scottish mode. The Scottish pronunciation would appear to have been the same in his days as now. This form of the word we have retained in our translation. We do not mean to institute any comparison between the Irish and Scottish mode. The difference clearly arises from the peculiarity of each dialect, the Irish almost uniformly, in words of two syllables, laying the accent on the last, and the Scottish upon the first syllable.</margin>
<margin>² The Dean's Finn is the Fingal of some writers. "Fionn," pronounced "*Fiughn*," genitive, "*Fhinn*," <note>The possessive form of the name</note> pronounced "*Ighn*," is the present Scottish and Irish form of the word, and we have preserved this in McGregor's own orthography. Scottish writers have, however, been unjustly accused of manufacturing the term "*Fingal*." It is not, as some Irish scholars have maintained, a modern corruption of *Fin son of Cumhall*, but a word known and in use for centuries. John Barbour, who wrote his metrical life of King Robert Bruce in 1375, uses it as a familiar term:—
> "He said, Methinks Martheoke's son
> Right as Goll son of Morna was won,
> To have from Fingal his company."
It would appear, from the analogies of the Gaelic language, to be the complete form of the word. "Fionn" means *fair*.</margin>

# THE BOOK OF

I’ve seen the household¹ of Art,²
He with the brown-haired son of gentle speech;
No better man I ever saw.
I’ve seen the household of Finn.
Who ever saw what I have seen?
I’ve seen Finn armed with Luno’s son.³
How sad the mournful memory.
I’ve seen the household of Finn.
Never can I recount the ills
Which now do crown my head.
Do thou free us for ever from pain.
I’ve seen the household of Finn.

->I’ve seen, etc.<-

being in reality an adjective noun; “Gal” is a common termination of Celtic proper names. Hence, “Fionn Ghal,” or “Fingal,” means the “fair-haired one.” In like manner, we have “Dubh,” *black*, “Ghal,” “Dubhghal,” or “Dugald;” “Donn,” *brown*, “Ghal,” “Donnghal,” *Dungald, Donald*, the brown-haired one. Hence, Fionn is a contraction of the name. It is remarkable as an instance of the changes which take place in the use of words, that in modern times “Fionnghal” is the name of a woman, and is usually translated “Flora.”

¹ In the original the word translated here, “household,” is “*tylych*,” or “*teaghlach*,” *a family*. The literal translation would be, “the family of Art.” But this would not convey the idea in the original, the Celtic *family* in such a case as this implying the military followers of the head or chief. <note>The translator explains that "family" in this context refers to a chieftain's war-band or retinue rather than a domestic unit.</note> It seems probable that these ancient Celtic chiefs, like chiefs in more recent times, had their armed followers in constant attendance on them.

² Art was King of Ireland, according to Irish authorities, in the beginning of the third century. Tradition says that he was the father of Grainne, the wife of Finn, whose defection and escape with Diarmad led to the event so famous in ancient Celtic poetry, the death of Diarmad. A poem relating the event will be found in this collection.

³ “*Mac an Loinn*,” or “*Luno’s son*,” was the famous sword of Fingal, manufactured by Loinn mac Liobhaidh, the celebrated smith, or “*Vulcan*,” <note>Vulcan was the Roman god of fire and metalworking; the author uses this as a comparison for the legendary Celtic smith.</note> of the Celts. The sword was so effective that in no case was it ever required to give a second stroke. The Gaelic words are, “*Cha d’ fhàg e fuigheall beuma*,”—It left no remnant for its stroke. <note>original: "Cha d’ fhàg e fuigheall beuma"</note> We have heard of a remarkable instance of the effective use of this phrase in the pulpit by a distinguished Highland minister, Mr. Lachlan M’Kenzie of Lochcarron. In illustrating the completeness of the one sacrifice of our Lord, he said, and to a Highland audience it was electrifying, “*Chuala sibh mu ’n chlaidheamh bh’ aig Fionn, nach d’ fhàg riamh fuigheall beuma*,”—*You have heard of Fingal’s sword, which never needed to give a second blow*. <note>original: "Chuala sibh mu ’n chlaidheamh bh’ aig Fionn, nach d’ fhàg riamh fuigheall beuma"</note> “*Loinn mac Liobhaidh*,” the “*Vulcan*” of the Celts, is in reality *Brightness*, the son of *polishing*, a fact which would go far to prove the mythical character of this famous artisan.

<header>THE DEAN OF LISMORE.</header>

### The author of this is Ossian :¹

<margin><unclear>swing gun</unclear></margin>
Long are the clouds this night above me;
The last was a long night to me.
This day, although I find it long,
Yesterday was longer still.
Each day that comes is long to me,
Such indeed was not my wont.
Now is no fight, or battle-field,
No learning noble feats of arms
Without maiden, song, or harp;
No crushing bones or warlike deeds,
No studious learning any more,
No hospitable heart or board,
No soft wooing, and no chase,
In both of which I took delight.
Without the battle-march or fight,
Alas! how sorrowful life's close;
No hunting of the hind or stag,
How different from my heart's desire!
No trappings for our hounds, no hounds.
Long are the clouds this night above me.
No rising up to noble feats,
No mirthful sport as we would wish,
No swimming heroes in our lakes.

¹ This piece is extracted and printed in the report on the Poems of Ossian, published by the Highland Society. Dr. Smith, however, who made the extract, appears not to have read it with much care or accuracy, and the concluding portion, from the twentieth line downwards, is suppressed altogether. This seems to have arisen from a desire to suppress all the references in those poems to St. Patrick, and thus to establish by all possible means their Scottish origin. <note>The "Ossian Controversy" involved debates over whether these poems were ancient Scottish epics or later Irish inventions; references to St. Patrick (an Irish figure) were often removed by Scottish editors to favor a local origin.</note> If any of the Poems are the genuine compositions of Ossian, there is sufficient evidence that the references to the Saint are of more recent introduction, in the fact, that if Ossian saw Art <note>Art mac Cuinn, a High King of Ireland</note>, who lived in the opening of the third century, as he tells us in the preceding fragment, he could hardly hold a dialogue with St. Patrick, who flourished in the fifth. In the present publication the poems of every kind are given just as they stand, without any reference to the effect on existing systems and theories, Scotch or Irish.

<summary>A melancholic poem attributed to the legendary bard Ossian lamenting his old age and the loss of heroic culture, accompanied by a scholarly note on the editorial suppression of St. Patrick's name in previous editions.</summary>
<keywords>Ossian, Highland Society, Dr. Smith, St. Patrick, Art mac Cuinn, Scottish literature, Irish mythology, Ossianic Controversy</keywords>

Long are the clouds this night above me;
In this great world none is like me,
So sad, how sad my case!
A poor old man now dragging stones. <note>This likely refers to the legend of Ossian's return from the Land of Youth, where he lost his strength upon touching the earth and was forced into menial labor or witnessed the building of churches.</note>

The Author of this is Ossian :

Once on a time when Finn my loved
Went to hunt on the “Fair maids’ hill,”²
With three thousand nobles of the Feine,
Their shields aloft o’er their heads.

---

¹ The bells used in Christian worship. “Patrick of the bells,” is a common appellation of St. Patrick in these compositions. <note>The sound of church bells often symbolizes the new Christian order displacing the old heroic age of the Fianna.</note>
² “Sliabh nam ban fionn,” <note>original: "Sliabh nam ban fionn"</note> or the “hill of the fair-haired women,” is said to be one of the mountains of Tipperary, in the neighbourhood of Clonmel. It is now called “Sliabh nam ban,” and has several traditions of the Feine associated with it. The writer is not aware of any mountain of the name in Scotland; besides, although the word “Sliabh” <note>A Gaelic word for a mountain or moorland.</note> is well known and in common use among the Scottish Highlanders, it is seldom found in the topography of the country, in which the almost uniform term for a mountain is “Beinn,” the English “Ben.” This is one of the marked differences between Irish and Scottish topography. The term under consideration has sometimes been called in Scotland “Sliabh nam beann fionn,” “the hill of the fair hills,” a manifest mistake, which the meaningless tautology should be enough to prove. Topographical phrases in the Gaelic language are usually not only grammatically accurate, but of remarkable elegance in their structure. The interpretations often put upon them are a monstrous outrage upon this sound and invariable principle.

<summary>Ossian laments his lonely old age as the last survivor of the Fenian warriors and begins a poem about a great hunt led by his father, Finn MacCumhail. The text includes a detailed scholarly note on the Irish geographical origins of the "Hill of the Fair Women."</summary>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Ossian! your words are sweet to me,
My blessing on the soul of Finn. <note>Finn MacCumhail, the legendary leader of the Fianna.</note>
Tell us the number of the deer
That fell on the “Fair maids’ hill.” <note>original: "Sliabh nam ban fionn," likely Slievenamon in County Tipperary.</note>

How vigorously we shook our spears,
For never hast thou sung of the deer
Slain on the “Fair maids’ hill,”
By the hand of Finn of the feasts.
Tell them the tale in full,
My blessing on your guileless lips.
Had you your dress and your armour
When you went forth to the chase?

We had our dress and our armour
When we went forth to the chase;
There was no Fian <term>Fian: a member of the Fianna, an elite band of warriors in Gaelic mythology</term> amongst us all
Without his fine soft flaxen shirt,
Without his under-coat of substance soft,
Without a coat of mail of brightest steel,
The covering for his head adorned with gems,
And in his hand he bore two spears,
Besides a fierce and conquering shield,
And sword that never failed to cleave the skull.
Were you to search the universe
You would not find a braver man than Finn;
Of noblest race and fairest form,
No arm from him could carry victory.
As he went forth to try his snow-white hound
Who amongst us all was like to Finn?
Westward we went, an ordered band,

<margin>1 It is obvious that parts of these compositions are dialogues, for the most part between Ossian and St. Patrick. The dialogue portion in these editions of Dean McGregor’s is generally either prefixed or added to the body of the poem. This is quite consistent with the genuineness of the work, and can be explained upon the supposition, that these portions were either prefixed or adjoined at a later period by some other hand to serve a purpose. This cannot be said of those poems which are in the form of dialogues throughout.</margin>

# THE BOOK OF

->To hunt on the “Fair maids’ hill.”<-

O Patrick, pupil of the church’s head,
Bright was the sun above us,
As in the midst of us sat Finn <note>Finn MacCumhail, the legendary leader of the Fenian warriors</note>.
Eastward and westward sweetly rung,
From hill to hill the voice of hounds,
Arousing boars and harts.
<margin>¹ Bran was the famous hound of Finn. The name means “a raven,” but used as an adjective it signifies “black,” which is apparently the origin of the name. Another of his dogs was called “Swift” <note>original: "Luath"</note>, also an adjective. These are common names for staghounds in the Highlands at this day. Reference is made a few lines before this to a white dog <note>original: "a choin ghil"</note>, translated “snow-white,” the word for white <note>original: "geal"</note> implying the most intense whiteness.</margin>
Then Finn and Bran ¹ did sit alone
A little while upon the mountain side,
Each of them panting for the chase,
Their fierceness and their wrath aroused.
Then did we unloose three thousand hounds
Of matchless vigour and unequalled strength.
Each of the hounds brought down two deer,
Long ere ’twas time to bind them in their thongs.
That day there fell six thousand deer,
Down in the vale that lies beneath the hill;
There never fell so many deer and roe
In any hunt that e’er till this took place.
But sad was the chase down to the east,
Thou cleric of the church and bells,
Ten hundred of our hounds, with golden chains,
Fell wounded by ten hundred boars:
Then by our hands there fell the boars,
Which wrought the ill upon the plain.
And were it not for blades and vigorous arms,
That chase had been a slaughter.

O Patrick of the holy crosier <note>The stylized staff carried by a bishop, symbolizing his role as a shepherd</note>,
Eastward or westward, hast thou ever seen,
Another chase, in all thy days,
Greater than that of Finn and of the Feine <note>The Fianna, the band of warriors led by Finn</note>?

<margin>² This composition the Irish call the battle of “Ventry Harbour,” a place in the county of Kerry in Ireland. There are, however, Fintrays in Scotland, which are apparently the same name, and Mr. Skene has called the attention of the writer to the fact, that there is a Sgùir Dhaire dhuinn, “the hill of Daire the Brown,” in Duror in Argyleshire. Irish Antiquaries say the battle was fought in the third century between the <term>Féine</term> <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill</note> of Ireland and Daire the Brown, King of the world. It will be seen in this copy that Daire is introduced as King of <term>Lochlin</term> <note>The Gaelic name for Scandinavia</note> or Scandinavia, although afterwards called King of the world. It is very probable that both to the Scotch and Irish Celt, Scandinavia was at this period synonymous with the world. This poem is here attributed to Ossian, although it is very doubtful whether it is so in the manuscript. The writing is so indistinct, that it is impossible to read the title correctly. It is hardly necessary to remind the reader that the poem is in the form of a dialogue between the bard, whoever he may be, and St. Patrick. The name of the saint seems to have been a favourite one with the bards, and was used no doubt to give consequence and currency to their compositions.</margin>

->To tell in human speech<-
->The glory of the Feine <note>The Feine, or Fenians, were a legendary band of warriors in Gaelic mythology led by Finn Mac Cumhaill.</note> of Finn.<-

Since without guile thou art,
And now that they are dead, dost live,
Watch thou for ever on,
And tell the deeds done by the Feine.

Should I be spared for fifty years,
Hearing thy music in thy cell
Till my death’s day, I could not tell
The noble deeds of the Feine of Finn.
The kingdoms of the earth in all its breadth
Belonged to us on every side.
Tribute we raised from all of them for Finn,¹
Else filled them with the shout of war.
In this wide earth there was not one
That dared refuse us,
Not ev’n in Alve² of the spotted spears,
With all its power and its untold renown.

<note>¹ This is a curious piece of Bardic exaggeration. But there may be some history hidden within its folds. There is sufficient evidence to prove that the Féine, whoever they were, were not confined to Ireland. We have numerous ancient Celtic compositions in which reference is made to the Féine of Scotland, and the Féine of Britain, meaning either Wales, or England and Wales together. The truth with regard to Finn and his Féine seems even yet to be a long way off.</note>

<note>² This is said to be “Almhuin,” or Allen, the residence of Finn, according to Irish accounts, in the county of Kildare. The word has a strong resemblance to “Alba,” the Gaelic name for Scotland; and in reading ancient Gaelic manuscripts, care must be taken to distinguish the two. We give the name in the Dean’s orthography, whatever the place may be.</note>

<note>ence. <note>This note appears to be a continuation from a previous page regarding sacred relics.</note> Two are well known in Scotland. One of them is the Quigrich, or crozier <note>A staff carried by high-ranking clergy, shaped like a shepherd's crook.</note> of St. Fillan, now in possession of a family of the name of Dewar in Canada, to whose progenitor it was intrusted by Robert Bruce at the battle of Bannockburn. An interesting notice of this relic has recently been published by Dr. D. Wilson of Toronto. Another is the crozier of St. Munn, now in the Museum of the Society of Antiquaries in Scotland, and long in possession of a family called the Barons of Bachul (a *crozier*, from the Latin *Baculus* <note>original: "Baculus", meaning a staff or stick.</note>), in the island of Lismore. A notice of this relic will be found in the transactions of the Scottish Antiquaries, from the able pen of Mr. Cosmo Innes. The word “breac,” or *spotted*, applied to the crozier, must refer to its ornamentation. The term will be found elsewhere applied to spears or swords.</note>

<summary>This page features a poem praising the legendary Fenian warriors and their leader Finn, accompanied by scholarly notes on Scottish relics and the geographic origins of the Feine. It highlights the difficulty in distinguishing between Irish and Scottish locations in ancient Gaelic orthography.</summary>

<keywords>Feine, Finn Mac Cumhaill, St. Fillan, crozier, Robert Bruce, Lismore, Almhuin, Gaelic poetry, Scottish antiquities</keywords>

Which was the stoutest arm
Among the men that followed Finn <note>Finn MacCumhail, the legendary leader of the Fianna</note>.

O Priest, thou pupil of the heavenly king,
I could not till the judgment day,
Tell of the Feine <note>The Fianna, a band of semi-independent warrior-hunters in early Ireland and Scotland</note>, the men and deeds.

Yet since it so fell out that thou outliv'st them
Ossian of sweet and pleasing songs,
Which would'st thou chuse of all the Feinn,
To stand in battle by thy shield?

Beyond all men who tread the earth,
Wearily dragging stones along

I have a tale which I would tell

Listen to Finn's prediction.
Shortly before you came, O Priest,
The hero was to build a fort,
On Cuailgne's<margin>1</margin> bare and rounded hill.
He commanded the Feine of Fail<margin>2</margin> <note>The Fenians of Ireland</note>

Two-thirds of all his famous fort

I answered, but not rightly,

The hero hard to vanquish,
He who knew no guile nor fear,
When my answer he had heard,
His words to me were these,
The words of Finn, prince of the Feine:

"You shall be dragging stones awhile
Before to your mournful home you go."

<margin>1 A hill said to be in the county of Armagh, celebrated in the ancient poem of "The Cattle Raid of Cooley" <note>original: "Tain bo Chuailgne"</note>. It is said to have been here that Cuchulain resided, from which likely comes his name, Cu Chuailgne, or Cuchulain, the Hound of Cooley. In Irish History Cuchulain is said to have lived a couple of centuries before the era of Fingal. The reader may be reminded that there is a mountain in Skye called Cuilinn, one of the grandest of the Scottish mountains. The person for whom both this and the Irish hills of the name were named is said to have been Cuilionn, a Druidical priest of great fame. The word means also the Holly tree, from which the derivation is sufficiently probable.</margin>
<margin>2 "Fail" is an ancient name for Ireland. The word signifies *fate*; hence "Innisfail," or the "Island of Fate," or, more probably, "the sacred island."</margin>

<summary>Oisín recounts a moment of rebellion against his father, Finn Mac Cumhaill, during the construction of a fort, leading to a prophecy that Oisín would one day be reduced to manual labor. This serves as a legendary explanation for Oisín's current state of servitude under Saint Patrick.</summary>

Hugh Garry’s son, who never quailed,

Whose liberal hand did music buy,⁵

<margin>¹ Conan is usually called “Conan the bald” <note>original: "Conan maol"</note>, and was known among the Feine for his thoughtless impetuosity. He was, in consequence of this peculiar temperament, ever getting into difficulties, and exposing himself to the ridicule and reprehension of his companions. There are both a river and valley in Scotland called after him, the River Conan and Strathconan in Ross-shire.</margin>

<margin>² “Bran,” the name of Finn’s celebrated hound, is here obviously the name of a man. There is a “strath” <note>A broad mountain valley</note> or valley in Ross-shire which still retains his name, Strathbran. The extent to which Fenian names are found in Scottish topography, goes far to show the close connection of the Feine themselves with that country.</margin>

<margin>³ Caoilte was one of the most famous of the Feine. He was distinguished for his swiftness, and not without reason, as is shown in his chase of Loinn son of Liobhaidh <note>original: "Loinn mac Liobhaidh"</note>, the Fenian blacksmith, whom he overtook, although he could cross a valley at a stride.</margin>

<margin>⁴ This Oscar must be distinguished from the famous Oscar, the Poet’s son. Of the latter he never speaks without applying to him some term of endearment, as “my son,” “my own son,” etc.</margin>

<margin>⁵ This line refers to the liberal rewards <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to the patronage of bards and musicians by the warrior class.</note></margin>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Ranged around Ossian and Finn,
Traversing valleys to dispense their gold.
Fearton and brave Carroll were there,
Who never fought but where they won.
I sing them, and generous Felan,
All of whom here have I seen.
->Here have I seen.<-

The author of this is Ossian, the son of Finn.¹

Tell us, O Patrick, what honour is ours,
Do the Feine <note>The Fenians or Fianna; the legendary band of warriors led by Finn MacCumhail</note> of Ireland in heaven now dwell?

In truth I can tell thee, thou Ossian of fame,
That no heaven has thy father, Oscar, or Gaul.

Sad is the tale thou tellest me, Priest,
I worshipping God while the Feine have no heaven.

Shalt thou not fare well thyself in that city,
Though ne'er should thy father, Caoilte, and Oscar be there?

Little joy would it bring to me to sit in that city,
Without Caoilte, and Oscar, as well as my father.

Better see the face of heaven's son each day,
Than all the gold on earth, were it thine to possess.

--- Page 134 ---
<meta>This page continues the poetic dialogue between the aging warrior Ossian and Saint Patrick, representing the clash between the heroic pagan past and the Christian present.</meta>

<page-num>18</page-num>
->THE BOOK OF<-

Tell us, thou Priest of the Holy city <note>Saint Patrick</note>, the tale;
In return I'll recount thee the battle of Gaura <note>The catastrophic final battle of the Fenian cycle where the warrior band was destroyed</note>.

If the tale of that city <note>Heaven</note> thou desir'st, old man,
No thirst, no hunger, want, reproach are there.

Who are heaven's sons? more noble are the Feinn <note>The Fianna, the legendary band of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhail</note>:
Are they hard of heart? have thou mercy, Cleric;

Unlike them are the Feine <note>The Fenian warriors</note>, unlike them altogether,
Never on the green plain did they seek the chase.

For thy love's sake, Patrick, forsake not the heroes,
Unknown to heaven's King, bring thou in the Feinn.

Though little room you'd take, not one of your race,
Unknown to heaven's King, shall get beneath his roof.

How different Mac Cumhail <note>Finn MacCool</note>, the Feinn's noble king,
All men, uninvited, might enter his great house.

Sad is that, old man, and thy life's close so near,
That thou should'st so unjustly judge of my great king.

Better the fierce conflict of Finn and his Feinn,
Than thy holy master, and thyself together.

Mournful, poor old man, that thou should'st folly speak,
Better God for a day than all of Erin's <note>Ireland's</note> Feinn.

Though few be my days, and my life's close near,
Patrick defame not the nobles of clan Boisgne <note>The ancestral clan of Finn Mac Cumhail</note>.

Thou can'st never tell, Ossian, son to the Queen,
How different your nobles from those of my Lord.

Were even Conan <note>Conan Maol, a famously foul-mouthed and irritable member of the Fianna</note> living, the least of the Feinn,
He would not suffer thy insolence, Cleric.

Speak not thus, Ossian, savage are thy words,
Take thee now thy rest, and guide thee by my rule.

<summary>A poetic debate between the aged warrior Ossian and Saint Patrick, contrasting the generous, earthly nobility of the Fenian heroes with the exclusive nature of the Christian Heaven. Ossian defends his fallen kinsmen against Patrick's insistence that only those known to God may enter the afterlife.</summary>

<keywords>Ossian, Saint Patrick, Finn Mac Cumhail, Fianna, Battle of Gaura, Irish Mythology, Paganism vs Christianity, Clan Boisgne, Conan Maol</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic dialogue between the aging warrior-bard Ossian and Saint Patrick, a central theme in the Dean of Lismore’s Book representing the clash between the heroic Gaelic past and the Christian era.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Did'st thou see the fight, and the noble banners,
Never would'st thou think but of the glory of the <term>Feinn</term> <note>The Fenians or Fianna; a legendary band of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill.</note>.

Ossian, Prince's son, 'twill be thy soul's great loss
That thou now think'st only of the battles of the Feinn.

Did'st thou hear the hounds, and the sounds of the hunt,
Thou would'st rather be there than in the holy city.

That is sad, old man, if the glory of the chase
Be greater than all which Heaven above can yield.

Say not so, Patrick, empty are thy words,
Indeed and in truth, better Finn and the Feinn.

By thy hand, <term>Boisgne's son</term> <note>A reference to Ossian’s lineage; Boisgne was the ancestor of the clan to which Finn belonged.</note>, not empty are my words,
Better is one angel than Finn and the Feinn.

Were I only now as I was at <term>Gaura's fight</term> <note>The Battle of Gabhra, the catastrophic final conflict where the Fianna were destroyed.</note>,
I would punish thy reproach of <term>Erin's</term> <note>Ireland's</note> noble Feinn.

Thy pride is all gone, for all thy future days,
None are now left of thy band but thyself.

Were my men in life I'd not hear thy howling,
And I'd make thee to suffer in return for thy talk.

Though all of these yet lived, and were now joined together,
I'd still not speak only of the Feinn's seven bands.

Seven times the number that thou hast of priests,
Fell all in battle by <term>Oscar</term> <note>Ossian’s son and the most formidable warrior of the later Fianna.</note> alone.

Thou'rt now in thy last days, old and senseless man,
Cease now thy speaking, and come away with me;
Did'st thou see the men of cowls <note>Monks or clerics.</note>, Finn's son, in <term>Alve</term> <note>Likely referring to Magh nAilbe, a site associated with early Christian settlements.</note>,
Thou would'st not as thou dost reproach the men of heaven.

No less was our great band, when we were met in <term>Taura</term> <note>Tara, the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland.</note>,
Reproachful are the words thou speak'st of the great king,
I will forgive thee, Cleric, although thou dost not tell
Tell.

### The author of this is Ossian.¹ <margin>¹ This composition is known usually by the name of “Fainesoluis,” or the sunbeam, derived manifestly from the comparison of the sunbeam in the twenty-first line. In Ireland it is called “The song of Mayre Borb” <note>original: "Laoidh an Mhoighre Bhoirb"</note>. The “Mayre” of Ireland will be found to be “Daire” in Scotland.</margin>

I know a little tale of Finn,
A tale that we should not despise,
Of Cumhal’s son, the valorous,
Which our memory still preserves.
Once we were a little band,
At Essaroy,² of gentle streams, <margin>² The topography of these poems is a subject of very deep interest to the student of our national antiquities. In the valley of the Roy in Lochaber, so famous for its parallel roads, is a waterfall called “The fall of Roy” <note>original: "Eas ruaidh"</note> or “Essaroy.” Was this the scene of the story of Fainesoluis? It may be objected that from there the sea lies at a distance of fifteen miles. On the Earn, near Ballyshannon, in the county of Donegal, is “Assaroe,” or “The waterfall of Hugh the red” <note>original: "Eas Aoidh Ruaidh"</note>, who was drowned there. This is nearer the sea; but at the period ascribed to Ossian, the name could not have been given to the spot, as the death of Hugh the Red, son of Badharn <note>original: "Aedh ruadh mac Badhairn"</note>, from whom the cascade was named, occurred, according to the Annals of the Four Masters, in the year of the world 4518.</margin>
Near the coast was under sail,
A <term>currach</term> <note>A traditional Irish boat made of a wooden frame covered in hide or canvas.</note>, in which sat a maid;
Fifty men stood by the King,
Brave in any fight or field,
Sad for them who faced their right arm,
For we ruled in every land.
All of us rose up in haste,
Save Finn of the <term>Feine</term> <note>Also known as the Fianna; a small, semi-independent warrior band in Irish and Scottish mythology.</note> and Gaul,
To welcome the boat as it sped,
Cleaving the waves in its course.
It never ceased its onward way
Until it reached the wonted port.
Then when it had touched the land,
The maid did from her seat arise,
Fairer than a sunbeam’s sheen,
Of finest mould and gentlest mien.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the poem "The Maid of the Land Under the Waves" from the Book of the Dean of Lismore, a 16th-century Scottish Gaelic manuscript. The previous page likely introduced the arrival of the mysterious woman.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Then before this stranger maid,
We stood and showed courtesy;
“Come to the tent of Finn with us.”
With grace she all of us salutes;
’Twas Cumhal’s son <note>Finn MacCumhal, the legendary leader of the Fianna</note> himself replied,
And salutes her in return.
Then did the King of noblest mien
Ask of the maid of fairest face,
“Whence is it thou hast come, fair maid?
Give us now in brief thy tale.”
“The King of the land beneath the waves,¹
My father is, such is my fate,
Through all lands where the sun revolves,
Thee and thy men I long have sought.”
“Princess, who hast searched each land,
Youthful maid of beauteous form,
The reason why thou cam’st so far,
Tell us now, and tell us all.”
“If thou be Finn, I ask defence,”²
So now did speak the youthful maid,

<margin>
¹ Kings among the Celts were, at an early period, persons of much less consequence than the name would now seem to imply. In Ireland, there were four provincial sovereignties besides the national one. But even this does not give an accurate representation of the Celtic polity. The fact is, every ruler of a district and leader of an army was called a King. Of this we have ample evidence in Caesar’s account of the state of Britain at the period of the Roman invasion <note>original: "Cæsar’s account"</note>. In Scotland, tradition points to several kingdoms. In the Western Highlands the Island of Mull is said to have formed a kingdom by itself, called “Kingdom of the Bar” <note>original: "Rioghachd na Drealluin"</note>, from “bar” or “latch” <note>original: "dreall"</note>, applied to the Sound of Mull, which shuts out all strangers from the Island. Islay was called the “South-west Kingdom” <note>original: "Rioghachd Modheadh"</note>, from its position. Morvern was called “Iorruaidh,” to which frequent reference is made both in Scottish and Irish Celtic tradition. Ardnamurchan was called “Sorcha,” the kingdom of Daire Borb’s father, from its mountainous character, “Sorcha” meaning “high,” whence “tripod” <note>original: "sorchan"</note>. “The land beneath the waves” <note>original: "Tir bàr fo thuinn"</note> was Tyree, from the lowness of the land appearing from a distance as if its surface were on a level with the sea. If there be any historical accuracy in these traditions, which have been gathered up from an intelligent old Highlander, skilled in the lore of his country, this story of Fainesoluis is one in which a daughter of the ruler of the Island of Tyree is pursued by a son of the ruler of Ardnamurchan. Poetry gives a consequence and magnitude to the event, which in all probability the naked reality did not possess.
</margin>

<margin>
² “My protection on thee” <note>original: "Mo chomraich ort"</note>—
</margin>

"Thou of soft speech, and purest race,
Grant me protection, grant it now."
Then spoke the wise and knowing King,
"Tell us now from whom thou flee'st;
Protection I thee grant, fair maid,
'Gainst every man that dares thee hurt."
"There comes in wrath across the sea,
Swift in pursuit, a warrior brave,
The well-armed son of Sorcha's King,
He whose name is Daire the fierce.¹
I laid me under heavy bonds²
That Finn should from the sea me have,
But that his wife I ne'er should be,
Though famed his beauty and his deeds."
Then Oscar spoke, of hasty speech,
The warlike conqueror of Kings,
"Though Finn should not thy pledge sustain,
Never shalt thou with him wed."
Then do we see borne by his steed³
A hero of unequalled size,
Travelling with speed across the sea,
Following the maiden in her course;
His helmet close about the head
Of this brave and dauntless man;
His right arm bore a round black shield,

<margin>Protection be on thee," was an appeal which the Celtic warrior could never reject when made by the weak and helpless. In Christian times, the word <term>Comraich</term> <note>Gaelic: protection or sanctuary</note> came to be applied to the "girths" or "sanctuaries" around places of worship and other sacred spots, where accused persons might flee for security. Hence the Gaelic name of the parish of Applecross in Ross-shire, <term>A Chomraich</term>, or "the Sanctuary," formed round the church dedicated to St. Malrube, an early Christian missionary.</margin>
<margin>¹ As already observed, this name is in Irish editions of the poem "Mayre." See Miss Brooks' *Reliques of Irish Poetry.*</margin>
<margin>² <term>Geasan</term>, "bonds." The word here appears to mean a simple pledge, although in most cases it implies the exercise of some magical power. In those cases of metamorphosis so common in Celtic tales, the persons who have been made to undergo the change are said to be <term>fo gheasaibh</term> <note>Gaelic: under spells</note>, "under spells." The mythology of the Celts has much in common with that of Eastern nations.</margin>
<margin>³ The magical steed of Daire the Fierce <note>original: "Daire borb"</note> was capable of carrying its master over sea and land. In many ancient Celtic Tales we read of ships which could also traverse sea and land.</margin>

<summary>A mysterious maiden seeks protection from Finn mac Cumhaill and his son Oscar to escape the pursuing warrior Daire, son of the King of Sorcha. The text explores the Celtic concepts of sanctuary and magical oaths or spells.</summary>

No gentle cast was that, in truth,
The hero's shield was split in twain.

The red-dyed belt from his left arm, <note>original: "criss zerk." This likely refers to a javelin (craoiseach) rather than a belt, suggesting Oscar may have been left-handed.</note>

The struggle and the fight were fierce.
Then did Mac Morne slay with his arm
The King of Sorcha's son, most strange!
Sad was the coming of the maid,
Now that the brave in fight had fallen.

And now that he had fallen thus,
Beside the sea, a sad event,

As our fifty warriors brave <insert>These lines are considered variations or additions to the main poem</insert>
Were now subject to his arms,
Helpless were we in his hands,
Our precious rights were all now lost.
His sword without a single check,

For ten long years his conquering arms,
To the victor did the King forbid;
For all that time the son of Morne
Was healing with Finn of the Feine.

<note>
These supplementary lines appear to be either additional verses or alternative readings. There are two provided in the original Gaelic <note>original: "Gàidhlig"</note> which are not translated here as they are of little consequence, and seem intended to be inserted between the ninety-eighth and ninety-ninth lines. The first eight lines of this additional fragment are likely intended to fall between the hundred and twenty-eighth and the hundred and twenty-ninth lines, with the last eight closing the poem. Dr. Smith, in the Report on Ossian’s Poems submitted to the Highland Society, states that the version given in this supplement is inconsistent with the main body of the poem and should be regarded as containing various readings.
</note>

### The author of this is Ossian, the son of Finn.¹

        'Twas yesterday week
        I last saw Finn;
        Ne'er did I see
        A braver man;
        Teige's² daughter's son,
        A powerful king;
        My fortune, my light,
        My mind's whole might,
        Both poet and chief.
        Braver than kings,
        Firm chief of the <term>Feinn</term> <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill, often referred to as the Fenians.</note>.
        Lord of all lands,
        Leviathan at sea,
        As great on land,
        Hawk of the air,
        Foremost always.
        Generous, just,
        Despised a lie.
        Of vigorous deeds,
        First in song.
        A righteous judge,
        Firm his rule.
        Polished his mien,
        Who knew but victory.

<margin>¹</margin> The following seems to have been Ossian's <term>eulogy</term> <note>A speech or piece of writing that highly praises someone, typically someone who has just died.</note> on his father Finn. The editor has not met with any similar composition either among Scottish or Irish collections, except a few lines extracted by Miss Brooks from a composition which she calls “Ossian's Blow” <note>original: "Buille Oisein"</note>, and it is therefore rescued from oblivion by having been seized by the Dean while floating on the stream of oral tradition, and treasured in his miscellany. In the original, the poetry is worthy of the name of Ossian, more so, indeed, than any of the pieces in this collection. It is quite impossible to produce in English the effect of the rhythm and <term>alliteration</term> <note>The repetition of the same letter or sound at the beginning of adjacent or closely connected words.</note> of the <term>Gaelic</term> <note>The Celtic language of Scotland and Ireland.</note>; but the editor has endeavoured, while giving an exact rendering, to retain, in as far as possible, the peculiar measure of the original. The piece is a fine tribute of filial love and admiration, nor is there much room to doubt its genuineness.

<margin>²</margin> Muirne, Finn's mother, is said to have been a daughter of Teige, a famous <term>Druid</term> <note>A high-ranking professional class in ancient Celtic cultures, often serving as priests, teachers, and judges.</note>, or, as others say, of a princely family of Bregia, in Meath <note>A historical region and county in Ireland.</note>.

<summary>This page presents a poetic eulogy for the legendary hero Finn Mac Cumhaill, attributed to his son Ossian, praising his character as a warrior, poet, and just ruler. The accompanying notes discuss the rarity of this specific poem in the oral tradition and provide genealogical context for Finn's family.</summary>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Who is like him
In fight or song?
Resists the foe,
In house or field.
Marble his skin,
The rose his cheek,
Blue was his eye,
His hair like gold.
All men's trust,
Of noble mind.
Of ready deeds,
To women mild,
A giant he,
The field's delight.
Best polished spears,
No wood like their shafts.
Rich was the King.
His great green bottle,
Full of sharp wine,
Of substance rich.
Excellent he.¹
Of noble form,
His people's head,
His step so firm,
Who often warred.
In beauteous Banva, <note>Banva (Banba) is an ancient poetic name for Ireland, named after a legendary queen of the Tuatha Dé Danann.</note>
Three hundred battles
He bravely fought.
With miser's mind
From none withheld.
Anything false
His lips ne'er spoke.
He never grudged,

¹ Some of the lines in this part of the original manuscript <note>original: "MS."</note> are very much defaced from age and bad usage. The line is the forty-fifth, where he has introduced a phrase sufficiently general to prevent the charge of in any measure tampering with his author. The other words will be seen by referring to the Gaelic.

<meta>This page continues the poetic lament of Ossian, the legendary Gaelic bard, following the death of his father, Finn Mac Cumhaill. It maintains the elegiac tone of the previous sections.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
->THE BOOK OF<-

No, never was there a Finn;
The sun never saw a King
Who excelled him.
The monsters in the lakes,
The serpent on the land,
In Ireland of the saints,<margin>1</margin>
The hero slew them all.
Never could I tell,
Though I lived forever,
Never could I tell
Even a third of his praise.
But sad am I now,
After Finn of the <term>Feinn</term><note>The Fianna; a legendary band of semi-independent warrior-hunters in early Ireland and Scotland.</note>!
With the chief gone,
My joy has all fled.
No friends among the great,
No courtesy remains.
No gold, no queen,
No princes and no chiefs.
Sad am I now,
Our head taken away!
I am like a shaking tree,
With my leaves all gone.
An empty nut,
A horse without a rein,
Sad, sad am I,
A feeble <term>kern</term><note>From the Gaelic 'ceatharnach,' meaning a light-armed foot soldier or peasant warrior.</note>.
I am Ossian, the son of Finn,
Now strengthless in my deeds.

<margin>1</margin> The word “naoimh,” here translated “saints,” is not necessarily associated with Christianity. The word “naomh,” *holy*, is a word belonging originally to the Gaelic language, and not introduced, like many church-related terms, from the Latin and Greek. The phrase may be rendered “sacred Ireland,” a character belonging to the island, in popular belief, even before the Christian period. It is a curious fact, that the feat of destroying all the vermin in Ireland was, in a later age, attributed to Saint Patrick. It would appear that this was but a transfer of a portion of the glory of Finn to the Christian Saint—a remarkable instance among many of early Christianity borrowing, not very wisely, the honors of paganism.

<summary>Ossian laments the death of his father, Finn Mac Cumhaill, describing his own state of decay and the loss of the heroic age. The marginalia notes how early Christian legends, such as St. Patrick banishing snakes, likely originated from older pagan myths about Finn.</summary>

<keywords>Ossian, Finn Mac Cumhaill, Fianna, Ireland, St. Patrick, Gaelic mythology, paganism vs Christianity, elegiac poetry</keywords>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

When Finn did live
All things were mine.
Seven sides had the house
Of Cumhal's son. <note>Finn Mac Cumhal, the legendary leader of the Fenian warriors.</note>
Seven score shields
On every side.
Fifty robes of wool
Around the King.
Fifty warriors
Filled the robes.
Ten bright cups
For drink in his hall.
Ten blue flagons,
Ten horns of gold.
A noble house
Was that of Finn.
No grudge nor lust,
Babbling nor sham;
No man despised
Among the Feinn. <note>The Feinn, or Fenians, were a small, semi-independent band of warriors in Irish and Scottish mythology.</note>
The first himself,
All else like him.
Finn was our chief,
Easy his praise,
Noblest of Kings.
Finn ne'er refused
To any man,
Howe'er unknown;
Ne'er from his house
Sent those who came.<margin>¹</margin>

<margin>¹ Hospitality was one of the highest qualifications of a Celtic chief. Ossian <note>The legendary poet and son of Finn, to whom these verses are traditionally attributed.</note> never fails to sing the generous, open-handed hospitality of his father Finn. Till a late period the same feature of character distinguished the Scottish Highlander, although modern civilization is fast uprooting it, and overlaying the character of the simple Highlander with the selfishness peculiar to itself. Even now, in most part of the Highlands, the door of a hut is never closed by day—a practice said to originate in the universal sense of the ready reception due to the wayfarer and the stranger. Is the *seven sides of Fingal's house* an orientalism? <note>A term used by 19th-century scholars to suggest a cultural connection to the Middle East or "the Orient."</note></margin>

<summary>This page features a poem from the Dean of Lismore's collection describing the magnificent hall of Finn Mac Cumhal and his legendary generosity. The accompanying marginal note reflects on the historical importance of hospitality in Gaelic culture compared to the "selfishness" of modern civilization.</summary>

The author of this is Allan MacRorie.¹

Glenshee,² the valley that lies close beside me,

Below the grassy height of great Ben Gulbin³

<margin>¹ This Poet is obviously a Scotsman; but judging from another of his compositions in this volume, he was, like the Scottish bards of his time, well acquainted with the bardic literature of Ireland. This arose from the frequent intercourse between the two countries during their early history, and the number of Scotsmen educated both in the Medical and Bardic Schools of Ireland. This is one of the circumstances which renders it so difficult now, in the absence of authentic historical documents, to extricate much of the social history of the two countries. Hence the rival Scottish and Irish claims to many of our Celtic literary remains. There is no doubt that the poets of both countries interwove with their compositions the traditions of the race, without much regard to whether these were Scottish or Irish. The Irish trace this common literature to the fact of the Irish colonization of Scotland, which they maintain was the origin of the Celtic population of the latter country; but it is by no means necessary to go so far back in order to find sufficient cause for the fact. Christianity seems to have formed the first solid basis of union between the two countries, and a common Christianity was without doubt the means of long maintaining it. This composition is usually called “*The Death of Diarmaid*” <note>original: "Bàs Dhiarmaid"</note>.</margin>

<margin>² A valley in the eastern part of Perthshire, where the grave of Diarmaid is pointed out to this day. MacRorie appears to have been an inhabitant of the neighborhood, for he speaks of Glenshee as being close beside him. The name of Glenshee is derived from the word “*Valley*” <note>original: "Gleann"</note>, and “*a hill of a peculiar form*” <note>original: "sìth"</note>. This word is found in the names of several Scottish hills, as “*The hill of the storm*” <note>original: "Sìth challain," Schihallion</note>; “*The fairy hill*” <note>original: "Beinn shìth," Ben Hi</note>; “*The great pointed hill*” <note>original: "An t-Sìth mhòr"</note>. “*A hillock*” <note>original: "Sìthan"</note>. The word has been often mistaken for “*Peace*” <note>original: "Sìth"</note>, whence the name “*Fairy, fairies*” <note>original: "Sìtheach, sìthichean"</note> has been absurdly rendered the *peace folk*, instead of the *folk of the hills*, referring to their reputed residence in earthen mounds.</margin>

<margin>³ “*Ben Gulbin*” <note>The mountain of the beak</note> lies at the head of Glenshee. There is a hill with a similar name in Ireland, whence Conall Gulbain, one of the kings of Ulster, took his designation. There is a “water of Gulbin,” and a “Torgul-</margin>

There is, as is common in Highland topography, a stream called Gulbin, whence a valley of the same name, and then a mountain. The names in similar cases seem to have been primarily attached to the streams, whence they ascended through the valleys to the hills. We have an instance of this in the River Nevis, Glen Nevis, Ben Nevis, the “neamhais,” <note>original: "neamhais" — likely referring to "heavenly" or "impetuous" qualities</note> referring to its impetuosity, having been in all likelihood originally applied to the stream.

<margin>
whose worship of it might have been one reason why it was pronounced unclear. Whether the Celts worshipped it or not, it is manifest that it was held in high esteem, for its figure is engraved on most of the ancient sculptured stones of Scotland. Among the Welsh it is a national emblem, and hence one argument for the ancient Picts being British, as these stones are found confined to the ancient Pictish territory. The word “muc,” *a sow*, enters largely into Scottish topography. We have “Eilean nam muc,” *the Isle of muck*, or *Sow island*. The ancient name of St. Andrews was “Muc ros,” *the Sow’s headland*, and we know that the sow is associated with the memory of St. Regulus. There is a “Bridge of Turk,” or the *Boar’s bridge*, near the Trosachs. There is a “Slochd muice,” or the *Sow’s hollow*, near Inverness, on the Highland road, a name derived from a hillock shaped like a sow’s back, in the bottom of the chasm a little to the west of where the coach road crosses; and there is “Sròn muice,” *The Sow’s snout*, on the north side of Loch Ness, derived from the resemblance of a hill-face to that part of the animal. These two latter names are manifestly derived from natural resemblances, and have nothing to do with mythology.

Alas! that he should never see his home.
Along the back he measures now the boar,
Light-footed Mac O'Duine of active step.

And measure, Diarmad, carefully the boar.”
It was indeed for thee a mournful deed,
Youth of the sharply-pointed piercing arms.
He went, the errand grievous was and sad,
And measured for them once again the boar.
Th' envenomed pointed bristle sharply pierced
The sole of him,¹ the bravest in the field.
Then fell and lay upon the grassy plain
The noble Mac O'Duine, whose look spoke truth;
He fell and lay along beside the boar,
And there you have my mournful, saddening tale.
There does he lie now wounded to the death,
Brave Mac O'Duine, so skilful in the fight;
The most enduring ev'n among the Feinn,
He lies upon the knoll I see on high,
The blue-eyed hawk that dwelt at Essaroy,²
The conqueror in every sore-fought field,

Slain by the poisoned bristle of the boar.

Brave, noble Diarmad Mac O'Duine! <note>A prominent hero of the Fenian Cycle, known for his beauty and tragic fate.</note>

Whiter his body than the sun's bright light,
Redder his lips than blossoms tinged with red;
Long yellow locks did rest upon his head,
His eye was clear beneath the covering brow,
Its colour mingled was of blue and grey;
Waving and graceful were his locks behind,²
His speech was elegant and sweetly soft;
His hands the whitest, fingers tipped with red;
Elegance and power were in his form,
His fair soft skin covering a faultless shape,
No woman saw him but he won her love.
Mac O'Duine crowned with his countless victories,
Ne'er shall he raise his eye in courtship more,
Or warriors' wrath give colour to his cheek;
The following of the chase, the prancing steed,
Will never move him, nor the search for spoil.
He who could bear him well in every fight,
Has now us sadly left in that wild vale.

The latter had what is called a <term>“Ball seirce”</term> <note>original: "love spot"</note>, or beauty spot, which no woman could resist. Hence Finn's jealousy and desire to destroy Diarmad. The word rendered here <term>“naire”</term> <note>original: "shame"</note>, shame, is in the MS. <term>“noor”</term>, *gold*. If this be the accurate reading, Grainne's dowery must have formed an element in the conflict.</margin>

<margin>² Yellow was the favourite colour of the ancient Celt. <term>“Falt buidh”</term> <note>original: "yellow hair"</note>, *yellow hair*, is an object of the highest admiration, and the longer and more waving the locks, the greater the admiration. The account the Celt gives of himself is somewhat different from that given by his neighbours, who would paint him a wiry, thin, black-haired, black-eyed man. Tacitus <note>A Roman historian who described the Caledonians as having red hair and large limbs.</note> gave a different description; and any man who travels the Highlands of Perthshire, where, perhaps, we have the purest Celtic blood in Scotland, will have ample evidence of the accuracy of the Roman historian. With reference to the admiration of the yellow colour among the Celts, it is interesting to trace how it intermingles itself with the vocabulary of the language; thus, a *fine* day is a *yellow* day. The name given to Beltin day <note>The Gaelic May Day festival marking the beginning of summer.</note>, the opening of summer, is, <term>“la buidhe Beallteine”</term>, *yellow Beltin day*; and anything propitious is called</margin>

### The author of this is Allan M'Rory.¹

To-night my mourning is great,
Thou tonsured priest <note>A common address to a Christian cleric by a secular poet</note> whom I love,
While I reflect on the fight,
With red-tree² Cairbar we fought,
Son to great Cormac, son of Conn <note>original: "Cormaig O'Cuinn"</note>,
Woe to the Feinn <note>The legendary band of warriors led by Finn MacCool</note> whom he seized;
A king who ne'er shunned the fight,
And feared not the face of man,
The Feinn to a man did serve,
Finn and the good race of Conn,
Till the day of Cairbar Roy;
Nor evil nor weakness fear'd.
Brave Cairbar his people addressed,
Deceitful indeed was the speech.

<margin>¹</margin> <note>The translator notes a linguistic connection between the word for "yellow" and "satisfaction."</note> yellow, as, “it is yellow or propitious for you” <note>original: "is buidhe dhuit e"</note>; and a man satisfied after a meal is called “yellow or satisfied” <note>original: "buidh-each"</note>.
This is the second composition of M'Rory's given in the Dean's manuscript. The present is as purely Irish in its incidents as the former was Scotch, the author having been in all likelihood, as already shown, a Scotchman, but perfectly familiar with the events of Irish history, and equally so with what is called the Irish dialect, although in the day of the Dean it was common to the literature of both countries, with a few variations. It will be seen, for instance, that in several of these pieces the Irish negative *not* <note>original: "ni"</note> and the Scottish *not* <note>original: "cha"</note>, are used indifferently. This composition has been published at much greater length than here, under the name of Ossian, but from manuscripts apparently of no antiquity. This poem is usually called “The Battle of Gaura” <note>original: "Cath Ghabhra"</note>, or “The Death of Oscar” <note>original: "Bàs Osgaír"</note>.
<margin>²</margin> The “red-tree” knights were the knights of Emania, or Ulster. Caesar mentions the order of *knights* <note>original: "equites"</note>, as one of the three great leading classes into which the Gauls were divided; so that the existence of such an order in Ireland, at an early period, is in no way inconsistent with what history relates of ancient Celtic policy. Cairbar was the son of Cormac, son of Art, son of Conn of the hundred battles, Irish kings of the Emanian race. Finn, according to O'Flaherty, was married to a daughter of Cormac, so that this battle with Cairbar was in reality with his brother-in-law. It seems to have originated in the Feinn, who are said to have been a species of militia, or rather a standing army in Ireland, becoming disposed to stretch their prerogative farther than was agreeable to the monarch, and that the object of Cairbar in this battle was to put them down. It is said that the Feinn were supported by the provincial king of Munster. This is Irish history, and it is remarkable to find these events sung by a Scottish Poet.

The kings did all own our sway,
Till the battle of Gaura <note>The Battle of Gabhra, the legendary final conflict of the Fenians (Fianna) where they were nearly destroyed by the High King of Ireland.</note> was fought.
But since that horrid slaughter,
No tribute nor tax we’ve raised.
Nor to us was tribute due,
Save by part of Erin’s <note>Ireland</note> soil.
Many were there on the earth
Of the folk who felt no grief.
To both sides how great the loss,
When we each other did destroy,
Should strangers fierce come over,
And seize on beauteous Erin.

Ossian, what would Finn have done
Were burdens laid on Erin?

By thy hand, most holy Priest,
There were none in all fair Banva,¹
Save a few aged heroes,
And some younger untried men;
What king might there plant his foot,
Could Fodla <note>Another poetic name for Ireland.</note> have for taking.
No fight, no conflict he’d need,
No stratagem nor struggle.

Eastward we sent ambassadors,
To Fatha of Con’s great son;

to perceive that the only true and satisfactory solution of the question regarding it, is that the name was applied to both countries, latterly under the distinctive appellation of Greater Scotland <note>original: "Scotia Major"</note> and Lesser Scotland <note>original: "Scotia Minor"</note>, as the countries of the Scots. Surely Scotland was as much a Scotland as Ireland, and Ireland as much as Scotland, in so far as they were both occupied by Scottish inhabitants. This identity of race, language, and at an early period religion, is not sufficiently allowed for in discussing questions involving the several claims of Scotland and Ireland to much of what was common to both. Scotland has suffered more than Ireland from the destruction of her early archives; but is not the life of Columba, so recently given to the world under the able editorship of Dr. Reeves, in reality a Scottish work?

¹ “ *Banva* ” is another name for Ireland. This is the Gaelic name for a *sucking-pig*, so that it also is probably mythological. It is in all likelihood the same name with our Scottish Banf.

There found I my own dear son
Laid, on his left arm resting,
His shattered shield beside him,
While his hand still grasped his sword;
His precious blood on every side,

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Flowed swiftly through his harness.
My spear I rested on the earth,
And o'er him stood as he lay;
Then thought I, O tonsured Priest, <note>A reference to Saint Patrick, to whom the narrator, Oisín, is telling the story.</note>
What, now lonely, I could do.
Oscar towards me now turns,
'Twas for me a grievous scene;
Forth to me he stretched his hand,
Wishing I should him approach.
Then my dear son's hand I seized,
And cried out with a bitter cry.
Forward from that time till now,
In this world I've useless been.
Thus to me my own son said,
As life was fast departing,

Thanks to the powers above,
That thou'st escaped, dear father.

Nothing do I tell but truth,
A word I could not answer.
Then approached the noble Caoilte, <note>Caoilte mac Rónáin, a famous warrior and swift runner of the Fianna.</note>
Who to visit Oscar came.
Gently did Mac Ronain¹ say,

How find'st thou thyself, dear friend?

Just as thou would'st have me be,
Going to a better world.

Cairbar Roy's spear had pierced, <note>Cairbre Lifechair, the High King of Ireland and antagonist of the Fianna.</note>
'Neath the navel, red-armed Oscar;
The arm of Caoilte up to its bend,
Followed in its course the spear.
Caoilte did deeply search the wound,
And well saw how all stood there.
The wound was through to the back,

Torn by the murderous spear.
Mac Ronain <note>The son of Ronan, referring to Caoilte</note> gave a loud shriek,
And, fainting, fell to the earth.
Then spake Caoilte, the warrior brave,
Recovering from his faint,

Dear Oscar, no more art thou ours;
Thou and the Feinn <note>The Fianna, the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill</note> must part,
So part must the Feinn with war,
Conn's race <note>The descendants of Conn of the Hundred Battles, the High Kings of Ireland</note> the tribute shall raise.

We had been thus a brief space,
Thou priest, the son of Alpin <note>Referring to Saint Patrick, to whom the narrator Oisín is telling the story</note>,
When leaving the slaughter we saw,
All of Fail's <note>Inisfail, an ancient poetic name for Ireland</note> Feinn now living,
There were but two thousand men,
The old and the young together,
And none unwounded returned,
Even of these hundred score.
Nine wounds them grievously pierced,
There were few of them with less.
Then raised we the noble Oscar,
Aloft on the shafts of our spears;
To a fair green knoll we bore him,
That we his dress might remove.
Of his body one hand's breadth
Was not whole, down from his hair,
Till you reached the sole of his foot,
Save his face, and that alone.
The entrails, the liver, the spleen,
Each draining the body till day.
The sons of the Feinn did then
To a fair knoll them betake;
His own son did no man mourn,
Nor did he mourn his brother:
As they saw how lay my son,
All, all did mourn for Oscar.

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Thus was it with us a while,
Watching the fair-skinned hero,
When we saw approach at noon
Finn Mac Cumhail, son of Trenmor <note>original: "mac Treinvor"; Trenmor was the great-grandfather of Oscar and a legendary leader of the Fianna</note>.
From the fierce slaughter escaped,
A third of the Fianna <note>original: "Feinn"; the legendary band of warriors led by Finn</note> still lived,
When they laid the sons of Boisgne <note>The clan or family line to which Finn and Oscar belonged</note>
Upon their biers, the fight being o'er.
With gashed limbs the men were halt,
The chiefs a dreadful sight.
We saw the standard of Finn
Raised on the shaft of a spear,
Which from the slaughter they bore;
Gladly to meet it we went.
All of us saluted Finn,
But no salute was returned,
As he climbed the warrior's hill,
Where deadly-armed Oscar lay.
When by Oscar Finn was seen,
As o'er him sadly he bent,
He turned to him his face,
His grandfather saluting.
Then did my Oscar thus speak
To him who was first of us all:

In death I have my desire,
Noble Finn of pointed arms.

Sad it is, my brave Oscar,
Thou good son of my own son;
After thee I'm but feeble,
And after Ireland's <note>original: "Erin's"</note> brave Fianna.
The heavy curse of Art the Solitary <note>original: "Art aenir"; Art mac Cuinn, a High King of Ireland whose lineage was often at odds with the Fianna</note>
Is on us to our great grief,
From the east it me pursued,
Following me along the field.
Farewell to battle and fame,

Farewell to the victor's spoils,

When Oscar had heard Finn's wail,
Convulsive pangs did him seize.

And his soft fair eyelid fell.
From us then Finn turned away,
And shed many bitter tears.
But for Oscar and for Bran <note>Finn's faithful hound, often mentioned alongside his closest kin</note>,
Never did he shed a tear.
There was none but Finn and I,
Greater than him of the <term>Feinn</term> <note>The Fianna; a legendary band of warriors led by Finn MacCumhail</note>.

Five score hundred, ten hundred and ten,

Of the Feinn dead on the field,
The number was nothing less.
No lie it is that double,
With Erin's king, great tale,
Perished on the other side,
Of Erin's well-armed men.
Finn cheerful or peaceful never
Was from that down to his death;
Since that fight it touched him sore
That our kings should want their land.
Ever since <term>Gaura's</term> <note>The Battle of Gabhra, the final catastrophic conflict of the Fianna</note> battle
My speech has lost all its power.
No night or day has e'er passed
Without a sigh for each hour.¹
To-night.

### The author of this here is Fergus the Bard.¹

High-minded Gaul,²
Who combats Finn,
A hero brave,
Bold in assault,

Thinking of the hard battle,
Which I and red, squint-eyed Cairbar fought,
That son of Cormac O’Chuinn;
Woe to us who fell under his hand;
A youth who knew no fear,
Whose hand delighted in the arrow, etc.
<note>original: "Smuaineachadh a chath chruaidh... Laogh gun ghràin cha do chuir, Annssa dh' a laimh iuthaidh, etc." This is the Scottish Gaelic version of the poem provided by the 19th-century editor for comparison with the Dean's phonetic script.</note>

Translation:—

My mourning is grievous this night,
Weeping is my condition,
As I think of the fierce fight,
Fought with red, squint-eyed Cairbre,
That son of Cormac O Chuinn;
Woe to them fell into his hands,
Hero who knew no coward fear,
Whose hand took delight in the arrow, etc.

The composition is very much the same with that of the Dean, but in many portions contains lines which the latter wants, and in others is comparatively defective. Although frequently superior in force, it is not, upon the whole, so smooth and regular as the Dean's edition. It will be given at greater length in the Appendix to this volume.

<margin>¹ <term>Fergus the Bard</term> <note>Fergus Filidh</note> was one of the sons of <term>Finn</term> <note>Finn Mac Cumhail, the legendary leader of the Fianna</note>, and consequently brother to <term>Ossian</term>. He was, from all we can learn, the chief Poet of the <term>Feinn</term> <note>The Fianna, a band of semi-mythological warriors</note>. <term>Ossian</term> was both warrior and poet; <term>Fergus</term> was chiefly poet. <term>Fergus</term> was probably somewhat like a modern Gaelic Bard, <term>John Macdonell</term>, commonly called <term>Ian Lom</term> <note>Bald John, a famous 17th-century MacDonald poet</note>, who, on being urged to fight at the battle between <term>Montrose</term> and <term>Argyle</term> at <term>Inverlochy</term>, replied with well-assumed indignation, “*That is not my business,—fight you and I'll relate,*” <note>original: "Cha-n e sin mo ghnothuch, cathaichibh sibhse 'us innsidh mise"</note>. In one of the odes preserved in the Dean's MS., <term>Fergus</term> is called “*Bard of the Feinn of Erin.*” <note>original: "Filidh Feinn Eirinn"</note></margin>

<margin>² This composition is usually called, “*The Ode to Gaul,*” <note>original: "Rosg Ghuill"</note> or the *Ode to Gaul.* <term>Gaul</term> <note>Goll mac Morna</note> was chief of one branch of the <term>Feinn</term>, the branch denominated “*Children of Morn,*” <note>original: "Clann Moirn"</note> or the *children of Morn,* as <term>Finn</term> was chief of the other branch, called “*Children of Boisgne,*” <note>original: "Clann Baoisgne"</note> or the *children of Boisgne.* The word “*Gall*” means a *foreigner*; “*muirn*” means a *body of men,* or *beauty, elegance,* in either sense giving a marked significance to the name. This poem represents a difference between <term>Gaul</term> and <term>Finn</term>, the cause of which will appear as the poem proceeds. <term>Fergus</term>, as was customary with the Bards, interposes as peacemaker, and represents to his father, whom he manifestly holds to be in the wrong, the danger of a difference with <term>Gaul</term>. The ode is a very remarkable one, having a striking resemblance to <term>Ossian</term>'s eulogy on <term>Finn</term>, as given already in this volume. Both bear decided marks of genuineness and antiquity. The language is peculiar, many of the words being obsolete. Indeed, the phonetic orthography of the Dean, the peculiar handwriting, and the discoloration and bad condition of the MS., with the obsoleteness of many of the words, made it a matter of no little labour to decipher the composition at all. There is an edition of it in <term>Miss Brooks' “*Reliques of Irish Poetry,*”</term> from which, however, the present differs considerably in many of the lines, besides having several additional lines, and several deficient in some parts. The peculiar rhythm of the original...</margin>

His bounty free,
Fierce to destroy.
Beloved of all,
Gaul, gentle, brave,
Son of great Morn <note>Morn, or Morni, was the leader of the Clan Morna and a rival to Finn MacCumhail.</note>;
Hardy in war,
His praise of old,
A comely man,
King, soldierly, free,
Of no soft speech,

In battle's day
He moved a prince;
Though soft his skin,
Not soft his deed,
Of portly mould,
A fruitful branch,
His heart so pure,
He trains the young.
'Bove mountains high
Rises in victory,
We ever fear
When he assails.

Avoid the man,
Terror of Gaul
Should make you quail;
Soothe him rather,

<column-break/>

The original, with the alliterations and vocalic
concords, give it remarkable smoothness
and force. The rhythm has, as far as
possible, been retained in the transla-
tion. But besides the language, the
sentiment of the piece is strong evidence
of its antiquity. Those features of char-
acter are commended which have always
been in favour in a rude age. Bodily
strength, courage, manliness, and size,
are dwelt on with all the warmth of an
ardent admiration; while as much of
literary cultivation is pointed out and
commended, as would show the acquaint-
ance of the hero with the traditions of
his race. Gaul is said to have been
“learned in history” <note>original: "eagnaidh a stair"</note>.
This and the ode to Finn have internal
evidence of being compositions belonging
to a very remote age.

It far resounds,
He's truly great,
Liberal, just,
Does not despise,
Yet firm resolves,
Gentle, yet brisk,
Forsakes no friend,
In fight of kings,
No powerless arm.
There, fierce his mien,
And strong his blow.
When roused his wrath,
He's third of the chase. <note>This refers to the legal entitlement to one-third of the hunting rights and spoils.</note>
Noble Mac Cumhail, <note>Finn, son of Cumhall, the leader of the Fenian bands.</note>
Soothe and promise,
Give peace to Gaul, <note>Gaul Mac Morna, leader of a rival clan within the Fenians.</note>
Check wrath and guile.

<margin>¹ It would appear from this latter part of the poem, that the difference between Finn and Gaul concerned the right to hunt. It would appear that such a cause of controversy is no modern affair, but that game and game-laws had their place among human interests and human contentions from the earliest period of our country's history. Finn seems to have claimed the right of chase over the whole territory of the Feinn <note>The Fenians or Fianna, the legendary warrior-bands of ancient Ireland and Scotland.</note>. Gaul resisted and claimed a share for the race of Morn <note>The Clan Morna.</note>. Hence the contest giving Gaul the appellation of “*Resister of Finn*,” <note>original: "Fear cogaidh Fhinn"</note> or *resister of Finn*. Fergus's intervention resulted in Gaul's obtaining from Finn, with good will, one-third of the territory called here “*wooded territory*,” <note>original: "fiodh"</note> or *wooded territory*, and one-third of the hounds. There is a curious reference to these hunting rights in one of the poems in praise of the MacGregors, given in an after-part of this volume, in which it is said that Finn himself dare not hunt without leave.</margin>
During my day,
Whate'er it be,
I'd give without guile,
A third of the chase.¹

Let's hear no more,
Soft dost thou speak,
Finn's love to Gaul,
And third of the hounds.

<margin>This is another of the compositions of Fergus, the son of Finn, and brother of Ossian. It will be found to be an account of the death of Oscar at the battle of Gaura. The account of Oscar's death given here, is in the way of reply to an inquiry of Finn.</margin>

With force he aimed a blow,
And smote him with his sword.
Then Art, son of Cairbar, fell, <note>original: "Art mac Cairbar"</note>
Struck with the second blow.
So it was that Oscar perished,
With glory, as a King.
Fergus the bard am I,
I've travelled every land,
I grieve after the Fenians, <note>original: "Feinn"; the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>
To have my tale to tell.
->Tell.<-

This tale is by Malcolm, son of the Chief Bard.¹ <note>original: "Gilliecallum M' an Olave"</note>

I have heard a tale of old,
A tale that should make us weep;
It is time to relate it sadly,
Although it should fill us with grief.
Rury's² race of no soft grasp,
Children of Connor and Connal;
Bravely their youth did take the field,
In Ulster's noble province.
None with joy returned home
Of Ireland's proudest heroes. <note>original: "Banva," an ancient poetic name for Ireland.</note>

<margin>¹ This is the composition of a Poet of whom we know nothing save what we find in this volume. From a poem of his in praise of the MacGregors, he would appear to have been a Scotsman. The name signifies Malcolm, the son of the chief bard or the physician. It is found still in the form of MacInally. This poem is the Celtic edition of the Persian tale of Zohrab and Rustum. The incidents are so similar, that the two tales must have had a common origin. Whether the Persians received the tale from the Celts, or the Celts from the Persians, or both from some other and older source, it is hard to say. The composition is in Gaelic, usually called, "The Death of Conlach." <note>original: "Bàs Chonlaoich"</note></margin>

<margin>² The Clan Rury, <note>original: "Clann Rughraidh"</note> a powerful race, who occupied the province of Ulster at an early period, after having expelled the Clan Deaghaidh, or the Dalcassians, afterwards of Munster.</margin>

<summary>This page concludes a poem by the bard Fergus on the death of the hero Oscar and introduces a new poem by Malcolm, son of the Chief Bard, concerning the tragic death of Conlach. The translator notes the striking similarities between this Celtic legend and the Persian story of Sohrab and Rustum.</summary>

Conlach had bound Connal.
