<margin>¹ Dunscaich, a stronghold in the Isle of Skye, on the coast of Sleat, of which the ruins still exist.</margin>
<margin>² Cuchullin, or the hound of Cullin, was a famous Celtic warrior, whose fame is celebrated both in Scotland and Ireland. The name is a curious one, and is thought by some critics to indicate the existence of Anubis worship <note>The ancient Egyptian jackal-headed god of the dead; the commentator suggests a possible link between dog-veneration in different cultures.</note>, or the worship of the dog, among the ancient Celts. There is a Cullin in Skye, and another in Ireland. From which of these the name was taken it is difficult to say. Certainly the Skye mountain is by far the more magnificent natural object, and Dunscaich is unquestionably in Skye.</margin>
<margin>³ Dundalgin is said to be the modern Dundalk.</margin>

### The author of this is the Blind O'Cloan. <note>The original text reads "in keich o eloan," likely referring to a blind poet named O'Cloan or a blind man from a specific meadow.</note>

"Tis the sigh of a friend from Fraoch's green mound,
"Tis the warrior's sigh from his lonely bier, <margin>The term used here is *Caiseal chrò* <note>original: "Caiseal chrò"</note>, which literally means a "stone pen" or "fortified fold." This likely refers to a stone coffin or cist used in ancient Celtic burials.</margin>
"Tis a sigh might grieve the manly heart,
And might make a maid to weep.
Here to the east the cairn, where lies
Fraoch, son of Fitheach <note>Fitheach means "Raven" in Gaelic.</note>, of softest locks,
Who nobly strove to favor Mai,
And from whom the Cairn of Fraoch is named.
In Cruachan <note>A famous royal site and mountain associated with Queen Medb.</note> to the east a woman weeps,
A mournful tale 'tis she laments;
Heavy, heavy sighs she gives
For Fraoch, son of Fithich, of ancient fame.
She 'tis, in truth, who sorely weeps,
As Fraoch's green mound she visits oft;
Maid of the locks that wave so fair,
Mai's daughter so beloved of men.
This night Orla's soft-haired daughter,
Lies side by side with Fraoch, son of Fithich.

<margin>The author of this composition is altogether unknown, nor is it easy to decipher even the name accurately. In the original it is distinctly “*the blind O'Cloan.*” According to the Dean's usual spelling, this should be “*An caoch O'Cluain,*” which means the *blind O'Cloan,* or the *blind man from the green mound.* Some of the readers of the manuscript have suggested it might be the name of a woman. We have given the form of the name most likely to be accurate. The composition itself is usually called “*The Death of Fraoch*” <note>original: "Bàs Fhraoich"</note>.</margin>

<margin>It is not easy identifying the geography of this poem. It is generally believed in Perthshire that the scene of Fraoch's death was in Glen Quaich, a valley lying between those of the Tay and the Almond rivers. We have a Loch Fraoch there, but I have not been able to identify the Cairn of Fraoch, or the Cairn of the Hand. Cruachan is spoken of as lying to the east, which supports the opinion that the scene of the poem was in Argyleshire, as Ben Cruachan is to the east of the locality so designated in that part of the country. I cannot find any lake in Scotland now called Loch Mai, although Loch Fraoch may have been so called.</margin>

<keywords>Fraoch, Blind O'Cloan, Celtic burial, death lament, Cruachan, Glen Quaich, Scottish topography, Gaelic balladry</keywords>

Many were the men who loved her,
She, of them all, loved Fraoch alone.

A rowan tree stood in Loch Mai,
We see its shore there to the south;
Every quarter every month,
It bore its fair, well-ripened fruit;
There stood the tree alone, erect,
Its fruit than honey sweeter far;
That precious fruit so richly red,
Did suffice for a man's nine meals;
A year it added to man's life,—
The tale I tell is very truth.
Health to the wounded it could bring,
Such virtue had its red-skinned fruit.
One thing alone was to be feared
By him who sought men's ills to soothe:
A monster <note>original: "A monster." The translator notes a parallel here between this Gaelic dragon and the Ladon who guarded the golden apples in the Greek myth of Hercules and the Hesperides.</note> fierce lay at its root,
Which they who sought its fruit must fight.
A heavy, heavy sickness fell
On Athach's daughter, of liberal horn; <note>A poetic description of a generous noblewoman who pours drink from a drinking horn</note>

Who asked her what 'twas ailed her now.
Mai said her health would ne'er return,
Unless her fair soft palm was filled

<margin>It is needless to point out the analogy between this tale and that of Hercules and the garden of the Hesperides. It will strike any one acquainted with the classical story.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the tragic Gaelic ballad of Fraoch, a hero of the Ulster Cycle, as recorded in the 16th-century Book of the Dean of Lismore. The previous section likely detailed the beginning of his fatal struggle with the water monster.</meta>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Fraoch, son of Fithich, and the beast—
It would have been better had they never fought.<gloss>1</gloss>
Fierce was the conflict, and it lasted long,
But the monster's head at length he took.
When the maiden saw what had happened,
Upon the shore she fell in a faint.
Then, when she awoke from her trance,
In her soft hand she grasped his hand:
“Although thou art now food for wild birds,
Thy final deed was nobly done.”
It is from the death which he met there
That the name is given to Loch Mai <note>Loch Mai is traditionally identified as a lake in the Menteith region of Scotland, though the legend is rooted in Irish mythology.</note>;
That name it will bear forever,
Men have called it so until now. A sigh.

They carry the hero’s body along
To Fraoch's green mound for his grave.
By his name they call the glen,
A sorrowful place for those he left behind.
Cairn Laive <note>Hand-Cairn</note> is the hill beside me;
Close by it, for many a happy day,
The hero lived, possessed of matchless strength,
The bravest heart in the day of battle.
Lovely were those lips, rich with welcomes,
Which women loved so well to kiss;
Lovely was the chief whom men obeyed,
Lovely those cheeks, red as roses.
Darker than the raven’s hue was his hair,
Redder than a hero’s blood were his cheeks;
Softer than the foam of streams was his skin,
Whiter it was than the whitest snow;
His hair fell down in curling locks,
His eye was bluer than the bluest ice;
His lips were redder than the red rowan berries.

<note>1 The story is simple and intelligible. Mai <note>The mother of the maiden</note> loved Fraoch and became jealous of her own daughter, usually called Geal-cheann, or Fairhead. She accordingly planned and accomplished Fraoch's destruction as related.</note>

### The author of this is Connal Cearnach M'Edirskol.¹ <note>Connal the Victorious, son of Edirskol.</note>

These heads, O Connal, are worthless;
Though thou must have blooded thine arms.
These heads thou bear'st upon that withe, <note>A "withe" is a tough, flexible branch or willow twig used for binding things together—in this grim context, for carrying severed heads.</note>
Can'st tell their owners, now thy spoil?

Daughter of Orgill of the steeds,
Youthful Evir, so sweet of speech,
'Twas to avenge Cuchullin's <note>Cuchullin (Cú Chulainn) is the central hero of the Ulster Cycle in Irish mythology, often compared to Achilles.</note> death,

Whose is that hairy, black, great head,
With cheeks than any rose more red,

<margin>¹ This is the most ancient of all the Ossianic <note>Ossian refers to the legendary blind bard of the 3rd century; "Ossianic" denotes poetry attributed to him or his era.</note> Poets. He was contemporary of Cuchullin, who flourished, according to Irish historians, in the first century. Cuchullin was his foster-son, and upon his being slain, Conall took vengeance upon his enemies by putting them all to death. In this poem he tells Evir, who was either the wife or the betrothed of Cuchullin, the names of those thus put to death, and whose heads he carried on a withe. The name M'Edarscoil is represented now by that of O'Driscol; "Cearnach" means *victorious*, so that the poet's name is really, The Victorious Conall O'Driscoll.</margin>

That which hangs nighest thy left arm,
The head whose colour has not changed?

That head the king of swift steeds owned,
Said Cairbar's son of vigorous lance;
In vengeance for my foster son,
I took that head and bore it far.

What head is that I see beyond,
Covered with smooth, soft, flowing hair,
His eye like grass, his teeth like bloom,
His beauty such as none is like?

Manadh, the man that owned the steeds,
Aoife's son, who plundered every sea;
I left his trunk bereft of its head,
I slew his people, every man.

What head is that I see thee grasp,
Great Connal of the gentle streams;
Since that Cuchullin¹ <note>Cuchullin is the preeminent hero of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology, known for his superhuman strength and "warp-spasm" battle frenzy.</note> now is dead,
Whom to avenge him didst thou take?

'Tis the head of Mac Fergus of steeds,
He in extremity so bold,
My sister's son from the tall tower,
His head I from his body wrenched.

What fair-haired head is that to the east,
Whose hand might well have seized the heads;
Well did I know his voice of old,
For he and I were friends awhile?

<margin>¹ Cuchullin, or *The Hound of Cullin*, is often spoken of simply as “The Hound” <note>original: "An Cu"</note>. In the Gaelic this is either “Hound” <note>original: "Cu"</note> or “of the Hound” <note>original: "Con"</note>, according as the word is in the nominative or genitive case. In the English version it is uniformly translated as Cuchullin, as it would be impossible to follow intelligibly the variations of the Gaelic grammar in the cases of a proper noun.</margin>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

How bushy the golden yellow locks,
Covering it with so much grace? <note>Evir is likely referring to a severed head brought back by Connal as a trophy of vengeance.</note>

The head of the son of Finn, the son of Ross the red,
The son of Cruith, slain by my stroke;
Evir, he was king, chief of them all,
In Leinster <note>A province in the east of Ireland.</note> of the spotted swords.

Great Connal, now please change thy tale,
Tell us the number slain by thine arm,
Of all the noble famous men,
In vengeance for the head of Con? <note>Referring to Cuchulainn, often shortened to "Cu" or "Con" in the Gaelic meter.</note>

Ten and seven score hundred men,
I tell the truth, the number is,
That fell by me, all back o'er back,
Fruit of my bravery and power.

Connal, tell how the women feel
In Ireland <note>original: "Innisfail," a poetic name for Ireland meaning "Isle of Destiny."</note>, the Hound <note>original: "Cu," referring to Cuchulainn, the "Hound of Ulster."</note> being dead;
Do they sadly, sorely mourn,
Now that like me themselves have grief?

O Evir, what am I to do,
Now that my Hound is taken away;
My foster-son of fairest form,
Now that he's left me desolate?

O Connal, lay me in my grave,
And raise my stone o'er that of the Hound;
In grief I'll soon from this depart,
Let my lips touch the Hound's lips in death.

Evir am I, of fairest form,
No vengeance can me satisfy;

# THE BOOK OF
<margin>¹ This poem is usually called “The Lay of the Heads” <note>original: "Laoidh nan ceann"</note>. It bears many marks of genuineness and antiquity. It is well known in the Highlands, but the Editor has never seen or heard any reference to its authorship, except in the manuscript of the Dean.</margin>
In tears no pleasure I can find,
’Tis sad that I am left behind.¹
->Connal.<-

<margin>² This is a remarkable composition, descriptive of an attempt of Caoilte to deliver his friend and patron Finn from the hands of Cormac Mac Art, King of Ireland, against whom the Fenians <note>original: "Feinn"</note> had been stirring up rebellion. Caoilte, after various strange doings, is told that he can only have Finn’s liberty on condition of bringing to Tara <note>original: "Tara"</note> a pair of all the wild animals in Ireland. A portion of the poem has been translated in Ireland, and published in the *Dublin University Magazine* for March 1854, in connection with an interesting paper upon the food of the ancient Irish, and the early zoology of their country, by Dr. Wilde. The translation is by that eminent Irish scholar, Mr. Eugene O’Curry. In that paper the poem is said to be at least a composition of the ninth century. It must be of extreme antiquity, as a reference to the language of it presents an amount of difficulty owing to obsolete words and phrases rarely met with. The Editor had the advantage of submitting his work to the review of Mr. O’Curry. For the topography of the poem, which is altogether Irish, the Editor has to refer the reader to the notes to the paper in the number of the *Dublin University Magazine*, referred to above, which are very full and very instructive.</margin>
The author of this is Caoilte Mac Ronan.²

<margin>³ Art, King of Ireland, was called “The Solitary” <note>original: "Aonfhir"</note>, from his love of solitude.</margin>
I set me off to rescue Finn,
To Tara <note>original: "Taura"</note> of the joyful streams;
With arms sure of victory,
To Cormac, son of Art the Solitary.³
I will not put forth my strength,
Though bloody and light of foot,
Until that with the Fenians of Ireland <note>original: "Feinn of Fail"</note>,
We have reached the shore of Loch Foyle.
<margin>⁴ Cuireach was a famous Leinster prince.</margin>
Then did we slay the mighty hero,
When we had slain Cuireach.⁴
We killed a mighty warrior
When we had killed their leader.

How I strove along to drive them,
Travelling over hills and ditches,

This race that I had swiftly run,
Was such as no man ran before me.
Then I brought them all to Taura <note>Tara, the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note>,

Then had I further much to suffer,
That night was to me very grievous.
Within the town there was a stronghold,
To which by nine doors there was entrance.
Cormaig <note>Cormac mac Airt, the High King</note> 'twas gave me the house,
As I now was very wearied.
Where I saw that they were placed
In the narrow, horrid dungeon;
Then came a loud and vigorous scream
From the throats of all the gathering.
There was a little ray of light
Reached them in through fifty openings.
Every door was closely shut,
Nor was the case an easy one;
They mournfully shut closely up,
While I as sadly was excluded.
My heart did now pour out its grief,
Watching by the doors till morning.
Though great the evil I had suffered,
As before they flew so swiftly,
Not one I suffered to escape
Till the day rose in the morning.
The name men gave to this great rabble
Was "**Caoilte's rabble**," and no wonder.
To see them standing side by side,
Was all the profit got by Cormag.

“Though pure are ye, they are not few

The maiden of the pure white robe
Drew near to where Mac Cumhal <note>Finn Mac Cumhaill, the legendary leader of the Fianna</note> sat.
She blessed the king of guileless heart,
And close beside him there sat down.
Finn asks her to give them her tale,
The handsome maid of whitest hand:

What virtue’s in thy spotless veil?”

That women, such as are not chaste,
Can in its folds no shelter find,—
None but the spotless wife it shields.”
“Give my wife the robe at once,”

NINE <margin>This is a curious piece. The dog without doubt entered largely into the mythology of the ancient Celts. Some have supposed that it was an object of worship with them. Mr. O’Curry <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a famous Irish scholar.</note> informs the editor that the “Dog-heads” <note>original: "Concheannaich."</note> were an ancient race who inhabited the Plain of the Dog-head <note>original: "Magh O’Coin-chinn," now Moygonihy, in Kerry.</note>. They were said to be great enemies of Finn. This poem may really describe an attack upon them. One of the Irish kings was called “Cairbar of the Cat’s-head” <note>original: "Cairbar Cinn Chait."</note>. The latter portion of the poem, referring to the banners of the Feine, has been preserved in tradition, although the editions of it are various.</margin> of us once did bind ourselves
To find material for a pup’s head,
To find material for a dog-pup’s head;
Though no attempt was more laborious.
We searched the plain of Leny Leirg,
And Glen Frenich of bloody swords;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.
Then did we search a dark, black glen,
A glen of deep corries <note>A "corrie" is a circular, mountain-side hollow formed by glacial erosion.</note>, full of stones;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.

<margin>It is difficult to say what this is. “Crom” was a Celtic deity. Here the word seems to indicate a banner. The word “Crom of the rock” <note>original: "Crom nan cairge."</note> will be found in the second volume of the publications of the Ossianic Society of Ireland, pp. 53, 58.</margin>

## THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

We searched the Fairy Mound <note>original: "Sian"</note> of Drum Cliff,
Fair after it seemed the plain of Liff;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.
We searched in Thurles of liberal hosts,
In Bregian Tara and Dun Dobhran;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.
We searched, too, through the Glen of the Cuckoo <note>original: "Glen a Cuaich"</note>,
Looking out for something noble;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.
We searched Moylena of slopes,
Through Bregian Tara and Kinsale;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.
We searched the whole of Ireland <note>original: "Eire"</note>,
Men and dogs ranging together;
True, we found not there one hound
From which we could obtain a pup.
Shortly were we thus engaged,
Ourselves, our followers, and friends,
<margin>¹ This seems to have been an imaginary monarch, like many of those in the "Prose Tales" <note>original: "Ursgeuls"</note>, or traditional stories of the Highlands. At least the Editor has not met with the name elsewhere.</margin>
When three battalions were seen,
Sons to the King of Rualay.¹
Cat-headed one battalion was,
Dog-headed was the one beside it;
The other behind them was white-backed,
Brown the rest, though white the back.
Aloft the mighty javelin shone
Of Finn, hero of bloody strength;
Above his noble, murderous shield
He bore that spear of hundred fights.
Bright was the glitter of the spear
In the white hand of Finn himself.

**SWEET** is man's voice in the land of gold,
Sweet the sounds the birds produce,
Sweet is the murmur of the crane,
Sweet sound the waves at **Bun Datreor**,
Sweet the soft murmuring of the wind,
Sweet sounds the cuckoo at **Cas a choin**.
How soft and pleasing shines the sun,
Sweet the blackbird sings his song;
Sweet the eagle's voice of **Eastaroy** <note>Likely Assaroe (Eas Ruaidh), a famous waterfall on the River Erne.</note>,
Above the sea of great **Mac Morn** <note>Goll Mac Morna, a leader of the Connacht faction of the Fianna and sometimes a rival to Finn.</note>;
Sweet the cuckoo 'mongst the branches,
Sweet the silence of the crane.
**Finn Mac Cumhail** is my father,
Who nobly leads the **Feinn's** seven bands;
When he his hounds lets loose to hunt,
To follow him is truly sweet.
->Sweet.<-

That whether others hear or not,
It may be heard by the son of Cumhall <note>original: "Mac Cumhail," referring to Finn MacCool, the legendary Irish hero and leader of the Fianna</note>.
The son of Cumhall once held a feast
On the slope of Allen <note>original: "Almhuin," the Hill of Allen in County Kildare, the primary seat of the Fianna</note>, with finest gold;
Over the music he presided,
Finn, who ever graced the feast.
Brave Oscar and Diarmad were there,
And good the son of Lughaidh <note>original: "Mac Luy," likely Lugaid Lághda</note>, warriors bold;
With two others who never shunned a fight,
Conan himself, and with him Oscar.
“Tell me now, my warriors brave,
As at the feast of Finn you sit,
Which do you count the sweetest music?”
“The clatter of gaming,” Conan said,
“The sweetest sounds I ever heard.”
Vigorous was his arm before the foe,
Yet never was there a man who more lacked sense.
“The sound of swords drawing against the foe,”
Said he who never spared in fight,
“The cleaving of men’s heads and legs,”
Was the sweetest music Oscar heard.
“The sounds which ever pleased me most,”
Said Diarmad of the slow-rolling eye,
“That which I loved most all my life,
Was a woman’s voice, as she talked softly.”
“My music, O son of Morna,”
Said the son of Lughaidh of the glittering arms,
“Is leaping amidst the tumult of my dogs,
As swiftly they gain upon the deer.”
“It is this that music is for me,”
Said Finn, the chief of all the host,
“To have my banner fluttering in the wind,
With heroes ranged by its golden side.”
“When of the bards I had no fear,
Ossian,” he said, as still he spoke;
“And when my Fianna <note>original: "Feinn," the band of semi-independent warriors led by Finn</note> were still around me,
Sweet was that music in my ears.”

<page-num>82</page-num>
# THE BOOK OF
<margin>¹ Hospitality was the most esteemed feature in the character of the Celtic chief. That of Finn is sung with untiring admiration by the poets. This virtue, with bravery, generosity, and liberal giving, no doubt to the poets themselves especially, seemed to make up what they looked upon as a perfect character for their leader. These feasts were apparently affairs of great moment, as the like are in our own day.</margin>
A GREAT feast was made by Finn,¹
I tell thee now, O tonsured Priest, <note>The "tonsured priest" refers to Saint Patrick, to whom the narrator Oisín is telling these stories.</note>
Many were the men were there,
Of the Feine <note>The Feine, or Fianna, were the legendary band of semi-independent warriors led by Finn.</note> of Alba <note>Scotland</note> and Erin <note>Ireland</note>.
The great Mac Morn <note>Goll Mac Morna, a former rival and later ally of Finn.</note> did ask
Of the queen of whitest hand,
“Did’st thou see so rich a feast
Since thou cam’st ’mongst the Feine of Erin?”
Finn of the Feine himself replied,
Chiefest of all both east and west;
He said she saw a richer feast
Than any Fenian feast in Erin.
We then saw coming from the waves
A warrior tall, manly, fair-haired,
No man was with him but himself,
And a noble man he was.
When he had come near the Feine,
Thus did he mildly, wisely say,
“Come, Finn, come along with me,
And take with thee a hundred men;
Thirty sons of the great Morn,
Let them be the first around thee;
One man and eight of thy own sons,
Take them and Oscar <note>Finn's grandson and a champion of the Fianna.</note> of the Feine’s Fians;
Let ten of the sons of Smoil be there,
And twenty of the sons of Ronan;
Let some of Muin’s sons be there,
Other ten, not counting Diarmad;
Take with thee Diarmad O’Duine, <note>Diarmuid Ua Duibhne, the famous hero known for his beauty and skill.</note>
He who could either court or hunt,
Both him and Caroll in thy ship;

## THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

“Let there be ten of men and crew,
Of thy men take with thee nine,
Of those whom thou'dst most like to have;
Besides them all thyself, O Finn,
Thou dauntless and well-armed man.
Take o'er the waves a hundred men
Of those that follow Finn Mac Cumhail <note>Finn Mac Cumhail is the legendary Irish hero and leader of the Fianna bands.</note>,
A hundred shields with golden studs,
For Finn Mac Cumhail, son of Trenmor <note>original: "Mac Tranevor"</note>.
Take now with thee also, Finn,
The two best hounds that are in Ireland <note>original: "Erin"</note>;
Bran and Scoilean¹ take them with thee,
The swiftest-footed of the pack.
Have no fear about thee, Finn,”
The tall and cheerful warrior said;
“Let them all be brought in peace,
And trouble not our men or ships.”
“Foolish the speech thou now hast made,
Thou man who cam'st amongst us;
Wert thou to approach nearer Finn,
Thy body soon would want its head.”
“Little care I for what thou say'st,
Bald-headed Conan of the gibes <note>Conan Maol was a member of the Fianna known for his biting wit and troublemaking nature.</note>;
Pity for thy friends that thou art there,
Ugly and feeble as thou art.”
“Rise ye up, ye sons of Boisgne <note>The Clan Boisgne was the family faction to which Finn belonged.</note>,”
Then spoke Conan, so well known;
Each man did seize a hero's arms,
From every side the Fians <note>The Fians, or Fianna, were semi-independent warrior bands in mythology.</note> came fast.
Then fell there slain a son of Finn,
One of the stalwart, white-hand Fians,
A man of Mac Morn's followers too,
A vigorous hand 'midst battle's blows.
“Fergus, now go rouse thee up,
And mingle boldly in the fight;
Ask whether Gaul <note>Gaul Mac Morna was a famous leader of a rival faction within the Fianna who eventually allied with Finn.</note> has aught to give

<note>¹ Scoilean was the second favourite hound of Finn.</note>

To Conan, whom he knows so well.”

Much do I weary, valiant Priest,

Finn of the feasts I loved most.

’Tis sad that the hill of the Feine,
Should now by the clerics be held,
And that the songs of men of books
Should fill the halls of clan Baoisgne <note>The family or sept of Finn MacCumhail</note>.
I myself was once in Rath Cruachan,¹
Happily beneath thy banks,
I little thought I e’er should find
A priest upon thy summit dwelling.
There would be found my shield and spear,

<margin>¹ Rath Cruachan, as Irish tradition
says, was the chief seat of the Feine of
Connaught, usually called the Sons of
Morna. The word “Rath” means a *Fort*,
and Cruachan an elevation more than
usually steep. It sometimes stands upon
another hill, as in Argyleshire, where we
have “Cruachan Beinne,” *The hillock*
*upon the mountain*, usually called Ben
Cruachan. The word “Rath” enters
largely into Scottish topography, and
assumes different forms in English, as
*Fort, Roth, Rothie, Rait, Raits*, etc.</margin>

<summary>A poetic lament by a surviving member of the Fianna, mourning the loss of his comrades and the displacement of heroic culture by the arrival of Christian priests. The text highlights the transition from the era of hunting and feasting to a time of religious study and psalms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the poetic dialogue between the elderly warrior Oisin (Ossian) and Saint Patrick. Oisin remains defiant, listing the legendary heroes of the Fianna who, if they were still alive, would drive the Christian clergy away from their sacred ancestral sites.</meta>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

My dogs and hounds along thy ridge,
Although to-night the Fenian <note>original: *Feine*; the legendary band of warriors led by Finn MacCumhail</note> hill
Is under clerics and their crosiers. <note>A crosier is a stylized staff carried by high-ranking Christian officials, resembling a shepherd's crook.</note>
Were the sons of Morn alive, <note>Clann Morna, a powerful faction of warriors from Connacht who were often rivals to Finn's family.</note>
The priestly order soon must quit;
You would find yourselves cut up,
Ye men of the spotted crooks.
Were Mac Luy alive, <note>Lugaid mac Luichead, a prominent member of the Fianna.</note>
With his six heroes bold,
Ere you had quitted the hill
You’d find your garments curtailed. <note>A threat that the priests' long robes would be violently shredded or cut short.</note>
Were the sons of Ceard alive,
Who never hypocrisy knew,
Neither your bells nor crooks
Would in place of their banners be found.
Were the sons of Muin alive,
Who knew no weakness in fight,
Men would not see thy people
So powerful amidst our hills.
Were the sons of black Garry alive, <note>Garadh mac Morna, a formidable warrior known for his role in the burning of the Fenian house at Almhuin.</note>
Or Caoilte, who was ever so brave, <note>Caoilte mac Rónáin, the nephew of Finn, famous for his extraordinary speed and longevity.</note>
Neither the sounds of bells or priests
Would now be heard in Rath Cruachan. <note>The ancient royal capital of Connacht, a site of immense spiritual and political importance in early Ireland.</note>
Were red-haired Ryno alive, <note>Raighne, a son of Finn MacCumhail known for his beauty and skill in battle.</note>
And brave Caol, son of Revan,
Thy books would not be so whole,
Oh man, who readest the Bible.
And for all thy hooked crosiers,
Which have travelled over the earth,
Thy staves would be in splinters,
Were only brave Oscar alive. <note>Oscar was the son of Oisin and the most valiant warrior of the Fianna's final generation.</note>
Thou of the yellow<gloss>1</gloss> garment,
Who sittest so much at thine ease,
’Tis well for thee that Conan is dead, <note>Conan Maol, the "Bald Conan," a foul-mouthed and quarrelsome warrior who would have had little patience for the Saint.</note>

---

<note>
1 “Yellow” was the favourite colour of the Celts. It seems that St. Patrick adopted it as the clerical colour, very probably as a means of commending himself and his cause to the good-will of the people.
</note>

<summary>In this poem, Oisin laments the displacement of the Fenian warriors by Christian clerics, specifically mocking Saint Patrick's authority. He lists various heroes like Oscar and Caoilte, claiming that their presence would have prevented the Church from taking over ancient sites like Rath Cruachan.</summary>

Else you would feel the weight of his fist,
Were the blue-eyed hero alive,
Bald Conan, the son of the Fenian <note>original: *Feine*; the legendary warrior band led by Finn Mac Cumhail.</note> band,
Cleric, though your office be sacred,
With his fist he’d strike you down.
Were the son of O’Duine alive, <note>Diarmuid O’Duine, one of the most famous and beloved members of the Fianna, known for his beauty and prowess.</note>
You man of the crooked staff, <note>A reference to Saint Patrick’s crosier, the stylized staff of a bishop.</note>
Your staff should be all in shivers,
Smashed at the pillar of stone.
You man of the bell, I do think,
If Daoruinn <note>A lesser-known member of the Fianna.</note> were now in life,
Your bell would be now in pieces,
Scattered before the pillar.
Were the red point seen, old man,
Of the swift-flying spear of Mac Ronan, <note>Caoilte Mac Ronan, known for his incredible speed and his skill as a hunter and warrior.</note>
Your bell would not be faintly sounding,
You who sing the howling song. <note>Oisin’s dismissive way of describing Christian chanting or psalms.</note>
I cannot be joyful now,
I see not Mac Cumhail <note>Finn Mac Cumhail (Finn MacCool), the leader of the Fianna.</note> in life,
I see not Diarmuid O’Duine,
I see not Caoilte Mac Ronan.
No wonder though I should be sad,
As I sit on this mound, Patrick.
I see not the son of Luy, <note>Lugaid, a prominent Fenian warrior.</note>
I see not the hero so loved,
I see not Fearluth <note>A Fenian hero.</note> by my side,
I see not the Fenian Oscar; <note>Oisin’s own son and one of the mightiest warriors of the Fianna.</note>
I see not warlike exercises,
I see not the noble hounds;
I see not the sons of Smoil,
I see not Gaul <note>Goll Mac Morna, a legendary leader of the Connacht faction of the Fianna.</note> of great feats,
I see not the generous Faolan, <note>One of Finn’s sons.</note>
I see not with him the Fenian band.
I see not Fergus, my brother,
So gentle and worthy of praise;
I see not Daire <note>Daire of the Songs, the Fenian bard.</note> of the songs,
Whose music we always enjoyed.

<summary>In this passage, Oisin grieves for his fallen companions, listing the names of the great Fenian heroes and expressing his disdain for Saint Patrick's Christian rituals. He portrays a world where the power of the Fianna would easily shatter the symbols of the new church, such as the bell and the crosier.</summary>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.
### 87

I see not Fatha Canan <note>original: *Fatha Canan*; a legendary figure of the third century, often identified as the ancestor of the Campbell clan.</note>,
Whose presence filled us with joy;
I see not one of our band <note>The Fianna, the legendary group of warriors.</note>,
Whose noise was like thunder in war.
I see neither music nor joy,
I hear not if music there be;
Before I was laid in my cave,
Freely I scattered my gold.
Patrick, I tell it to you now,
If I chose to share my knowledge,
It is not in my power to relate
How much of their joy I have seen.
I and the clerics of the mass-books <note>A reference to the Christian monks and their liturgical texts, which Oisin views as sterile compared to the oral traditions of the Fianna.</note>,
Are two that can never agree.
Though this night I am so mournful,
I am sad for the hill of the Fianna <note>original: *Feine*; the heroic warrior-hunters of Irish mythology.</note>.

->'Tis sad.<-

I now will tell you, O Grainne <note>The wife of Finn Mac Cumhail, famously known for her tragic elopement with Diarmuid.</note>,
What I have seen with the son of Cumhall <note>original: *Mac Cumhail*; referring to the great leader Finn MacCool.</note>.
The misery I suffer now
I cannot much longer endure.
I have seen sport and rejoicing
Among those who are now despised <note>Referring to the fallen status of the old pagan heroes in the new Christian era.</note>.
I have seen maidens and men,
I now will tell you, O Grainne.
Courtesy and cheerfulness too,

Joy and merriment were his,
All his people were with him.
When from the drinking hall we rose,
We saw the whole of the <term>Feine</term> <note>original: *Feine*; while usually referring to Finn mac Cumhaill's Fianna, here it is used broadly for the warrior-host of the Ulaid or Ulstermen.</note>.

The most loved thing of all we had

Killed on the mountain side,
With the skill of the shot for her fired.

of four different pieces: The Irish bardic account of the name of "Srubh Brain" <note>original: *Srubh Brain*; translates to "The Raven’s Snout," a headland at Loch Swilly.</note>, or the "Raven's snout," at Loch Swilly <note>A glacial fjord in County Donegal.</note>; the legend of "Cuchullin's sick-bed;" the death of Conlaoch; and a short but imperfect account of the battle of Cnucha, in which Cumhal <note>The father of the legendary hero Finn mac Cumhaill.</note> was killed. Each of them wore round his neck

> At the time that Cumhal was slain,
> For the deed which then was done,
> Take vengeance now if you will.”

->The author of this is Big Duncan <note>original: "Duncan Mor"</note> from Lennox.¹<-

PITY the man who lost his voice,
When he is called on to recite,
Who cannot speak so fast as needs be,
And yet’s unwilling to give up.
Who cannot sing an air or tune,
And cannot well recite a <term>lay</term> <note>A short narrative poem, often intended to be sung.</note>.
Who cannot put aside his harp,
Yet cannot sing as he would wish.
Pity him ever with his “dring, drang,”
Trying his verses to recite,
When men can neither hear his harp,
Nor understand the songs he sings.
Pity the man neglects his health,
And strives not his vigour to retain.
Pity the man who ever strives
To have the fruit he cannot reach.
Were I to wish to have such fruit,
Fruit which I could not reach on high,
I’d cut the tree down at the root,
Let men be angry if they will.
<meta>catchword: Pity.</meta>

> Not in darkness was thy family formed,

<columns>
2

First of the clans in all the earth,
Each man of them is worth a hundred.
The noblest clan which you can find,
A race as brave as they are peaceful;
The clan whose praise does fill the lands,
Famed for their faith and godliness.
The clan so faithful, bold, and brave,
The clan so swift amidst the fight,
The clan so gentle among men,
And yet in battle none so fierce;
The clan most numerous of all
Whose number has been ever known.
The clan which never vexed the Church,
And ever dreaded its reproach.
Of all that dwell in green <term>Albin</term> <note>The Gaelic name for Scotland, often used in poetry to evoke the ancient kingdom.</note>,
This is the bravest ever baptized.
The third of every land is theirs,
Their bravery is like the falcon's.
The clan most numerous and famous,
Of finest form and fairest mould;
The clan that has the largest hearts,
Most patient and most liberal.
They, sons of kings, deserved no gibe
When asked in trouble to give help.
Noble were they since the time
When there was giving and poor ones.
The clan for wine and shelter best,
The first in prowess and in strength.
'Tis sad how short the length extends
Given by him who spins your thread <note>A reference to the Fates of classical mythology who spin and cut the thread of life.</note>.
They were not wicked and rough,
Nor were they gentle and weak.

---

[The author was in all] likelihood an ecclesiastic <note>A member of the clergy or a priest.</note>. Several of the early <term>Lords of the Isles</term> <note>The MacDonald chiefs who ruled the Hebrides and western Highlands as semi-independent sovereigns until the late 15th century.</note> were liberal benefactors to the Church, and it is not unlikely that this liberality called forth the praises of our bard. The word “<term>bochd</term>” <note>Gaelic for "poor."</note>, *poor*, associated in one of the

<column-break/>

lines of this composition with “<term>brontachd</term>” <note>Gaelic for "bestowing" or "giving."</note>, *bestowing*, is often by early Gaelic writers applied to monks and hermits, who lived upon the beneficence of the wealthy, and became finally enriched by their gifts.

</columns>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

In the midst of trial and hardships,
No rock was harder than they <note>A metaphor for the clan's unyielding strength and resilience under pressure.</note>.
The clan without pride or misdeeds,
When the spoils of battle are theirs.
Amongst them you would find gentlemen,
And common people alongside them.
Pity him who has lost their defense,
Pity him who forsakes their protection.
There is no clan like the Clan Donald <note>The MacDonalds, who held the title of "Lords of the Isles" and were the most powerful Gaelic dynasty in medieval Scotland.</note>,
The noble clan of the firmest mind.
Who is there who can number their gifts?
Who is there who can count their nobles?
Without limit, beginning, or end,
Of the excellencies found among their gentry.
First of all with the Clan Donald,
There is knowledge which they learn;
Last of all, there is among them
Polish <note>In this context, "polish" refers to courtly refinement or sophisticated manners.</note>, generosity, and modesty.
’Tis in sorrow and in grief
That understanding and learning are gathered
By him who would possess them.
No joy exists without the Clan Donald.
Loud was the sound of their thunder,
This race so wise and faithful;
Though they may now be reproached <note>Likely a reference to the declining political power of the Lordship of the Isles during the time of the manuscript's compilation.</note>,
There is no joy without the Clan Donald.
This people so great in fame,
In courtesy, mind, and firmness,
There is no right without them,
There is no joy without the Clan Donald.
The son of his virgin mother <note>A reference to Jesus Christ.</note>,
Who has earned for us freedom from pain,
Though He be faithful and true,
There is no joy without the Clan Donald.

Alas! alas! this is the head <note>This "head" refers to the skull or the physical remains of the fallen warrior being addressed or viewed by the poet.</note>
Which belonged to the blue-armed Conull; <note>The "blue-armed" description likely refers to the color of his polished steel armor or weapons.</note>

Alas! alas! this is the eye

Round which the eyelid wound,
Benevolent it was and manful.
Alas! alas! this is the mouth
In which no bard did folly find;
Its lips so thin, like apples red,
Sweet as honey the mouth of Conull.

The hand of him so brave in battle,
The hand of Conull my first beloved.

It was a hound from Mull that came,

The foot of him in fight most brave,
The foot of the shielded son of Scanlan.
Alas! success e’er followed Conull,
Where’er it was he battle fought;
But now that my tale is done,
This place is the dwelling of tears.

-># THE DEAN OF LISMORE.<-
->## 99<-

### The author of this is John of Knoydart <note>Knoydart is a mountainous region on the west coast of Scotland. The author’s identity is known only through this attribution.</note>

Thou head of Diarmad O’Cairbre, <note>Diarmad was an Irish harper who assassinated Angus Og Macdonald.</note>
Though great be thy trouble and pain,
I grudge thee not all thou hast suffered, <note>The poet is saying he feels no sympathy for the assassin's suffering.</note>
Although it be painful to tell.
I grudge not though thy ragged locks
Be searched by the winds from the glens.
I grudge thee not that thou art bound,
Thou head of Diarmad O’Cairbre ;
Pity the thought e’er filled men’s breasts,
That thy friendship was not hatred ; <note>The poet suggests it would have been better if the assassin had been an open enemy rather than a false friend.</note>
Pity, alas ! thou turn’dst not back,
Thou head of Diarmad O’Cairbre ;
Thou hast the King of Isla slain, <note>Angus Og, the "King of Isla," was the son of the Lord of the Isles and a powerful Gaelic leader.</note>
Who freely gave his wine and money,
Him of the soft and flowing locks,
Thou head of Diarmad O’Cairbre ;
Isla, king of well filled horns, <note>Drinking horns were symbols of high-status hospitality in the Highlands.</note>
Who with his friends so kindly dealt ;
Alas ! who gashed his soft white skin,
Thou head of Diarmad O’Cairbre !
Beloved was that liberal hand,
Which never grudged his gold or silver,
And which in feast or hunt was first,
Thou head of Diarmad O’Cairbre.

---

<columns>2</columns>
We have no surviving oral traditions about the author of this fragment. All we know from this text is that he was a man from Knoydart—a rugged, mountainous area between Loch Hourn and Morar on the west coast of Inverness-shire. Until recently, this land was held by the Macdonells of Glengarry.

In a historical account of the Macdonalds (published by the Iona Club), it is said that Angus Og of Isla—the "King of the Isles" who famously fought his own father at the Battle of the Bloody Bay—was assassinated by Art 

<column-break/>

O’Carby, an Irish harper. O’Carby was allegedly incited to the crime by the Chief of the Mackenzies, whose daughter had charmed the impressionable musician. In the Irish annals, this harper is called Diarmad. This poem commemorates the event by describing the punishment of the assassin, who was executed by being pulled apart by horses. As these lines were written shortly after the murder (which occurred around 1490) and were preserved by the Dean of Lismore, they provide strong evidence for the historical accuracy of the story.

<summary>This page contains a poem by John of Knoydart addressing the severed head of Diarmad O’Cairbre, the assassin of Angus Og, the Lord of the Isles. The poem and accompanying notes describe the murder of the "King of Isla" around 1490 and the subsequent gruesome execution of his killer.</summary>

<keywords>John of Knoydart, Diarmad O’Cairbre, Angus Og, King of Isla, Lord of the Isles, assassination, Irish harper, Clan Donald, Scottish Highlands history, 1490</keywords>

Melancholy earth upon the breast of Nial,
Melancholy its depth upon his grave;
Neither nobility nor fame can save,
Since that the King of the North is dead. <note>Niall Glúndub, the High King of Ireland.</note>

He is traced from the noble race of Nial, <note>The Uí Néill dynasty.</note>
The men who proudly governed all this land.
I left the gentle, kind, Mac Cuilenan,²

Of feasts and wine I could have abundance;

Soft were his locks, and grey his eye.
Wrath grew upon the mighty deep,
The wind blew in strength from the east,³
Nial then bent him on his knee,

-># THE DEAN OF LISMORE.<-

Stronger it blew and without fail;
It suffered no happiness nor peace.
The wind never ceased its sound,
Neither fort nor tree was spared,
Since that the courteous king is dead.
Since Niall,¹ son of Áed, died yesterday,
Numbers on numbers sorely mourn;
And though cups and horns are filled,
Sore is the blow to Conn’s great race. <note>The "race of Conn" refers to the Connachta and the Uí Néill, descendants of the legendary High King Conn of the Hundred Battles.</note>
Without him prosperity is joyless,
His form my heart with sorrow fills,
That I’m till judgment left behind,
Is that which fills my heart with grief.
->Melancholy.<- <note>This repeats the opening word of the poem (from page 100), a traditional Irish poetic device called "dúnadh" used to signal the poem's conclusion.</note>

### Gormlaith, daughter of Flann.²

Monk, remove thy foot,
Lift it off the grave of Niall.
Too long dost thou heap the earth
On him with whom I fain would lie;
Too long dost thou, Monk, there
Heap the earth on noble Niall.
Thou brown-haired friend, though gentle,
Press not with thy sole the earth.
Do not firmly close the grave,
O Priest, whose office is so sad.
Raise off the fair, black-kneed Niall, <note>A reference to his epithet "Glúndub," which literally means "Black-knee."</note>
Monk, remove thy foot.
Son of the O'Neill of finest gold,
’Tis not of my will thou’rt bound.³

¹ Niall was the son of Áed Findliath, King of Ireland. ² This is another elegy of Gormlaith, Queen of Ireland, on her husband, Niall Glúndub. There is something extremely mournful in these compositions of the

<column-break/>

youthful queen. Mr. O’Curry <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a pioneering scholar of Irish manuscripts.</note> has kindly furnished the Editor with Irish copies of these two elegiac pieces, which he is about to publish in a second volume of his admirable Lectures.

³ Referring, undoubtedly, to the mode of laying out the dead. <note>The Queen expresses her grief that the King is being physically bound or constrained by the burial shroud and the weight of the earth.</note>

Wounded thou hast been, great man,¹
Whate'er the men were thou hast met;
Though thou art truly sad and sick,
'Tis joy to us that thou'st got fame.
Ten hundred ships from Greece of the Gael, <note>Medieval Irish tradition, as found in the *Lebor Gabála Érenn* (Book of Invasions), claimed the Gaels originated in Scythia and spent time in Greece and Egypt before arriving in Ireland.</note>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Not good to be of ill-famed race;
Not good is a dirty woman;
Not good to write without learning;
Not good are grapes when sour;
Not good is an Earl without English; <note>This likely refers to the cultural shift where Gaelic lords were expected to navigate the English-speaking world of law and administration to maintain their status.</note>
Not good is a sailor, if old;
Not good is a bishop without warrant; <note>A "warrant" in this context refers to legitimate ecclesiastical authority or a proper commission from the Church.</note>
Not good is a blemish on an elder;
Not good a priest with but one eye; <note>Medieval Canon Law often required "canonical fitness," meaning a priest should be free of significant physical defects to serve at the altar.</note>
Not good a parson, if a beggar;
Not good is a palace without play; <note>"Play" here suggests hospitality, music, and the entertainment expected of a noble household.</note>
Not good is a handmaid if she’s slow;
Not good is a lord without a dwelling;
Not good is a temple without a burying ground;
Not good is a woman without shame; <note>In the medieval sense, "shame" refers to modesty and a sense of social propriety.</note>
Not good is a harper without a string;
Not good is fighting without courage;
Not good is entering a port without a pilot;
Not good is a maiden who backbites;
Not good is the poverty of a debtor;
Not good is a castle without an heir;
Not good is neglecting the household dogs;
Not good is disrespect to a father;
Not good is the talk of the drunken;
Not good is a knife without an edge;
Not good is injustice in judging;
Not good is the friendship of devils
For thy son, oh Virgin most honoured; <note>The poem pivots here to address the Virgin Mary, contrasting the "badness" of worldly failings with the divine sacrifice.</note>
Though he has saved the seed of Adam, <note>A reference to Christ’s redemption of humanity.</note>
Not good for himself was the cross. <note>A paradoxical statement: while the Crucifixion was "good" for humanity’s salvation, it was a "not good" or agonizing experience for Christ personally.</note>
Not good is a reader without understanding;
Not good for a man to want a friend;
Not good is a poet without a subject;
Not good is a tower without a hall;

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

Not good is a web without fulling; <note>Fulling is the process of cleansing and thickening cloth, particularly wool, to make it sturdier and warmer.</note>
Not good is sport without laughter;
Not good are misdeeds when prosperous;
Not good is marriage without consent;
Not good is a crown without supremacy;
Not good is ploughing by night;
Not good is learning without courtesy;
Not good is religion without knowledge.
->Not good.<-

***

I MYSELF, Robert, went <note>original: "I MYSELF, Robert, went..." These lines are shared as a reflection of public sentiment regarding monasteries during this period. The author appears skeptical of the monastic system, suggesting they were perhaps exclusive or inconsistent in their rules regarding women.</note>
Yesterday to a monastery,
And I was not allowed in,
Because my wife was not with me.

I DISLIKE to journey for a year; <note>This begins another specimen of aphoristic poetry, which originally appeared in rhyme. It is likely attributed to Phelim McDougall. The "dislikes" genre was a popular way to list social grievances and personal pet peeves.</note>
I dislike the table where a woman sits; <note>In a formal or monastic setting of the time, gender-segregated dining was common; this may reflect a preference for traditional masculine company or a specific social protocol.</note>
I dislike sorrow and sadness;
I dislike a great house without joy; <note>Hospitality and the presence of musicians and poets were the hallmarks of a "joyful" noble house in Gaelic culture.</note>
I dislike seeing a good man with a bad wife;
I dislike a frown upon a prince’s face;
I dislike weak drink at a high price;
I dislike a noble without courtesy;
I dislike war when it is peace,
When nothing is allowed to pass;
I dislike a rough and cruel chief;
I dislike the men who cannot fight;

<column-break/>

<term>fulling</term>: A step in clothmaking involving the beating of woven wool to shrink and thicken it.
<term>aphoristic poetry</term>: A style of verse consisting of short, memorable statements of truth or principle.
<term>monastery</term>: A building or complex of buildings occupied by a community of monks living under religious vows.
<term>Phelim M’Dougall</term>: A likely author for several of the poems collected in the Dean's book.
<term>Robert</term>: The narrator of the satirical monastery verse, possibly the author himself.
<term>chief</term>: A leader of a clan or a person of high social rank in the Gaelic order.

The first milch cows <note>Cows kept specifically for milking; their "bellowing loud" perhaps suggests a noisy or demanding household.</note> that bellow loud;
'Tis best to have nought to do with women.
The wife who listens to my speech,
Who listens to my voice and cry,
Just as if wax were in her ears;
'Tis best to have nought to do with women.
Her husband she to wrath provokes,
Different her manner with all else,
For them she lightly steps about;
'Tis best to have nought to do with women.
Were she to see a weeping eye,
With any youth of handsome form,
To him she would not run but leap;
'Tis best to have nought to do with women.
Where is the young and sprightly maid,
Who would not quietly give her kiss,
To any lips that she might meet?
'Tis best to have nought to do with women.
Though married from the altar and the church,
From the good priest's worthy hand,
Still are her way and temper bad;
'Tis best to have nought to do with women.
'Tis best to have nought to do with women,
Wrath and annoyance they provoke;
He who does not this proclaim
Is sure to find a woe himself.
->Pity.<- <note>In Gaelic poetry, it is common to end a poem with the word it began with (a "dúnadh" or closing). This poem began with "Pity the man" on the previous page.</note>

The coquetry <note>Flirtatious or vain behavior.</note> of Duncan from Taid's daughter <note>original: "ingen Taidhg"; the daughter of Teague or Thady.</note>,
The most impudent coquetry men have seen,
The coquetry of the wife of David,
Coquetting like the wind in her red-tailed skirts,
Men had thought that I was dumb,
Whilst I'd three reasons not to speak.

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

## The author of this is the Bard M'Intyre. <note>Nothing is known of this specific poet. While the famous Duncan Ban MacIntyre (the "Bard of Glenurchy") is well-known in Gaelic literature, this author represents an older, perhaps forgotten, namesake from the late 15th or early 16th century.</note>

What is this ship on Loch Inch, <note>Loch Insh, a lake formed by the River Spey in the Badenoch district of the Scottish Highlands.</note>
Of which we now may speak?
What brought this ship on the loch,
Which songs cannot overlook?
I would like much to ask,
Who was it brought that ship,
Afloat upon that angry loch,
Where changes often come?
The fierce wind from the hills,
And bitter storms from the glens,
Oft has the vessel from the shore,
Stolen upon the dangerous sea.
Stranger, who sawest the ship,
On the rough and angry stream,
What should hinder thee to tell
About her and about her crew?
An old ship without iron or stern,
Never have we seen her like,
The vessel all with leather patched,
It is not even watertight beneath the waves.
Her boards are trifling bits of deals, <note>Thin planks of pinewood.</note>
Black patches down along her sides,
Useless nails to fix them on
Upon her scanty, stinted ribs.
What woman cargo is in the black ship

---

¹ Nothing is known of this poet. The modern M'Intyre, the bard of Glenurchy, has a place second to none among the composers of Gaelic poetry; but it would appear that there was an older poet of the name, and one not unknown to fame. Four hundred years may produce no little change in the place which not a few men of note in our day hold in the temple of fame, and greater stars than the bard M'Intyre may have their lustre dimmed by time. He is another writer of

<column-break/>

satires on women, a kind of composition wonderfully popular, judging from our manuscript at the period. We only give a few specimens of these, but there are several in the miscellany, and some of a character which, in modern days, one wonders the Dean <note>James MacGregor, the Dean of Lismore, who compiled this collection between 1512 and 1542.</note> could have admitted to his collection.

² The only loch of this name with which the Editor is acquainted is Loch Inch, on the Spey, in Badenoch.

--- Page 224 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical poem attributed to the "Bard M'Intyre" from page 107. The poem uses a poorly-constructed ship as a metaphor for a group of undesirable women. The text then transitions into a brief, sharp satirical jab at the Macdougall family of Dunolly.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

Pulling her along through the waves,
With a cargo heartless and mindless too?
Widows with foolish minds,
A boasting, talkative crew,
A troublesome and wicked load,
Quarrelsome and greedy,
With evil minds and evil deeds.
Their habits and conversation are foul,
A band of notorious reputation,
With no truth in anything they say;
Drunken, singing with empty levity,
A misshapen and mischievous lot,
Who live on both sides of Loch Inch. <note>Loch Insh, a lake formed by the River Spey in the Highlands.</note>
In spite of you and of their ship
Upon the dark face of the stormy sea,
No decent woman would board that vessel,
No matter how great the necessity.
Only the worst of women go to sea in her;
Others cannot offer them any help.
Let this ship be driven from the loch,
Down into the fierce and roaring deep;
Let the wind pursue the vessel
To the ancient point of Seananach. <note>original: "Seananach." Likely a local landmark or headland near the loch, though the specific name has faded from modern maps.</note>
There I will leave upon the current
That ill-favored, ill-doing ship,
Full of wicked widows out on the sea,
Without a single psalm or prayer ever being said.

<meta>catchword: What.</meta>

---

The reputation of the house of Dunolly <note>The ancestral seat of the MacDougall clan near Oban.</note> brings little favor when they are seen driving a herd. <note>An insinuation that the family were cattle thieves, a common insult between rival clans.</note>
This brute is much like a dog, always greedy for stolen meat.

<columns>2</columns>
1 These satirical lines directed at the family of Dunolly are presented as an example of the harsh tone often employed by the bards to satisfy their grudges against rival clans. It should be noted that the
<column-break/>
Macdougalls of Lorn were a race as famous for the antiquity of their lineage as they were for the high character of many who bore the name.

<note>The term <term>bard</term> refers to the professional poets of the Gaelic world who held significant social power; their praise could immortalize a chief, but their satire was feared as a literal curse.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the satirical verses of "Bard M'Intyre" found on page 108 to a more lyrical, mournful poem by John MacMuirich. While the previous page focused on biting social commentary and clan rivalries, this entry moves into the tradition of courtly love and personal lament, common among the professional bardic families.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

## The author of this poem is John MacMuirich.¹

My fate is heavy and sad,
Though men do not understand it;
Suffer me not, O Son of God,
To endure this pain today.
Little did the school <note>The "school" refers to the traditional bardic colleges where poets were trained in the complex arts of Gaelic meter and history.</note> imagine
That such would be my destiny;
The fate that has overtaken me
Is what has brought me to ruin.
The pain I suffer now
Is three times what I have ever felt;
The trouble I have found
Is as heavy as a stone.
Because of her who caused my grief,
My anger and my passion are great.
Her skin is like the foam of the waves,
Her hand is soft and rosy,
Her lips are like red berries;
She has gently captured my soul.
Since I slept last night,
My state has been truly sorrowful.
I thought she was there beside me,
That I saw her smiling.
But she has not been here since the day
When my grief first began.
She of the curling locks
And the rich, red color—
Five jewels tied in a knot
Form the maiden's name. <note>In Gaelic poetry, bards often used riddles or numerical symbols—here "five jewels"—to hide the name of their beloved, likely indicating a name with five letters such as Mairead (Margaret).</note>

---

¹ This John MacMuirich (or MacVurrich) was, in all probability, a member of the family who served for many years as the hereditary bards to the Clanranald. They took their name from their famous ancestor in the thirteenth century, Muireach Albanach <note>Muireach Albanach (c. 1180–1250) was a renowned Scottish-Gaelic poet and the founder of the MacMhuirich bardic dynasty, which lasted for centuries.</note>. A list of the names of these bards spanning

<column-break/>

eight generations was provided by Lachlan MacMhuirich, who was living in 1800, in his formal declaration recorded in the *Report of the Highland Society on the Poems of Ossian*. However, that specific list does not include this John. This piece is primarily interesting for the historical references found at its conclusion.

<summary>A poignant poem of unrequited love by John MacMuirich, a member of the famous MacMhuirich bardic dynasty. The text includes a historical note identifying the author's prestigious lineage as hereditary poets to the Clanranald.</summary>
<keywords>John MacMuirich, Clanranald, Muireach Albanach, bardic school, Highland Society, Ossian, Gaelic poetry, courtly love</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the mournful love poem by John MacMuirich from the previous page, transitioning into a short moralizing verse by Duncan M’Pherson and the beginning of a famous poem regarding the death of Alexander the Great. These entries demonstrate the Dean's interest in both contemporary Gaelic lyricism and classical historical themes.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

It is a pity she is not with me,
And that others do not possess her.
Oh, that I myself might obtain
That friend for evermore.
Even if I were to suffer
What other men have felt—
The spear of great Cuchullin, <note>Cuchullin (Cú Chulainn) is the preeminent hero of Gaelic mythology, famous for his supernatural strength and his deadly spear, the Gáe Bulg.</note>
The horse of white-steed Teague, <note>Teague (Tadhg mac Céin) was a legendary figure in Irish lore; the "white steed" likely refers to the magical horses often associated with high-ranking Celtic nobility.</note>
The unbroken purple shield,
So famous in every war;
The speed of Mac Erc's coursers, <note>Mac Erc (Muircheartach mac Erca) was a 6th-century High King of Ireland; "coursers" is an archaic term for fast, powerful battle horses.</note>
Though it is a great deal to say—
Alas, it is more sorrowful for me,
The trouble I must endure.

### Duncan M'Pherson.¹

Alexander, have you left your sadness,
Or is it that you simply cannot?
Have you passed another year without God,
Or do you intend to live this way forever?
Have you not found your God,
Now that you are aged and grey?
If sadness is a form of prosperity,
Then the gifts you have received from God are rich indeed.

---

**FOUR** men met at the grave,²
The grave of Alexander the Great;
They spoke words of truth,

¹ This writer was probably a member of the clergy, <note>original: "ecclesiastic"</note> but nothing else is known of him.

² This composition is one of the few more recent fragments in this manuscript which appear in other historical sources. We have a copy of it in the collection of Gaelic 
<column-break/>
poems compiled by Ronald M'Donald, a schoolmaster from the Isle of Eigg, and son of Alexander M'Donald, the famous bard from Skye. M'Donald's version was most likely recorded from oral recitation, and it is remarkable how little it differs from this version in the Dean’s collection, written 250 years earlier.

<meta>This page continues the poem regarding the death of Alexander the Great, which began at the bottom of the previous page. The poem features four philosophers or wise men offering aphorisms on the vanity of earthly power at the tomb of the great conqueror.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE
<meta>catchword: Four.</meta>

Over the hero of Greece the fair.
The first man of them said,
> "There were yesterday with the king,
> The world’s great hosts, sad the tale,
> Though to-day he lonely lies."

> "Yesterday the world’s great king
> Proudly rode upon the earth;
> But to-day it is the earth
> That rides upon the top of him." <note>This is a play on the imagery of a king riding a horse; in death, the "earth" (the burial mound) is what sits atop the rider.</note>

Then did the third wise speaker say,
> "Yesterday Philip’s son owned the world;
> But to-day he only owns
> Of it all not seven feet." <note>A common medieval expression referring to the standard length of a grave, emphasizing that even a world-conqueror needs no more land than a peasant once dead.</note>

"Alexander, brave and great,
Who won and treasured gold and silver,
To-day," the fourth man wisely said,
> "The gold it is that treasures him." <note>An ironic inversion: instead of Alexander possessing his wealth, the precious metals of his coffin or the earth itself now "possess" or "keep" him.</note>

The palm among trees was Philip’s son,
The moon among the lesser stars,
As gold above the finest gems,
As among fish leviathan. <note>In this context, leviathan refers to a great whale or sea monster, symbolizing immense power and scale.</note>
The lion amidst carrion,
The eagle among other birds,
As Sion hill <note>Mount Zion in Jerusalem, often used in literature as a symbol of spiritual and physical height.</note> amidst the hills,
As Jordan <note>The River Jordan.</note> amidst other streams.
The polished gem ’mongst common stones,
The sea amidst all lesser streams,
Noble was the fearless man,
The man above all other men.
The man above all other men,
Save heaven’s great and holy King.
King he was of herds and boars,
Ruler of hosts and heroes too.
True was the talk these speakers had,
As at the great king’s grave they met.
Unlike to women’s empty talk,
Were the sayings of the four.

<summary>This page concludes a philosophical poem about the death of Alexander the Great, using four wise men to reflect on the transience of power and wealth. Alexander is compared to the greatest elements of nature and scripture before his mortality is ultimately contrasted with the eternal kingship of God.</summary>

<keywords>Alexander the Great, Philip of Macedon, death and mortality, vanity, medieval philosophy, leviathan, Mount Zion, River Jordan</keywords>

### John M'Murrich said this.

->All excellence is found in Alexander.¹<-

### Finlay, the red-haired bard, said this.²

->Gael-like is every leap of the dun horse,<-
->A Gael she is in truth.<-
->It is she who conquers and wins,<-
->In all that I’ll now sing.<-
->The praise of speed to her limbs,<-
->In every fierce assault.<-
->Marked, and famous her strength,<-
->While quiet at the house of prayer.<-
->The birds are they who could,<-
->Strive with her in the race.<-
->Not false is the fame of that horse,<-
->The steed both sturdy and swift,<-
->Liker she was to Duseivlin,³<-
->Than to the beast of Lamacha.⁴<-
->They who would view her size and triumphs,<-
->Can nowhere find her match.<-

¹ Alexander was a family name of the MacGregors of Glenstrae. In the obituary contained in this manuscript is the following entry:—1526. <note>original Latin: "Obitus Gregorii filii Johannis M'Gregor, alias M'Evine M'Allester de Glenschray"</note> "The death of Gregor, son of John MacGregor, otherwise known as Gregor, son of Ewen, son of Alexander of Glenstrae." This would indicate the period of Alexander as about the middle of the fifteenth century.

² This Finlay is the author of several pieces in this miscellany. He is apparently the Finlay MacNab to whom another composition is attributed, and is called in one place <note>original Gaelic: "Am bard maith"</note> *The*

<column-break/>

*good poet.* He seems to have been the family bard of MacGregor, the praise of whose horse he here proclaims in most poetic strains.

³ Duseivlin was one of the famous steeds of the Feinn <note>The Feinn, or Fenians, were a legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill in Gaelic mythology.</note>.

⁴ The beast of Lamacha, <note>original: "Aidhre an Lamacha"</note> is entirely unknown to the Editor. The animal is called <note>original: "Beisd"</note> *a beast* (or monster), in a subsequent part of the poem. It must have been some animal famous in the country for its ugliness.

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

H

Just like the wheeling of the mountain winds,
Is the action of the prancing steed.
Hundreds admire her paces,
Like one in frenzy passing.
Like the point of an arrow this horse,
Famous are all her doings.
Bands of the great witness her course,
As with speed she rushes.
Though far before her stands the groom,
No blunderer is her rider.
Few are the words would tell her praise,
Like birds on wing her movements.
Her triumphs and paces the same,
Whether 'mong rocks or bogs she moves.
Before that horse all men do fear,
When she comes in the trappings of war.
In the troop, the hunt, or the conflict,
That horse a noble horse is.
That horse is all full of spirit,
As fameworthy she follows the banner.
That wave-like steed, hardy and keen,
Will win for her rider the praise of men.
Forth from her stall she takes the lead,
That gentle, great, and active horse.
She will triumph in speed and slaughter,
Till that the day in evening sinks.
Ready to treasure the <term>girdle of gold</term>,¹
The field with violence shakes.
Startling, rounded, bright, well shod,
Gentle, broad-backed, coloured well.
A horse of such great fame as this,
I long had heard that they possessed.
Where was ever found her match,
Not he, the beast of <term>Lamacha</term> <note>A reference to the divine horses of Laomedon, King of Troy, famous in medieval Gaelic adaptations of classical mythology.</note>.
Mac Gregor 's the master of that horse,

### Finlay the red-haired bard.

I am a stranger long to success,
’Tis time that I should have it.
’Tis time now to desist,
From satire justly due.
The way that I shall take,
To seek a noble branch,

Where are no worthless guests.

When summer time comes round,
Peace he never knows,
He’s in the throat of all his fellows.

As Gregor of the blows,

Flocks and herds before him.

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE

The king at <term>lifting cattle</term> <note>original: "lifting cattle"; referring to the traditional Gaelic practice of the cattle raid (creach), which was considered a legitimate and noble form of warfare and a test of a young chief's ability to provide for his people.</note>,
I myself will sing,
Mouth to mouth at daybreak.
When his sharp-armed men see,
Mac Gregor at the <term>Bealach</term> <note>original: "Bealach"; meaning a pass or passage. Historically, this refers to Taymouth, once the seat of the MacGregor clan before they were displaced by the Campbells.</note>,
His way so gently soft,
No weight to them their burdens.
Then when war arises,
Proclaimed in enemies' hearts,
It is to him they'd gather,
Clothed in martial dress.
'T is of Mac Gregor's fame,
When fighting's left behind.
To men not to be cruel,
His castle full of mirth;
When victory I had left
Upon the field of war,
When of the fight I spoke,
Nought loved my patron more.
Though sad, on the stormy lake,
To tell men of my grief,
To have a crew of mariners,
Is best in battle's day.
Remember I'll be with thee,
Mac Gregor without stain,
In face of any foe,
Long, long's the time.
Gentle Elizabeth,
Change thou my state;
Woman of softest locks,
And of the loftiest brow.

<meta>This page continues the satirical elegy for a character named Lachlan, whose death has supposedly left the "profession" of begging in a state of crisis. The text is attributed to Sir Duncan Campbell of Glenorchy, a prominent 15th-century knight and poet. The poem uses humor and irony, listing the absurd and trivial items Lachlan would beg for to mock his persistent nature.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE

Who will beg soles for his shoes?
Who will ask for a peacock's feather?
Who will beg an eye for his belt?
Who will mix in any mischief?
Who will beg an old felt hat?
Who will beg a book to read?
Who will beg an early meal?
Who is it that wears weapons with his dress?
Who will beg for boots and spurs?
Who is it will beg for bristles?
Who will beg for <term>sids</term> <note>The inner husks of oats, often used to make a simple porridge called sowans.</note> and meal?
Who will beg a sheaf of rye?
Who will ask for a <term>sporran</term> <note>A traditional Gaelic pouch or purse worn at the waist.</note> spoon?
Who will gather without shame?
Since Lachlan the hero is dead.
Who will now afford us sport?
Who will beg for maidens' shifts,
Since old-shoed Lachlan is dead?
Sad is the fate that he should die;
Who will ask men for a <term>rullion</term> <note>A crude, homemade shoe made of untanned hide with the hair still on it.</note>?
Who will steal the servant's feather,
And who is it that cannot tell the truth?
Who likes to travel in a boat,
And likes to visit his old friends?
Who will beg the hen with her eggs?
Who will beg a brood of chickens?
Who will ask for the hen’s surplus,
Even after receiving a handful of money?
Who will beg for a headless pin?
Who can read people as he could do?
That Lachlan should leave no heir,
Is what makes his death so mournful.
Who will beg for a hook and line?
Who will seek for open doors?
Who will beg for unboiled <term>rennet</term> <note>A substance used to curdle milk for cheesemaking; begging for it "unboiled" suggests a desperate or odd request.</note>?
Who will beg for anything at all?
Who won't give a penny to the poor?

And yet does he even beg from the naked?
Who would oppress a mere child,
And show such cruelty to the infant?
Who would beg for wool and butter,
So they might have it—following after Lachlan?
Who would beg for a woman’s collar?
Who is it that loves a filthy heap?
Who would beg from young women,
Or from little dogs and weasels?
Who would take the fire away from an infant?
Who would steal even from the dead?
Who pretends to be sick when he is well?
Who asks for butter to put on his gruel?
More sorrowful to me than this man’s death,
Is the fact that he has left no heir.
For fear that the trade of beggary should die out,
With no one found to keep it going.
Do not forget this man,
You men of the earth, do not forget;
Each of you, compose your own rhymes for him,

If Lachlan died this past Monday,
Every man will be full of joy.
It is a pity that for his death,
There is no one left to truly mourn.

<meta>This page continues a sequence of bardic poetry from the "Book of the Dean of Lismore." It completes the praise-poem by Duncan M'Cabe addressed to a leader of the MacDougall clan, emphasizing his military might and his rightful lordship over the lands of Lorn.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE</header>

It is true that you are indeed
The man to take the richest spoils.
Like a bull that is fierce for the fight,
It is thus you go forth to make war.
It is you who traverses **Cruachan**,¹ <note>Ben Cruachan is the iconic mountain overlooking the MacDougall heartland in Argyll.</note>
Casting your spear beneath its hills.
Your fame is like that of the leopard,
You are Duncan of **Durinis**. <note>Likely referring to a specific MacDougall holding or residence.</note>

You quickly subdue your enemies,
You stain both your hands with blood.
You cheer us when we are weary,
You are the source of all our joy.
He is the man who is easiest
To praise in a song like mine,
Which I compose among heroes—
The generous dragon of **Connal**.² <note>The "dragon" is a traditional Gaelic metaphor for a fierce protector and warrior-chief.</note>

Another kind of fame belongs to him:
The skill he shows with his gun. <note>This is a fascinating early reference to firearms in Highland poetry, showing the transition from medieval to early modern warfare.</note>
The bravery and skill of <term>Erin</term> <note>A poetic name for Ireland, often used in Gaelic poetry to denote high culture or martial excellence.</note>
Is bound firmly in all his blows.
Whatever skill a king’s son possesses,
He has it as well, without any defect.
The purest eloquence has been given to him;
This will be found in your son.

Now I see you raising the tax,
Truly from every homestead—
A noble king of bravest deeds,
Descendant of that warlike race.

->Macdougall.<-

¹ <margin>"Ben Cruachan," the highest mountain in Lorn, the ancient territory of the clan Dougall.</margin>

² <margin>Connal Ferry, the remarkable narrow <column-break/> at the mouth of Loch Etive, where the stream at times becomes a salt-water cascade. The name "Connal" (<note>original: "Con-thuil"</note>) means a roaring stream.</margin>

<meta>This page continues the mourning poem (elegy) for John MacDougall of Dunollie, which began on page 121. The author, John MacEacharn, continues to describe the profound communal grief felt by the clan and the landscape itself following the death of their leader.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

Before men recounted the news of you,
It is a pity they had not first lost their hearing.
The heavy weight of my agonizing grief
Has almost taken my eyesight away;
This is no small or weak mourning for John,
Yet tears for him I find I cannot shed; <note>The speaker suggests their grief is so deep it has moved beyond the point of simple tears into a state of shock or blindness.</note>
Mourning for our buried prince,
The death of MacDougall of Dunolly.
His was the noble form of <term>Conn's great race</term>, <note>Conn of the Hundred Battles (Conn Cétchathach) was a legendary High King of Ireland. Many Highland clans, including the MacDougalls, claimed descent from him to establish their ancient and royal legitimacy.</note>
Like a nut kernel, fair and rounded;
His death has been a grievous breach in our wall,
The very waves sing his lament.
Above the beach,¹ since John has died,
There is no ceasing of the people's sorrow;
Men do not speak even now of any joy,
Since this grief has taken hold of them;
A bitter sorrow has filled them all,
No word of sport is heard, and no music;
That path is now called the "sacred way,"
The one that leads from the beach to the grave.
So do the men thus mourn their loss,
And the women too, who loved you well.
Shall I receive honor from your soft locks?
In place of honor, I have only ruin.
I mourn as I stand here upon your grave,
All that I see causes me to lament;
The women will not leave your graveside,
So truly heavy is their sorrow.
Raise up a tomb for our fair prince,
Let it be as wide as <term>Cruachan's cell</term>, <note>original: "Cruachan's cell". This likely refers to the chapel or the sacred burial grounds at Ardchattan, situated near the massive mountain Ben Cruachan.</note>
So that men may see by the work of their hands,
How heavily their grief weighs upon them.
The clan mourns you with constant weeping,
Their souls are sad, and they cannot sleep.
The race of Dougall, before your death,

<note>¹ The MacDougalls of Dunolly were traditionally buried at the old Priory of Ardchattan, located on the banks of Loch Etive.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a formal lament for John MacDougall of Dunollie, emphasizing the communal nature of Gaelic mourning where the landscape, the music, and the people all reflect the loss of their chief. It highlights the burial traditions of the clan at Ardchattan Priory.</summary>
