--- Page 239 ---
<meta>This page continues the elegy for John MacDougall of Dunollie, following the expressions of profound grief from the clan members and the landscape itself on page 122. The poet continues to describe the atmosphere of mourning surrounding the Chieftain's death.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE

He never feared the face of a foe.
Chieftain, your death has fallen upon them;
It would be no boast for another to rule them now. <note>The poet suggests that the clan is so diminished by grief and the loss of their leader that ruling them would no longer be a point of pride for a conqueror.</note>
Many are the women at John's grave,
Pouring out their tears from day to day;
Bands of women even by night,
With bare heads gathering on the plain;
It is no wonder that they should mourn,
Because of John of brightest fame.
No day can pass but hearts are full
Of this sad tale that awakens our mourning.
I care not though it be thus with them,
Though they should feel what I do not like;
Your death is hard for us to bear,
Sore is the state to which it brings us.
I stand amidst the gloom of death,
No word is heard of our usual song;
My heart is truly rent in two
As we speak of his departure.
Never were we in such a state before,
Where it brings only grief to ask, "How is he?" <note>A poignant observation that the common greeting or inquiry after one's health has become a source of pain because the subject is deceased.</note>
It is a cause of sorrow that he is absent;
I mourn that he is no longer with us.
Now that he sleeps in his cold grave,
The feelings men describe are melancholy;
They cannot cease from shedding tears,
Castle and cottage are both in sorrow.
The rising tide has swept over the hills—
So do his comrades mourn for John;
Yet there is no heaving of the sea,
Not even of the boisterous sea at Connal;¹
For you are mourned, great <term>chief of Conn</term>, <note>Conn refers to Conn of the Hundred Battles, a legendary High King of Ireland from whom the MacDougalls and other noble Gaelic clans claimed descent to establish their royal pedigree.</note>
In all the borders of <term>Clan Dougall</term>; <note>The MacDougalls were a powerful Highland clan whose seat was at Dunollie Castle near Oban.</note>
The land itself seems to weep for you,
Loud is the cry, with much distress,
From the musicians of Dougall's race.

<note>¹ Connal refers to the Connal Ferry on Loch Etive, known for the "Falls of Lora," a dramatic tidal race that creates loud, turbulent water.</note>

<summary>This page concludes or continues a funeral elegy for John MacDougall of Dunollie, focusing on the visual of women keeping vigil at his grave and the widespread grief reaching from the coast of Loch Etive across all MacDougall lands.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the elegy for the MacDougall chieftains of Dunollie, specifically mourning the deaths of John, his father John, and Alexander. It follows the previous page's description of a clan in deep mourning and the natural world reflecting their loss.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

The learned men and the leading bards are in mourning.
Over John’s grave lies a heavy stone;
It brings me grief to tell this story.
Quite differently than Neil <note>The identity of "Neil" is uncertain; he may be a rival or a member of the MacNeil clan whose intentions toward the MacDougalls were hostile.</note> would wish,
Does every scandal now appear;
Have they no concern to visit his grave,
Now that John has triumphed over them? <note>The poet suggests that John's legacy or his peaceful rest has defeated the ill-intent of his enemies.</note>
The descendants of Conn <term>Conn</term> <note>Referring to Conn of the Hundred Battles, a legendary High King of Ireland and ancestor of the MacDougalls.</note> are now few in number,
Since death has taken away MacDougall.
There is no pleasure in the sounds of the violin,
Nor in the writing of poetry without him;
The art of poetry brings no honor now,
Since death has seized the son of Mary. <note>Likely referring to the Chieftain's mother.</note>
Few are the voices that can now describe
How great her privilege and standing were,
Now that both the leaders of Clan Dougall
Have been laid upon their backs in death.
I do not find that great power John once held,
So soon after the loss of his father, John.
May God preserve your noble spirit,
You who learned wisdom from your teacher in Islay; <note>Islay was a significant center of administration and learning under the Lordship of the Isles.</note>
Horses cannot guarantee a triumph;
Men must eventually leave them and depart.
After the loss of these three, our grief is great;
In truth, my heart can find no comfort.
It is mournful to see such loss in one's youth;
Death has seized two Johns and one Alexander. <note>This identifies the three principal losses: John MacDougall, his father John, and Alexander MacDougall.</note>

> Alexander, whom no restraint could bind,
> That I of him should also tell;
> Thy breast was stout to the rushing wave,
> Thy body now! alas my sorrow;
> Never do ye seek again
> That John's young heir should go to battle;

->The author of this is Finlay M'Nab.<- <note>Finlay M'Nab (Fionnlagh Mac an Aba) was a 16th-century Scottish Gaelic poet and chief of the Clan MacNab. He is known for his sharp wit and satiric style.</note>

> The sluggard's Book of Poems,¹
> If it were your wish to write in it,
> Among what they have left you'll find
> Enough wherewith to fill it.
> Though many the men there be,
> Who cruelly the people oppress,
> Never will these be found,
> Honoured in famous songs.
> Of all the fruits of sluggards,
> Though there be of them a thousand,
> The house in which these do meet,
> They ne'er can by any means reach.
> They are both gentle and simple,
> Dressed in their Sunday coats;
> And yet of all their productions,
> It happens we never can hear.
> I won't their genealogy tell,
> Of their history nothing I know,

<margin>The "rule of the stranger" likely refers to the increasing influence of the Scottish crown or Lowland Stewarts over the traditional Highland territories.</margin>
Probably the two Johns and Alexander of our bard. This holding of the Stewarts may explain the bard's reference to the rule of the stranger. <note>original: "Gall," usually meaning a foreigner or a Lowlander.</note>

¹ The word <term>duanaire</term> <note>original: "duanaire," a Gaelic term for a song-book or a poetic anthology.</note>, here used in the original, means "a miscellany of poetical compositions." The Dean's manuscript is a "duanaire," but not a "duanaire

<column-break/>

of the stranger," <note>original: "nan strangair." This is a play on words suggesting that while the Dean (James MacGregor) compiled a great book, he was no "sluggard" or "stranger" to the hard work of preservation.</note> for the Dean seems to have been a most industrious compiler. This piece of M'Nab's is a satire on lazy composers or compilers. It is valuable as showing that the ancient bards wrote their compositions. The number of such books must have been large, although during the course of centuries they seem to have perished with few exceptions.

But that they are out at evening,
Followed close by their hounds.
Dugall, thou art their fellow,
John's son of the polished blade,
In whom flows the sluggard's blood,
Write thou in the Book of Poems. <note>The "Book of Poems" likely refers to the very manuscript the reader is holding—The Book of the Dean of Lismore—suggesting the poet is urging the scribe to record the deeds of contemporaries.</note>
Write knowingly, intelligently,
Write their history and their life;
Don't bring a poem on the earth,

Of priests or of tenantry;

Thy death has filled me with grief,
The hand round which I lived so long,

And that I saw it not depart;

Well was it known in every land.

<meta>This page continues the elegy by Eafric M’Corqudale, mourning the death of her husband, the MacNeil of Castle Sween. The previous page established her personal grief; here, she expands the lament to show the loss felt by the entire community and the nobility of the MacNeil lineage.</meta>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Lion of Mull, with its white towers,
Hawk of Islay, with its smooth plains,
Shrewdest of all the men we knew,
Whom guest ne'er left without a gift.
Prince of good men, gentle, kind,
Whose mien was that of a king's son,
Guests came to thee from Dunanoir,¹
Guests from the Boyne² for lordly gifts.
Truth it is they often came,
Not oftener than gave thee joy.
Shapely falcon of Sliabh Gael,³
Protection to the bards thou gav'st,
Dragon of Lewis of sandy slopes,
Glad as the whisper of a stream ;
The loss of but a single man
Has left me lonely, now he's gone.
No sport, no pleasing song,
No joy, nor pleasure in the feast ;
No man whom I can now love,
Of Niall's race down from Niall the Younger; <note>original: "Nial òg". 'Óg' is the Gaelic word for young or junior, often used to distinguish a son from a father of the same name.</note>
Among our women there's no joy,
Our men no pleasure have in sport,
Just like the winds when it is calm,
So without music is Dun Sween.⁵
See the palace of a generous race,
Vengeance is taken on Clan Neil,
The cause of many a boastful song,
And will till they lay us in the grave ;

---

¹ Dunanoir <note>Gaelic: *Dún an Óir*, meaning 'Fort of the Gold'</note> was a castle on the island of Cape Clear, on the south-west coast of Ireland. See *Miscellany of the Celtic Society* p. 143.
² The river Boyne. From Dunanoir to the Boyne included all Ireland. <note>By mentioning landmarks at the extreme ends of Ireland, the poet emphasizes that the Chief's reputation for generosity reached across the entire Gaelic world.</note>
³ Sliabh Ghaidheael <note>Gaelic: 'Mountain of the Gaels'</note>, a range of hills in Kintyre.
⁴ In Innes' *Original Parishes* <note>original: "Orig. Par."</note> vol. ii. pt. i. p. 41, we find that Hector MacTorquil Mac Neil was constable of Castle Sween in 1472. In 1481 the office and lands were
<column-break/>
conferred on Colin Earl of Argyle. Between these two periods Mac Neil would appear to have died, leaving no heir in the direct line.
⁵ Castle Sween is an ancient stronghold at the mouth of Loch Sween in Knapdale, said to take its name from Sween of Argyle, who flourished in the thirteenth century; but the Irish annalists make mention of it at a much earlier period. It was probably a stronghold of the Dalriadic monarchs. <note>The Dalriadic monarchs ruled Dál Riata, a kingdom encompassing parts of western Scotland and northeastern Ireland from the 6th to 9th centuries.</note>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

And now 'tis hard to bear, alas!
That we should lose on every side.
Didst thou, son of Adam, crush
Any cluster of three nuts, <note>A traditional Gaelic metaphor for dividing shares; the most precious part is reserved for the most beloved.</note>
It is to him thou lovest most
The largest third of them thou 'dst give.
Thus of their husk the topmost nut,
Does to clan Neil, ungrudged, belong.
The bountiful have often poured
Their gifts on the dwelling of clan Neil.
The prince, who was the last of all,
Is he who me with gloom has filled.
In half my purpose I have failed,
Jewel, who has roused my grief.
Broken my heart is in my breast,
And so 't will be until I die;
Left by that black and noble eyelid,
Jewel, who hast roused my grief.
Mary, mother, foster-mother of the king,
Protect thou me from every shaft;
And thou, her Son, who all things mad'st.
Jewel, who hast roused my grief.
Jewel. <note>The poem ends by repeating the opening word, a traditional Gaelic poetic device called 'dúnadh' or 'closing'.</note>

'T were long to sing his race's glory;
Of this there is no doubt 'mong men,
That he is the first of the race of kings.

<meta>This page continues the poem by Dougall Mac Gille glas in praise of John MacGregor of Glenstray (died 1519), chief of the Clan Gregor. The previous page established John’s royal lineage and his reputation for bravery.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.
->I<-

He is the boldest chief in any land;
Between his gold and the <term>Saxons'</term> spoil, <note>In Gaelic poetry, "Saxon" (*Sassunnach*) refers to Lowland Scots or the English, often portrayed as the traditional rivals of the Highland Gaels.</note>
Well may he live in ease and peace.
Choice for courage of the Grecian Gael, <note>A reference to the legendary origin of the Gaels, who claimed descent from Fenius Farsaid and Scota, tracing their lineage back through Greece and Egypt.</note>
Whose meed of praise shall ne'er decay,
Abounding in charity and love,
Known in the lands of the race of kings.¹
White-toothed falcon of the three glens,²
With whom we read the bravest deeds,
The boldest arm 'midst fight of clans,
Best of the chiefs from the race of kings.
When on <gloss>Son of Patrick</gloss> <insert>Mac Phadrick</insert> of ruddy cheeks,
Wrath in battle's hour awaked,
The men who with him share the fight
Are never safe amidst its blows.
Grandson to Malcolm of bright eyes,
Whom none could leave but felt their loss,
The generous, gentle, shapely youth,
The readiest hand when aught's to do.
The race of Gregor stand round John,
Not as a weak one is their blow;
The famous race without a fault,
Round him like a fence they stand.
Clan Gregor who show no fear,
Even when with the king they strive,
Though brave Gael may be the foe,
That they count of little weight.
Gael or Saxon are the same,
To these brave men of kingly race,
Sons of Gregor bold in fight,
Bend not before the fiercest foe.

To Gregor of golden bridles, heir,
Pity the men whom you may spoil,
Worse for them who you pursue.
Chief of Glen Lyon of the blades, <note>Glen Lyon is a long, dramatic glen in the Perthshire Highlands, historically a stronghold of the MacGregors.</note>
Shield and benefactor of the Church,
His arm like **Oscar’s** in the fight, <note>Oscar was the son of Oisín and grandson of Fionn mac Cumhaill, known as the most formidable warrior of the legendary Fianna.</note>
To whom in all things he is like.
Kindness mantles on his red cheek,
Thy praise he justly wins, ungrudged ;
Benevolence when to men he shows,
Horses and gold he freely gives.
Mac Gregor of the noble race,
No wonder though bards should fill thy court ;
To his white breast there is no match,
But he so famous 'mong the **Feinn**. <note>The Feinn (or Fianna) were a legendary band of warrior-hunters in Gaelic mythology.</note>
Three fair watches him surround,
Never as captives were his men ;
His arm in battle’s struggle strong,
Well did he love to hunt the deer.
In mien and manners he was like
The king who ruled amongst the **Feinn**. <note>This refers to Fionn mac Cumhaill, the leader of the Fianna.</note>
Mac Gregor of the spoils, his fortune such
That choicest men do covet it.
Good and gentle is his blue eye,
He’s like **Mac Cumhail** of liberal horn, <note>Mac Cumhail refers to Fionn mac Cumhaill. The "liberal horn" refers to his reputation for hospitality and sharing wealth at the feast.</note>

Like in wooing or in hunt,

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

Who go to make up my fame.
I am of the blood of Clan Dougall, <note>The MacDougalls were once the powerful Lords of Lorne.</note>
A race of unquestioned right;
But brave and bold though they be,
It is not from them alone that I have my all.
My kinsman is <term>Mac Chailein</term>, <note>original: "Mac Chailein"; This is the traditional Gaelic title for the Chief of Clan Campbell, the Earl of Argyll.</note>
Who freely gives gold to the bards;
Why should I be sorrowful,
My native place is in Earla.^1
My native place is in Earla,
Clan Donald lie off to the west;
My dwelling is with <term>Clan Gillean</term>, <note>original: "Clan Gillean"; The MacLeans, who were dominant on the Isle of Mull.</note>
The men who in battle can fight.
MacPhee of Colonsay,
No stranger is he to my race,
And MacNeil of Barra,
Of pure and gentle descent.
MacNee I also remember,
And also the powerful MacSween,
Clan Leod and Clan Ranald,
Chiefs from whom I descend.
Cattanachs and MacIntoshes,
They too are among my friends;
The Camerons and Clan Gregor,
The men from Breadalbane who come.
Stewarts, though widely they be
Scattered throughout the whole earth,
Old, certain, and swift-footed is the tale,
That of them was my father’s grandmother.
In Balquhidder and Breadalbane,
My friends are numerous found;
Kindly men in bringing aid,
These are my kindred true.
Clan Lauchlan and Clan Lamont,
Clan Neil, who learn feats of war;

->Fond.<- <note>This word marks the "dúnadh," a traditional Gaelic poetic device where the poem ends with the same word or syllable it began with (in this case, "Friends/Fonn").</note>

## No Author.⁴

For the race of <term>Gael</term>, <note>The Gaelic people of Scotland and Ireland.</note> from the land of Greece <note>Gaelic origin myths, specifically the Lebor Gabála Érenn, trace the lineage of the Gaels from Scythia and Greece through Egypt and Spain.</note>

Have your swords with sharpened blades,

<margin>¹ The three sons of <term>Milidh</term> <note>Known in English as Milesius, the mythical ancestor of the Gaels.</note> of Spain, from whom the Milesian races are descended, according to Celtic story.</margin>
<margin>² The Editor can give no account of these names. The traditions respecting them seem to have perished.</margin>
<margin>³ A famous Celtic hero, from whom Ben Lomond and Loch Lomond are said to derive their names.</margin>
<margin>⁴ There is a portion of the beginning of this ode wanting, and we have no means of knowing who the poet was. A reference to the history of the time, and the names introduced into the poem, however, suggest very emphati-</margin>
<column-break/>
<margin>cally the occasion of it. It is addressed to Archibald, Earl of Argyle, Chancellor of Scotland, who was killed at the battle of Flodden in 1513. The Saxons assailed so virulently by the poet are, in all likelihood, not the Lowland Scotch but the English, and the poem is probably a <term>“Brosnachadh catha,”</term> <note>An "incitement to battle," a traditional Gaelic poetic form used to encourage warriors before a conflict.</note> or incitement to the rising of the Scotch, and particularly the Argyle men, previous to the disastrous battle of Flodden. This invests the fragment with peculiar historical interest. There seems not to be much of the poem wanting, probably only a few lines.</margin>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

<columns>2</columns>

Let your spears stand by your sides,
Let us not forsake our country,
Let us fiercely, bravely fight.
It is said by the <term>Gael</term> <note>The Gaelic-speaking people of Ireland and Scotland.</note> of <note>original: "Banva"; A poetic name for Ireland.</note> Ireland,¹
Our fathers did the tale repeat,
And I have heard there was a time,
Long ago, that <note>original: "Innis Aingin"; An island in the Shannon, famous as the retreat of St. Ciaran, here representing Ireland.</note> Innis Aingin²
Was ruled by the <term>Fomorian</term>³ race,
Who raised from it a heavy tax.
Thus for a while the <term>Saxons</term> <note>In this context, the English.</note> have
Our country burdened heavily;
And now each clan is full of fear,
And we are plunged in grievous doubt.
But now that a gathering's begun,
There's need that chiefs should rouse them up;
For with them, 'tis my opinion,
We will share a common fate.
Who is the man, whom we can tell,
Will from the English save the Gaelic race?
Who in our day has won much fame,
And whose house is truly noble?
Know a man, were he but willing,
Of whom we readily might tell,
His power in Ireland widely known,
Men all bound with him to gather.
Archibald of the pointed head,
Of thee it is that men now speak.
Earl of Argyle,⁴ I thee beseech,
Be as a hero in the conflict;

---

¹ Ireland.
² There was an Innis Aingin, an island in the Shannon, famous as being the place to which St. Ciaran retired to spend the latter part of his life. It is here apparently taken to represent a portion of Ireland. See Mr. O'Curry's Lectures, p. 58.
³ An early race of pirates <note>Commonly known as the Fomorians in mythology.</note>, said to have infested the Irish coasts. The word is 
<column-break/>
said to be derived from <note>original: "fo mhuir"</note> "under the sea," and is supposed to indicate their coming from the low sea coasts of Holland or Denmark.
⁴ Archibald, Earl of Argyle, Chancellor of Scotland, who was killed at the Battle of Flodden in 1513. He is called <note>original: "Iarla Oirthir Ghaidheal"</note> "Earl of the Coastlands of the Gael," which would seem to be the true origin of the name "Argyle."

</columns>

->Remember well your ancestral line,<-
->Down from Alpin, the heir of Dougal.<-
->Twenty and one generations, besides yourself,<-

---

^1 This is clearly a mistake for Kenneth. The person intended is Kenneth MacAlpin, King of Scotland. In the genealogy found on page 144 of the manuscript, this Kennan is identified as the High King of Scotland, to distinguish him from the lesser chiefs whom the Gaels also called kings.

<column-break/>

^2 Alpin, King of the Scots, who flourished in the beginning of the ninth century. Several generations in the MacGregor genealogy appear to be missing from this poem. Even the name of Gregor, from whom the clan takes its name, is sometimes omitted or misplaced in various versions of the lineage.

<summary>This page provides a rhythmic genealogy of the MacGregor clan, tracing the lineage of John MacPatrick back twenty-one generations to the Scottish King Kenneth MacAlpin. It serves to legitimate the clan's status by linking their "blood of noble kings" to the very foundations of the Scottish monarchy.</summary>

<keywords>Clan Gregor, MacGregor genealogy, Kenneth MacAlpin, John MacPatrick, Glenorchy, Scottish kings, Highland lineage, Alpin</keywords>

--- Page 255 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy of John MacPatrick of the Clan Gregor. The previous page traced his immediate ancestors back to King Alpin; this page extends that lineage further back to the legendary King Fergus M‘Erc and links the family to iconic figures of British and Irish mythology, including King Arthur and Conn of the Hundred Battles.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

John the Black, not black in heart.
Your genealogy leads us truly
To Fergus, son of Erc, the prosperous.
Of your race, which does not vanish like foam,
Six generations wore the crown.
There were forty-three kings in total,
Their blood and origin are well known.
Three reigned in the north and three in the south,¹
After the time of Malcolm Canmore <note>Malcolm III (r. 1058–1093), a pivotal King of Scotland.</note>.
Ten of the race did wear the crown,
From the time of Malcolm back to Alpin.
From Alpin moving upward we find
Fourteen kings until we reach Fergus.
Such is your genealogy
To Fergus,² son of Erc the prosperous.
How many ancestors of your race,
Must there have been from you back to Fergus!
Noble lineages mingle with your blood,
In numbers we cannot even calculate today.
The bardic schools³ would weary of our tale,
Numbering the kings from whom you are sprung.
The blood of Arthur⁴ is in your heart,
Precious is the life that fills your veins;
The blood of Cuan, the blood of Conn,⁵
Two wise men, the glory of the race.
The blood of Grant is in your apple-red cheek,
The blood of Niall, the fierce and mighty.
Fierce and gentle, at all times,
Is the story of the royal race.
->The history.<-

---

¹ Both sides of Loch Tay, the ancient MacGregor territory, are still called “Tuaruith” and “Deasruith,” meaning the *north and south sides.*
² The first king of the Dalriadic Scots <note>The Gaelic kingdom of Dál Riata, located in modern western Scotland.</note>.
³ The schools of the bards, which were numerous in Scotland and Ireland at this period—though chiefly in Ireland, as may be seen from this collection, for 

<column-break/>

most of the poets were undoubtedly trained there. Poetry and genealogy were the chief branches of study.
⁴ Arthur, King of the Strathclyde Britons, from whom the Campbell family is also said to be descended.
⁵ “Conn of the hundred battles” <note>original: “Conn ceud catha”</note>, a legendary High King of Ireland.

<summary>This page completes the formal genealogy of John MacGregor, tracing the clan's royal roots back to Fergus M‘Erc and asserting connections to King Arthur and High King Conn. It highlights the importance of bardic schools in preserving these complex oral and written histories.</summary>

->Son of Janet, of a royal lineage,<-

->All my sadness would vanish.<-

<sup>1</sup> This name is very faint in the manuscript and cannot be identified with absolute certainty.

<sup>2</sup> In 1480, William Macleod of Dunvegan was killed at the Battle of the Bloody Bay <note>A naval battle fought near Tobermory between John MacDonald of Islay and his son Angus Og MacDonald.</note>, and was succeeded by his son Alexander, usually called "Alastair Crotach," or the hump-backed <note>original: "Alastair Crotach"</note> (Gregory's *History of the Western Highlands and Isles of Scotland*, page 74). Historical records provide no information

<column-break/>

regarding a John who was a son of this William Macleod. However, William’s father was named John, whose own father was also likely a William; this John, who flourished in the early fifteenth century, may be the individual described here.

<sup>3</sup> This is an ancient name for the Isle of Skye <note>original: "Clar Sgith"</note>, and its use here casts some doubt on the commonly accepted origin of the name "Sgiathanach" <note>The "Winged Isle," often used to describe the shape of Skye.</note>.

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

Of Finn <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill, the legendary hunter-warrior of Gaelic mythology.</note> of spears and sharp sword,
Cumhal’s son of famous deeds:
That of Erin <note>Ireland</note> the hunting and lordship
Belonged to Mac Cumhal of long locks,
Patrimony and lordship he had not
Over the lands of the race of Gaul. <note>Goll mac Morna, leader of a rival faction within the Fianna who often contested Finn's authority.</note>
Forest right they had all his life,
From Kerry north to Carn Valair.
But he possessed the old rights
Which previously were his.
From Hallowmas <note>The festival of Samhain (November 1st), marking the beginning of winter when the Fianna traditionally took up quarters in the homes of the people.</note> on to Beltin <note>The festival of Bealtaine (May 1st), marking the start of summer when the Fianna returned to the wild to hunt and live off the land.</note>,
His Feinn <note>The Fianna; Finn’s legendary band of landless warriors.</note> had all the rights.
The hunting without molestation,
Was theirs in all the forests.
Many the tributes I cannot tell,
Belonged to Finn and his men.
Tribute in Erin possessed
By Mac Cumhail from the forests.
A noble’s forest right to the Feinn,
On the banks of every stream.
But Malcom’s <note>Referring to Maol-Coluim MacGregor, the chief mentioned on the previous page.</note> large tributes
Did not belong to Mac Muirn <note>A name for Finn, meaning "Son of Muirne" (his mother’s name). The original text notes: *This is usually the name of Gaul, but here it is Finn.*</note>;
Finn himself would never hunt
Without first asking leave.
The hunting of Scotland, without leave,
Belongs, with its spoil, to Malcom.
Constant in the hunt together
Are M‘Gregor and his fierce men;
No oftener did the blood-red hounds
Enter the fort of clan Boisgne. <note>Finn’s paternal ancestral clan.</note>
A fighting band of chieftains
Arose with him in battle’s day,
Men whose dress sparkled with gold,
Men who conquered in the fight.
The head of clans and of huntsmen

<summary>This page features a poem comparing the hunting rights and lordship of the legendary Irish hero Fionn mac Cumhaill (Finn) with those of the MacGregor chiefs. It emphasizes that while Finn was a great hero of the past, the current MacGregor lords hold even greater territorial authority in Scotland.</summary>

Is the common fame of his race.
No trial of bravery or skill
Will show weakness in M'Gregor. <note>The clan MacGregor, often called the "Children of the Mist," claimed royal descent and were known for their martial prowess in the Scottish Highlands.</note>
Many in his halls are found together,
Men who carried well-sharped swords,
Red gold glittered on their hilts,

Men with dice-boxes in their hands,

--- Page 260 ---
<meta>This page continues the satirical work of Finlay, the red-haired bard, shifting focus from the praises of the MacGregors to a scathing condemnation of Allan MacRuari, a powerful Highland chief known for his violence and raids on sacred sites.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE

The story is a simple one to tell,
Of one armed with the devil’s poisonous spear.
But he was surely, firmly bound,
Before leaving the dark house of hell,
To that same stronghold to return,
And leave behind the Star of Paradise. <note>A poetic reference to Heaven or the state of grace.</note>
Then, when the black-skinned boar <note>A derogatory nickname for Allan MacRuari, suggesting both ferocity and filth.</note> arrived,
Many devils followed in his train,
Each of them making a horrible sound,
Their voices all raised in one loud cry.
So that he would not be left with nothing,
It was decreed by MacRuari,¹
As a covenant firmly fixed,
That he would live as a dog in hell.
The claim is righteous and just
Which Allan now holds against the devils.
Whatever share of hell they own,
He, I think, should have much of the "glory." <note>The poet uses "glory" ironically here to mean infamy or a high rank among the damned.</note>
It is time now to turn away from that crowd
Of cruel hearts and horrible sounds.
MacRuari, though far from the ocean,
You have gained wealth without any effort.
It is a report we cannot ignore,
For I must answer to Columba, <note>Saint Columba (Colm Cille), the 6th-century founding father of the monastery on Iona. The poet suggests he is writing on behalf of the saint’s legacy.</note>
It is a report that fills the whole land,
Bald-headed Allan, you who are so faithless,
That you have committed more than one crime,
You have plundered *Iona*² and the *Cemetery of Oran*.³
Fiercely did you then destroy
The priests’ vestments and the vessels for the Mass.

---

<columns>2</columns>
¹ We learn from *Gregory's Highlands* <note>original: "Greg. High." referring to Donald Gregory’s 1836 history of the Western Highlands.</note> pp. 65, 66, that Allan MacRuari, great-grandson of Ranald and chief of Clanranald, was one of the primary supporters of Angus, the young Lord of the Isles, at the Battle of the Bloody Bay <note>A significant naval battle fought near Mull c. 1480.</note>; he also followed Alexander of Lochalsh in his invasion of Ross and Cromarty in 1491,

<column-break/>

receiving a large share of the plunder taken on that occasion. The poet describes him as a sacrilegious marauder <note>Someone who robs or destroys sacred places.</note>.
² The religious community at *Iona* <note>original: "I"</note>. The Protestant Reformers likely had less to do with the destruction of the buildings on *Iona* than is commonly believed; much was done earlier by local raiders.
³ The church and burial ground of *Saint Oran* <note>original: "Relig Oran"</note> on the island of *Iona*.

<meta>This page continues the satirical poem by Finlay the Red-haired Bard, transitioning from the desecration of Iona to a direct personal condemnation of Allan MacRuari's character and his violent history in the mainland Glens.</meta>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

You are the great curse of the Hebrides,¹
You plunder her wealth and her strongholds;
You are the man whose heart is the most wicked
Of all those who have followed your chief,
Except for the one who stands at his left hand—
And he, MacRuari, is your own brother.
Now we never hear of your bravery in battle,
But we hear you being cursed from the cross; <note>This likely refers to a formal ecclesiastical curse or excommunication proclaimed publicly.</note>
The two who stand beside you are "fine" companions,
But they are truly black in their nature. <note>The poet implies Allan is flanked by demons or men of dark souls.</note>
When you first went to war,
There was the Abbot’s gruesome corpse,
In addition to that other lawless raid
Against Saint Finan² in Glengarry.
Your own cruel and hateful deeds
Have brought a curse upon your bald-headed body, Allan,
Just as crime always does—
It takes its revenge on the one who commits it.
The entire countryside, with its outcry,
Has etched a mad rage onto Allan’s face.
Your own country and your own friends,
You have cruelly oppressed.
The very last of your goodness was lost
Between the River Shiel and Loch Hourn.
It is no wonder you have kept
Far away, Allan, from the gallows.
The reputation that men have given you
Extends even to your mother and your sister.
It is time now to end this satire,
You worthless, cruel son of Ruari.
Though you have learning that does nothing for your character,
The sound of your wailing is pleasant to hear.
->The unique demon.<-

---

¹ The Hebrides were known as the "Islands of the Strangers" <note>original: "Innse-gall"</note>, probably since the rise of the Norsemen's kingdom there.
<column-break/>
² The church of Kilfinan, at the east end of Loch Lochy, where the burial place of the MacDonells of Glengarry is still located.

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page marks a shift from the previous page's sharp satire against Allan MacRuari to a spirited eulogy for a member of the MacLeods of Lewis. The focus moves from the misdeeds of the mainland clans to the virtues of the maritime nobility of the Outer Hebrides.</meta>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.
#### PAGE 146

THE son has been found to be just like his father,¹
Superior to all the chiefs we have ever known,
In his bearing, his expression, and his mind,
And he dwells here with me in Lewis.
He possesses the wisdom and spirit of a leader,
Through which he will bring about prosperous times.
I say of this young son we have received,
That he is the very image of Roderick;²
How similar to one another are their locks of hair,
He brings his father’s honor to his own ringlets.
In battle, too, how like the famous father
Is the praise earned by Torquil.³
Of all the events that may occur in Torquil’s time,
None of his friends shall suffer any loss;
There will be great deeds and victories,
The kind of stories that Mac Calman⁴ may relate.
Many are his gifts which we might celebrate,
Torquil of that illustrious race;
He possesses a hero’s strength and vigor,
Which he carries with him into the fight.
I speak of him, and I speak the truth,
Since I have come to know him so well,
That never in his time has there been
A better king to rule in Lewis.
To him belonged the "Great Galley,"⁵ <note>original: "Cairge mhordha"</note>
The finest treasure that ever sailed the sea,
It was given to the Son of the Son of Torquil, <note>original: "Mac Vic Torquil"</note>
To carry him home to the land of his people.

---

³ Torquil was the second son of Roderick MacLeod of Lewis, the eldest having been

⁴ Likely the primary bard of the Seed of Torquil.

⁵ "Cairbhe" <note>original: "Cairbhe"</note> is a *ship*. This was likely the name of Torquil’s galley, though spelled "cairge" by the Dean.

<meta>This page continues the eulogy for Torquil MacLeod of Lewis, shifting from his personal leadership qualities to his material wealth, his military strength compared to legendary heroes, and finally his prestigious marriage alliance with the House of Argyll.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.
## PAGE 147

Mac Ruarie <note>The son of Roderick.</note> of cheerful music,
Had also the old cleaving sword,
Another jewel of sure effect,
’Twas given him by the King of Aineach. <margin>Aineach: a castle of the O’Kanes, located within a few miles of Derry. The King of Aineach was the chief of the O’Kanes.</margin>
Since he possessed so many presents,
It was needless for him to go and seek more.
A shield he had, notched at the top,
Another treasure, sounding loud in battle.
Outside he kept a noble herd
Of horses, with their trappings of red;
It would not suit a man of his standing
Not to have many swift-paced steeds.
His was the *Du Seivlin*, <note>original: "Dubh-Seimhlinn," likely the name of a famous black horse.</note>
The MacLeod’s horse, of whom the bards would sing.
It was hard for any to capture that horse,
Whoever they were that might come seeking him.
Torquil had many young warriors
Who never trembled in battle,
Who seized all lands for his family line,
A race that always loved the conflict.
Not braver in his time was Cuchullin, <note>Cuchulainn: the legendary hero of Gaelic mythology, often compared to Achilles for his prowess in battle.</note>
Not hardier was he than Torquil,
He of the ready, vigorous arm,
Who boldly breaks through any breach.
Beloved though the Son of the Son of Torquil <note>original: "Mac Vic Torquil"</note> is,
I cannot praise his beauty enough;
He who is as fair as he is brave,
The key to every woman's heart.
There is no son of king or chief
Of whose fame we have ever heard,
Though we have had much to do with such men,
That is better to us than Torquil.
Catherine, <note>Catharine, daughter of Colin, 2nd Earl of Argyle, who married Torquil MacLeod of the Lewis.</note> daughter of Mac Cailen, <note>Mac Cailein: the Gaelic title for the Chief of Clan Campbell.</note>
Whose soft hand is worthy of thy race,

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE
## PAGE 148

Daughter of the Earl of Argyll,
Best of the women we have found.
To our isle we've got a woman,
Branch of a great and famous tree.
Daughter of Mac Cailen <note>original: "Mac Cailin," the Gaelic patronymic for the Campbell Earls of Argyll, named after their ancestor Cailin Mór.</note>, young and gentle,
Whose locks in flowing ringlets fall.
->The son.<-

### The author of this is Gilliecalum Mac an ollave.¹ <note>original: "Gille Caluim Mac an Ollaimh," meaning Malcolm, son of the Physician/Scholar.</note>

The cause of my sorrow is come,
This year has not prospered with me;
Foolish is he who cannot understand
How my grief has come upon me;
He who cannot understand
How my grief has come all at once;
Since these wounds my body received,
Such wounds I have that I must mourn.
Pleasant now, though bitter too,
To mourn my sad distress;
Sorrow fills my inmost heart,
Great was my love for him who's gone;
My heart is broken in two,
No wonder it should be so;
My body has neither flesh nor blood,
Like a strengthless sufferer.
'Tis no wonder if I so grieve
For Margaret's² son who now is gone,
Remembering all his virtues,
And that we are left without a chief.

---

¹ It has been said in a previous note that nothing is known of this poet. However, the present composition would indicate his being one of the celebrated Beatons, who served as physicians and <term>sennachies (traditional Gaelic historians, genealogists, and keepers of family lore)</term> to the Lords of the Isles. There is a charter of lands in Islay, written in Gaelic by Fergus <column-break/> Beaton in 1411. There were several members of the family whose name was Gilliecalum <note>original: "Gille Caluim," the Gaelic form of Malcolm.</note>. "Mac an Olaimh" means *son of the physician*.

² Margaret Livingstone, daughter of Sir Alexander Livingstone, who was married to Alexander, Lord of the Isles. <note>The son mentioned in the poem is likely John, the last independent Lord of the Isles.</note>

<meta>This page continues the poem by Gilliecalum Mac an ollave (Malcolm, son of the Physician), mourning the decline of the Lordship of the Isles. The previous page established the poet’s deep personal grief for his patron, and this page focuses on the historical tragedy of the family's succession and the poet's desire to leave his homeland now that his protectors are gone.</meta>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Sore is the loss that he is gone,
Now that in the world we're weak,
My grief now that thy days are ended,
Is the injury done by Angus.¹ <note>original: "Aonghas." This refers to Angus Og, the legitimate son of John, the last Lord of the Isles. Angus led a rebellion against his father, resulting in the naval Battle of the Bloody Bay.</note>
Though it be hard for me to part
With John's² son of sweetest speech,
What is worst of all is this,
That ne'er to his place he'll return.
Though I were from happiness far,
Pursued by my foes' reproach,
Whatever good might me o'ertake,
From them never would I buy.
No wonder though heavy my heart,
As another lord's seen in thy place,
That my whole man should be feeble,
Now that my king is dead.
Bitter is my pain since he left,
'Tis easy the tale to relate,
'Tis hard my great sorrow to bear,
For the hero so famous who's gone.
Great is my grief, and no wonder,
My mourning is true, sincere;
That which sorely has me pained,
Is that in Scotland <note>original: "Albin" (Alba), the Gaelic name for Scotland or Britain.</note> we've no race.³
Now since that I must leave,
As others with reproach me load,
Since he is dead, I fain would go,
Away from the rough isles of Scotland <note>original: "Albin." Referring to the Hebrides.</note>.
Yet 'tis sore for me to leave,
Although I feel that go I must,
Now that my beloved is dead,
My country I must leave behind.

---

¹ Angus Og, son of John last Lord of the Isles, who fought the battle of the Bloody Bay against his father.
² John, son of Alexander Lord of the Isles.

<column-break/>

³ Both John and Angus, sons of the last Lord of the Isles, died before their father. He was forfeited <note>The legal loss of lands and titles as a penalty for treason against the Scottish Crown.</note>, and died in a monastery in <term>Paisley</term> <note>A town in west-central Scotland; the Abbey there served as the final resting place for the last Lord of the Isles after his titles were stripped by King James IV.</note>.

**Vocabulary and Historical Figures:**
*   **Angus Og**: Young Angus, a charismatic but rebellious leader of the MacDonalds.
*   **Lord of the Isles**: The ruler of the Hebrides and parts of the western Highlands, once a semi-independent principality.
*   **Battle of the Bloody Bay**: A fierce naval battle fought near Tobermory (c. 1480s) between the supporters of the Lord of the Isles and his son.
*   **Albin**: The ancient name for Scotland.
*   **Paisley**: The site of the monastery where the Lordship effectively ended in 1503.
*   **John of the Isles**: The 4th and final Lord of the Isles.
*   **Alexander Lord of the Isles**: John's father and a powerful 15th-century magnate.

<summary>This page concludes a poignant Gaelic lament for the MacDonald Lords of the Isles, detailing the poet's despair following the death of his patrons and the collapse of their dynasty. It captures a pivotal moment in Highland history when the semi-independent Lordship of the Isles was forfeited to the Scottish Crown.</summary>

### Blind Arthur Mac Gurkich. <note>Arthur Mac Gurkich (Artúr Mac Gurcaigh) was a blind Irish poet. The editor notes that his style is deliberately archaic, imitating the difficult, formal language of ancient Irish bards.</note>

The assembled fleet at <term>Castle Sween</term>, <note>Castle Sween (Caisteal Suibhne) in Knapdale is one of the oldest stone castles in Scotland and was a vital stronghold for control of the sea lanes.</note>

other in the Dean’s manuscript. <term>Broin</term> <note>original: "broin"</note> is an ancient word for a troop or band, and <term>cleath</term>, <term>comhlan</term>, and <term>nòs</term> <note>original: "cleath," "comhlan," "nòs"</note> are all words for a warrior, though they are now entirely obsolete. There is a manifest attempt in the composition to use these obsolete words, but the Editor trusts the meaning has been correctly rendered.

Spotted cushions were provided,
Couches for the nobles' wives.
Spotted coverings of fine linen,
This was the covering of the ship;
Handsome, easy, as she rocked,
Purple linen round each mast.
No hardened hands, no tightened belt,
Nor roughened by their usual toil;
Heroes were there, nor did they labour,
Bands of men of sweetest lips. <note>In Gaelic poetry, "sweetest lips" often refers to the eloquence of the nobility or the singing of the ship's company.</note>

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Above, the gentle women were.
Who is he provides this fleet,
At Castle Sween¹ of many hills?
A vigorous man who fears no blast,
His masts upraised, seeking his <term>right</term>. <note>In this context, "right" refers to a hereditary or legal claim to land and titles that had been lost or contested.</note>
John M'Sween,² sail thou the ship,
On the ocean's fierce-topped back;
Raise aloft the vessel's masts,
Let thy bark now test the sea.
A leading wind then for them rose,
At <term>Kyle Aca</term>³ as rose the tide; <note>Likely a local name for the entrance to Loch Sween where the tide runs strongly.</note>
The speckled sails were roundly bellied,
As John ran swiftly for the land.
We entered the cheerful anchorage
In the bay of fruitful Knapdale;⁴
The noble hero, lordly, shapely,
Comely, masted, swift, victorious,
He was then near <term>Albin's</term> walls, <note>original: "Albain." The Gaelic name for Scotland.</note>
Helpful, welcoming his men.
Fair was then the youthful hero,
Abundant dew distilling round,
Favourable at Slieve Mun's⁵ streams,
To Mac Sween, him of <term>Slieve Mis</term>. <note>original: "Sliabh Mis." A mountain in County Antrim, Ireland (Slemish), identifying the hero's Irish power base.</note>
Speakers then come near to ask,
They deal as with him of sharpest eye.
Branches are laid beneath their knees,
To welcome those of valour great.
Their safety in each harbour nook
Suffers from the welcome they give John. <note>This likely suggests the harbor was overwhelmed or crowded by the sheer scale of the celebration and the size of the fleet.</note>

<note>1</note> This lady is elsewhere called the <note>original Latin: "Contissa Ergadien," meaning "Countess of Argyll."</note> From the name given here, she appears to be a daughter of the Earl of Argyll, but as the Countess she might be styled *Ni vic Cailein*, or the daughter of Colin. We know that *Sir Colin Campbell* of Glenurchy was the uncle and guardian to Archibald, the first Earl of Argyll. Having himself married Margaret, the eldest daughter of Stuart, the last Lord of Lorn, he arranged a marriage between his nephew and Isabella, the second
<column-break/>
daughter, who became the Countess of Argyll. She was most likely the author of these lines, though we have no specific record of the domestic history or the "secret love" to which the poem refers.

<note>2</note> There is a poet named <note>original: "Duncan Og Albanach," meaning "Duncan the Younger of Scotland."</note> among the writers of religious poetry in the manuscript. He was most likely a member of the MacVurrich family—a famous dynasty of hereditary bards—and the author of these lines. It is worth noting that the poet is referring here to the "seven mortal sins" <note>The seven deadly sins: pride, greed, lust, envy, gluttony, wrath, and sloth.</note> recognized by the Church.

# THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

## The author is Murdoch the Scot.<gloss>1</gloss> <note>original Gaelic: "Murdoch Albanach"</note>

> 'T is time for me to go to the house of Paradise,
> While this wound's not easily borne,
> Let me win this house, famous, faultless,
> While others can tell of us nought else.
> Confess thyself now to the priest,
> Remember clearly all thy sins;
> Carry not to the house of the spotless King,
> Aught that may thee expose to charge.
> Conceal not any of thy sins,
> However hateful its evil to tell;
> Confess what has been done in secret,
> Lest thou expose thyself to wrath.
> Make thy peace now with the clergy,
> That thou may'st be safe as to thy state;
> Give up thy sin, deeply repent,
> Lest its guilt be found in thee.
> Woe to him who forsook the Great King's house,
> For love of sin, sad is the deed;
> The sin a man commits in secret,
> Much is the debt his sin incurs.
> This is a sermon for Adam's race,
> I think I've nothing said that's false,
> Though men may death for a time avoid,
> 'T is true they can't at length escape.

1 Murdoch of Scotland <note>original: "Albanach"</note> was the first of the great race of the Macvurrichs <note>A famous family of hereditary bards in the Highlands</note>, bards to Macdonald of Clanranald. From all that can be gathered regarding him, he was an ecclesiastic, and, according to the measure of light he possessed, a man of earnest and sincere religion. It was not known, until this volume of Dean MacGregor's was searched, that any remains of his compositions existed; but here we find several, all very much of the same character. There is one long poem to

Cathal the Red-Handed <note>original: "croibhdhearg"</note> and Murdoch of Scotland <note>original: "Albanach"</note> after they had both entered the monastic brotherhood <note>original: "anns na braithribh"</note>, they sang:—

I will shave mine for Mary, this decision is a heart-of-gold decision,

Seldom has it happened to you, fair youth, to have a knife upon your head to shave you,
More often has a soft, sweet queen combed her hair toward you.

### TRANSLATION.

Cathal Red-Handed and Murdoch of Scotland,
on entering among the brethren, sung:—
Murdoch, whet thy knife, that we may shave
our crowns to the Great King,
Let us sweetly give our vow, and the hair of
both our heads to the Trinity.
I will shave mine to Mary, this is the doing
of a true heart,
To Mary shave thou these locks, well-formed,
soft-eyed man.
Seldom hast thou had, handsome man, a knife
on thy hair to shave it,
Oftener has a sweet, soft queen, comb'd her
hair beside thee.
Whenever it was that we did bathe, with
Brian of the well-curled locks,
And once on a time that I did bathe, at the
wall of the fair-haired Boroimhe <note>Refers to Brian Boru, the famous High King of Ireland</note>,
I strove in swimming with Ua Chais <note>The descendant of Cas, a reference to the O'Brien lineage</note>, on the
cold waters of the Fergus.
When he came ashore from the stream, Ua
Chais and I strove in a race.
These two knives, one to each, were given us
by Duncan Cairbreach,
No knives of knives were better, shave gently
then, Murdoch.
Whet your sword, Cathal, which wins the
fertile Banva <note>A poetic name for Ireland</note>,
Ne'er was thy wrath heard without fighting,
brave, red-handed Cathal,
Preserve our shaved heads from cold and from
heat, gentle daughter of Joachim <note>Traditionally known as the father of the Virgin Mary</note>,
Preserve us in the land of heat, softest branch,
Mary.
Murdoch.

### GENEALOGY OF THE MACGREGORS.

John son of Patrick, son of Malcolm, son of John the Black, son of John, son of Gregor, son of John, son of Malcolm, son of Duncan the Little, son of Duncan from Srulee <note>Likely referring to Srubhla (Struan) or a specific local landholding.</note>, son of Gillean <note>original: "Gilelan"</note>, son of Hugh of Glenorchy <note>original: "Urchy"; Hugh of Glenorchy was a pivotal figure in the early MacGregor line.</note>, son of Kenneth, son of Alpin; and this Kenneth was High King of Scotland, in truth, at that time; and this John is the eleventh man in descent from Kenneth, of whom I spoke. — And Duncan the assistant <term>servitor; in this context, a secretary or clerk who maintains records</term>, son of Dougal, son of John the Grey <note>original: "grizzled"; meaning grey-haired or aged</note>, wrote this from the books of the genealogists of the kings; and it was done in the year of our Lord One thousand five hundred and twelve.

# THE ORIGINAL GAELIC.

### The author is Ossian, son of Finn. <note>original: "A houdir Ossan M'Finna." This is Oisín, the legendary poet-warrior of the Fianna.</note>

I saw the household of Finn, and it was not the household of a weak man,
I saw with me a great company, the people of the man of yesterday.
I saw the household of Art, a man among men, noble and sweet-voiced,
Better than any I have seen. I saw the household of Finn.
Though I saw what I have seen, I see my followers under the earth,
Alas, and a pity that I saw them. I saw the household of Finn.
I go to the King of the youth <note>Likely referring to God or the King of the Otherworld</note> though I am now in a poor state,
Because of the departure of my friends from the Fenians, for I saw the household of Finn.
->I saw the household.<-

### The author of this is Ossian. <note>original: "A houdir so Ossin." This poem is the famous "Long tonight the clouds above me," a classic lament of the Fenian cycle.</note>

Long tonight are the clouds above me, long to me was last night;
This day, though it be long to you, yesterday was long enough.
Every day that comes is long to me, for it was not thus I was accustomed,
Without a fight, without the rush of battle, without learning close-ranked arts.
Without women, without the woods, without the harp, without generous giving or great deeds;
->love<- <note>original: "gray"; likely "gràdh" or "gràth", referring to love or perhaps "gràth" as a period of time.</note>
Without the distribution of gold by scholars, to be without horses, without the drinking-feast;
Without interest in wooing or in hunting, the two sports I once enjoyed;
Without the desire for the shout or the battle—alas, but my fate is bitter.
Without a watch for the hind or the deer, that was not the way I used to be;
Without a leash on a hound or on dogs, long tonight are the clouds above me.
Without the usual deeds of valor, without playing games as we were wont;
Without swimming our horses across the lake—long, etc.
In the world as I am now, pitiable is the life we lead.

<summary>This page contains two poems attributed to the legendary poet Ossian, lamenting the lost glory of the Fianna and the hardships of old age. These verses are written in a unique phonetic Scots-Gaelic orthography used by the Dean of Lismore in the 16th century.</summary>
<keywords>Ossian, Oisín, Finn Mac Cumhaill, Fenian Cycle, Fianna, Gaelic poetry, lament, aging, Dean of Lismore, Scottish Gaelic</keywords>

# MODERN VERSION.

## The author is Ossian, son of Finn. <note>original: "An t-ùghdair Ossian mac Fhinn." Ossian is the legendary narrator of the Fenian Cycle, depicted here as an old man looking back on the glory of his youth.</note>

I saw the household of Finn, and it was not the household of a timid tribe,
I saw there a pillar <note>original: "sabh"; used metaphorically to mean a hero or a strong support of the people</note> of the people of the man of yesterday.
I saw the household of Art, a man whose sons were noble and sweet-voiced,
A better man I never saw. I saw the household of Finn.
I did not see as I have seen [before]; I saw the "Son of Luinn" <note>original: "mac an Luinn"; the name of Finn mac Cumhaill’s famous sword</note> with Finn.
Alas! It is a pity for those who did not see it. I saw the household of Finn.
In my life, it is no surprise that every evil now falls upon my head.
May we be delivered and remain free from pain. I saw the household of Finn.
->I saw the household.<-

## The author of this is Ossian.

Long tonight are the clouds above me, long to me was last night,
Though this day be long to me, yesterday’s day was long enough,
Every day that comes feels long to me; it was not thus I was accustomed,
Without strife, without making battle, without learning close-ranked feats <note>original: "cleas dlù"; refers to the specialized martial arts and maneuvers of the Fianna warriors</note>.
Without maidens, without music, without the harp, without the breaking of bones, without
->great deeds,<-
Without further sharp learning, being without festivals, without the drinking of feasts,
Without love for courting, or for the hunt—the two crafts I used to follow,
Without going into conflict or battle; alas! it is tearful for me,
Without catching a doe or a deer; it was not such things I desired,
Without mention of hound-gear or of dogs; long tonight are the clouds above me,
Without the rising of customary heroism, without playing as we would wish,
Without our warriors swimming on the loch; long tonight are the clouds above me,
To the world as I am now, pitiful is our existence as we are.

### The author of this is Ossian. <note>original Latin: "Auctor hujus Ossin"</note>

On a day that Finn of my prosperity went, to hunt on the Mountain of the Fair Women <note>Sliabh na mBan in County Tipperary, a site steeped in Fenian mythology</note>
Three thousand good men of the Fianna, no fear was in their heads.
"Ossian, sweet to me is your voice; a blessing yet on the soul of Finn,
And tell us how many deer fell on the Mountain of the Fair Women."
"Though great to you was the shaking of the way, or the death-shouts in the day,
There fell on the Mountain of the Fair Women, by the hand of Finn of the weapons.
Tell us every story, a blessing on your mouth without deceit.
Did you have clothing or arms, going with you to the hunt each day?"
"We had clothing and arms, going with us to the hunt as of old;
The Fianna were not among them in the field without shirts, bright and smooth,
Without a quilted cotton doublet <term>chottone</term>, without a sharp, polished breastplate <term>lurych</term> <note>original: "lurych"; from the Latin lorica, a shirt of mail or protective armor</note>,
Without a close-fitting helmet <term>chenvart</term> of stones, and two spears in the hand of every man,
Without a gold-patterned shield, without a hard-edged sword.
In the clothing of the world itself under the sky, there was none more sharp than Finn;
He was the pinnacle of hospitality and youth, no hand was better than his head.
Going to visit the white hound, as we had a man like Finn,
The battle-array we saw in the west, a-hunting on the Mountain of the Fair Women.
O Patrick of the head of the clerics, small was the grain <note>possibly a metaphor for "meager reward" or "low status" compared to the past</note> on our heads
When Finn sat... to the sweet-voiced ones, east and west,
Sending a shout from hill to hill, between the boar and the deer.
There were Finn and Bran <note>Finn’s most famous and beloved hound</note>, both sitting on the mountain,
Each man of them at his hunt, or the sharp rising of the anger of men.
We unleashed three thousand hounds, the pinnacle of speed in their way, fiercely;
Each hound of them killed two deer before... in the field or the height.
Six thousand deer fell... down in the glen that was under the mountain;
And twenty and... never was there a hunt like that.
That was the end of our hunt in the west, O cleric of the voices and the bells,
Ten hundred hounds with gold collars fell with the blow, and ten hundred boars.
The boars fell by us... the portion of the hunt on the slope;
As if they were... and our hands would bring slaughter on the hunt.
O Patrick of the true crozier <note>original: "baichill"; the staff carried by Saint Patrick as a symbol of his office</note>, did you ever see in the east or the north,
A hunt in your day like the one we made with Finn of the victories?"

MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

In my old age pulling stones, long tonight are the clouds above me.
The end of the Fianna is here now, and I am great Ossian, son of Finn,
Listening to the voices of bells <note>A common motif in Fenian literature where the aged Ossian complains about the sound of Saint Patrick’s church bells replacing the sounds of nature and hunting</note>, long tonight are the clouds above me.
Obtain for us from God, Patrick, knowledge of the state in which we shall be,
That our lives may be freed from sin, long tonight are the clouds above me.
<meta>catchword: Is fad.</meta> ->Long tonight.<-

### The author of this is Ossian.

One day when Finn my beloved went to hunt on the Hill of the Fair Women <note>Sliabh na mBan, a famous mountain in County Tipperary associated with the legends of the Fianna</note>,
Three thousand of the nobles of the Fianna, with their fine shields above them.
"Ossian, sweet to me is your voice; a blessing also on the soul of Finn,
And tell us how many deer fell on the Hill of the Fair Women."
"Though great and swift we shook our spears, for your fame did not depart from you,
There fell on the Hill of the Fair Women, those who went with Finn of the feasts.
Tell them every story before you, a blessing on your mouth without guile.
Was there clothing or arms going with you to the hunt each day?"
"There were clothing and arms going with us to the hunt in that manner;
Not one of the Fianna would be there without a shirt of smooth silk,
Without a soft quilted tunic <term>cotan</term>, without a sharp, clean breastplate <term>luireach</term>,
Without a helmet <term>ceannbheart</term> set with precious stones, and two spears in each man's hand,
Without a venomous shield that would win victory, without a hard sword to split
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;heads;
In the search of the whole world, there was no one sharper than Finn,
He was the best in lineage and fortune, no hand ever rose above him,
Going to attend his white dog—who among us was a man above Finn?
In order we went westward, to hunt on the Hill of the Fair Women.
O Patrick, foster-father and head of the clergy, beautiful was the sun above us,
When Finn sat down before us, sweet to the east and the west,
The voice of the hound from hill to hill, awakening the boar and the deer.
Finn and Bran <note>Finn mac Cumhaill’s legendary hound</note> were sitting for a while on the hill,
Each man of them in the eagerness of the hunt, until their battle-fury arose.
We let loose three thousand dogs, the best in strength and those that were fierce.
Each of those dogs killed two deer, before the leash was put up high.
Six thousand deer in total fell, in the glen that was below the hill.
All that you have of deer and roe, never made a hunt like that.
That was the end of our hunt in the east, O cleric, foster-father of the clergy and the bells,
Ten hundred dogs under golden chains, ten hundred boars fell under wounds,
The boars fell by us, those who did the evils on the plain.
Were it not for our blades and our hands, the hunt would have turned to a slaughter of us.
O Patrick of the true croziers, did you see in the east or the west,
A day's hunt in your time, from the Fianna of Finn, that was greater than that?"

But that was the hunt which was made by Finn, son of Alpin <note>original: "v'alpin"; likely a phonetic rendering or a specific regional variant of the hero's patronymic</note>, not small in fame.
Short was the distance between them, though the leap was melodious each day.

# The author of this is Ossian <note>original: "Actor hujus Ossin"</note>.

One day when he went to his church <note>original: "keill", referring to a cell or chapel</note>, Patrick of the slender crozier <term>bachall</term>...
He took Ossian with him in joy, [even though his] voice was...
"It is my desire to ask of you, Ossian of the shouts who is not timid,
Who was the man without a fleet, who brought a frown upon the Fianna of Finn?"
"O cleric of the spotted crozier <term>bachall</term>, it would be great in my opinion if you were to speak with us,
To mention the customs of the ancestors, and not be mocking the Fianna of Finn."
"Since it has happened to you to be without strength <note>literally 'without noon', perhaps implying the twilight of life</note>, O Ossian, without following in their footsteps,
He [the Lord] shall be watching you forever, as you watch the customs of the Fianna."
"For fifty years I was a hero... in the world,
I could not tell you exactly the many exploits the Fianna of Finn performed.
Throughout the world... we were...
Tribute was being raised for Finn of the Fianna...
There was nothing in this great world that was not rightfully [theirs]...
There was no one in Scotland <note>original: "alwe" referring to Alba</note> of the spotted blades...
"If I were to tell you now, O Ossian of the ungentle journey,
Who was the man of the strong hand, who was [defeated?]..."
"Great is the pain you put on me, O cleric...
I will not tell until the Day of Doom <note>original: "lay looin"</note>..."
"Since it has happened that you are after them [the Fianna], O Ossian...
Who were the heroes you thought best with shields when going into the heat of battle?"
"Oscar, and Caoilte, and Goll, and Mac Lughach of the good blades,
Around the son of Cumhal [Finn], who was the king in the battle.
Red-fisted Farquhar, son of the king, and Cairill of the braided [hair?]...
Diarmuid of the beautiful color without pride, the king of shields who was good.
Colla, son of Caoilte, of the many fames; Corc, the hero of the good heads,
And Raighne, son of the king, [and] Muirne who did not avoid the spear.
Faolan of the fair hair was our pride, and Garaidh of the sharp weapons,
Daire, son of Dorn, without weakness, [and] Aodh son of Garaidh of the good hand.
Myself and [Goll] son of Smail, and [Daire] son of Ronan,
Three sons of the smith without flaw, at the hour of striking they were a fierce band.
As I would tell you a tale, O great cleric of the relics <term>mionn</term>,
Not one of the Fianna was ever struck, but every man would keep his promise.
Sit in your chair and take your psalms <term>psailm</term> and leave all weapons aside.
To every man of the strong hand, who came from the battles to the Fianna."
"The King of Norway <term>Lochlann</term> came over the sea, [with] a dark brown shield of great appearance."

## The author of this is Ossian.

One day when he went to the church, lovely Patrick of the crozier <term>bachall</term>...
He took Ossian with him in joy, so that he might be...
"And I would wish to hear from you, Ossian of the raids who is not timid,
Who was the one man, though unnamed, who brought the most gloom upon the Fianna of Finn?"
"O cleric of the dappled crozier <term>bachall</term>, great was the respect for you in your time,
To put into customary words how much glory belonged to the Fianna of Finn."
"Since it has happened to you to be without strength <note>literally "without noon," implying his old age</note>, O Ossian, without following after them,
Be you on watch forever, as you recount the customs of the Fianna."
"If I were to live for fifty years, listening to your music in the church,
I could not tell you until my death the many exploits the Fianna of Finn performed.
The lands of the powerful world, we ourselves held on every...
Tribute was being raised for the Fianna of Finn, or else the encounter...
There was none in the great world, to whom it was right...
There was no one in Almha <note>Almha, or the Hill of Allen, was the legendary seat of the Fianna</note> of the speckled blades, of the rough...
Of whom they would tell them now, Ossian of the raids that [did not fail]...
Who was the one man of the sharpest hand, who was...
Great is the pain it puts on me, O cleric foster-father...
I would not tell until doomsday <note>literally "the day of the Monday/the last day"</note> all those who were mentioned...
Since you have remained after them, Ossian, who would make...
Who were the warriors you preferred about your shield, when the Fianna went into battle?"
"Oscar and Caoilte and Goll, and the Son of Lughaidh of the good blades,
Around the handsome Son of Cumhall, a company that was steady in battle,
Bloody Fearghon, son of the king, and Carroll of the most venomous sting,
Beautiful, colorful Diarmuid <note>Diarmuid ua Duibhne, the famous lover and warrior</note> without submission, who received a good head-shield,
Colla, son of Caoilte at the smooth feast, Corc the hero of the good staves,
And Roinn, son of the king, a champion <note>original: muirichinn; usually 'family' or 'burden', here implying a heavy-hitter</note> not smooth in battle,
Fair-haired Faolan who was Finn's son, and Garaidh, the defense against enemies,
Dearuinn son of Dobharan without stain, Aodh son of Garaidh of the good hand,
Myself and Garaidh son of Smail, and oaken Daoire son of Ronan,
The three sons of the Smith <term>Ceàrd</term> without guile, whose turning made for red weapons.
As I would speak to you, great cleric of the Port of Oaths,
I knew none of the Fianna but those who took hostages in victory.
Sit in your chair and take your pen, and you shall have the whole army listed.
Every single man of the sharp hand, who came from battles to the Fianna.
The King of Norway <term>Lochlann</term> came over the sea, Daire of the brown shield of great form,"

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Ossian begins a detailed account of the Battle of Ventry (Cath Fionntrágha), where the Fianna defended Ireland against the "King of the World" and his international allies. ### THE BOOK OF ANCIENT.

The tribute of Ireland was demanded at the harbor, where all the host arose;
Help came to our Fianna <note>the *Fianna* were the legendary band of warriors led by Finn mac Cumhaill</note>, from the north they came from every hill.
Cairbre Luscra of the skilled hand, led four full battalions to the port;
Seven battalions of the Fianna arrived, coming from the west from the Half of Conn <note>Leath Chuinn: the northern half of Ireland</note>.
There was no [enemy] that did not meet our blades, a choice selection of you were safe from our men.
Such was the fleet of ships, containing the men of the world in that own household;
Thirty-five battalions of swiftness, who did not desert us, our own true Fianna.
They were gathered upon the strand, and Cuan Craer was a hand without fault;
He brought the hosts to assemble, and he raised the head of a king upon a hill.
Cuan, son of the King of Ulster of the great deeds, and Dolair of the heavy blows;
We left them on the strand, lying beneath the man of the waves <note>original: *fo zar tonni* — likely meaning they were buried near the tide or drowned</note>.
The three sons of Doghadh, though they were stout, fell from the midst of the ships;
Fear Teannas and Circal the wet, we left their bodies stripped bare.
The gold-armed daughter of the King of Greece, and the crews of heavy blows;
We left them under the cry of the battle-crow <note>original: *byve* (Badhbh), the Irish goddess of war who took the form of a crow or raven</note>, and we did not leave the crow in sorrow.
The four sons of the King of Norway <term>Lochlann</term> <note>Lochlann refers to Scandinavia, specifically Norway in this context</note>, it was they who were slaughtered by the venom of weapons;
The three Mutes from the Sea of Gold, they did not escape us without woe.
The King of the World, though he was great, Dáire Donn of the bright-faced shield;
We left his body upon the strand, wounded by the combat of the Fianna.
The strong world was recounted; none of them escaped from us in the slaughter,
Except the King of France, as he is, the finest blade of all that breed.
For fear of the great Oscar <note>Oscar was the son of Ossian and the most formidable warrior of the later Fianna</note>, he did not let himself be brought down by us;
To Glen Bolcán <note>original: *glen baltan*. Legend says the King of France went mad with terror at the battle and fled to this glen, a sanctuary for the insane</note> as the story goes, it is there he took rest and stillness.
On the strand of Ventry <note>original: *fintrath*</note> of the harbors, the man who put the hosts to slaughter;
Against the kings of the strong world, we ourselves poured out our excellence.
We dealt death to kings with our weapons, heroes who lay dead on the ground;
Swords and shields were scattered like flowers there upon the strand.
On Ventry strand of the ports, there were the bodies of many men;
There were heroes under the cry of the battle-crow, and there were the spoils of slaughter.
O Patrick, son of noble Alpin <note>original: *Phatrik V'Alpin*. A variation on the saint's usual lineage</note>, there did not come of our Fianna from that time,
But two flawless battalions in order, and not a single body among them was whole.
One battalion of the Clan Baiscne <note>the core family of Finn mac Cumhaill</note> of them, a troop whose hand never failed;
One battalion of the Clan Morna <note>a rival but allied faction of the Fianna</note> of the battles, and the other with the Clan Smól.
By the length of the fighting, O strong Holy-born one <term>Tailgean</term> <note>a common epithet for St. Patrick, referring to his tonsure or "holy-birth"</note>, that is what left our Fianna in slaughter.
Five organized battalions of our host were struck down from us on the strand;
Thirty-five battalions without luck, with three thousand men in each battalion of them.
Of the men of the world certainly, not one returned upon the waves again.
O Holy-born one <term>Tailgean</term>, if I should answer clearly, since every story is mine to tell,
Until the Battle of Gabhra <note>the final, catastrophic battle that destroyed the Fianna</note> of the glens was fought, no story came to an end in my day.
We took their fleet of ships, and the heavy silver of the king;
At that time, enclosed in the night, in Ireland on every side of it.
O good Patrick of the holy relics, in your church may I find death;
Put the earth itself over my skin, since you are the one who knows my story.

<summary>Ossian recounts the Battle of Ventry (Cath Fionntrágha), detailing the destruction of the "King of the World's" army and the heavy losses sustained by the Fianna. The poem concludes with Ossian expressing his desire to die in Patrick's care now that his story is fully told.</summary>
<keywords>Battle of Ventry, Fionntrá, Finn mac Cumhaill, Oscar, Lochlann, Norway, Dáire Donn, Glen Bolcán, Clan Baiscne, Tailgean, St. Patrick</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Ossian provides a poetic inventory of the losses and the legendary enemies defeated at the Battle of Ventry (Fionntrágha). He lists the various kings and clans involved, shifting the narrative toward the total destruction of the invading forces and the heavy price paid by the Fianna.</meta>

### THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

The tribute of Ireland was claimed against Conn <note>Conn of the Hundred Battles, a legendary High King</note>, and all our host was sorrowful because of it.
Help came to our Fianna, a host that gathered from every hill,
Cairbre the Tall, who had a skilled hand, brought four full battalions to the port,
Seven battalions of the Fianna arrived; three were brought from the east of Conn’s Half <note>Leath Chuinn: the northern half of Ireland</note>.
There was almost none of us left whole who were not cut down by Daire Donn <note>The "Brown King," a legendary "King of the World" who invaded Ireland in the tale *Cath Fionntrágha*</note>.
Such was his fleet of ships, Daire Donn and his own household,
Thirty battalions of a host, who did not escape from us back to their own land.
Waiting for them on the strand was Conn the Shaker, whose hand was without fault,
He overtook the host of India for them, and he raised a king's head upon a hill,
Conn, son of the King of Ulster of the great deeds, and Dollar of the heavy actions,
We left them on the shore, drowned beneath the roar of the waves.
The three sons of Dubhtach, who were once strong, came from the middle of the ships,
Feartan and Cearcul of the cheek—we left their bodies stripped bare.
Omhar the armed, daughter of the King of Greece, and Forna of the heavy blows,
We left them under the laughter of the war-goddess <note>original: *baoibh* (Badhbh), the Irish goddess of war who often appeared as a carrion crow over battlefields</note>, and that did not leave the joyful in sorrow.
The four sons of the King of Norway <term>Lochlann</term> <note>Lochlann is the traditional Gaelic name for Scandinavia</note>, whom we destroyed with the venom of weapons,
The three Balaidh from the eastern Burren—they did not leave us but in misery.
The King of the World, though he was great—Daire Donn of the shield of bright appearance—
We left his body on the shore, wounded under the blows of the Fianna.
Of the hosts of the strong world, none escaped from us in the slaughter
Except for the King of France; like a bird of prey, he was carried off with his brood;
For fear of the terrible Oscar, he did not let his soles touch the ground,
To the Valley of the Madmen <note>original: *Gleannabaltan* (Gleann na nGealt in County Kerry), where tradition says the King of France fled in a fit of madness after the battle</note>, as his reputation stands, it was there he took rest and stayed.
On the shore of Ventry by the sea, where the hosts performed the slaughter,
Over the kings of the strong world, we ourselves drank our fill of victory.
Many, from the fury of our weapons, were the warriors lying dead on the ground,
Many were the swords and shields, in pieces scattered on the shore.
On the shore of Ventry of the ports, many were the bodies of men,
Many heroes were under the laughter of the war-goddess, and many were the spoils of war.
O Patrick <note>Saint Patrick</note>, son of noble Alpin, none of our Fianna returned from the shore
Except for two ordered battalions without fault, and not a single body among them was whole.
One battalion of the Clan Baoisgne <note>The clan of Finn mac Cumhaill</note>, a group whose hands were not small,
One battalion of the Clan Moirne of the terrors, and the second with the Clan Smail.
By your hand, O strong holy one <note>original: *thailginn* (Adze-head), a common epithet for St. Patrick referring to his tonsure</note>, this is what we left of our Fianna in the slaughter:
Five ordered battalions of our host were lost from us upon the strand.
Thirty battalions without success—thirty times ten hundred in each battalion—
Of the number of Daire Donn’s host, who never reached the waves again.
O holy one to whom the clergy respond, since you desired every tale from me,
Until the Battle of Gabhra <note>The final, catastrophic battle that destroyed the Fianna</note> of the glens was fought, the end of our days had not come.
We took possession of their ships and the heavy silver of the king,
The gold, the armor, and the wealth, throughout every part of Ireland.
O good Patrick of the holy relics, shall I find death within your church?
Place the earth yourself upon my skin, for you are the one who knows my story.

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the narrative shifts from the bloody conclusion of the Battle of Ventry to a more reflective and elegiac tone.
