# 10 THE BOOK OF ANCIENT [GAELIC POETRY]

Oisín, since you are weary, make now your peace before death;
Give your turn to the church, and pray to God both morning and day.
On the Mount of Sion on Monday, and the hosts upon the ground,
May Michael and Mary and the Son of God take you by the hand;
The twelve apostles of the world, may every cleric and prophet pray
To stand between me and hell, for my days have been hard.

->Day.<-

->Author of this: Oisín, son of Finn <note>original: *Auctor hujus Osseane m‘fynn*</note><-

Weak tonight is the strength of my hand, my power is no longer on the earth;
There is no vigor in me; I am sorrowful, a wretched, aged man.
Wretched is the thing that has happened to me, more than to any man on the face of the earth:
The dragging of stones from the salt-marsh to the cemetery on the summit of the hill.
I have a tale for you, regarding our people, Patrick;
Listen to a little story of the past, O Adze-head <term>Adze-head</term> <note>original: *talgin* (Tailceann), a nickname for St. Patrick referring to the shape of his tonsure</note>.
A banquet was once prepared by the hero on the slopes of the bare, smooth Cooley;
It was given to the Fianna of Ireland, over whom the hero held command.
Two thirds of that noble banquet Finn gave to the Clan Morna <note>A rival faction within the Fianna often at odds with Finn’s own family</note>,
And the other third was for myself and for the Clan Baiscne <note>Finn Mac Cumhaill's own clan and immediate kin</note>.
I gave an answer that was not right to the son of Cumhall, son of Trénmhór <note>Finn Mac Cumhaill</note>;
I said I would not long be under his control, and would not perform his service.
Finn remained silent for a long time—that hero who was not easy to restrain—
A man without malice, without fear, when he heard that dark answer.
"This is the decision I would give to you," said the prince of the generous Fianna:
"You shall spend a time dragging stones, if you reach the state of wretchedness."
I rose up in great anger, I, the son of Finn of the red-gold sword;
I followed the Fianna across the country, through four bloody battles.
Though I am now with the Fianna <note>meaning the souls of his departed comrades</note>, my own judgment is brought upon me;
Among the people who were once in my house, even if I am now weak.
Harsh was the counsel given to the Fianna in the battle of Cronvone;
->Weak<-
Many a one who was not there—for that reason I am now weak.
->weak<-
Weak tonight is the chest of my body; I believe in the word of Patrick;
Between hand and foot and head, I am all weak, weak.
->Weak.<-

->The author of this is Oisín.<-

Here I saw the Fianna; I saw Conan and Goll,
Finn and Oscar my son, Raighne and Art and Diarmuid the brown-haired.

<summary>This page features Oisín in his old age, lamenting his physical decline and his labor for Saint Patrick. It includes a nostalgic retelling of a dispute between the Clan Morna and Clan Baiscne, framing Oisín's current suffering as a prophecy fulfilled by his father, Finn.</summary>

# MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE. 11

Oisín, since you are weary, make now your peace before death;
Give your prayers and seek mercy, and pray to God every morning.
On Mount Sion on the Day of Judgment <note>original: *la luain* (Monday), a traditional Gaelic term for the Day of Judgment</note>, with the multitudes upon the ground,
May Michael and Mary and the Son of God take you by the hand.
The twelve apostles in their glory, every good cleric and prophet,
May they stand between me and Hell, for my life has been hard.
->Day.<-

---

### The author of this is Oisín, son of Finn.

Weak tonight is the strength of my hand, my power is no longer on the earth;
It is no wonder I am sorrowful, a wretched, aged man in rags.
Wretched is everything that has been permitted to me, more than to any man on the face of the earth:
To be dragging stones for the salt-marsh, to the churchyard on the hill of the Adze-head <term>Adze-head</term> <note>original: *tailghin* (Tailceann), a nickname for St. Patrick referring to his tonsure</note>.
I have a tale for you, Patrick, about our noble people;
Listen to the prophecy of Finn, a short time before the arrival of the Adze-head.
A banquet was prepared by the hero on the bare, smooth Slieve Cooley;
He gathered there the causes of strife for the Fianna of Ireland <note>original: *Fianaibh Fail*</note>.
Two-thirds of that noble banquet Finn gave to the Clan Morna <note>The rival branch of the Fianna</note>,
And the other third of it to me, and to the Clan Baiscne <note>Finn and Oisín's own family</note>.
I gave a reply that was not fitting to the son of Cumhall, son of Trénmhór;
I said I would not long be under his command, nor would I yield to him.
Finn the hero remained silent for a long time—he was not easily provoked—
A man without blemish or fear, when he heard that dark reply.
This is the speech he spoke to me, Finn, the fierce lord of the Fianna:
"You shall spend a time dragging stones, before you enter into your sorrow."
Then my anger rose up against the son of Cumhall of the red-spear;
And I left the fierce Fianna with the fourth hard, brave battalion.
I was once a leader of the Fianna, and my own judgment was respected;
The people who once followed me are gone, and now I am weak.
I was the brave counselor to the Fianna in the battle of heavy blows.
->Weak,<-
Many a person unknown is there, from the earth I have become weak,
->weak,<-
Weak tonight is the chest of my body, I believe the words of Patrick;
In hand, foot, and head, I am all entirely weak, weak.
->Weak.<-

---

### The author of this is Oisín.

Here I saw the Fianna, I saw Conan and Goll;
Finn and Oscar my son, Roinn, and Art, and brown-haired Diarmad,

# THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT.

Mac Lughach <note>original: *M'lowith*, a prominent member of the Fianna and grandson of Finn</note>, Caince of the fierce blades, Red and Aodh the Little,
And Aodh, son of Gary of the shout, the three Finns and Fiachra,
Glas and Gaoi and Garaidh, Galva of the hundreds, and Conan the swift,
Goll and Cuinn, son of Cuall, the companions of Finn, and the hound Bran,
Caílte, son of Rónán <note>Finn’s nephew, famous for his incredible speed</note> of the deep battles, Coireall and the "Leap of the Glens,"
And Caedith who bestowed gold, and the man from the river Boyne who was so sweet-voiced,
Beine, son of Breasal of the blades, the son of Cromchinn, the strong son of Smail,
And Oscar, son of the rough Garaidh, the three silent ones and the three shadows,
Three Boynes of the satin glen, three Ruadhs from the royal Boyne,
Seven sons of Caílte of the cups, three Glasnis of the noble armor,
Three Beaths of the hill... <unclear>durt be veddeis</unclear> who were usually under great affection,
The ten sons of Eitheach of the Great Morna <note>The rival clan within the Fianna</note>, I saw them coming on the path.
Here I saw the Fianna, a troop of heroes all gathered for music,
Around Oisín and the clear-eyed one <note>referring to Finn</note> who bestowed gold.
Fear Lugh and hard Coireall who won victory in every battle,
Amidst music and treacherous valor, I saw them all here.

->The author of this is Oisín, son of Finn.<-

Tell us, O Patrick, for the honor of your learning:
Is there a heaven for the heroes of the Fianna of Ireland?
I tell you truly, Oisín of the exploits <note>original: *ni glooyn*</note>,
There is no heaven for your father, for Oscar, or for Goll.
"But sad is the tale you tell, O cleric;
Me practicing devotion while there is no heaven for the Fianna of Ireland.
Do you not think it good, old man, to be in the City <note>The City of God, or Heaven</note>,
Without Caílte, without Oscar, and without your father there with you?"
"Little good I find in being alone in that City,
Without Caílte, without Oscar, or my mother and father there with me."
"Better is the face of the Son of God to look upon for a single day,
Than all the gold in the world to have for yourself entirely."
"Tell us then, Adze-head <term>Adze-head</term> <note>original: *halgin* (Tailceann), a nickname for St. Patrick</note>, the stories of this new City;
And I will give you truly the story of the Battle of Gabhra <note>The final, tragic battle of the Fianna where most were killed, including Oisín's son, Oscar</note>.
If it is the story of the City you ask for, old man:
There is no nakedness, no hunger, no want, and no shame there."
"Who among the people of heaven is as noble as the Fianna of Ireland?
Is there a cross, or a heart, or... <unclear>deilli</unclear> like the path of the clerics?
The birds and the Fianna, they are not like each other <note>Oisín finds the quiet, structured life of the monastery alien compared to the wild freedom of the woods</note>;
The glory of the battle-cry was never bitter or meager.
For the love of the strong Patrick, do not leave them in darkness;
Without the knowledge of the new King, bring the Fianna inside."

### MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE. 13

Mac Lughaidh of the fierce blades <note>original: *geangach nan colg*</note>, Red Garaidh, and little Aodh,
And Aodh, son of Garaidh who was never timid, the three Finns and Fead,
Glas, and Gobh, and Garaidh, Galabh of the locks and swift Conan,
Goll and Crudhain, son of Goll, Socach, son of Finn, and the hound Bran,
Caoilte, son of Ronan <note>The swiftest of the Fianna</note> of the feats, a noble man of the race and the leap over the glen,
He was the first to bestow gold, and the man with the sweetest voice from top to bottom,
Baithean, son of Brasail of the blades, the son of swift Cromchinn, son of Smail,
And Oscar, son of rough Garaidh, the three Silent ones and the three Stories,
Three companies of Glen Sróil, three Ruaills from the King’s mountain,
Seven sons of Caoilte of the feats, three Glasas from Glasraidh of the freemen,
Three Beathachs of the hill of Duird, who were always held in high affection,

Around Ossian and Finn, traversing the glens and bestowing gold,
Fearluth and hardy Carroll, who would win victory in every battle,

->The author of this is Ossian, son of Finn.<-

Tell us, O Patrick, by the honor you hold:
Is there a heaven as joyful for the nobles of the Fianna of Ireland?
I tell you for certain, Ossian of the great deeds,
That your father, Oscar, and Goll have no place in heaven.
But wretched is the tale you tell me, cleric,
That I should practice devotion while the Fianna of Ireland have no heaven.
Would you not like to be alone, sitting in the heavenly chair <note>original: *caithir*, referring to a throne or a place in the celestial city</note>,
Without Caoilte, without Oscar, or your father by your side?
It is of little good to me to be sitting in that chair,
Without Caoilte, without Oscar, or my father by my side.
It is better to see the face of the Son of Heaven peacefully during your day
Than to have all the gold in the entire universe.
Tell us, O Adze-head <note>original: *Thailginn*, a traditional epithet for Saint Patrick referring to his tonsure</note>, the story of the holy city;
In return, I would give you the joyful tale of the Battle of Gabhra <note>The final, tragic battle of the Fianna</note>.
If it is the story of the city you seek, old man,
It is a place without thirst, without hunger, without want, and without blemish.
Though they are the people of heaven, the Fianna of Ireland are noble;
Is there such hardness in their hearts <note>The hearts of the saints</note>? Do not betray them, cleric.
They are not like the Fianna; they are not like one another,
They do not stand behind a locked door when someone seeks their cattle <note>An argument for the superior hospitality of the pagan heroes compared to the perceived stinginess of the new religious order</note>.
By your firm love, Patrick, do not leave out my companions;
Without the King of Heaven knowing, bring the Fianna inside.

<header>THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT MANUSCRIPTS].</header>

### ->The author of this is Ossian.<-

I know a little story of Finn, a story not to be ignored,
Of Mac Cumhaill <note>original: *v'cowle*; Fionn mac Cumhaill, the leader of the Fianna</note> of the goodly deeds; it has been a memory throughout my life.
We were but a few of our host at Eas Ruaidh <note>original: *essroyg*; Assaroe Falls, a famous fishing spot and site of legends in County Donegal</note> of the gentle waters;
We saw coming along the shore a great coracle <note>A traditional boat made of waterproof hides stretched over a wooden frame</note> with a woman within.
Fifty brown-haired warriors stood around the hero [Finn], whose deeds were good in every field;
Though there were men behind us, it would be a pity to see them, for we would take strength from every land.
We all rose up quickly, except for Finn of the Fians and Goll.
Toward us came the coracle at a high speed, her course splitting the waves;
There was no delay in her coming until she took harbor in the port.
Coming up from the waterfall, there rose out a maiden;
Brightness shone in her face <note>original: *Gilli a darli*; literally "brightness happened/appeared"</note>, noble was her form and appearance.
The maiden who came from afar, we stood before her pleasantly.
She came to us, to the tent of Finn, and greeted him with grace.
Mac Cumhaill alone answered her, with a greeting without fault.
The king of the goodly face asked loudly of the maiden of pure color:
"From what shore has the woman come? Give us your story quickly."
"I am the daughter of the King of the Land Under the Wave <note>original: *re heir fa hwne*; Tír fo Thuinn, a Celtic supernatural realm</note>; I will tell my case truly.
There is no shore under the sun where I have not sought a noble, generous hero."
"O princess of all the paths, O young maiden of goodly form,
The errand for which you came from afar, tell it to us now for certain."
"I seek your protection <note>original: *chomryth*; 'comairce' or 'protection/sanctuary' was a sacred duty in Gaelic society</note> if you are Finn," said the maiden's voice.
"Accept my word and my oath; I grant you my protection quickly."
The king of goodly knowledge asked, "Tell us now, what land do you flee from?
I take you under my protection, O woman, against every man in the world."
"There is a warrior coming over the sea, a hero of great valor on my track;
He is the son of the King of Sorcha <note>original: *re na Sorchir*; Sorcha is a mythical 'Land of Light,' often identified as a kingdom to the East or in the Otherworld</note>, sharp are his weapons; Daire Borb <note>Daire the Fierce</note> is his name.
I put bonds <note>original: *gessi*; 'geasa' or magical prohibitions/obligations</note> upon him that Finn would carry me over the sea,
And that I would not be his wife, though his deeds and youth be good."
Then Oscar spoke with a voice like a man who would check any king:
"Even if Finn had not granted his protection, you would not go with him as a wife."
We saw coming over the sea a warrior on a steed, in size above any man,
Traveling the sea quickly, in the same path the woman had taken.
A tight, sturdy helmet was upon his head; he was a man not timid, but strong.
A brown-rimmed shield hung on his left side; on his back was a collection of feats.
A heavy, powerful, mighty sword hung tight on the side of the great man.
He performed feats as he came toward the host <note>This refers to 'cleasa,' the stylized martial arts or acrobatic displays characteristic of Irish heroes</note>.
By his strength, his skill, and his valor, there was no man like him.
A prince’s face and a king’s eye were in the head of the man of the red locks;
Good was his appearance, white his teeth, and swift was his steed as it crossed the current.

<summary>This page contains the opening of the "Lay of the Maiden," where a princess from the mythical Land Under the Wave arrives at the camp of Finn mac Cumhaill seeking sanctuary. She is pursued by the formidable Daire Borb, whom the hero Oscar vows to defeat.</summary>

MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

# The author of this is Ossian.

I know a little story about Finn, a story not to be set aside,
Of the son of Cumhall <note>original: *Mac Cumhail*; Fionn mac Cumhaill, leader of the Fianna</note> of goodly valor; it has been a memory throughout my life.
We were a small host at Eas Ruaidh <note>original: *Easruadh*; the Falls of Assaroe in County Donegal, a site famous in legend for fishing and gatherings</note> of the slow eddies,
When we saw, under sail by the shore, a great coracle <note>A traditional boat of hide stretched over a wooden frame</note> with a woman within.
Fifty fierce warriors stood around the king, men of goodly deeds on every field;
As for the men we left behind, it would be a pity to see them, for we would take strength from every land.
We all rose up quickly, except for Finn of the Fians and Goll <note>Goll mac Morna, a high-ranking leader and rival-turned-ally of Finn</note>,
To watch the coracle of the highest prow, which was in its course splitting the waves.
It made no pause in its coming until it took harbor in its usual port.
Coming to the land of the waterfall, there rose out of it a maiden <note>original: *maca mnatha*; literally "child of a woman," a poetic term for a young woman</note>;
Brightness shone from her like the sun’s rays, and graceful was the nature of her form.
The maiden who came from afar—we ourselves were welcoming toward her.
She came to us, to the tent <note>original: *pubull*; likely a pavilion or temporary shelter used by the Fianna while hunting</note> of Finn, and greeted them with grace.
The son of Cumhall alone answered her with a sweet greeting without fault.
The king of goodly appearance asked loudly of the maiden of pure color:
"From what tribe has the woman come? Give us the news precisely."
"I am the daughter of the King of the Land Under Waves <note>original: *thir fo thuinn*; a supernatural realm in Irish mythology, often reached through water</note>; I tell my encounter truly.
There is no tribe under the circling sun that I have not asked for a generous hero."
"O princess who has traveled every path, O young maiden of goodly form,
Tell us truly the purpose for which you have come from afar."
"My protection upon you, if you are Finn," said the maiden to us,
"For the excellence of your speech and your host, take me under your protection quickly now."
The king of good knowledge said, "Tell us now who is pursuing you;
I take you under my protection, woman, against every man who has a heart."
"There is one coming with fury across the sea, a warrior of great valor on my track,
The son of the King of Sorcha <note>original: *Sorcha*; a mythical kingdom often translated as 'The Land of Light,' sometimes identified in later folklore with Portugal or an Eastern realm</note>, skilled with weapons, and Daire Borb is his name.
I placed vows <note>original: *geasan*; a "geas" is a binding taboo or solemn vow in Gaelic tradition that cannot be broken without great misfortune</note> against him, that Finn would take me upon the sea,
And that I would not be his wife, however great his deeds and his luck."
Oscar <note>The son of Ossian and grandson of Finn, known as the fiercest warrior of the Fianna</note> said with a spirited voice—he who could best every king:
"Though Finn should not help your vow, you would not go with him as a wife."
We see coming across the sea a steed, and a warrior in size above every man,
Traveling the ocean fiercely, following the same path the woman took.
A tight helmet was about his head—a man who was not timid but brave—
A round black shield on his left <note>original: *deas*; though 'deas' usually means right, in many old descriptions of armor it refers to the shield-side or the 'ready' side</note>, its back full of feats upon his chest.
A heavy, substantial sword, by no means small, was tight on the side of the great man;
He performed feats above his head as he came to meet the host.
His two spears of heroism with victory stood at the shoulder of his shield;
In strength, in heroism, in valor, there is no man so spirited as he.
The cloud of a prince and the eye of a king were in the head of the man of lovely form;
Goodly was his complexion and white were his teeth; swifter was his steed than any stream.

<summary>This page contains the "Lay of the Maiden," a classic Fenian ballad where a princess from the Land Under Waves seeks Fionn mac Cumhaill's protection from the giant Daire Borb. It describes her arrival at Assaroe and the subsequent appearance of her formidable pursuer.</summary>

### 16 THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT.

Then there came a steed <note>original: *stead*; likely referring to a knight on horseback</note> behind us, following the same path we had traveled. 
Fifty warriors were we there together, on the hill to meet him. 
For fear of the man and his heat, there was not a warrior among us without dread; 
As soon as he came in sight, the King <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill</note> of goodly fame spoke: 
"Do you know yourself, O woman, if that is the man of whom you spoke?" 
"I know him, son of Cumhall <note>original: *v'coulle*; Mac Cumhaill</note>, and it is a cold welcome I give his coming; 
He seeks to take me by force, despite my strength and my beauty." 
Oscar and Goll <note>Oscar was Fionn’s grandson, and Goll mac Morna was the leader of a rival clan within the Fianna</note> arose, fierce in the slaughter and the fury of battle; 
The Fianna stood near the host, between the great man and the prince. 
Then came the warrior of goodly attire, with fury and with the strength of his blade; 
And he snatched from us the woman who had been at our side. 
The son of Morna <note>original: *m'Morn*; Goll mac Morna</note> made a swift cast, hard and straight with his spear; 
The cast did not miss its mark, it went through the shield and split it in two. 
Oscar shook his spear in great anger, clutching it in his left hand; 
And he slew the horse of the great man—a great deed he performed there. 
When the steed fell on the plain, the rider turned with anger and fury, 
And the fierce man proclaimed a challenge of combat against fifty warriors. 
He turned toward us himself, and toward the fifty warriors who were not weak or pale; 
Though his heat and his stride were great, he dealt out death with his hand. 
He slew them two by two, and he slew them quickly, every man of them there; 
We were all under a cloud of sorrow, as we saw the combat of that man. 
He bound three times nine men <note>A common poetic grouping in Gaelic myth</note> in that hard struggle before he ceased; 
Hardly were the three bindings of the three groups put upon each one of them. 
Goll of the fierce spirit drew near to hew the man in the path before him; 
Though the struggle between them was long, that was a harsh and bitter contest. 
The son of Morna slew with his hand the King of Sora <note>original: *re nyth sorchir*; The King of Sorcha, a mythical "Land of Light" often located in the distant East or Scandinavia</note>—a great tale! 
Woe to the land from which the woman came, for whom that man fell in the path before us. 
And upon the fall of the great man, in the narrow, hard, and cruel step, 
The daughter of the King of the Land of the West <note>original: *re heir*; likely the King of the Land of the West or the Land Under-Wave</note> was with Finn and the Fianna for a year. 
Flann, son of Morna, hard in the struggle, died—it was a great sorrow; 
There was no warrior who came out of that fight without his skin full of wounds. 
I myself was there, of goodly form; anyone who saw me then would not believe it now, 
Now that I am at the end of my deeds—I know the story well. 
->I know the story.<-

---

He took him to his shield, or as the story says, into the boat that was there, 
Or else he dealt great slaughter upon our men, until he reached Finn himself.

---

Though there were fifty fierce warriors in the assembly, with armor enough, 
We were without help or power, when he took the tribute from us. 
The swords were without ceasing, cleaving bodies and shields; 
So good was the combat of those two, the like of which I saw not again in my time. 
We buried him by the waterfall, a man of great strength and deeds.

<summary>In this poem attributed to Ossian, a mysterious pursuer—the King of Sorcha—challenges the Fianna to reclaim a woman. After a devastating battle where he defeats many warriors, he is finally slain by Goll mac Morna, and the woman remains with Fionn for a year.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from page 16, where the warrior from the sea (the Son of the King of Sorcha) has just arrived and challenged the Fianna to reclaim the maiden who fled to Fionn for protection.</meta>

# MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

That steed came to land, and the man who was not gentle toward the Fianna <note>The Fianna were the legendary war-band of Fionn mac Cumhaill</note>.
Fifty warriors were we there, who went out to meet the man;
For fear of the man and his approach, there was no warrior without dread.
As he came to land from the waves, the King of goodly fame <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill</note> asked:
"Do you recognize him yourself, O woman? Is that the man you spoke of?"
"I recognize him, O Son of Cumhall, O Fionn; he is a source of grief for me and your Fianna.
He intends to carry me away with him, despite your great strength, noble Fionn."
Oscar and Goll <note>Oscar was Fionn’s grandson; Goll mac Morna was a famous leader of a rival clan within the Fianna</note> arose—fierce were the slaughterers in the heat of battle—
Standing close to the host, between the great man and the prince.
The warrior of handsome appearance came, with fury and with strength in his head;
And he snatched from us the woman, who had been near to Fionn's shoulder.
The Son of Morna <note>Goll mac Morna</note> made a swift cast, hard after him with the spear;
The cast was not weak, and it split the man's shield into two pieces.
Oscar, in great anger, shook his red strap <note>original: *chrios dhearg*; likely a spear-thong or weapon harness</note> from his left hand,
And he slew the man's steed—a great deed was performed there.
When the steed fell on the field, the rider turned with anger and fury,
And he proclaimed—a fierce fit of temper—a challenge of combat against the fifty warriors.
Facing me and Fionn, the fifty warriors were not timid;
Though their stance and their outcry were strong, they yielded to the slaughter of his hand.
He would deal two blows swiftly and vehemently to each of those men;
We would all have been laid under the earth, like helpless things <note>literally "earth-worms"</note> fighting a man.
He bound three-times-nine men <note>twenty-seven warriors</note> victoriously in that hard struggle before he stopped;
He placed the "binding of the three narrows" <note>a traditional Gaelic poetic term for a bond tightening the wrists, ankles, and neck</note> tightly upon each of them.
Goll of the swift spirit drew near to mangle the man who stood before him;
Whoever might see them there, fierce was their courage and their combat.
The Son of Morna felled by his hand the Son of the King of Sorcha <note>Sorcha: a mythical "Land of Light" often appearing in Gaelic tales as an otherworldly kingdom</note>—a great story!
Woe to the tribe from which the woman came, for whom the man fell nearby.
And upon the falling of the great man near the sea—a hard journey—
The daughter of the King of the Land Under the Waves <note>Tìr fo Thuinn: one of the Celtic Otherworlds</note> stayed a year with Fionn and the Fianna.
Flann mac Morna <note>likely referring to a specific warrior or a variant name for Goll's kin</note>, hard in trial, died—it was a great deed;
None of those who escaped were without a skin full of wounds.
My own father <note>Oisín, the traditional narrator of these poems</note> was of goodly appearance; never did he refuse a poor man.
And now this is the end of the deed; the story of Fionn is well-known to me.
<meta>catchword: The story is well-known to me.</meta>
->The story is well-known to me.<-

---

As there were fifty stout warriors, facing his weapons was enough for him;
We would have been without help under his power, as he took his due from us.
The sword would have been without silence, destroying bodies and shields;
As good a combatant on my right hand I never saw in my time.
He was buried by us at the waterfall, the man of great strength and deeds.

<meta>Continuing from page 16 and 17, following the battle against the mysterious warrior from the sea. The narrative now shifts from the action of the battle to a poignant eulogy for Fionn mac Cumhaill, spoken by his son, the poet-warrior Oisín.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT.

Placed under wrath <note>original: *wrayth*; possibly referring to the heavy burden of grief or the "wreath" of gold</note> with greatness under gold in honor of my king,
Ten years Goll <note>Goll mac Morna, Fionn’s former rival turned ally</note> was in arms, a great strength that the king did not slight;
The Son of Morna was at my side to heal me, with Fionn of the feasts.

->The author of this is Ossian, son of Fionn.<- <note>original Latin: *Actor hujus Ossane M'finn*</note>

It is a long time until today since I have seen Fionn;
I have never seen in my time one who was better than him.
The son of the daughter of the king of the heavy-tressed hosts,
Greatest in prosperity, my music and my hounds.
He was a poet, he was a prince, he was a king over valor;
Fionn, prince of the Fianna <note>term: *Fianna* (the legendary band of semi-independent warriors led by Fionn)</note>, was a lord over every land.
He was a great, noble soldier, he was generous on the plain;
He was a pure hawk of the wind, he was peace to every friend.
He was a giver of gifts, a rider of spirited horses;
He was valiant in deed, he was a pillar in every trial.
He was a man of true judgment, steady in his house;
He was the teacher of youth, he was a judge of victory.
He was the high messenger for courage and for music;
He refused the ignorant, and he loved the music of the harps.
His skin was like the chalk, his cheek like the rose;
Pure blue was his eye, his hair like the gold.
He was the hope of the learned and the poor, the shepherd of the herds;
He was noble in deed, and gentle toward women.
He was a great soldier, the son of Muirne <note>Muirne was Fionn's mother</note> of the noble heart;
The point of the blades, the tree above every branch.
The king was wealthy, his great halls were green-gray;
From the field where he took profit, he never failed . . .
. . . . . . . . . . . . white sorrow
. . . over the hosts he was brave and stout.
Defending the sun of Banba <note>term: *Banba* (a poetic name for Ireland)</note> and its women,
Until the prince fought thirty battles for her sake.
By the dread of God, the Son of Cumhall <note>Fionn mac Cumhaill</note> did not fail;
In his word he was good; no lie was ever heard in his mouth.
He never refused any man, whether for gold or for cattle;
There was nothing but the sun and the moon above his head <note>An idiom suggesting his fame and nobility reached the heavens</note>.
He feared no monster in the lake, nor any enemy in the night;
. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . .
No deed of his was told without truth;
No strength was hidden from me in the heat of battle.
But it is ill that I am, now that the Fianna are gone;
The prince has departed; though it was good to live after him.

<summary>A lyrical eulogy attributed to the poet Oisín, praising the physical beauty, moral character, and heroic leadership of his father, Fionn mac Cumhaill. The poem contrasts Fionn's legendary virtues with Oisín's current state of lonely old age after the fall of the Fianna.</summary>
<keywords>Oisín, Fionn mac Cumhaill, Fenian Cycle, eulogy, Gaelic poetry, Dean of Lismore, Muirne, Goll mac Morna, Banba</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the poetic cycle from the Dean of Lismore's Book. While previous pages described battles and the character of the warriors, this page provides a "modernized" transcription of a famous eulogy for Fionn mac Cumhaill, attributed to his son, the poet-warrior Oisín (Ossian). It focuses on Fionn's dual nature as both a fierce protector and a gentle, cultured patron of the arts.</meta>

MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE. 19

Placed on every finger is a ring of gold in honor of my king;
Ten years in weapons of power, the fierce hero did not diminish the king;
The Son of Muirne <note>original: *Mac Muirne*; Muirne was Fionn's mother. This refers to Fionn himself</note> was known to me, being healed by Fionn of the feasts.

### The author of this is Ossian, son of Fionn. <note>original Latin: *Auctor hujus*</note>

It is a long time until today since I have seen Fionn;
I have never seen in my time a hero who was better than him.
The son of the daughter of O'Tadhg <note>original: *O’ Theige*; referring to Tadg, Fionn's maternal grandfather, a powerful druid</note>, king of the heavy blows.
My joy and my luck, my wisdom and my hounds;
He was a poet, he was a prince, he was a king of valor.
Fionn the prince, king of the Fianna <note>term: *Fianna* (the legendary band of semi-independent warriors led by Fionn)</note>, was a lord over every land;
He was a great beast of the sea <note>A metaphor for a powerful, unstoppable champion</note>, he was generous on the field;
He was a pure hawk of the wind, he was peace to every craft;
He was a proper teacher, a sorrow that never deceived;
He was ready in deed, he was a foundation for every melody;
He was truly just in judgment, a dweller among the people;
He was learned in his luck, he was banner-bearing in victory;
He was the high messenger for courage and for music;
He never refused a guest <note>original: *diùltadh nan daimh*; a hallmark of Gaelic nobility was never turning away a stranger or a poet</note>, since he left the company of the harps.
His skin was like the chalk, his cheek like the rose;
Clear blue was his eye, his hair like the gold.
He was the hope of guests and men, the watcher of the fields of battle;
He was noble in deed, and gentle toward women.
He was a great hero, the son of Muirne of every plain;
He was the one who polished the blades, the tree above every wood.
The king was wealthy, with his great gray vessel
Of sharp, strong wine... <unclear>tairbh noch char threa</unclear>
. . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . of the fair belly,
. . . over the host, he of the sturdy step.
Winning the deed for fair Banbha <note>term: *Banbha* (one of the traditional poetic names for Ireland)</note>,
The prince fought three hundred battles for her sake.
In his bounty, Mac Cumhaill <note>Fionn’s patronymic: Son of Cumhall</note> did not hide;
It is said of him, no lie was ever heard in his mouth.
No refusal of anything did any man ever get from Fionn;
There was never under the sun a king above him.
He left no monster in a loch, nor venomous serpent
In Ireland of the saints, that the noble, gentle one did not kill.
I could not tell of his deeds, even if I were here until eternity;
I could not tell even a third of his victory and his goodness.
But I am in a poor state, now that Fionn of the Fianna is gone;
Every good thing that followed has passed away with the prince.

<vocab>Fionn, Ossian Mac Fhinn, Feinn, Eirinn, Banbha, Muirne, M'Cumhail</vocab>

<meta>This page continues Oisín’s lament for his father, Fionn mac Cumhaill, and the lost glory of the Fianna. It transitions from the general praise found on page 19 to a more personal expression of Oisín's grief in old age, before introducing a new poem by Allan MacRorie concerning the tragic death of the hero Diarmaid in Glen Shee.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF ANCIENT.

Without the great recognition <note>original: *angnow*; likely *aithne* (knowledge/recognition)</note>, without the pure beauty of the face,
Without the gold of the king's wife and without the strength of the heroes;
I am sorrowful in my weakness after the loss of the warriors;
I am the tree that trembles, I am the flesh on the bone;
I am the hollow nut, I am the horse without a bridle;
Wretched is my glory, I am the people without a chief;
I am Oisín, son of Fionn <note>original: *ossin m'fynn*</note>, once strong in my deeds,
Since Fionn is gone, everything is bare to me.
Seven sides were on the house of Mac Cumhaill <note>original: *m'kowl*</note> without...
Seven score skillful shields on every side of them;
Fifty around the hearth surrounding my king;
Fifty heroes without weakness in every single company;
Ten bright cups in the hall for drinking;
Ten blue spears, ten horns of gold.
But good was the household of Fionn of the Fianna <note>original: *finni ni vane*</note>;
Without stinginess, without strife, without...
Without contempt for any man for his...
Going on every path, everyone was at his command;
Fionn, prince of the host...
King of us all, at the red fort, who never refused;
Fionn never refused any man, however small his following;
He never sent anyone from his house who came there.
Good was the man Fionn, good was the man he was;
He never let anything pass that he did not overtake.
<meta>catchword: Sai.</meta>

---

->The author of this is Allan MacRorie.<-

Glen Shee <note>original: *Glennschee*; a valley in eastern Perthshire</note>, this glen beside me, where the deer and the elk are sweet;
Often the Fianna ran there, after this time in our day;
This glen beneath blue Ben Gulbin <note>original: *wenn Zwlbin*; a mountain famous in legend for the death of Diarmaid</note>, whose slopes are under the sun;
Where the streams ran red, after the hunt of Fionn of the Fianna.
Listen a little, if you wish to hear a story of the noble company,
Of Ben Gulbin and of Ireland <note>original: *fail*; Inis Fáil</note>, and of the son of O'Duibhne <note>original: *M'ezoynn*; referring to Diarmaid</note>, a tragic tale.
It was through Fionn, a sorrowful hunt, for the son of O'Duibhne of the red eyes,
To go to Ben Gulbin to hunt the boar that weapons could not tame.
By the son of O'Duibhne of the sharp weapons, though he was killed by the boar;
The most gallant lad that Fionn ever saw, and she told of your harm.
On whom the encounter happened, the son of O'Duibhne, beloved of the schools <note>original: *graw nin sgoll*; referring to the bardic schools who sang his praises</note>;
But here is the story that made women sorrowful, as he took the boar in his hands.
To grapple with the hero of the Fianna, who drove it from the hill;
The old boar of the fairy hill <note>original: *tork schee*</note>, it was fierce, a tusked beast of the herd of swine.

<meta>This page continues the transition from Oisín’s personal lament for the lost glory of the Fianna—the legendary band of warriors led by his father—into a specific narrative poem. The second half of the page introduces a poem by Allan MacRorie regarding the tragic hunt at Ben Gulbin, a famous episode in Gaelic mythology involving the hero Diarmaid.</meta>

### MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Without the recognition <note>original: *angnath*; likely *aithne*, meaning recognition or acquaintance</note> of any great one, without the pure honor of the wind,
Without gold and kingly women, and without the judgment of the heroes.
I am sorrowful in my weariness after the leader of hundreds,
I am the trembling tree, and my hair has left me,
I am the hollow nut, I am the horse without a bridle,
Wretched am I now, I am the people without a dwelling,
I am Oisín <note>The narrator, son of Fionn mac Cumhaill, known for his poetry and for surviving into old age after the rest of the Fianna had died.</note>, son of Fionn, at but a third of my former deeds;
As long as Fionn lived, everything was mine.
Seven sides were to his house, the son of Cumhall of the feasts;
Seven score feat-shields <note>Shields used for displaying martial skill or "feats."</note> were on every one of those sides,
Fifty drinking-suits <note>original: *uidheam olaidh*; likely referring to sets of formal attire or the equipment used for ceremonial drinking</note> surrounding my king,
Fifty heroes without anxiety in every one of those companies,
Ten white cups in his hall for drinking,
Ten blue goblets, ten horns of gold;
Truly good was the household that Fionn of the Fianna <note>term: *Feinn* or *Fianna*; the legendary band of semi-independent warriors in early Ireland and Scotland.</note> possessed,
Without churlishness, without lust, without vanity, without hoarding,
Without contempt there for any single man of his Fianna;
In proceeding with every matter, the others were in accord with him.
Fionn, prince of the host, whose praise is a whisper of fame,
King of every fortune, who never refused any man;
Fionn never refused anyone, however small his worth,
He never cast out from his house anyone who came there.
A good man was Fionn, a good man was he,
No one ever bestowed gifts like those he bestowed.
->He.<-

---

->The author of this is Allan MacRorie.<-

Glen Shee <note>original: *Gleannsith*; a valley in Perthshire, Scotland, traditionally associated with the death of Diarmaid.</note>, this glen beside me, where the deer and the elk are sweetest-voiced,
Often did the Fianna run upon this strath <note>term: *strath*; a wide mountain valley.</note> following their dogs;
This glen below blue Ben Gulbin, the loveliest hill under the sun,
Where the streams run red after the hunt of Fionn and the Fianna.
Listen a little to how a hero departed—O dear company, hear me—
Concerning Ben Gulbin and generous Fionn, and the son of O'Duinn <note>Diarmaid ua Duibhne, a central hero of the Fenian cycle, famous for his beauty and his tragic death.</note>, a sad tale:
To Fionn, it was a sorrowful hunt for the son of O'Duinn of the ruddiest face,
Going to Ben Gulbin to hunt the boar that no weapon could ever harm.
By the son of O'Duinn of the lucky arms, it was destined that the boar should fall;
Yielded before him by Fionn's deceit—it was he who did the harm.
The man who inspired love, the son of O'Duinn, the darling of the schools <note>original: *sgoil*; likely referring to the groups of warriors or poets who gathered around the hero.</note>,
But here is the tale that makes women mourn: he took the boar in hand,
A match for the hero of the Fianna, he drove it from the hill,
The old boar, the fiercest ever seen; the wild pig's bellow was heard.

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues the poem attributed to Allan MacRorie, transitioning into the central tragedy of the Fenian cycle: the death of Diarmaid O’Duibhne. The previous page established the setting and Fionn mac Cumhaill’s growing resentment toward Diarmaid.</meta>

### THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT AUTHORS].

Fionn of the dark countenance sat, by green Ben Gulbin <note>original: *Zwlbin*; likely Ben Gulabin in Perthshire, though in Irish tradition it is Ben Bulben in Sligo.</note> of the many hunts,
He sent Diarmaid <note>original: *dimit*; Diarmaid O’Duibhne, the hero of the Fianna known for his beauty and his "love spot" that made him irresistible to women.</note> to the boar, the great, fierce beast of the hunt.
To hear the murmuring of the Fianna, as their weapons approached the peak,
As the beast woke from its sleep, and moved away from the glen.
The hero set out to find it, the boar of the magic hill <note>original: *tork schee*; a supernatural or "sidhe" boar.</note> in fierce wrath,
Its tusks were sharper than a spear, it was more powerful than the *Gáe Bulg* <note>term: *Gáe Bulg*; the legendary notched spear of the hero Cuchulainn, used here as a comparison for the boar’s ferocity.</note>.
Mac O'Duibhne <note>original: *M'ozunn*; the phonetic spelling used by the Dean for Diarmaid’s patronymic.</note> of the sharp weapons replied to the evil beast;
With his royal side and heavy, venomous spear, he cast it at the boar.
The shaft was broken in three, on the back of the swine;
The spear from his heroic hand did not enter its body.
He drew the thin blade from its sheath, that weapon that won many victories;
Mac O'Duibhne killed the beast, and he himself remained whole.
Then sorrow fell upon Fionn of the Fianna, and he turned to the hill;
Mac O'Duibhne, who never refused a request—Fionn was sorry he had come safely from the boar.
After staying a long time in silence, Fionn spoke with deceit:
"Measure, O Diarmaid, with your feet, how many feet are in this boar."
He did not refuse Fionn's request—sorrowful is it that he did not return home;
He measured the boar on its back, Mac O'Duibhne of the light step.
"Measure it again, Diarmaid, measure the boar gently,
Against the grain of the bristles <note>Fionn’s trick: the boar possessed venomous bristles, and by making Diarmaid measure against the grain, he ensured a bristle would pierce Diarmaid's heel, his only vulnerable spot.</note>, O youth of the sharp and bitter weapons."
He went on that journey of sorrow, and measured the boar for them;
The sharp, poisonous bristle pierced the foot of the hero, fierce in the fight.
He fell there on the field, Mac O'Duibhne who never practiced deceit,
Lying by the side of the boar—that was a bitter sight for you.
There he lies now under the clay, Mac O'Duibhne of the handsome curls,
The bloody champion of the Fianna, in this tomb under the earth.
The blue-eyed hawk of Assaroe <note>original: *essroye*; a famous waterfall in Donegal, frequently mentioned in Fenian poetry.</note>, the man who won victory in every battle,
After being slaughtered by the boar, he lies here beneath the summit of this hill.
Diarmaid Mac O'Duibhne, the noble, who fell in the time of jealousy <note>original: *nin noor*; referring to the hour of Fionn’s envy over his wife Gráinne’s love for Diarmaid.</note>;
Whose neck was whiter than the sun, whose lips were redder than the blossom.
Beautiful was his hair, his long eyes were clear and calm,
His eyes were blue and grey, the most beautiful curls of hair.
Sweetness and grace were in his voice, his skin was white and bright;
Stature and strength were in the hero, slender and soft was his fair skin.
The companion and charmer of women, Mac O'Duibhne of great victories;
No one could look upon his face without their eyes being drawn to its beauty.
Better were your clothing and horses, O man of the heavy plunder whom I loved;
A youth of the highest valor and peace—but alas, you are now in this glen.

<meta>catchword: Glenshee.</meta>

<summary>This page contains the climax of the "Lay of Diarmaid," detailing how Fionn mac Cumhaill, driven by jealousy, tricks Diarmaid into measuring a venomous boar against the grain of its bristles. This leads to Diarmaid's tragic death and concludes with a poignant lament for his unmatched beauty and bravery.</summary>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Lay of Diarmaid" from the Dean of Lismore's Book. Following Fionn mac Cumhaill’s growing resentment because Diarmaid eloped with his wife Gráinne, Fionn has organized a hunt for the magic boar of Ben Gulbin, knowing Diarmaid has a fatal vulnerability in his heel.</meta>

### [MODERN TRANSCRIPTION.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Fionn of the ruddy face is joyful, beneath green Ben Gulbin <note>original: *Bheinn Ghulbain*; Ben Gulbin in Glenshee, Perthshire, the legendary site of this tragedy in Scottish tradition.</note> of the chase,
The hunt went out for the boar; great was the evil that hunting wrought.
Hearing the collective shout of the Fianna <note>term: *Fianna*; the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>, as their weapons closed in around it,
The beast rises from its sleep, and moves away through the glen.
The old boar sets out to leave the heroes, and he is in a fierce rage;
He was sharper than the points of spears, his charge stronger than the *Gae Bulg* <note>term: *Gath Bolga*; the "spear of mortal pain," a legendary weapon often associated with the hero Cuchulainn, used here to describe the boar's lethal power.</note>.
Mac O'Duibhne <note>original: *Mac O’Dhuinn*; Diarmaid Mac O'Duibhne, the hero of the poem, renowned for his beauty and skill.</note> of the sharp weapons answers the evil beast;
From his side a heavy, venomous spear was cast; he sends the spear against the boar.
The shaft is broken by him in three, for the shaft was no match for the swine;
The spear from his red-palmed, smooth hand—it did not enter its body.
He draws the thin blade from its sheath, which won great victory in arms;
Mac O'Duibhne kills the beast, and he himself came away unharmed.
Gloom falls upon Fionn of the Fianna, and he sits upon the hill;
Mac O'Duibhne, who never refused a guest <note>original: *daimh*; meaning friends or kin, implying Diarmaid's noble character.</note>—it was evil in Fionn's sight that he survived the boar.
After being long in silence, he said—though it was a wicked thing to say—
"Measure, O Diarmaid, from its snout, how many feet the boar is."
He did not refuse Fionn's request—alas for us that he never returned home!
He measures the boar along its back, Mac O'Duibhne of the light step.
"Measure it against the bristles <note>original: *’n a aghaidh*; literally "against it," meaning against the grain of the hair.</note> again, O Diarmaid, measure the boar carefully;
For it is a sorrowful task for you, O youth of the sharp-edged weapons."
He goes—it was a journey of anguish—and he measures the boar for them;
A sharp, venomous bristle pierces the sole of the hero who was fierce in fight.
He falls there upon the field, Mac O'Duibhne—there was no joy in this treachery;
Lying there by the side of the boar—there he is for you, in a hard state.
He is there, suffering from his wounds, Mac O'Duibhne, beloved in the struggle;
The one enduring son of the Fianna, on this hill you see him laid low.
The blue-eyed hawk of Assaroe <note>original: *Easruaidh*; the waterfall at Ballyshannon, a place often used in poetic titles for Irish heroes.</note>, the man who won every victory,
After being brought down by a boar, beneath the brow of this hill he lies.
Great Diarmaid Mac O'Duibhne, fallen through the jealousy of gold <note>original: *eud an òir*; literally "jealousy of gold," but likely referring to Fionn's envy or the price of his betrayal.</note>.
Whiter was his breast than the sun, redder his mouth than the blossom...
Yellow and lovely was his long hair, a clear eye beneath his brow,
Blue and grey was his eye, beauty and curl in the locks of his hair.
Sweetness and elegance were in his voice, whiteness in his red-palmed, smooth hand;
Stature and effectiveness in the hero, slender and noble beneath his white skin.
A champion and a charmer of women, Mac O'Duibhne of the great virtues;
He will not lift his eye in the tower, from the fresh color on his cheek.
Leader of the hunt and of horses, a man who never loved the distress of plunder;
The lad who was best in valor and peace—but sad is how he perished in the glen.

Glenshee <note>original: *Gleannsith*; The "Glen of the Fairies," the traditional setting of the poem.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the climax of the Lay of Diarmaid, where Fionn mac Cumhaill tricks the hero into measuring a venomous boar against the grain of its bristles. Diarmaid is fatally poisoned in his heel, leading to a mournful elegy describing his legendary beauty and prowess.</summary>
<keywords>Diarmaid, Fionn mac Cumhaill, Ben Gulbin, Glenshee, Fenian Cycle, boar hunt, betrayal, elegy, Scottish Gaelic poetry, Highland folklore</keywords>

--- Page 302 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific tragedy of Diarmaid’s death to the wider destruction of the Fianna at the Battle of Gabhra. The poem is attributed to Allan MacRory, though it is voiced by the legendary poet-warrior Oisín, who recounts the events to Saint Patrick. This manuscript, known as the Book of the Dean of Lismore, is famous for its "phonetic" spelling of Gaelic using Middle Scots orthography.</meta>

### The author of this is Allan MacRory. <note>original: *A houdir so seiss Allan M'Royre*; Allan MacRory was a 15th-century bard.</note>

Great is my own grief tonight, O holy man <note>original: *a halgin*; likely referring to Saint Patrick, to whom Oisín tells his tales.</note>, as I recall
The memory of that hard battle we fought against Cairbre of the red spear <note>original: *carbryth cranroy*; Cairbre Lifechar, the High King of Ireland.</note>,
The son of Cormac, son of Conn <note>original: *ochwnni*; referring to the lineage of Conn of the Hundred Battles.</note>, whom we met under a heavy yoke,
A king without weakness in the fight, a king without fear in his deeds.
We gathered all our strength under my command, and the fair clans of Conn;
Powerful was that red Cairbre, who thought of nothing but evil and malice.
Cairbre spoke with swift words—and his spirit was hardened—
Saying it was better for him to fall on the field with all the Fianna together,
Than for the kings of the world to be alive and the Fianna to possess Ireland.
The High King spoke quickly: "Remember Muireadhach and Art <note>original: *mwkre agis art*; ancestral figures of the royal line.</note>,
Our ancestors who fell there through the treachery of the Fianna."
"Remember the hard conditions, and remember the many causes [of our strife],
And that there was no power in war before us, except for the sons of Cumhall <note>original: *vakkowle*; refers to the Mac Cumhaills, Fionn’s clan.</note>."
This was the counsel of the clans of Conn and of Cairbre on that heavy day:
That they themselves should give us battle and destroy us utterly,
So that no remnant of us would live to see another year, nor any of the Fianna remain in Scotland <note>original: *nalwin*; Alba.</note>.
They chose death by our hands rather than that we should be as we ought to be.
That fierce and angry king brought about that battle—the Battle of Gabhra <note>term: *Cath Gabhra*; the legendary final battle of the Fianna where the order was destroyed.</note>.
The Fianna fell there, sole to sole, along with the kings of all Ireland;
There was no one from the East or the West, to the farthest edges of the world,
No king who was not under our thumb until that battle, O holy man.
From that time forward we did not receive tribute or tax,
And no one shared with us, except for our own portion of the land of Ireland.
Many throughout the great world will not be alive after that host,
Those warriors who fell, day by day, one by one.
If twenty foreign ships had come at that time to Ireland's shores,
Oisín <note>original: *Ossin*; the narrator, son of Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>, what would we have done, we who survived for our Ireland?
<margin>✻</margin>By your hand, O holy cleric <note>term: *clèireach*; a common address for Saint Patrick in these poems.</note>, there was not in fair Ireland
But a small few of our heroes left, and youths without strength.
Though a king might have stood there, he would have had Ireland for free,
Without battle, without struggle, without terror, without violence, without reproach.
We sent our messengers to the son of Conn,
To tell him that we would no longer serve the kings of Ireland.
Great was the silence that fell upon us, O king of Tara <note>original: *reith taureith*; the High King's seat.</note>, it was a great sound;
A flood of grief came over us as we went to the slaughter.
Oisín, tell us the news of that fierce struggle,
How your son fell in the battle, and what you said of him.
Oscar <note>original: *Oskin*; Oisín’s son, the greatest warrior of the later Fianna.</note>, my son Oscar—I found him after the slaughter was done;
Then came Caílte <note>original: *Keiltyth*; one of the few surviving Fianna leaders.</note>, the swiftest of the clan;
The noble hero of our band came to us, he found Oscar in his own blood.

<summary>Oisín recounts the catastrophic Battle of Gabhra to Saint Patrick, explaining how the High King Cairbre Lifechar sought to destroy the Fianna to break their power over Ireland and Scotland. The page ends with Oisín discovering his dying son, Oscar, on the battlefield.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the downfall of the Fianna, moving from the death of individual heroes to the collective tragedy of the Battle of Gabhra. The speaker is Oisín, the last of the Fianna, who is recounting these events to Saint Patrick. The poem is attributed to Allan MacRory, a 15th-century poet, though it deals with much older legendary material.</meta>

[MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE. 25

->The author follows here, Allan MacRory.<-

Great tonight is my own mourning, O cleric <note>original: *a thailginn*; a term for a shaven-headed monk, specifically referring to Saint Patrick to whom Oisín is speaking.</note>, as I yield to what is destined,
Thinking of the hard battle we and red-speared Cairbre fought,
That son of Cormac, grandson of Conn; woe to the band that fell under his yoke!
A king without hesitation, sent to the battle without dread for his life.
He bound us, all the Fianna <note>term: *Fianna*; the legendary band of independent warriors led by Fionn Mac Cumhaill.</note>, to Fionn and the dear children of Conn;
Until that red Cairbre came, we thought of no evil or strife.
Cairbre spoke to the host—and it was a harsh saying—
That he would rather fall upon the plain, and the Fianna with him,
Than for the kings of the world to be under the earth and the Fianna ruling Ireland.
Barruinn spoke quickly: "Remember Muireadhach and Art <note>Ancestral figures of the High King’s royal line.</note>,
Our ancestors who fell there by the treacherous judgment of the Fianna;
Remember the hard vows, and remember the great insult,
And that there was no war in their time except that which the son of Cumhall <note>Fionn Mac Cumhaill, Oisín’s father.</note> raised."
The counsel of the clans of Conn and of heavy-handed Cairbre was
To give themselves to our destruction and to wound us,
So that a feast might last after them without the Fianna being in Almu <note>original: *Almhainn*; the Hill of Allen in County Kildare, the traditional seat of the Fianna.</note>,
And death would be an honor when duty no longer exists.
We fought fiercely and angrily that battle, the Battle of Gabhra <note>The catastrophic final battle where the Fianna were destroyed by the forces of the High King.</note>.
The Fianna fell man for man, along with the noble kings of Ireland.
From India in the east to Ireland <note>original: *Fodla*; an ancient poetic name for Ireland.</note> in the west of the world,
There was no king who was not under our control in our time until that battle, O cleric;
Since our slaughter was carried out there, no tax or tribute was taken for us,
And no man feared us, but only their portion of the land in Ireland.
Many across the great world who are no longer present after that slaughter;
It is no idle hand-to-hand fight to fall by one another,
Should fierce foreigners then come upon them in pure-landed Ireland.
"Oisín," [asked the cleric], "what would Fionn do, and the remnants across Ireland?"
By your hand, O holy cleric <note>original: *a chléirich chaidh*</note>, there were none in fair Ireland <note>original: *Bhanbha*; another ancient name for Ireland.</note>
But a few aged heroes and youths without proof;
Whosoever king should stand there would get Ireland for nothing,
Without battle, without strife, without success, without honor, without reproach.
We sent our noble messengers to the grandson of Conn <note>Cairbre Lifechar.</note>.
They went from us to meet him to take the kingship of Ireland;
Great was that house that departed from us, for the King of Tara <note>original: *Teamhra*; the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland.</note> was of great fame—
Another reason that brought about the killing of us all.
"Oisín, tell them the story of when you fought the great struggle;
When your son fell in the battle, did you catch his words?"
Over my son, the noble Oscar, I came after the slaughter was made;
Caoilte then came over his seven children;
Those of our Fianna who were still alive came over their own friends.

<meta>This page continues Oisín’s lament to Saint Patrick regarding the Battle of Gabhra. Having described the gathering of the armies and the initial conflict on the previous page, the narrative now shifts to the devastating aftermath: the discovery of his dying son, Oscar, the greatest champion of the Fianna.</meta>

[ANCIENT.] THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE. 26

Those who spoke of us are gone; others remain without leaders.
O cleric of the white staff <note>original: *baichil bane*; the "Bachall Isu" or Staff of Jesus, the legendary crozier of Saint Patrick.</note>, though the account is hard to bear,
Great was the sorrow in our time to see the nobles of Ireland fall.
Many a protecting, beautiful helmet; many a sturdy, heroic man;
Many a shield shattered in the fight, and many a king’s son left without life.
None of that host escaped who were upon that field of victory;
None brought us word from the battle, save for the king in his high majesty.
I found—great was my grief—my own son lying there,
Propped upon his left elbow, his shield shattered on the plain,
And his blade still gripped in his right hand.
Beautiful Oscar <note>Oscar was the son of Oisín and grandson of Fionn Mac Cumhaill; he is the quintessential tragic hero of the Fenian cycle.</note> on every side was bleeding from his wounds.
I lowered my side to the ground and remained standing over him;
I thought of what I should do after he was gone.
Oscar looked up at me, and it was enough of a cross for me to bear.
He reached out his hand to me, struggling to meet me.
I took the hand of my own son and sat down at his side;
And since the day I did that, I have placed no value on the world.
My brave son said to me, even as he was losing his weapons:
"O you of the dark eyes, it is well that his father is safe."
I could make no answer to them, for I had no words to give.
Then great Caoilte <note>term: *Caoilte mac Rónáin*; a cousin of Fionn known for his incredible speed and as one of the few survivors of the Fianna.</note> came to us, approaching Oscar;
The son of Rónán asked in a whisper: "How does my beloved friend fare?"
"I am as Oscar must be," he said, "departing to the world of spirits;
The spear of red Cairbre <note>Cairbre Lifechar, the High King of Ireland, who died by Oscar’s hand even as he dealt Oscar this mortal blow.</note> has pierced through Oscar’s navel."
Caoilte felt the wound carefully, searching for the point of the spear;
He searched his back, for the wound went through to the spine.
The son of Rónán shrieked at that and fell to the ground.
Caoilte said: "A thousand pities that you are facing your end,
Truly, O noble Oscar, you are parting from us today."
The battle-strength of Fionn’s host has departed; the tribute is now with the great race of Conn.
We were there for a short time like that, O son of Alpin, O cleric <note>Patrick is often addressed as "son of Alpin" in these ballads, likely a corruption of his father's name, Calpurnius.</note>;
Until we saw coming toward us from the west the remnant of the people of Ireland.
Twenty hundred of them were there, both young and old;
Not a single man among them was whole from that two thousand,
Save for one man in nine, and even they were weak with wounds.
We lifted the noble Oscar up on the shafts of our spears,
We bore him to the hill of the valley, to take off his armor.
From the palms of his hands to his body, no part was sound,
Until we reached the soles of his feet—nothing but his face alone.
That night we remained there, refusing to leave the body until day,
Until the Clan of Baoisgne <note>term: *Clann Baoisgne*; the family of Fionn Mac Cumhaill, the core lineage of the Fianna.</note> raised him up on high, beautiful hills.
No man ever lamented his own son, nor did a brother ever lament a brother,
As everyone who saw my son there lamented the death of Oscar.

### [MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE. 27

Some of them spoke of that, while others remained without life.
O cleric of the white staff <note>original: *a chleirich a bhachuil bhàin*; Oisín is addressing Saint Patrick, referencing the saint's famous crozier.</note>, whoever might have seen that slaughter,
It was a great misery in our time to see the nobility of Ireland fall.
Many a fair, sturdy helmet; many a noble, spirited breastplate;
Many a shield lay across the plain, and its lord without life.
That host was not gloomy, for they were a race who had known victory;
We brought nothing with us from the battle but the spoils of kings and high heroes.
It was there I found my own son lying on his left elbow,
His shield shattered on the ground, and his blade in his right hand.
With beautiful blood on every side, it flowed over the links of his breastplate.
I rested the butt of my spear on the ground and stood over him in silence;
I thought of his loss then, and what I should do after he was gone.
Oscar turned his gaze up toward me, and his suffering <note>original: *a chrois*; literally "his cross," used here to mean his torment or agony.</note> was enough for me to bear.
He stretched out his hand to me, desiring my presence near him.
I took the hand of my own son and gave a bitter cry,
And from that moment onward, I have cared nothing for the world.
My own son spoke to me, a man on the very edge of life:
"I thank the elements <note>original: *na dùilibh*; the natural forces or "the gods" in a pre-Christian sense.</note> that you are safe, father."
I will tell no lie: I had no answer for him.
Then great <term>Caoilte</term> <note>Caoilte mac Rónáin, the famed swift runner of the Fianna and Oisín's companion.</note> came to us to see Oscar.
The blessed Son of Ronan said, "How is it with you, my beloved?"
"I am," said Oscar, "as is right, departing toward the world of spirits."
The spear of Red Cairbre <note>Cairbre Lifechar, the High King who fought the Fianna at Gabhra.</note> had pierced beneath Oscar’s navel;
Caoilte’s hand went in up to the elbow as he searched the spear-wounds.
Caoilte searched the wound properly and found the truth of it:
He found the back cleanly pierced, cut through by that sharp spear.
The Son of Ronan screamed then and fell to the earth;
Brave Caoilte spoke, as a dark cloud of grief passed over him:
"It is the truth, noble Oscar, you are parting from the <term>Feinn</term> <note>The Fianna, the legendary band of warriors led by Fionn mac Cumhaill.</note>."
"And battle parts from the Fianna; the hardship will remain with the great seed of Conn." <note>The "seed of Conn" refers to the royal dynasty of the High Kings, descendants of Conn of the Hundred Battles.</note>
We were there only a short time, O Son of Alpin, O cleric,
Until we saw coming from the slaughter all who remained alive of the Fianna of Ireland.
Twenty hundred men arrived, both the young and the old;
But not one man of those twenty hundred was whole—
The "healthiest" man had nine venomous wounds, and many had more.
We lifted the fierce Oscar high on spear-shafts,
And bore him to a lovely hill to remove his armor.
There was not a palm’s width of his body whole from his hair
Down to the very soles of his feet, except for his face alone.
In the fortress and the field, his body yielded until the day;
We raised the children of the Fianna on those high, joyful hills.
No man wept for his own son, nor did he weep for his brother at the last;
Seeing my son in such a state, everyone else was mourning Oscar.

# THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT.

Briefly were we in that state, leaning over that fair, white body
Until we saw coming toward us at evening **Finn mac Cumhaill**, son of Trénmór <note>original: *m'kowle vic tranevor*; Trénmór was the great-grandfather of Oscar and founder of the clan.</note>.
Until they gathered there in their strength to make a protective wall
Upon finding the children of <term>Baoisgne</term> <note>The royal lineage of the Fianna to which Finn and Oisín belonged.</note> in the slaughter, within a wall of blood in that place.
Great was the crying of the men and the shrieking of the soldiers
Until we saw the banner of Finn on spear-shafts above us.
They came toward us from the field of slaughter; we took up our places.
We all saluted Finn, but he did not answer us.
He went to the mound of the strong, where **Oscar** of the sharp arms lay.
When Oscar perceived Finn coming to stand over him,
He raised his bloodied face <note>original: *neachla*; literally "unclean" or "clouded," referring to the gore of battle.</note> and saluted his grandfather.
Then Oscar said to the son of Muirne <note>original: *m'murnaith*; Finn was the son of Cumhall and Muirne.</note> at that hour:
"I welcome death now, since sharp-armed Finn has come."
"Pity, O Oscar of the slaughter," [said Finn], "O God, son of my own son.
I am weak after you, and Ireland is a wandering wilderness after you."
<margin>A curse upon the day.</margin>
"A curse on that victorious King <note>likely referring to Death or Fate</note>, for it is your end that has silenced him."
My spirit was wounded... <note>text unclear: likely referring to the fading light of evening mirroring the fading life.</note>
"Farewell from me to the quest, and farewell to every tribute to be lifted;
Farewell to all good things from me now, for I would not be in this state [if you lived]."
Upon hearing the thin moaning of the **Fianna**, the soul of Oscar departed;
He stretched out both hands and closed his clear, bright eyes.
Finn turned his back to us and shed tears gloomily;
Except for Oscar and for **Bran** <note>Finn’s favorite hound, often cited as the only other creature Finn truly wept for.</note>, he never shed a tear on earth.
Save for myself and Finn, there was no one left to stand over him.
They gave three cries in that hour, which were heard throughout cold Ireland.
Five score hundred and ten hundred of the Fianna fell at **Gabhra**;
Of the dead upon the plain, there was not one man without his loss.
Twice that number—it is no lie—and the King of Ireland, a story of more death;
There were dead on the other side, of the clean-armed hosts of Ireland.
Finn was never cheerful or at ease from then until his death came.
From that day, it was no better for the kings...
From that battle, the Battle of Gabhra, I have made no more great songs;
There was not an hour of the day or night that I did not heave a great sigh.

->Great is the loss.<-

### The author of this below is Fergus the Poet. <note>original: *Farris filli*; Fergus was the brother of Oisín and the chief herald and poet of the Fianna.</note>

High the mind of **Goll**, the man of Finn’s wars,
A fierce, leaping hero, a man who is not timid.
Noble and generous, of the fair and graceful form,
A leader of the host, of the fair and bloody hand,
The son of **Morna** <note>Goll mac Morna, the leader of a rival faction within the Fianna who eventually became Finn's most loyal but tragic ally.</note> the Great, of the heart of gold...

[MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

Briefly were we in that state, leaning over that fair, white body,
Until we saw coming toward us at evening, **Finn mac Cumhaill**, son of Trénmór <note>original: *Fionn mac Cumhail mhic Threinmhoir*. Finn is the legendary leader of the Fianna.</note>.
They brought into the field of slaughter a third of the living <term>Fianna of Ireland</term> <note>original: *Fiannaibh Fail*. The Fianna were a semi-independent band of warriors.</note>,
Upon finding the children of <term>Baoisgne</term> <note>The royal clan of the Fianna.</note> in the slaughter, within a wall of blood in the heat of battle;
The men were very broken and the warriors were a gruesome sight;
Until we saw the banner of Finn on a spear-shaft above us,
They came toward us from the slaughter; we went to meet them,
We all saluted Finn, but he did not answer us,
Going to the mound of the strong, where **Oscar** of the sharp arms lay.
When Oscar perceived Finn coming to stand over him,
He raised his steadfast face and saluted his grandfather.
Then Oscar said to the son of Muirne <note>original: *Mac Muirne*; Finn’s mother was Muirne of the White Shoulder.</note> at that hour:
"I welcome death now, since I have seen you, sharp-armed Finn."
"Alas, O fierce Oscar," [said Finn], "good son of my own son,
I am weak after you, and after the loss of the Fianna of Ireland."
"A curse upon the man of victory <note>original: *a fhir co 'm buaidh*; likely referring to the High King Cairbre or the personification of Fate.</note>, for it is the darkest thing that has come upon my people."
It followed me from the east, or wherever I should go in a gathering;
"Farewell from me to the battle and the glory; farewell to the taking of every tribute;
Farewell to every good thing from me now, save for what I receive from this body." <note>A reference to his impending death and release from physical suffering.</note>
Upon hearing Finn's mournful lament, Oscar's side heaved in a sharp pang,
He stretched out his two hands and closed his fair, bright eyes.
Finn turned his back to us and shed tears bitterly;
Save for Oscar and for **Bran** <note>Finn’s favorite hound, often given human-like status in the legends.</note>, he never shed a tear for anyone on earth.
Only myself and Finn of the Fianna were there above him;
They gave three shouts in that hour, which were heard throughout glorious Ireland.
Five score hundred, and ten and ten hundred <note>twelve thousand</note> by our own count,
Of the Fianna lay dead on the plain, with not a single man missing from the tally.
Twice that number again—and it is no lie—and the King of Ireland's story is even greater,
Were dead on the other side, of the army of Ireland of the fine weapons.
Finn was never cheerful or mild from then until the day of his death;
Because of that struggle, he cared nothing for kings being deprived of their lands.
Since that battle, the Battle of Gabhra <note>The final, cataclysmic battle that destroyed the power of the Fianna.</note>, I have not spoken with strength;
There has never been an hour or a day that I have not let out a great sigh.
->Great tonight.<- <note>original: *Mòr an nochd*; a traditional poetic closing that repeats the opening word of a poem.</note>

## The author of this below is Fergus the Poet.

High is the spirit of **Goll**, Finn's man of war,
A tall and fierce hero, whose raiding is never timid;
Noble in honor above all, noble and scarred is his side,
Powerful over the hosts is Goll, shapely and kind,
The swift son of Morna, who was valiant in the heat of battle.

<meta>This page continues the praise-poem (panegyric) begun by Fergus the Poet at the end of the previous page. Having finished the account of the Battle of Gabhra and Oscar's death, the focus shifts to a celebratory description of the hero Goll mac Morna, leader of the rival Clann Morna, who was both a rival and a core pillar of the Fianna.</meta>

# THE BOOK OF THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

His fame was of old, a man of ancient lineage <note>original: *fear geinnoll sen*; standard Gaelic: *fear ginealaigh shean*. The poet emphasizes Goll's noble pedigree.</note>,
A King of the **Fianna of Ireland**, not timid in his speech;
Not weak was his character, a great and pleasant hero.
When he goes into the battle, the path of the prince of the fight,
Though smooth is his skin, he is not soft in the conflict.
Great in his size, a high and fruitful tower <note>original: *oosi geagi torri*; likely *uas géagaibh torra*. A metaphor comparing the warrior to a sturdy, branching tree.</note>,
A clear-minded wise man, a teacher of the schools <term>Bardic Schools</term> <note>These were the centers of learning where poets, lawyers, and historians were trained.</note>;
Above the peaks of the mountains, an eagle over the headland.
In the presence of the host, his coming brings us terror.
I say to you, O Finn, do not turn your back on him <note>original: *na drillis noon*. The poet Fergus is addressing Finn mac Cumhaill, warning him of Goll's power.</note>,
For the fear of his eye weighs heavily upon us.
Without being put to flight in the battle or the field,
He attacks everyone, a leader of the host.
Gentle his face, yet the blood of men is on him;
A protector of the schools, noble in his deeds.
Fresh as a calf, a generous giver of hospitality,
His battle-spear is firm, a prince of solid character.
Dark is his hair, his breast like chalk,
His body is complete, fully filled with your love.
Vigorous is his mind, shapely is his countenance,
No other shall go above him, nor shall it be hidden from you.
He is the strength of the fortress, a sun-like champion,
Princely is his steadfastness, beautiful is his skin.
In the movement of his feats, he is not clumsy in his stance;
Generous in his gifts, terrifying in his anger.
The gentlest of men, the greatest of destroyers;
To his wealth forever, both gold and horses belong.
He does not yield to anyone, the heroic warrior.
The conqueror of the lasting fight, the joy of the women,
The murmur of the sea, the height of the waves.
A lion in valor, the heart of the heroes <note>original: *croyth na grewith*; standard: *cridhe na gcuradh*.</note>,
Generous his hand, the choice of the kings.
The joy of the road, the light of his teeth,
He puts his hand to every great deed of valor.
Steadfast the spear of the man, he has victory in conflict,
Wide is his fame, the wisdom of the stars.
A warrior of fierce memory, heroic in his time,
He offers battle, a man of surging spirit.
A hero of great arms, angry in his wrath,
His sword is fierce upon him, a leopard in his path.
A man of white shields, the choice of the love of women,
He shall be without a name if he does not always excel
In the path of life—a path that is not weak.

<summary>This page contains a "Rosg" or panegyric poem by Fergus the Poet in praise of Goll mac Morna. It uses vivid nature imagery and traditional heroic epithets to establish Goll's status as a peerless warrior and patron of the arts.</summary>
