--- Page 309 ---
<meta>This page continues the panegyric (praise-poem) for Goll mac Morna, the leader of the Clann Morna. The poem is attributed to Fergus the Poet and is part of the Fenian cycle of literature, which chronicles the deeds of Finn mac Cumhaill and his warriors. This specific section emphasizes Goll's physical beauty, his role as a patron of learning, and his terrifying prowess in battle.</meta>

## MODERN.] THE DEAN OF LISMORE.

His fame was of old, a man of noble lineage <note>original: *fear geanail sin*; likely derived from *geinelach* (genealogy) or *geanail* (pleasant/noble).</note>,
A generous King of the **Fianna** <term>Fianna: A legendary band of semi-independent warriors in early Ireland and Scotland.</term>, not timid in his glory;
Not distorted is his wisdom, a great and joyful hero.
When he goes into battle, following the path of a fierce prince,
Though his skin is smooth, he is not soft in the conflict.
His stature is not small, like a fruitful branch <note>original: *os geug an toradh*. A common metaphor in Gaelic poetry comparing a leader to a sturdy, fruit-bearing tree.</note>,
He is of the clearest heart, a teacher of the schools <term>Bardic Schools</term> <note>These schools were where poets and historians were trained; a great leader was expected to be their patron.</note>;
Above the mountain peaks, he rises in the field.
I say to you, O **Finn**, do not move against him <note>Fergus is addressing Finn mac Cumhaill, warning him of the dangers of crossing Goll.</note>,
For the fear of Goll <note>Goll mac Morna, the rival-turned-ally of Finn.</note> is great, though his mind be heavy.
He adds to his virtue, and in battle he is no weakling;
Attacking everyone, a leader of the host.
His hospitality is not small, yet he is a man of blood;
Foremost of the schools, noble is his nature.
Splendid are his people, bountiful and generous,
His battle-strides are lasting, his wisdom above a prince's.
Dark is his hair, his breast like chalk <note>The comparison of a hero's skin to chalk or lime was a standard poetic device to indicate high birth and beauty.</note>,
His body is perfect, fully filled with grace.
Ireland <note>original: *Eire*</note> under tribute, that should be his cause;
High-spirited he is, shapely in his countenance.
There is no king above Goll—I hide it not from you, O **Finn**;
The strength of the waves is in his elegant valor.
Princely still, bright is his skin,
Goll of the feats is not softened by the heat of battle.
Generous in his dealings, furious in his might,
Manly and mild, soldierly and great.
Forever shall be told of his fortune and his fury;
Lethal to others, a dexterous hero.
A pillar of lasting conflict, happiness is in his spirit;
Murmuring like the sea, fierce in the fray.
A lion in success, valiant in his deeds;
Long is his hand <note>original: *leobhar a làmh*. A "long hand" suggests a leader's reach, power, and ability to strike from afar.</note>, the choice of kings.
Success is on his path, bright are his teeth;
He inflicts a wound on every champion, however great.
Lasting is the man's reign, victory in combat is his;
Mighty in his valor, wise in his history.
A sharp-wounding, fierce hero, lethal with the blade;
Haughty is Goll, a valiant one to us.
A fierce and swift warrior, angry in his turn;
A furious look upon him, a leopard <note>original: *onchu*. This term refers to a mythical beast or a leopard/hound, used to describe a particularly ferocious warrior.</note> in the fight.
Manliest of warriors <note>original: *nan con* (of the hounds); "hound" was a common honorific for a warrior.</note>, the chosen love of women.
Scholars shall be without stain, always in his presence;
With whom the path would be, his path is not soft.

<summary>This page contains a series of poetic stanzas praising Goll mac Morna, highlighting his physical perfection, his patronage of the Bardic schools, and his overwhelming strength in battle as a warning to Finn mac Cumhaill.</summary>
<keywords>Goll mac Morna, Finn mac Cumhaill, Fergus the Poet, Fianna, Bardic Schools, Gaelic panegyric, Fenian Cycle</keywords>

--- Page 310 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise-poem for Goll mac Morna, focusing on his virtues and his rivalry with Finn mac Cumhaill, before transitioning into a new poem by Fergus the Poet concerning the tragic Battle of Gabhra. The transcription uses a unique phonetic Scots-Gaelic orthography typical of the Dean of Lismore's collection.</meta>

<header>THE BOOK OF [ANCIENT.</header>

Great is his love, his face in full bloom,
Noble his form, the source of his prosperity;
Not like the flooding stream, nor the path of the wind,
The son of Morna is swift, under the golden banner of the host <note>original: *fa orryth a zoyl*; likely *faoi ór-fhracht an t-shluaigh*, referring to his armor or standard.</note>.
In the hour of glory, he is the anvil of the trees <note>Likely a metaphor for strength, being the sturdy base upon which enemies are broken like wood.</note>,
Like a swift sea-tide, his wrath is a fierce flood.
Without contempt or reproach, a man of great forays,
The son of Tadhg is kind <note>original: *Mak teadis cheiwe*. Tadhg was a common name in the genealogy of the Fenian heroes.</note>, who never forsook his own.
Without the king's war, his hand does not weaken,
Fierce is his sword, and bold is his voice.
When he rises in anger, a strong hunter of deer,
O son of Cumhall <note>Addressing Finn mac Cumhaill.</note> the pleasant, who is equal to him in brightness?
He is a leader of the foreigners, without novelty or pride.
In the time of his day, his sleep is short,
A man without deceit, a strong hunter is he.
He did not overflow in the assembly, for his bond was firm,
Tightly bound in love, a strong leader of the pack.
He left the path of greed, he was the peace of the ranks,
Without a drop of venom, a strong foe of Finn <note>Refers to the long-standing tension between Goll's Clann Morna and Finn's Clann Baoiscne.</note>.
You were with us, recounting the tale,
My back is weary, my desire is not great.
A friend without guile, with thin red lips,
Active in the field, his fame is on high.
->High is the mind of Goll.<-

---

## The author of this is Fergus the Poet <note>original: *Farris Filli* (Fearghas Filidh). He was the brother of Oisín and the chief bard of the Fianna.</note>.

"Tell us, Fergus, poet of the **Fianna** <term>Fianna: A legendary band of semi-independent warriors in early Ireland and Scotland.</term> of Ireland <note>original: *errin* (Éirinn).</note>,
How did it go for us in the Battle of Gabhra <note>The Battle of Gabhra was the legendary final conflict that resulted in the destruction of the Fianna.</note> of the blows?"
"Not good, O son of Cumhall, is my news from the Battle of Gabhra;
Beloved Oscar <note>Finn's grandson and the most champion-like of the warriors.</note> lives no more, who won many brave victories.
The seven sons of Caoilte live no more, nor the youth of the Fianna of Allen <note>original: *alwe* (Almhuin), the headquarters of the Fianna in County Kildare.</note>,
Who fell, the youth of the Fianna, in their beautiful armor.
The son of Lughach is dead, and his six sons with their father;
The youth of the Highlands fell, and the King of Britain was slain.
The son of the King of **Scandinavia** <term>Lochlan: The Gaelic term for the lands of the Northmen, often associated with Norway.</term> fell, he who was a lingering help;
He was of a generous and manly heart, a brave hand always.
Tell me, O poet, my grandson and my soul,
How was Oscar, the cleaver of the battle-ranks?"
"It would be difficult to tell, so great was the work;
No one died in that battle who fell not by the arms of Oscar;
Neither the swift nor the joyful, nor the hawk among the flocks..."

<summary>This page concludes a poem praising the hero Goll mac Morna's character and moves into a famous lament for the Battle of Gabhra, where the Fianna were decimated. The dialogue between Finn mac Cumhaill and Fergus the Poet highlights the loss of Oscar and other key heroes.</summary>
<keywords>Goll mac Morna, Finn mac Cumhaill, Fergus the Poet, Battle of Gabhra, Oscar, Fianna, Lochlan, Gaelic poetry, lament</keywords>
